# THE EXAMINATION OF PLANTS <note>original: "Phytobasanos" - a Greek-derived title meaning "The Plant Touchstone" or "Plant Examination."</note>

->OR<-

## A HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

->IN WHICH ARE DESCRIBED VARIOUS<-
->kinds of plants that are more authentic, and more closely<-
->correspond in appearance and properties to the<-
->descriptions of the ancients—Theophrastus, Dioscorides,<-
->Pliny, Galen, and others—which have<-
->hitherto not been noticed by others.<-

->*and of new plants, by the same author.*<-

->CARDINAL OF THE HOLY ROMAN CHURCH, MARCANTONIO COLONNA.<-

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS
## AND MOST REVEREND LORD,
### CARDINAL OF THE HOLY ROMAN CHURCH,
# MARCO ANTONIO
## COLONNA. <note>Marcantonio Colonna (1523–1597) was a powerful Italian prelate and librarian of the Vatican.</note>

---

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'C' featuring a small human figure amidst floral scrolls and vines.</image-desc>

The Poets, and among them the ancient theologians, *Most Distinguished Cardinal*, imagine that *Mercury gave the Lyre—which he himself had invented—as a gift to Apollo; though it was not yet highly polished, it was nonetheless essential.* <note>original: "Mercurium inuentam sibi Lyram... Apollini dono dedisse." This refers to the mythological origin of the lyre, which Hermes (Mercury) traded to Apollo.</note> Was this not indeed a magnificent gesture? For truly, the son of Latona <note>Apollo</note> abounded more than enough in every kind of blessing, and he was able to bestow upon that instrument all its ornaments and render it almost miraculous. The son of Maia, <note>Mercury</note> however, was surely not of such great means that he could adorn such a significant gift himself, or fill the hearts of mortals with such delight. It seems to me that I have acted in a similar way, relying upon your kindness...

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the work to Cardinal Marcantonio Colonna, using the mythological metaphor of Mercury gifting the lyre to Apollo to represent the author's humble offering to a great patron.</summary>
<keywords>Marcantonio Colonna, dedication, Mercury, Apollo, mythology, patronage, lyre</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the formal dedication of the work to a high-ranking patron, following the catchword "scien-" from the previous page.</meta>

...to be, since I have offered and dedicated these inventions of mine—perhaps not useless, though by no means polished—to you, most great **Hero** <note>original: "Heros"; a common Renaissance honorific for a noble patron of exceptional virtue</note>. For who among mortals excels you in the gifts of the mind and the body? Who imparts greater distinction to affairs? Finally, who draws men to themselves as equally as you do with the wonderful **wisdom** <note>original: "sapientia"</note> with which you are endowed? Receive therefore, I beseech you, the **first fruits** <note>original: "primitias"</note> of my talent: for I have prepared them with great labors so that I might reveal my mind to you, a thing I have always longed for since my childhood. And since such things, of whatever quality they may truly be, are owed to leading men, look favorably and kindly upon this inclination of my will toward you. Indeed, if at this time they are not of great value, then with your approval, as a **memorial** <note>original: "μνημόσυνον" (mnemosynon)</note> of my respect for you, I shall perhaps undertake greater things. In the meantime, I earnestly ask you to bring aid to these efforts of mine, and to add spurs to me as I run; for truly **beautiful things are difficult** <note>original: "τὰ καλὰ χαλεπά" (ta kala chalepa); a Greek proverb suggesting that excellence requires great effort</note>. I have certainly dared this: for you were born for all things, and you alone seem most worthy of the name of a Prince, a fact which is hidden from no one. For with the great admiration of all, you sustain many affairs and preside over them diligently, and you are most learned in **Greek and Latin letters**...

<summary>The author formally dedicates his work to a noble patron, praising the recipient's wisdom and leadership while asking for support to continue his difficult intellectual labors.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, patronage, wisdom, Greek and Latin studies, intellectual labor, first fruits</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "peri-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "peritia" (skill) or "periculum" (danger/trial), continuing the discussion on the expertise required for compounding medicines.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'P' depicting a landscape or figure, possibly a scholar or traveler in a classical setting.</image-desc>

*Ancient histories testify that the knowledge of plants <note>original: "Plantarum cognitionem"</note>, and of other natural things, was celebrated among both the ancients and the moderns, and held in the highest honor, inasmuch as it is both profitable and delightful—not only to Philosophers, but even to Kings. Nor does it seem necessary for me to bring forth the utility of knowing them in this place, since anyone who has ever made use of the industry of Physicians will have it well known. And indeed, I can be a witness and affirm that nothing can provide for the safety of the human body except the practical application of this science: by which even animals devoid of reason, taught by nature—let alone men—protect themselves from the dangers of diseases and emerge strong against them. For they themselves know what is healthful for them: we, however, unless we have been taught by someone and by the experience of others, could by no means have known these things. This, moreover, is the fault of the human race, whereby it is necessary to obtain by labor not only the supports of life, but also the remedies for health. For what would it profit to live this life if we were vexed by diseases daily affecting it? For life would be no life <note>original: "βίος ἄβιος" (bios abios), a Greek wordplay meaning a life not worth living</note>. Truly, the frequent invasion of diseases—especially Epilepsy—which required me to renounce my other studies at that time, and from which I escaped by the mercy of God the Best and Greatest and the intercession of the Blessed Virgin Mother, brought my mind to this subject. For this reason, I began to turn over the writings of Hippocrates, Galen, and others: for I wished to weigh whether what the Physicians answered should be done for the cure of the disease was also confirmed by the testimonies of the ancients; indeed, I seemed thus to satisfy both my health and my soul. So also, I wished to see whether the pharmacists <term>pharmacopolæ: literally "drug-sellers," the historical predecessors to modern pharmacists</term> observed the formulas of the Physicians, and whether both the Physicians themselves and the pharmacists understood those things which are necessary for compounding syrups and medicines—namely, Plants, Minerals <note>original: "Metallica"</note>, and the rest—and the method of composition, by reason and by ex-peri-*

<summary>In this preface, the author argues for the vital importance of botanical and medical knowledge, citing his own recovery from epilepsy as the motivation for his deep study of ancient medical authorities and the practical preparation of remedies.</summary>
<keywords>botany, history of medicine, epilepsy, Hippocrates, Galen, pharmacology, minerals, providence</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the author discussing how his own struggle with illness, specifically epilepsy, turned his mind toward the study of natural remedies. This page continues his narrative of how he pursued that knowledge through both books and direct observation.</meta>

...and they recognized it through proven experience. Desiring to possess this knowledge, I wished to read through the authors who had written **histories** <note>In this context, "histories" refers to *Historia Naturalis*, or descriptive accounts of the natural world.</note> of **Plants, Animals, and Minerals**; and in them I perceived many things that were uncertain and unknown to the moderns, and even to the ancients themselves. These matters had been overlooked by translators and commentators, either through ignorance of the subject, or because the texts were mutilated and corrupted, or due to the difficulty of the words and meanings. It pleased me, applying myself to these matters as much as I could, to investigate and explain them with all possible diligence and zeal.

And before I could reach an interpretation and understanding of the more difficult matters, it was necessary for me first to know every small detail, even the most common ones—not without some labor. How much I have sweated in acquiring these things, those who have themselves been worn out by these studies will easily be able to understand. Indeed, from the beginning, it was my effort—through study, reading, and judgment, and with the highest industry and force of my intellect—to seek out and investigate the **Plants, Animals**, and other **minerals** <note>original: "metallica"</note> that came into my hands. My goal was to match them with their descriptions in the ancient texts, with no one demonstrating or teaching me—as my close associates well know. 

After I realized that I had acquired a certain number of these, and nearly the greater part of them—obtaining knowledge through the direct inspection of the objects and comparison with the written accounts—I wanted to be certain, through conversation with other professors of the same subject, whether they also knew them in the same way I did and named them by the same reasoning. Thus, I saw that I had not spent my labor in vain. 

But this did not seem sufficient to me: therefore, I decided to seek out greater things, so that I might show that I had applied myself to these studies with even greater diligence than before, and so that I might be seen to have contributed something, at least, to the common benefit of our studies. To achieve this, I began to consider the way of speaking used by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and botanist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>, his choice of words, and how he used them. So that I might do this more easily, I examined the individual words, the descriptions of similarities, and the specific meanings of the terms within them, as well as the similarities of plants in their root, leaf, stalk, flower, seed, or their entire **habit**— <note>original: "habitu," referring to the general appearance or growth form of a plant.</note>

<summary>The author describes his self-taught journey into natural history, identifying errors in ancient texts through direct observation and comparing his findings with other scholars. He announces a deep linguistic and physical study of Dioscorides to improve the common understanding of medicinal plants.</summary>

<keywords>natural history, botany, Dioscorides, scientific method, mineralogy, philology, plant morphology, Materia Medica</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s account of his systematic study of the natural world. Having previously mentioned his inspection of physical specimens, he now explains how he organized this knowledge into an index to help resolve the confusing terminology found in ancient botanical texts, specifically the work of Dioscorides.</meta>

*...of them, and finally of all other things, I have nearly completed an index. And I have used that index as a kind of guide in my investigation of plants, which has been quite useful. But there are several points to consider that might cause confusion; specifically, the words **shrub** <note>original Greek: θάμνος (*thamnos*)</note>, **little shrub** <note>original Greek: θαμνίον (*thamnion*)</note>, and **small shrublet** <note>original Greek: θαμνίσκιον (*thamniskion*)</note>, with which he names certain herbs that have no actual relation or resemblance to shrubs. For these plants are not woody or twig-like, nor do they become so with age; instead, they are succulent, tender, and leafy right from the root—characteristics of herbs, not shrubs—even if they share a similar density of branches, which is perhaps why they might be confused with shrubs. He often confuses **fruit** <note>original Greek: καρπὸν (*karpon*)</note> and **seed** <note>original Greek: σπέρμα (*sperma*)</note>. He also describes the similarity of a flower, sometimes in its shape and sometimes in its color, without providing any further distinction. Furthermore, he describes the similarity of leaves sometimes in part and sometimes as a whole, and he calls both the lobes or segments and the entire leaves simply **leaves** <note>original Greek: φύλλα (*phylla*)</note>, confusing the part with the whole. Finally, he asserts that certain plants lack flowers and fruit, when in fact they simply produce them very late or very quickly, or they are plants in which these parts are difficult to recognize because they are so small.*

*In these matters, however, it must be noted—as the same Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical reference for over 1,500 years.</note> testifies in his preface—that he did not see every plant in person (which would have been no easy task), but instead gathered information from the accounts of others and from the descriptions provided by people living in the regions where the plants grow. Therefore, it is no surprise if he made some mistakes in certain instances. To understand these errors, I spent a long time studying them, repeatedly reading the historical accounts and inspecting the physical specimens themselves. Because of this, you will find many observations in my descriptions of individual plants that clarify their true form and appearance. These observations could have been organized into a formal set of rules, but I would only seem to be repeating myself here. Having gained this knowledge, Dioscorides' descriptions became clearer to me, and it was far easier to offer a new, and I believe more accurate, interpretation of the more difficult and unknown plants. For each one, I have added the necessary reasoning and physical observations, which will make it obvious to everyone which reading of Dioscorides is more truthful, which characteristics of the plant are essential, and how they ought to correspond. Also, the descriptions of plants by other—*

<meta>catchword: descri- (likely "descriptiones" or "descriptions")</meta>

<summary>The author identifies common errors in Dioscorides’ botanical writings, such as confusing seeds with fruit or herbs with shrubs, attributing these mistakes to Dioscorides' reliance on second-hand reports. He explains that his own work aims to correct these inaccuracies through direct physical inspection of plants and rigorous comparison with ancient texts.</summary>
<keywords>Dioscorides, botanical terminology, plant classification, shrubs, herbs, scientific observation, botanical history, ancient medicine</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his methodology. Having just discussed the terminological confusion regarding shrubs and reproductive parts in ancient texts, he now describes how he uses the works of Dioscorides as a standard for comparison to correct the botanical errors of his contemporaries.</meta>

<sig>¶¶</sig>
<meta>catchword: erraf-</meta>

*...the refutation of those descriptions will be recognized, which I have placed at the end of the **History** of each plant. This was done so that once the plant I have proposed is known, and a comparison with the history provided by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the author of "De Materia Medica," the primary botanical authority for over 1,500 years.</note> has been made, it can be concluded from those facts that I have described a plant that corresponds more closely to the others, and is truer in its appearance and powers.*

*And just as goldsmiths do—having first made a mark of the finest gold on a **touchstone** <note>original: *coticula*; a small dark stone used to test the purity of precious metals by the color of the streak they leave behind</note>, they afterwards accept the proof or rejection of another, different gold through comparison—so, with the outline of Dioscorides established (which rivals a touchstone marked with the finest gold), I have compared the plant with it. Even if my plant perhaps does not seem perfect in every single part, I have no doubt that it far surpasses those described by others when compared at the end of the description; indeed, I have strived to achieve exactly that.*

*For I have recognized that names were given to many plants by others which—even if one were to use **forceps** <note>The author uses a metaphor of surgery or manual manipulation to suggest that other botanists "forced" their specimens to fit ancient descriptions by trimming or distorting the evidence</note> to make their leaves jagged or serrated—still did not seem to achieve a true likeness. For they differed either in their great size or in their smallness, as well as in their substance and powers. For many other plants, on the contrary, it would have been necessary to make the leaves much larger for them to be considered worthy of that name. For some, it was necessary to "adjust" the flowers and **seed-pods** <term>siliquas: the elongated seed-vessels characteristic of the pea or mustard families</term>; for others, the entire habit of the plant had to be changed. For this reason, it was better to substitute suitable plants in their place.*

*This stirred my mind in no small way to search for and restore plants that were more corresponding to the originals, or even the genuine ones if I could find them. I am not unaware that these will be criticized, at least in some part, by the unskilled out of rivalry and envy. To these critics, so that they may seem to have rejected my work with a fair mind, they must strive to produce plants that are truer and more corresponding in appearance and powers. And then I shall be filled with greater joy: when I recognize that someone, through rivalry with me, is applying themselves more vehemently to these studies and bringing truer things to light for the common good. It was not, in truth, to refute the plants of others, but to show truer ones, that I wrote this **account of their properties** <note>original Greek: τὴν δύναμιν (*tēn dynamin*), referring to the "faculty" or medicinal power of a plant</note>. Nor do I dare to assert these plants so stubbornly that I think better ones could never be found by someone else. I search again and again so that I myself might achieve this. For I shall not be ashamed if by chance I find a better opinion on the matter, or a truer plant...*

<summary>The author explains his "touchstone" method, using the ancient authority of Dioscorides as a standard to prove that his plant descriptions are more accurate than those of his contemporaries. He critiques previous botanists for "forcing" plants to fit names and invites future scholars to improve upon his work for the sake of the common good.</summary>

<keywords>Dioscorides, botanical methodology, touchstone, plant descriptions, seed-pods, medicinal powers, Renaissance botany, scientific accuracy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on his botanical methodology. He transitions from discussing the errors of others to admitting the possibility of his own mistakes, emphasizing the importance of direct observation and the generosity of fellow naturalists like Ferrante Imperato.</meta>

...to admit that I have erred before, or that I did not notice this; for <note>original: *αἰδέσθαι γὰρ ἐστι τὰ ἁμαρτάνειν*; a Greek sentiment suggesting that acknowledging one's errors is a mark of modesty.</note> to feel shame is to acknowledge one's errors. And indeed, he seems to me worthy of the highest praise who, despising the opinion of some men who boast wonderfully of themselves, most highly approves of <note>original: *δευτέρας φροντίδας*; "second thoughts," referring to the proverb that second thoughts are often wiser.</note> **second thoughts**. Nor are these things fixed, but are judgments confirmed by reasons, or rather by conjectures, upon which more certain signs can always follow, and by these we can recognize truer things. 

Therefore, if I attain any truth or more certain evidence after the publication of this edition which I had not noticed, as well as of plants not yet described and other matters, I promise that I will bring them forth to be joined to another edition—unless I finish and publish a new **Dioscorides** with a brief and truer commentary, along with the addition of many more new plants, which I have already begun in order to satisfy the wishes of my friends. This work cannot reach its perfection unless, through the gift and generosity of those devoted to this subject, I obtain certain plants and mineral specimens. 

Of these, <term>Ferrante Imperato</term> <note>Ferdinandus Imperatus (1550–1625), a Neapolitan apothecary and naturalist who owned one of the most famous "cabinets of curiosities" in Europe.</note>, who is most studious in this matter and should rightly be considered a <note>original: *φυσιοταμίας*; a "steward" or "treasurer of nature."</note> **steward of nature**, has given me many as a gift. I am indeed very close with him, and he reveals his affection for me through his actions; for he often gives me the opportunity to see his **Museum** and his living plants—which he nourishes in countless numbers with the greatest diligence—and he always generously offers many of them. For this reason, those who receive anything from my late-night labors should also give thanks to him; for unless I had first attained a knowledge of many plants, I would by no means have been able to investigate and choose the specimen that corresponds more closely to ancient descriptions. 

I retain many plants in my mind and as an idea which I have not yet been able to see in reality. Therefore, I frequently try to see and consider not only the plants I grow myself—of which I have fashioned <term>illustrations</term> <note>original: *icones*; these were likely "nature prints" made by inking the plant itself, a "new art" the author mentions here.</note> from the plants themselves by a certain new art I devised, and of which there is no small number—but also those growing wild, whenever I can, so that an investigation into some of them might present itself to me. For it is not enough to see cultivated plants unless you have inspected them in the place where they grow wild. Indeed, many plants degenerate through cultivation, just as even 

& apples <meta>catchword: & mala</meta>

<summary>The author emphasizes the importance of revising one's botanical work through "second thoughts" and direct observation. He credits the Neapolitan naturalist Ferrante Imperato for providing access to specimens and describes his own "new art" of creating plant illustrations directly from life.</summary>
<keywords>botanical methodology, Ferrante Imperato, Dioscorides, nature printing, plant cultivation, wild plants, scientific collaboration</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s technical discussion on the factors that influence plant growth and his philological approach to botanical texts. He transitions from discussing the generosity of other collectors to the environmental conditions that affect a plant's appearance.</meta>

¶¶ 2

...and bad cultivation: many plants also rejoice greatly in cultivation, while others indeed wither; some, unless they are illuminated and warmed by continuous rays of the sun, do not grow; while others reach perfection with little or no sight of the sun. Often, through cultivation or even neglect, plants are transformed and put forth different leaves; a change also occurs in the taste, smell, form, and size of the plants themselves, as well as their fruits and leaves. Leaves, indeed, often become narrower or broader, or more or less divided into segments <note>original: *lacinijs*; referring to the lobes or jagged edges of a leaf.</note> according to the diversity of the soil, the climate, and the cultivation. Some plants love a slender and lean soil, others a sandy one, some a gravelly one, others indeed a rich soil, and still others a muddy and well-watered one. 

For these reasons, they not only either degenerate or become more lush <note>original: *lætiores*; literally "happier," but in a botanical context meaning vigorous, healthy, or abundant.</note>, but it also happens that they become sterile. For just as plants cannot perfect their fruits when the moisture of the soil is diminished, so too if they have excessive nourishment—becoming "wanton" <note>original: *lasciuientes*; a term used for plants that grow too vigorously in foliage at the expense of fruit or seeds.</note> through an abundance of moisture—they may begin to produce plentiful fruit but later cannot bring them to full ripeness, and thus, exhausted, they perish. 

Therefore, it is necessary to observe both cultivated and wild plants, so that in the wild ones, the <term>locus natalis</term> <note>The "birthplace" or natural habitat where a species is indigenous.</note>, the plant's stature, and its <term>habitus</term> <note>The general appearance, form, or "architecture" of a plant.</note>—which are extremely necessary for the knowledge of plants—may be considered. In the cultivated ones, however, the smallest and most specific parts can be better observed; since you have them at home, you can visit them more often and inspect them from their first sprout until the perfection of their seeds, which does not happen in the same way with those growing spontaneously in the wild. 

I have observed this not a little, and it is necessary to do the same almost daily; and not only should one consider the plants themselves, but also their "histories" <note>In the Renaissance, a "history" was a complete descriptive account of a subject's properties and characteristics.</note>, especially those described by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and the author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note>. In his text, more than in the writings of others, the characteristics of plants are described with greater care. It seemed right to read these not only in Latin, but also in Greek; indeed, I would have understood many things only with great difficulty, or never at all, had I not observed the Greek words and their way of speaking. And this was the reason why I have provided the Greek text of **Dioscorides** and a word-for-word translation for each description of the plants. Many things will be identified by this translation of mine, which readers will judge to be different from others, and perhaps changed...

<meta>catchword: so [continuing 'immutatam' as 'changed/unchanged' on the following page]</meta>

<summary>The author explains how environmental factors like soil and sunlight alter a plant's physical characteristics and argues that studying garden plants allows for a more detailed observation of their life cycle than wild ones. He also emphasizes the necessity of studying Dioscorides in the original Greek to ensure accurate botanical identification.</summary>
<keywords>cultivation, plant morphology, soil types, botanical observation, Dioscorides, Greek philology, plant life cycle, habitus</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory remarks on the physical variations of plants caused by environment and cultivation, transitioning into a philological critique of how these variations led to the corruption of ancient botanical texts, specifically those of Dioscorides. It then opens a dedicatory poem by Francesco Mauro to the reader and his patron, Girolamo Colonna.</meta>

...so corrupted and distorted. The reason for this suspicion will surely vanish if the Latin words are compared with the Greek ones. For the Greek words serve the convenience of comparison. It remains for me to show that Dioscorides’ *History* <note>original: *Historiam*; referring to the foundational pharmacological text *De Materia Medica*.</note> was corrupted and adulterated by later professors of plants, who added those diverse lists of synonyms <note>original: *nomenclaturas*</note>. Deceived by the commonality and variety of these names, others added many chapters inscribed with different titles, in which the same plants are described, differing only in name. Others, having removed the original descriptions of their properties, substituted different, even contradictory ones, taken from chapters regarding entirely different plants. They even moved many chapters from one place to another, which are also suspect in the judgment of other more learned men. For this reason, we can hardly assert with certainty what should be decided about those plants; however, what I have reached through conjectures may be seen in their proper places. Therefore, receive these labors of mine <note>original: *lucubrationes*; literally "work done by lamplight," signifying deep scholarly effort.</note> with a cheerful heart, and expect as soon as possible, God favoring, what I have promised.

# PLANTS
## OF SOME ANCIENTS
### HISTORY OF THOSE THAT MORE CLOSELY RESPOND TO ANCIENT DESCRIPTIONS.
### BY FABIO COLONNA.

---

-><image-desc>Typographic ornament featuring symmetrical floral and scroll motifs.</image-desc><-

->*The Isopyrum of Dioscorides.*<-

<margin>Name and Etymology.</margin>
It is called *Isopyron*, from "fire" <note>Greek: *pyr*</note>, not from "wheat" <note>Greek: *pyros*</note>: because it produces a flower similar in shape to a flame. For the flower has its tips gathered together in such a way that it represents the flames of a fire; for from a broad base, those five uppermost parts end in a point, being twisted and bent inward so that they resemble a flame tossed by the wind. Indeed, the flowers are pyramidal, which is why it is called "fire-like" <note>original: *pyroeides*</note> from the word for "fire," having the appearance of fire. From this we get the word "pyramids," because they end in a point from a wide base in the manner of a flame. Others, however, call it *Phasiolon* <note>likely referring to a type of kidney bean or a plant with similar curved parts</note> because of the resemblance; for as Dioscorides says, 
<margin>Book 4.</margin>
*at the tip, the leaf produces a tendril* <note>original Greek: *kat’ akron to petalon helika pherei*</note>. Here, "leaf" <note>original: *petalon*</note> must be understood as the petal of the flower; for the same
<margin>Book 1.</margin>
Dioscorides, in the chapter on Rose Ointment, says, *one thousand petals of dry roses* <note>original Greek: *Rhodon abrochon arithmo chilia petala*</note>. The word *helika* means a "tendril" <term>capreolus: a small climbing organ of a plant, like a vine's curly shoot</term>, which Pliny translates here as a "vine-shoot" <note>original Latin: *pampinus*</note>, as he does elsewhere. Thus, the meaning is: the petal of the flower produces a tendril at the top (if you consider its position) or at its very end (if you consider the part itself), and it is bent in the manner of a tendril. This becomes clearer from Pliny's text, for he says: *the leaf is twisted into vine-shoots*: the flo- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the catchword "ris" indicates the next word is "floris" or "flores" (flowers).</note>

<summary>This page introduces Fabio Colonna's botanical study, specifically identifying the 'Isopyrum' described by ancient Greeks. Colonna argues that the plant's name derives from its flame-like appearance rather than a resemblance to wheat.</summary>
<keywords>Fabio Colonna, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, botany, Isopyrum, etymology, plant anatomy, Renaissance science</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical analysis of the plant *Isopyrum*. He is currently reconciling the Greek descriptions of Dioscorides and the Latin of Pliny with his own observations, specifically focusing on how the plant’s "tendrils" or "curls" define its name.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

...ends, namely, at the tip into a vine-shoot—that is, a tendril—and is twisted in the manner of a little vine. There is a similarity between the *Phasiolon* <note>The ancient name for a type of kidney bean, *Phaseolus*</note> and the *Isopyron*, such that the flowers of the *Isopyron*, bent into tendrils, almost represent the position of the flowers of the *Phasiolon* which hang down together from their stalks. This is very beautifully and exactly expressed by the plant I have proposed, and it seems to fulfill the entire account given by Dioscorides, who says:

<margin>Description.</margin>
*Isopyron, which some call Phasiolon, because it resembles the kidney-bean, in that it bears a spiral <note>original Greek: helika</note> at the tip of its petal. There are thin heads at the top of the stalk, full of tiny seeds, similar to Black Cumin <note>original Greek: melanthio</note> in taste; and the leaf is like Anise.* <note>Colonna provides the Greek text: Ἰσόπυρον, οἱ δὲ φασίολον καλοῦσιν, ἀπὸ τοῦ ἐοικέναι φασιόλῳ, τῷ κατ' ἄκρον τὸ πέταλον ἕλικα φέρειν. ἔπεισι δὲ κεφαλαὶ ἐπ' ἄκρου τοῦ καυλοῦ, λεπταὶ, σπερματίων πλήρεις, παραπλησίων μελανθίῳ τὴν γεῦσιν . τὸ δὲ φύλλον ἀνίσῳ .</note> These words I have rendered into Latin as: *Isopyron, some call Phasiolon, since it is like the Phasiolus, because at the tip it produces a leaf-tendril. Moreover, there are thin heads at the top of the stem, full of small seeds, similar to Black Cumin in taste: the foliage, however, is like Anise.* But as far as I have seen, no one until now has handed down the distinction between the foliage of the plant and the "leaf" of the flower <note>Colonna is noting that ancient authors often used the word "leaf" (*folium* or *petalon*) to refer to what we now call petals</note>. Jean Ruel <note>original: Ruellius (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist</note>, a most learned man, in order to appear to have translated Dioscorides into very pure Latin and to avoid the charge of ignorance, described a mutilated or corrupted text of Pliny as his 

<margin>Book 27, chapter 11.</margin>
translation, which says: *Isopyron, some call Phasiolon, because the leaf, which is like Anise, is twisted into tendrils.* The meaning of this, to make it agree with the text of Dioscorides from which this passage was transcribed, should be: *Isopyron, which is like Anise (understand this as referring to the leaves), some call Phasiolon, because the leaf (namely, of the flower) is twisted into tendrils.* And I believe it should be read in this way.

<margin>Form.</margin>
This plant has a fibrous root, somewhat thick and pale; the leaves are the shape of Anise leaves, but larger, and of a bluish-green color, smooth, growing on a long, thin, purple, and hairy stalk <term>petiole: the stalk that joins a leaf to a stem</term>. They are tripartite <note>divided into three parts</note>, so that they seem to have three leaves; but each leaf, when the plant is two years old (for it is not completed in one year), is divided into threes with their own stalks; these also appear divided into threes because of very deep clefts, coming forth not from three stalks, but from a single origin. It produces a hairy stem, reddish, round, thin, striated, and angled, two feet tall, sometimes larger. At the top of the stem and the branches, from those leaves that cling to the stem without stalks and are divided into three so that they look like "three-leaves" <note>clover-like</note>, thin stalks come forth in the manner of the *Phaseolus* <note>Kidney bean</note>. Because of the weight of the flowers hanging from their tips, these stalks are seen to be curved while the flowers are in bloom. These flowers seem to be composed of five flowers of the so-called *Consolida regia* <note>Larkspur</note> joined together:

<meta>catchword: tur:</meta>

<summary>Colonna identifies the plant Isopyrum by correcting previous translations of Dioscorides and Pliny, arguing that the "tendrils" described by ancients refer to the flower petals rather than the leaves. He provides a detailed morphological description of the plant's roots, leaves, and stem to support his identification.</summary>
<keywords>Isopyrum, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Jean Ruel, botanical nomenclature, plant morphology, Phasiolon, Anise, Larkspur, Black Cumin</keywords>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...they hang down in such a way that their tendrils—that is, the thinner twisted parts—are higher than the base. Thus, they seem to represent a sort of appearance of flames, not only in form (since those five outermost parts are bent upward in the manner of a little vine and reflect the appearance of flames), but also in color. For those that are red appear like flames, though others are seen in various colors: blue, white, yellow, and others of multiple colors, yet all possess the same form. They mimic the arrangement of the flowers of the *Phasiolus* <note>The kidney bean, *Phaseolus vulgaris*</note> and the flames of a fire, differing only in color and the number of small blossoms. When these fade, the flower stalks <note>term: petioli</note> gradually rise day by day, forming small heads similar to those of *Melanthium* <note>Black Cumin, *Nigella sativa*</note>, but not so swollen; rather, they are narrower, thinner, and divided as if into several parts. They are full of small black seeds that are angular, having the form and color of the common Black Cumin, but smooth and shiny. In flavor and taste, they are somewhat bitter and a little harsh—as Galen maintains—

<margin>Book 6, *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*</margin>
recalling the seeds of Black Cumin. Indeed, if you were to taste the seeds of both plants, you would perceive little or no difference in flavor, and you would hardly know which seed you were tasting unless the Black Cumin were sharper. Nor is the same sharpness felt in the seeds of Black Cumin, as Dioscorides says, as is found in the Yellow variety, although we cannot deny that heat is present in them. Pliny, however, unwilling to dispute the matter of taste, wrote:

> The heads at the top of the stalk are thin, full of the seed of Black Cumin;

which seems to refer to the form. But this plant upholds the descriptions of both authors, since it reflects the form and taste of Black Cumin, satisfying both the history of Dioscorides and the variety noted by Pliny.

<margin>Time.</margin>
It flowers in the month of April and reaches maturity in May. 
<margin>Place.</margin>
It is cultivated here by scholars and in gardens on account of the elegance of its flowers. In Germany, France, and England, it is asserted to grow on hills, mountains, and in meadows.

<margin>Powers.</margin>
Dioscorides attributes these powers to it:

> The seed of this plant, drunk with honey-water <note>original: aqua mulsa</note>, soothes pains around the chest and calms coughs. It is also suitable for those spitting blood and those with liver complaints.

Pliny transcribed these same powers into his own work, with the exception of the power to stop blood.

<margin>Book 6, *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*</margin>
Galen, however, writes:

> *Isopyrum* or *Phasiolum* has a bitter and somewhat harsh seed. It therefore cleanses and cuts <note>A medical term meaning to thin or break down</note> thick humors, while also causing the body to contract and tighten. For this reason, it is beneficial for clearing the chest, purges the liver, and is not adverse to those spitting blood; indeed, it is believed to be helpful.

For because of its mixed temperament, it seems to be suitable even for contrary conditions. Modern writers <note>original: Neoterici</note> call it by the names *Aquilegia* and *Columbina* <note>The modern Columbine flower</note>, perhaps from a corrupted word...

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>The catchword "onis-" suggests the next page continues with the word "passionis" or a similar derivation regarding the naming of the plant.</meta>

<summary>Colonna identifies the plant "Isopyrum" described by ancient authorities as the modern Columbine, detailing its flame-like flowers, its similarity to Black Cumin seeds, and its medicinal uses for chest and liver ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Isopyrum, Columbine, Aquilegia, Black Cumin, Melanthium, Dioscorides, Pliny, Galen, botanical description, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical argument regarding the plant Isopyrum. On the previous page, Colonna compared the seeds and taste of his specimen to Black Cumin (Melanthium) and cited Galen’s observations on its warming properties.</meta>

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

They attributed these powers to the *aselion* <note>original: ασήλιον</note>, or *phaxiolon* <note>original: φαξίολον</note>; namely, that it can be usefully given to women for obstructions of the liver and gall bladder in cases of the "royal disease" <note>term: royal disease (regio morbo) — a historical term for jaundice, so named because it was thought to require "regal" treatments or because of the golden color of the skin</note>. One drachm <note>original: 3. i.</note> is given with an obol <note>a small Greek weight</note> of Saffron. These powers do not differ from those described by Dioscorides <note>An ancient Greek physician and botanist</note>; they even recommend it for the pestilential plague. Pena <note>Pierre Pena, a 16th-century French botanist</note> states that the seed is slightly astringent in taste, and of a tempered dryness and heat, and therefore the common people use it for inflammation of the throat, pharynx, and windpipe, and for small ulcers. That species of *Melanthium* <note>Black Cumin</note> is not the *Isopyrum* of Dioscorides, nor is the Marsh Clover, since there is no characteristic in that plant that corresponds to the notes of Dioscorides. I am amazed that Dodoens <note>Rembert Dodoens, a famous Flemish physician and botanist</note>, a most learned man, was so mistaken as to want to pervert and change the whole text of Dioscorides here, failing to perceive his meaning and words, as well as Pliny’s translation; and therefore, what he did not understand, he judged to be excessive and superfluous. Nor is the Indian Bladder-plant <note>term: Vesicaria Indica — likely the Balloon Vine (*Cardiospermum halicacabum*)</note>, called Heart-pea or Indian-heart, to be thought of as *Isopyrum* (according to Pena, following a friend's opinion); for it is a creeping plant, and one cannot correctly speak of the "top of the stalk" in it, as gathered from the chapter on *Chamaesyce* <note>Prostrate Spurge</note>. In that chapter, Dioscorides says it does not have a stem, but rather little vines spread across the ground, and the flower is not twisted into tendrils, nor does it have thin little heads, but rather large bladders, not with small seeds but with three large seeds in each bladder, and they are full of air. Even the taste, in the author's judgment, is different—namely, that of a Pea—nor has anyone found its powers to match those of Dioscorides; rather, all doubt whether it is a species of *Halicacabum* <note>Winter Cherry or Physalis</note>, and thus it is named from the shape of its little pod. Nor does it have the petal <note>original: πέταλον - petalon</note> of the *Phasiolus* <note>Kidney Bean</note>, which some also interpret as a leaf; Pena, being ignorant of this himself, objects to Dodoens as if he understood it. But I have now demonstrated that until now everyone was ignorant of this. Therefore, the plant proposed by me must be considered the *Isopyrum* of Dioscorides, since there is nothing that can oppose it; indeed, it clarifies its own history, which until now was shrouded in darkness due to the difficulty of the words and meaning, which had not been unraveled by others, nor even by the translators.

FABIO COLONNA

# *The Malinathalle of the Egyptians described by Theophrastus, and the Anthalium of Pliny.*

<margin>
Name.
Book 4, chapter 10.
Description.
</margin>
AMONG the edible roots that Egypt produces, the *Malinathalles* are numbered by <term>Theophrastus</term><note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school; often called the "father of botany."</note>. Indeed, speaking above about those plants which arise in water, he says: <note>original Greek: "Ἐν δὲ τοῖς ἀμμώδεσι χωρίοις..."</note> *In sandy places, not far from the river, there grows in the earth what is called the Malinathalle. It is round in shape, about the size of a medlar, without a stone or a rind; it puts out leaves like those of the galingale.*

<margin>
Book 21, chapter 15.
Etymology.
</margin>
Pliny, borrowing from this text, transcribed *Anthalium* for *Malinathalle*. In Greek this would be written as *anthalion*, composed from *ana* and *thaleo* or *thallo*, which means "to sprout again." This differs little from the word *malinathalle*, which is forged from the adverb *mala* <note>meaning "very" or "greatly"</note> and *anathallo* <note>meaning "to sprout again"</note>. It thus signifies "to sprout greatly," which is the proper etymology for this plant; for when a single root is planted, it propagates itself like the <term>Chondrilla</term><note>A genus of plants in the dandelion tribe, known for spreading via roots.</note> of Dioscorides and produces innumerable others. Pliny's words are these: *The Anthalium grows further from the river, having the size and roundness of a medlar, without a stone, without a rind, and with the leaf of a galingale.* This plant seems to be entirely similar—and indeed the same—as the one called *Trasi*, also known as the sweet and edible <term>Cyperus</term><note>Likely referring to Cyperus esculentus, the Tiger Nut or Chufa.</note>.

<margin>
Form.
</margin>
For it has small globules hanging from thin fibers; these are smooth, round, and oblong, bulging more in some parts, and having a divided tip like the fruit of a <term>Pomegranate</term>, mimicking the "crown" of a <term>medlar</term><note>The medlar fruit (Mespilus germanica) has a distinctive open calyx at the top that looks like a hollow crown.</note>. As can be seen in the illustration, they are marked with a few lines drawn obliquely around them, as if they were composed of several parts joined together. In size and shape, they approach the fruit of the smaller medlar of the "Setanean" variety. We call this the "boneless medlar" here because it has stones that are soft and fit for eating. The exterior part also reflects the color of the medlar. This exterior cannot be peeled off, and therefore they can be said to be "without rind"—since a rind, properly speaking, is that which can be stripped off as useless and does not hinder the inner part or the fruit. Inside, they contain a solid, white flesh, without a stone...

<meta>catchword: ...stone (nucleo)</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna identifies the ancient Egyptian "Malinathalle" and Pliny’s "Anthalium" as the same plant, providing an etymological analysis and comparing it to the Tiger Nut (Trasi) and the medlar fruit.</summary>
<keywords>botany, Theophrastus, Pliny the Elder, Malinathalle, Anthalium, Tiger Nut, Chufa, medlar, Cyperus, etymology, Egyptian plants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Malinathalle (Tiger Nut), moving from its etymology to its physical characteristics, habitat, historical culinary uses, and medicinal properties. ### HISTORY OF SOME PLANTS

[...with a certain kernel], with a sweet taste almost like <term>chestnuts</term><note>The author is comparing the flavor of the Tiger Nut to the sweet chestnut common in Europe.</note>: <margin>Time.</margin> it puts out leaves like our common <term>galingale</term><note>Cyperus, a genus of sedges.</note>, but longer; with a stem and flowers similar to it in summer. <margin>Location.</margin> A rare plant here, nor is it seen cultivated elsewhere except in the hanging gardens of scholars, brought from the furthest and sandy shores of Sicily exposed to the South wind, where it grows spontaneously. Its origin seems to confirm the plant, since it is said by <term>Theophrastus</term><note>A Greek philosopher often called the "father of botany."</note>, *“In sandy places, etc.”* to differentiate it from plants growing in muddy and swampy places, of which he had spoken before, and therefore he added: *“it grows in the earth not far from the river, etc.”* <term>Pliny</term><note>Pliny the Elder, author of the Natural History.</note>, truly, to distinguish it from the <term>Colocasia</term><note>Egyptian bean or taro.</note> and the <term>Personata</term><note>Burdock or a similar large-leaved plant.</note>, which he says enjoy the gifts of his Nile, recorded that it: *“arises further from the river.”* From these and the previous accounts, it is gathered that the Malinathallen loves sandy and southern places that are not too dry. *“The inhabitants,”* Theophrastus continues, *“gathering these, boil them in <term>barley beer</term><note>original: "bryto hordeaceo"</note>; they become quite sweet, and they use them for desserts.”* Pliny, <margin>Book 21, chapter 29.</margin> says: *“they chew it prepared by fire,”* and elsewhere, *“Of the Anthalium, which the Egyptians eat, I have found no other use.”* <term>Matthiolus</term><note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a renowned 16th-century Italian physician and botanist.</note>, however, asserts that it grows in the territory of Verona; and there children carry around full baskets for sale, and they call them **Dolce Trasi**; they eat them raw and cooked as desserts. <margin>Potency.</margin> Their cream, as Matthiolus records, purges the ailments of the chest and sides, which makes them very useful for those coughing. For they are pounded and steeped in meat broth, and from there the cream is squeezed through a linen cloth; some modern writers even mix this into <term>aphrodisiac potions</term><note>original: "venereis potionibus"</note>. This same cream, when drunk, is effective for the burning of urine and for dysentery, for it blunts the sharpness of the humors, especially if drunk with water in which glowing iron has been quenched. These roots are, as can be conjectured by experience and their sweetness, of the kind that are moderately heating and moistening. But they do somewhat produce gas.

<margin>Book 6, On the Causes of Plants, chapter 16.</margin> There are those who judge that these are the edible Galingales of Theophrastus, wherefore they cite his text, in which he gives the reason why, if the grace of taste and smell is present in the roots of plants, their other parts correspond so little; and by way of example he cites, *“Grass, Galingale, Beet, Celery, Water-parsley, and those things which grow suitable for food in marshes and rivers.”* <margin>Book 1, ch. 16; Book 4, ch. 9 & 11, On Plants; Book 6, ch. 17, On the Causes of Plants.</margin> At which point it is replied that Theophrastus nowhere taught that Galingales differ in their roots and taste. But he always numbers them among the marsh plants and those living in water, and says they have fragrant roots; nor would he have listed Galingale among the fragrant plants in the following chapter,

<summary>This page describes the physical traits, habitat (Sicily and Egypt), and culinary uses of the Tiger Nut (Malinathalle/Anthalium), noting its sweet, chestnut-like flavor and its medicinal use in soothing coughs and digestive issues.</summary>
<keywords>Tiger Nut, Cyperus esculentus, Theophrastus, Pliny, Matthiolus, Dolce Trasi, Egyptian botany, medicinal plants, Sicily, galingale</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical investigation into the "Trasi" or Tiger Nut, specifically debating its identification within the classical texts of Theophrastus, Pliny, and Dioscorides. Colonna is here addressing a potential error in the Greek manuscripts and their Latin translations regarding the naming of sedges (Cyperus) versus sword-lilies (Gladiolus).</meta>

# OF FABIO COLONNA #

in such a short interval, without any mention of a difference, if he had spoken in the preceding chapter about another **Cyperus**—namely, the sweet kind with odorless roots that also differ in shape—for which this **Trasi** plant might be understood; since what he had written about the former **Cyperus** in the preceding chapter could not have slipped from his memory so quickly. Therefore, I first held this opinion: namely, that the text in which **Cyperus** is numbered among the sweet roots is corrupt. I thought that instead of *kypeirou* <note>original: "κυπείρου"</note>—spelled with the diphthong *ei*, which is **Cyperus** in Latin—it should be replaced with *kypirou* <note>original: "κυπίρου"</note>—spelled with an *i*, which refers to the **Gladiolus** <term>Gladiolus</term><note>Literally "little sword," referring here to the Corn Flag or sword-lily, which has a bulbous root.</note>. For **Theodore Gaza** <note>A 15th-century Greek scholar who translated many of Aristotle’s and Theophrastus’s works into Latin.</note> in other passages translated **Cyperus** not without error, confusing **Cyperus** and **Cypirus**, and always interpreting it as **Gladiolus**. And moved by the authority of **Pliny**, I thought the Greek text should be corrected in that place to read *kypirou* instead of *kypeirou*. In other places, he seems to speak of the aromatic **Cyperus**,

<margin>
Book 1, ch. 4.
Book 21, ch. 18.
</margin>
namely, the triangular <term>Rush</term><note>A type of wetland plant; Colonna is identifying the Cyperus as a triangular-stemmed sedge.</note> called "marsh-rush," about which **Dioscorides** and **Pliny** wrote. Pliny's words are these: *"Certain people even now make one genus of triangular Rush, which they call **Cyperon**. Many do not distinguish it from **Cypirus** because of the similarity of the name. We shall distinguish both: **Cypirus** is the **Gladiolus**, as we said, with a bulbous root, etc."* and further down: *"**Cyperus** is a Rush, as we said,* 
<margin>
Book 21, ch. 17.
</margin>
*angular."* And elsewhere: *"The same is also another **Gladiolus**, which they call **Cypiron**, and it is itself a marsh plant."* And in the same chapter above: *"To the genus of bulbs some also add the root of the **Cypirus**, that is, of the **Gladiolus**. It is sweet, and when boiled it makes bread even more pleasant and heavier when kneaded into it."* From these accounts, it is gathered that **Theophrastus** is by no means to be understood as referring to the **Trasi** plant in that passage: for if *kypeirou* (**Cyperus**) is used there—since it has fragrant roots, differing in shape, and is a marsh plant that delights more in muddy places—it will be **Cyperus**. And it must be understood as the one which lives in water, not in stony places, of which **Theophrastus** always speaks; which, because of the greater abundance of moisture taken from the water, yields a sweeter flavor, somewhat astringent, fragrant, fit for food, and pleasant—understand this to be when it is fresh. For when dried, it becomes slightly bitter and more fragrant; for in a dry state, there is no sweetness, as the same **Theophrastus** says. And **Dioscorides** understands it this way in the
<margin>
Book 1.
Book 4.
</margin>
chapter on **Cyperus**. For elsewhere, speaking of the fresh root, he compares the root of the **Chrysocome** <term>Chrysocome</term><note>Goldilocks or a similar yellow-flowered plant.</note> to it, saying it is sweet and somewhat harsh, in these words: *not unpleasant to the taste, being like Cyperus, with a slightly harsh quality within the sweetness.* <note>original Greek: "γλυκυδίῳ οὐκ ἀπηνῆ, κυπείρῳ ἴσω οὖσαν, ἐν τῷ γλυκεῖ ὑπαύκυρον"</note>

<meta>catchword: exi-</meta>

<summary>Colonna analyzes textual corruptions in classical botanical works, arguing that scholars like Theodore Gaza confused the aromatic Cyperus (sedge) with the edible Cypirus (Gladiolus). He uses these distinctions to clarify the identity of the Trasi plant (Tiger Nut) based on habitat and flavor profiles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical analysis of the "Trasi" (Tiger Nut), concluding the argument regarding its identification with the ancient "Malinathallen" of Theophrastus before moving on to a new section on the plant Chondrilla as described by Dioscorides.</meta>

# THE HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS #

existing as somewhat astringent from its sweetness; and thus the **Trasi** <note>The Tiger Nut or Chufa (*Cyperus esculentus*).</note> cannot be that which has no astringency, and it grows in sandy places, both in Sicily and Egypt, in which locations it is most frequent and wild. From there it was perhaps brought to us and to Verona, where it is cultivated in rich and well-watered soil, as **Pena** <note>Pierre Pena, a 16th-century French physician and botanist who co-authored the *Stirpium Adversaria Nova*.</note> says. But this plant, when sown in rich soil, is easily ruined, for worms eat away at the root; however, in sandy and gravelly soil, worms are hardly produced, and the plant comes forth more joyfully, though the place must be frequently irrigated unless it is naturally watered. Therefore, since **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany."</note> seems to speak of the **Cyperus** <term>Cyperus</term><note>A genus of sedges; here specifically the edible variety.</note> in that passage, it is no longer doubtful that the **Trasi** plant is not the **Cyperus** of Theophrastus, as it fully corresponds to his **Malinathallen** in form, flavor, origins, and use—specifically in desserts—as Pena and everyone else says, being even now sought after everywhere. Some object to this plant because its size is not equal to a **Medlar** <note>A small fruit (*Mespilus germanica*) that looks like a large rosehip.</note>, and they understand it to be larger; to whom I respond that it is enough to approach any species of Medlar. For out of the three kinds of Medlars of Theophrastus, it approaches the smaller species of the *Setanean* <note>A specific variety of medlar mentioned by ancient authors.</note>, which we, as I said, call the "stoneless medlar." And perhaps it is equal to or larger than a third kind unknown to us, which is of less account; and it can be said that in Egypt the roots become larger due to the suitability of the location, wherefore there is no need to deliberate further on this. Some, not knowing the etymology of **Anthallium** and **Malinathalles**, write "Apple Anthallium" <note>original: "Malum Anthalium"</note>, and think it is named so, while others point to a certain exotic plant as **Malinathalle** which has a fruit equal to a walnut—the ignorant may decide whether that should be accepted.

### The first Chondrilla of Dioscorides ###

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a human figure or cherub amidst decorative floral and scrolled designs.</image-desc>

<margin>Etymology.</margin>
<margin>Book 2.</margin>
<margin>Book 22, ch. 22.</margin>
<margin>Form and Place.</margin>
**Chondrilla** <note>Likely Gum Succory (*Chondrilla juncea*), a plant known for its milky sap and resinous "lumps."</note> was named from *chondros* <note>original: "χόνδρος"</note>, which name signifies a "lump" or "grain," and *illein* <note>original: "ἴλλειν"</note>, which means "to contain," as if "containing lumps," which **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and the author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary pharmacological text of the era.</note> says are similar to **Mastic** <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree (*Pistacia lentiscus*).</note> in these words: *around the small branches themselves you will find a gum similar to mastic* <note>original: "περὶ δὲ τοῖς κλωναρίοις αὐτοῖς εὑρίσκετε κόμμι ὅμοιον μα- στίχῃ"</note>. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of *Natural History*.</note> indeed says it has mastic closest to the earth with a tubercle the size of a bean, etc. This plant is seen here in summer on dry road embankments, having its leaves spread on the ground, which burst forth in spring, at first similar to **Chicory**, but after-

-> **Chondrilla** <note>original: *Χονδρίλλη* (*Chondrille*). This name, derived from the Greek word for "lump" or "grain," refers to the gummy resin that forms on the stems of the plant, commonly known today as Skeletonweed.</note> <-

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of a plant previously compared to chicory-like herbs, focusing here on its root, sap, and identification with classical sources.</meta>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

[the leaves] become more jagged, and approach the plants called **Sow-thistle** or **Dandelion**; they are fleshy, easy to break, and full of milk. The root is long: I found one equaling three feet in length, and as thick as a pinky finger. It is yellowish on the outside and whitish on the inside, swollen with milk. If you dig it up, no matter how much care you take, it will at least be bent, and then it splits crosswise in many places, and a viscous milk comes out, which after a short interval—less than a quarter of an hour—hardens like gum. In the root, you will always find little clods of earth hardened by this same milk, which exuded while the plant was more vigorous; this sap is so sticky that, while fresh, it cannot be washed off the fingers with water. When the stem of this plant breaks through, the leaves that were spread on the ground wither in almost all cases; for this reason, you will rarely find the plant having them, or only a few wilting ones. The stem sometimes reaches a height of three feet; it is hairy and rough near the ground, but the rest is entirely smooth, round, and filled with many woody, flexible branches, so that from a single plant you could fashion a broom, much like **Broom** <note>original: *Genista*</note>, which it somewhat resembles in color and habit. It has small, thick leaflets at alternating intervals, without jagged edges, similar to those of **Toadflax** <note>original: *Linaria*</note> or Broom, turning from green toward blue. From each of these, yellow flowers emerge in summer, like those of cultivated **Chicory**, small, flying away in <term>pappus<gloss>the feathery bristles on seeds, like those of a dandelion, that allow them to be carried by the wind</gloss></term>
<margin>Season.</margin>
once the seed has ripened. The seed is oblong, tawny, and hairy. The whole plant has a bitter taste. I have judged that this should be established as the first *Chondrilla* of Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD)</note>, since it seems to agree with his description; for Dioscorides says:
<margin>Description.</margin>

> *Chondrilla*, which some also call *Cichorion* <note>Chicory</note> or *Seris* <note>Endive/Succory</note>, has leaves, a stem, and flowers similar to Chicory, for which reason some have said it is a species of wild *Seris*, but the whole plant is more slender. Around the small branches themselves is found a gum similar to Mastic <note>A resin obtained from the mastic tree</note>, the size of a bean, etc.

Galen <note>An influential Roman physician and philosopher of Greek origin</note>, describing the same plant, says:
> It is a certain vegetable similar to wild lettuce, which they name *Chondrilla*, and it breaks into a stem more quickly; it has a manifest bitterness and a certain viscous, white juice, etc.

With which characteristics [this plant] seems to agree.

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: these</meta>

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical description of Chondrilla (Gum Succory), noting its long root and the sticky latex that hardens into gum. The author identifies the specimen as the "first Chondrilla" described by the ancient authorities Dioscorides and Galen.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the plant Chondrilla, specifically discussing the unique properties of its sap and comparing Colonna's findings with classical authorities like Dioscorides, Pliny, and Galen. The previous page ended discussing the plant's seeds and appearance.</meta>

# OF FABIO COLONNA

<meta>catchword: ordina-</meta>

...these things, for the whole plant is filled with a viscous and white juice; and if you bend any part—the root, leaves, stem, branches, or even the flowers—a milky juice exits by force from the crack. A little while later, this juice hardens with the hardness and color of **Mastic** <note>original: *Maſtiches*; a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used historically as an adhesive and medicine</note>, though it turns black after a long time. And although **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> says that the juice thickens, **Marcello Virgilio** <note>Marcello Virgilio Adriani (1464–1521), a Florentine scholar who translated Dioscorides</note>—following **Pliny**, who says *the Mastic nearest the earth*—interprets this as occurring on the lowest branches. **Galen**, however, did not record this hardening, but only that the plant was filled with much viscous milk. Since this hardening does not always happen, it can occur either from an abundance of overflowing moisture (and this is always found in the root) or by some external force, such as a strong wind or some other cause arising from four-footed animals grazing there, by which the branches were bent; thus, in that curved part, a crack is made, and from there the milk emerges, which is afterwards found hardened, as I have observed many times, and not only on the stem, but on the branches and foliage. Indeed, in the root you will always find it, and in many parts you will find earth clinging to it; you will see new milk come out if you bend it slightly, and see it harden into what I said resembles Mastic. I have shown this to many friends who did not believe it at all, as they had never observed it, for they considered this plant of no value. You will find that none of the other plants marked by others with the name <term>Chondrilla<gloss>from the Greek 'chondros' meaning lump or grain, referring to the way its sap hardens</gloss></term> do this; those plants certainly have a milky juice, but it is not viscous, nor does it easily harden by its nature, even over a long time. Therefore, you should no longer call those plants Chondrilla, but say that this is the first Chondrilla of Dioscorides, and know that the powers attributed to it by Dioscorides are present in it, for he says:

<margin>Powers.</margin>
*There is found around the branches themselves a gum similar to Mastic, the size of a bean. Which, when ground with Myrrh to the size of an olive and applied on a linen cloth, draws out the menses. The herb itself, crushed together with the root and mixed with honey, is formed into lozenges, which, when dissolved with added nitre <note>likely natron or saltpeter used as a cleansing agent</note>, wipe away skin spots. The gum also glues hairs together. But the fresh root also serves the same purpose if a needle is dipped into it and applied to the hairs. It is also beneficial against vipers when drunk with wine. Its juice stops the bowels if boiled with wine and drunk, or by itself. Pliny wrote of it thus: It has the Mastic nearest the earth in a small bean-sized lump, which, when applied, is said to draw out the menses of women. Crushed entirely with the roots, it is divided into lozenges, a proven argument against serpents. For indeed, field mice, when harmed by these, are said to eat this. The juice of the plant boiled in wine stops the bowels. It also holds the hairs of the eyelids in place...*

<vocab>Chondrilla, Dioscorides, Pliny, Galen, Mastic, gum, Myrrh, viper, vitiligo, powers</vocab>

<summary>Fabio Colonna identifies the "true" Chondrilla of antiquity by its unique viscous sap that hardens like mastic resin, a feature he uses to distinguish it from lookalikes and validate the medicinal uses described by Dioscorides and Pliny.</summary>

->PLANT. ALIQVOT HIST.<-
<meta>catchword: most orderly</meta>

...most orderly, it most effectively rules in place of gum. **Dorotheus** <note>likely Dorotheus of Sidon, a 1st-century poet who wrote on medicinal plants</note> declared it useful for the stomach and for digestion in his poems. Some have thought it harmful to women and the eyes, and to the reproductive power of men. **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note>, however, says of **Chondrilla**—and some call this *Serin* <note>original: *Serin*; likely referring to a type of chicory or endive</note>—that it possesses almost the same faculty as that other variety, except that it is somewhat bitter in taste and therefore has a drier quality. **Lobellius** <note>Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist after whom the Lobelia flower is named</note> calls this the "twiggy and viscous Chondrilla," and he noticed globes of hardened gum, at least in the root. Nor did he follow those who come after him, who assert that a birdlime <note>a sticky substance spread on branches to catch birds</note> made by mixing this root with black honey is frequently made by boys for catching small birds.

---

## The Second Chondrilla of Dioscorides.

<margin>Etymology of the Name.</margin>
And this can certainly be called **Chondrilla**, for it has roots from which hang lumps, called *chondroi* <note>original: *χόνδροι*; meaning grains, lumps, or cartilage</note> by the Greeks. And **Dioscorides**, just as with the former plant, called this Chondrilla, and recorded that it has a round, small root, etc., in these words: *a root slender, at the tip, light, and round* <note>original: "ῥίζαν δὲ λεπτήν, ἔπακμον, κούφην στρογγύλην"</note>. **Pliny**, however, describing this more accurately, says: *with a root similar to a bean, sometimes numerous* <note>Pliny the Elder, Natural History, Book 22, Chapter 22</note>. He described it by confusing the descriptions of both plants, putting the second variety first, and in the second place the first variety, saying: *It has the nearest...*
<margin>Lib. 22. c. 22.</margin>
<margin>Form and Location.</margin>
This herb, therefore, grows in rocky mountains and in places where the earth does not exceed nine inches <note>original: *dodrantem*; a Roman measure of 3/4 of a foot</note> in depth, where the **Rock Rose** (*Cistus*), **Wild Marjoram** (*Agrioriganum*), and **Calamint** (*Calamintha*) also arise. It is found far from the sea, and in places safe from the South wind. It has a round root, the size of a small bean, which is juicy, yields a little milk, and is whitish, sometimes turning toward purple. It is solid and bitter (the larger ones do not exceed the shape of a **hazelnut**, but are more oblong and smooth, and when dug up no earth clings to them, so that they appear translucent and almost like a pearl). That which is found larger and older is seen to have three or four cavities separated by membranes; and for that reason it is called *chondrille* from the word *chondros* <note>original: "χονδρίλλη ἀπὸ τοῦ χόνδρου"</note>, which is understood to mean cartilage or a dividing membrane—from which the **hypochondrium** <note>The anatomical region of the abdomen under the rib cartilage</note> is named, because it is placed "under the cartilage" <note>original: "ὑπὸ χόνδρου"</note>. The root turns yellow, contracts, and becomes wrinkled. Often, many of these roots are found hanging from thin white fibers creeping through the earth, as Pliny observed...

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal properties and classification of Chondrilla (Gum Succory), citing classical authorities like Galen and Pliny, and explains the plant's Greek etymology related to its "gristly" root structure.</summary>
<keywords>Chondrilla, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Matthias de l'Obel, botany, etymology, hypochondrium, medicinal herbs, gum, root structure</keywords>

->HISTORY OF SOME PLANTS<- <note>original: *PLANT. ALIQVOT HIST.*</note>

...most orderly, it most effectively rules in place of gum. **Dorotheus** <note>Likely Dorotheus of Sidon, a 1st-century Greek poet who wrote didactic verses on medicinal plants and astrology</note> declared it useful for the stomach and for digestion in his poems. Some have thought it harmful to women and the eyes, and to the reproductive power of men. 

**Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (c. 129–216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium</note>, however, says of **Chondrilla**—and some call this *Serin* <note>original: *Serin*; likely referring to a type of wild chicory or endive common in the Mediterranean</note>—that it possesses almost the same faculty as that other variety, except that it is somewhat bitter in taste and therefore has a drier quality. 

**Lobellius** <note>Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist whose system of classification based on leaf shape was a precursor to modern botany</note> calls this the "twiggy and viscous Chondrilla," and he noticed globes of hardened gum, at least in the root. Nor did he follow those who come after him, who assert that a birdlime <term>Birdlime: a sticky substance, often made from bark or berries, spread on branches to trap small birds by their feet</term> made by mixing this root with black honey is frequently made by boys for catching small birds.

---

## The Second Chondrilla of Dioscorides.

<margin>Origin of the Name</margin>
And this can certainly be called **Chondrilla**, for it has roots from which hang lumps, called *grains* <note>original: *χόνδροι* (chondroi); meaning grains, lumps, or cartilage-like nodules</note> by the Greeks. And **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and the author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary pharmacological text of antiquity</note>, just as with the former plant, called this Chondrilla, and recorded that it has a round, small root, and so on, in these words...

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...he changes it, since he says with a root like a bean, sometimes numerous. For indeed, even one of those bulbs produces a plant, and likewise several together hanging from the fibers. From the beginning of these, leaves break out in autumn—smaller than those of **Chicory**, oblong and gnawed-around <note>The term "circumrosa" suggests leaves with jagged or eroded-looking edges, common in the dandelion family</note>, or lightly wavy. Their lower part tends toward blue, while they have a reddish back; they are fleshy, smooth, easy to break, and at that moment they make a crunching sound. For this reason, the herbalists here call it **Squeaking Herb** <note>original: *Herba stridola*</note>, and they give off a bitter milk. The largest leaf is less than nine inches long. From the middle of the leaves, a single stalk rises: bare, smooth, hollow, rounded, reddish, swollen with milk, a foot high—sometimes smaller, or even larger, according to the weather of the place and time.

<margin>Time of growth.</margin>
For Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> holds that the stalk is less than a foot long, at the top of which is a single yellow flower that comes forth in the month of April, like that of **Chicory** or what is called **Dandelion** <note>original: *Dens Leonis*, literally "Lion's Tooth"</note>. Its base, and the stalk near it, are seen to be covered with a black hairiness; the seed flies away when mature in downy tufts <note>term: <term>pappos</term> (the feathery bristles on seeds, like those of a dandelion, that allow them to be carried by the wind)</note>.

I have determined this to be the second kind of **Chondrilla** <note>also known as Gum Succory</note> of Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, since it corresponds to the history handed down by him:

<note>original Greek: Ἔτι δὲ καὶ ἕτερον εἶδος χονδρίλλης, φύλλον δὲ ἔχει περιβεβρωμένον, πρόμηκες, ἐπὶ γῆς ἐστρωμένον, καυλὸν δὲ ὀποῦ μεστόν. ῥίζαν δὲ λεπτὴν, ἔπακρον, κούφην, στρογγύλην, ὑπόξανθον, ὀποῦ μεστήν.</note>

<margin>Description.</margin>
"There is also another kind of Chondrilla, having a leaf that is gnawed-around, oblong, and spread on the ground; a stalk full of sap; a small root, vigorous, smooth, round, yellowish, and full of sap."

<margin>Book 22, chapter 22.</margin>
Which Pliny transcribed thus: "Chondrillon, or Chondrille, has leaves similar to **Endive** that are gnawed-around, a stalk less than a foot long, dripping with a bitter sap, and a root like a bean, sometimes numerous." We can fit this description to no other plant than one which has bulbous roots and can produce a separate plant from a single planted bulb, and which can also have several little globes, as Pliny wishes. The leaves, although they are not spread flat on the earth as in **Hound’s-tongue**, **Plantain**, and similar plants, has no others except those which come forth from the root and are closest to the earth—some of which are turned toward the ground, so that we can say they lie on the earth. Therefore, nothing seems to me to stand in the way of it being considered the genuine plant of Dioscorides, especially since it becomes clearer from the supplement of Pliny's text; we know that Pliny transcribed many things borrowed from Dioscorides "without greeting the host" <note>idiom: *insalutato hospite*; a Latin expression meaning to take or use something without giving due credit or acknowledgment to the source</note>. **Lobellius** <note>Matthias de l'Obel, a Flemish physician and botanist</note> calls this **Sea Chondrilla**, but here it is found more rarely in maritime regions; he also calls it the **Perdicium of Theophrastus**, and he doubts whether it is the Chondrilla of Dioscorides.

The author debates the plant's classification, noting its milky sap, yellow flowers, and unique bulbous root structure.</summary>
