# OF FABIO COLONNA

...drilla; for which reason he describes another with the root of an **Asphodel** <note>A hardy perennial herb often associated with the underworld in mythology</note>, which **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a 16th-century Italian physician and botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides</note>, not knowing if it grew among us as well, called **Chicory of Constantinople**; this consists of multiple roots ending in a sharp point. He followed the very learned man **Marcellus Virgilius** <note>Marcello Virgilio Adriani, an Italian humanist who translated Dioscorides</note>, who translated "at its peak" <note>original Greek: *ἔπακμον*</note> not as "vigorous," but as "ending in a point." He did not notice that the word for "at its peak" is derived from the verb "to reach full bloom" <note>original: *ἐπακμάζειν*</note>, which is composed of the words "I am at my peak" and "that," which is interpreted as "I flourish" or "I grow up." Therefore, it should be interpreted as "vigorous" or "youthful," as everyone else has translated it. 

He even wants to corrupt the text so that it does not say "smooth" <note>original Greek: *κούφην*</note>, but rather "summit" <note>original Greek: *κορυφὴν*</note>, a word which is not found in any Greek text, nor in the version by **Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician who published a famous Latin translation of Dioscorides in 1516</note> which I have in many printed editions; instead, "light" and "smooth" are found written there. Although he says otherwise in his volume *On the Nature of Plants*, he is writing there as a historian, not as a translator. This not being enough for him, he turns his words against **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, author of *Natural History*</note>, whom he thinks was hallucinating, not perceiving nor knowing which reading or which plant he ought to accept. Finally, he happens upon the **Chicory of Constantinople** of Matthiolus, even rising up against him. Yet he, who acts as an examiner of that man, should have been ashamed, for he does not consider what he himself writes, being entangled in a multitude of similar plants and readings. At last, he describes it. But it is not the same plant, since it propagates with many roots rather than a single one, and it far exceeds the form and size evident from the texts of **Dioscorides** and Pliny. Nor is it numerous and multiple only sometimes, but always—contrary to the opinion of Dioscorides and Pliny; it is not a single root like the one in the plant proposed by me, which will have these powers according to Dioscorides:

<margin>*Powers.*</margin>
> *The leaves and the stalk have the power of aiding digestion. The juice, however, glues together the hairs on the eyelids* <note>This refers to the ancient treatment for trichiasis, where the juice was used to keep misplaced eyelashes from irritating the eye</note>. **Myconius** <note>Francisco Micó, a Spanish botanist</note> calls this **Strumous Chicory**, and asserts that he has learned from experience that it contributes much to curing <term>strumas</term> <note>Swellings of the glands, particularly scrofula (tuberculosis of the neck)</note>; five or six whole roots taken with honey for many consecutive days are wonderfully beneficial. The distilled water of these roots and a decoction provide the same benefit, but less effectively; likewise, a preserve prepared from them with honey is very helpful. The herbalists here sell <term>Sonchus</term> <note>Sow-thistle</note> as useful for women to draw out milk; they prepare it thus: they pound the roots of this herb and, having added **marjoram** <note>original: *sampsucho*</note>, they mix it with eggs and eat cakes made from these.

# FABIO COLONNA

## *Alisma, or the Damassonium of Dioscorides.*

<margin>The origin of the name.</margin>
ALISMA, which is also called *Alcea* <note>A name often associated with mallows, but here used as a synonym for Alisma</note> and *Damassonium*, derives its names from its power to repel the poisons of the "Sea Hare" <note>A type of toxic sea slug, *Aplysia*</note>, the "Bramble Toad" <note>The common toad, *Bufo bufo*, once thought to be venomous</note>, and opium, as is read in Dioscorides. Lobellius <note>Matthias de l’Obel, a renowned Flemish botanist</note>, however, says the etymology of *Alisma* comes from the word *halismos* <note>original: "ἄλισμὸς"</note>, which means the dashing and tossing of the sea; he claims this fits the *Alisma* because it lives in water. But if this were true, it should have been written *Halisma*, for the Greek letter *alpha* is marked with a rough breathing sound <note>The 'h' sound represented by the 'spiritus asper' mark</note>, which is absent in *Alisma*. This origin would be true if the plant he proposed were actually *Alisma*; but it is certainly quite different and does not match Dioscorides' description at all, even though he tried to twist the words to fit his own plant. Furthermore, many things are still missing from that plant which are clearly described by Dioscorides, as I shall demonstrate. For Dioscorides says: <note>original: "Ἄλισμα, οἱ δὲ ἀλκέαν, οἱ δὲ δαμασώνιον καλοῦσι. φύλλα μὲν ἔχει ἀρνογλώσσῳ ὅμοια, στενώτερα δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ γῇ κλώμενα. καυλὸς δὲ λεπτὸς, ἁπλοῦς, ὑπὲρ πῆχυν κεφάλια, θυρσοειδής. ἄνθη λεπτὰ, λευκὰ, ὕπωχρα. ῥίζαι ὡς μέλανος ἐλλεβόρου λεπταὶ, εὐώδεις, δριμεῖαι, ὑπολίπαροι. ὑδρηλὰ δὲ φιλεῖ χωρία."</note>

<margin>Description.</margin>
<margin>Book 3.</margin>
*Alisma, which some call Alcea and others Damassonium, has leaves similar to Plantain <note>Arnoglossum</note>, but narrower and bent toward the ground; a thin, simple stalk, more than a cubit <note>About 18 inches</note> high, having heads in the shape of a thyrsus <note>A staff-like flower cluster</note>; flowers are delicate, white, and somewhat pale; roots are thin like those of black Hellebore, fragrant, sharp, and somewhat oily. It loves watery places.*

<margin>Book 25, chapter 10.</margin>
From this text, Pliny recorded the history of *Alisma* in his books as follows: *Alisma, which some call Damassonium and others Lyron. Its leaves are like Plantain, were they not narrower and more fringed, and bent toward the earth; otherwise, they are similarly veined. It has a simple, thin, cubit-high stalk with a thyrsus head; roots are dense and thin like black hellebore, sharp, fragrant, and oily. It grows in watery places. There is another kind of the same plant in the woods, darker and with larger leaves, etc.*

From the following description, it will be clear how well the plant I have proposed matches these delineations.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It has a root that is thick at the top, equal to a large finger, reddish on the outside, from which many pale fibers hang; these are fleshy and thin, not unlike Hellebore, fragrant, and sharp to the taste. It puts forth many leaves in a circle, leaning toward the ground, of a pale color, smooth, and fleshy, in the shape of narrow-leaved Plantain, though the veins are not very conspicuous.

<summary>Fabio Colonna critiques the botanical identification of Alisma (Water Plantain) by his predecessors, specifically Lobellius, by comparing their claims against the original Greek descriptions of Dioscorides and the Latin of Pliny.</summary>
<keywords>Alisma, Damassonium, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Matthias de l’Obel, plant morphology, etymology, Plantain, Hellebore, pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of Alisma and related species, moving from nomenclature to a detailed physical description and medicinal applications, specifically focusing on the mountain-dwelling Primula species often confused with it.</meta>

->### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS<-

->C 2<-
<meta>Catchword: fius. [Referring to Leonhart Fuchs]</meta>

...they appear; in the upper and wider part, they are serrated with minute and small teeth. Those leaves closer to the ground are flaccid and drooping. It sends out a stem, not from the center of the leaves, but from the side of the root, a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the fingertips, about 18 inches.</note> long or even larger when the plant is older. Above this, small, somewhat wide leaves come out in a radiating pattern, of which one is three times larger than the others (as seen in the illustration), forming the base of the flowers. These flowers hang between them like an umbel or a cluster; they are yellowish-white, faint-smelling, hollow, thin, and divided into five parts at the top, as if they were constructed from five small petals. They are not unlike the flowers of the commonly named Spring Primroses, having the calyxes <note>The green, cup-like base of a flower that holds the petals.</note> from which they emerge at the bottom. Once these have dried, the fruits remain: round, hard, and having a point, covered by a soft husk also divided into five parts, as is seen in the *Ocymoides* <note>A genus of plants similar to campions or catchfly.</note> genus. Inside is contained a small, dark, angular seed, similar to yellow *Nigella* <note>Also known as black caraway or fennel flower; the seeds were used both medicinally and as a spice.</note>. It blooms in the months of February and March, and completes its fruit in Summer.

<margin>Time. Place.</margin>
It grows in mountainous places and on cliffs where moisture flows down from the higher parts of the mountain; it delights in shady and cold spots. It is frequent on the humid cliffs of Palinuro <note>A famous cape in Southern Italy, named after the pilot of Aeneas in Virgil's Aeneid.</note>. More recent authors call this **Bear's Ear** (*Ursi auricula*) <note>Now known as *Primula auricula*.</note> from the shape of the leaves—specifically those that are just emerging, for then they resemble a small ear. They also call it **Sanicle** and the **Paralysis Herb** (*Paralyticam*), on account of the remarkable powers it possesses. Because of the early blooming of the flowers, they classify it among the **Spring Primroses**, to which it is related. Some have thought these were *Phlomides* or "Little Mulleins" (*Verbasculos*), but they are far removed from the Mulleins <note>original: "Verbascis"</note>. Many species of these are depicted by plant collectors, distinguished by the name of the place in which they grow; for some, like this one, delight in mountainous places, others in woods, and others in meadows. Yet all indeed love shady and damp places. To these, the most learned Leonhart Fuchs <note>A prominent 16th-century German physician and herbalist.</note>, under the name of "Little Mulleins" (for no truer name had been given to them), attributed these powers:

<margin>Powers.</margin>
<margin>Book 6, *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*.</margin>
*Obstructions of the bladder and kidneys are dissolved by a decoction of the root.* Although this is not mentioned by Dioscorides, Galen <note>A Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> nevertheless puts forward these points concerning *Alisma*, or *Damasonium*. *Dioscorides records in his third book that its root, when drunk, heals dysentery and stops the bowels, and mitigates swelling; however, we ourselves have not tested those things. But that the water in which it has been boiled, when drunk, breaks down stones formed in the kidneys: that, certainly, we have experienced.* From which it is clear that it possesses a certain cleansing property. Fuchsius—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal properties and historical identifications of the plant Alisma (Damasonium), specifically referencing the works of Leonhart Fuchs, Pietro Andrea Mattioli, Pliny the Elder, and Dioscorides.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

[Leonhart Fuchs]. <note>The text begins mid-word 'fius,' completing 'Fuchsius' from the previous page.</note> The juice of the herb is given as a drink and applied as an ointment for ruptures, dislocations, and bruises; indeed, the temperament of these plants clearly teaches what they are capable of. For if you taste them, they are slightly astringent, bitter, and moderately pungent, so that no one should doubt they possess the power of drying and cleansing. Even women concerned with their appearance rub their faces with the juice expressed from the flowers, because it marvelously cleanses spots, wrinkles, and other such blemishes.

They are recommended for intestinal hernias <term>enterocelas: a type of abdominal hernia where the intestine protrudes</term> and wounds to the chest, according to 
<margin>Book 25, chapter 10.</margin>
Mattioli. Pliny, however, transcribed only these words from Dioscorides: *The use of the root of both types against sea-frogs and sea-hares <note>original: "ranas, & lepores marinos." These refer to poisonous marine organisms; the "sea-hare" is a type of toxic sea slug (Aplysia)</note> is in the weight of a drachm <term>drachm: an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.4 grams</term> taken in a drink of wine.* To these words, what Mattioli writes regarding the related primroses seems to correspond. He says that distilled water extracted from the flowering plant is useful when drunk for weaknesses of the heart and the whole body; for, as those who have tested it say, it helps in a wonderful way and adds strength to the heart. The flowers, when applied, assist against the stings of scorpions and venomous spiders; and the leaves, flowers, or root—or even the whole plant—applied topically, heals the pestilential plague and wounds. Dioscorides, however, says: 
> *The root, drunk with wine in the weight of one or two drachms, is beneficial to those who have swallowed a sea-hare, those bitten by a bramble-toad <note>original: "rana rubeta," likely the common toad, once thought to be venomous</note>, and those who have consumed opium. It is given as a drink for griping pains and dysentery, either by itself or with an equal measure of wild carrot <note>original: "Dauci"</note> seeds. It is useful for convulsions and for conditions of the uterus. The herb stops the bowels, promotes menstruation, and, when applied, soothes tumors.* Likewise, 
<margin>Book 26, chapter 5.</margin>
Pliny, speaking of plants useful for scrofula <term>strumas: a swelling of the lymph nodes, usually associated with tuberculosis</term>, says: *Damasonium, which is also called Alcea*, collected at the summer solstice, is applied with rainwater; the leaf is crushed, or the root is pounded with hog’s lard <term>axungia: animal fat used as an ointment base</term>, so that the application is covered by its own leaf. This is also done for all pains of the neck and tumors in any part of the body. From these accounts, it can be gathered that the plant I have proposed is indeed the *Alisma* or *Damasonium* of Dioscorides, in both appearance and power, as all its characteristics correspond. Everyone will be able to judge from these notes how far removed this is from other plants thought to be *Damasonium* or *Alisma*; for the root, leaves or serrations, stem, odor, taste, and powers of those other proposed plants do not match at all, though it would be too tedious to repeat those details here. There is also a second kind of *Alisma* mentioned by Pliny, which I shall discuss in the following pages.

<meta>This page continues Fabius Columna’s (Fabio Colonna) botanical examination of the plant Alisma, specifically focusing on the second variety described by the Roman natural historian Pliny the Elder. Colonna is attempting to reconcile ancient descriptions with the plants known in his time, such as primroses.</meta>

->FABIUS COLUMNA<-

### Another kind of Alisma or Damasonium according to Pliny.

<margin>
Description.
Book 25,
Chapter 10.
</margin>

In the description of Alisma or Damasonium transcribed from Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, Pliny adds these words: *Another kind of the same in the woods, darker, with larger leaves;* and below the first description of Alisma, he intersperses these words: *in others also similarly veined.* From these words it becomes clear that the plants I present here in illustrations are of the same genus, and represent this second kind of Alisma mentioned by Pliny.

<margin>Form.</margin>
For they all have a root like that of Hellebore <note>original: *Elleborus*</note>, fibrous, thick, slender, fragrant, and sharp, from which the leaves cover the ground in a circle, raised slightly above it. These are very similar to lettuce leaves, hairy on the underside, and very veined or ribbed, which makes them look wrinkled <note>or "crisped"</note>. They are gently curved around the edges and of a greener, deeper color than the first Alisma depicted, which is why Pliny called it the "dark kind," and rightly so. The first of these illustrated does not produce a stalk in the form of a wand <note>original: *thyrsus*</note> with small heads above it, but sends forth several bare and hairy stems. At the top of these are single flowers emerging from a cup <note>original: *calyx*</note>, yellowish-white and fragrant; when these dry, little pods remain containing small, brownish, somewhat round seeds, which ripen in May. The second type, however, produces a stem and flowers in the manner of the first kind of Alisma, and of the same color.

<margin>Time.</margin>
All of them bloom in early Spring, and for that reason more recent authors have called them "Spring Primroses" <note>original: *Veris Primulas*</note>, while others call them "woodland" varieties; they attributed the same medicinal powers to all of them, confirmed by the same experiments. Noticing this, Pliny said:

<margin>Powers.</margin>
> *Another kind of the same in the woods is darker, with larger leaves: the root of both is used against the poison of "frogs" <note>likely referring to the venomous "sea frog" or anglerfish</note> and sea-hares <note>toxic marine mollusks</note>, in the weight of a drachma taken in a drink of wine.*

To these should be added what I set down above in the description of the first kind of Alisma; for both recent authors and the ancients attributed the same powers to all of them. You may see others related to these in the works of plant collectors under the names of <term>Primula</term> (Primrose), <term>Sanicula</term> (Sanicle), <term>Ursi auricula</term> (Bear's Ear), and <term>Paralytica</term> (Cowslip) <note>literally "the palsy plant," named for its use in treating tremors</note>. You should include all of these under the names Alisma and Damasonium. You shall call others mountain varieties (which grow on mountains), others woodland, and finally others meadow-dwelling—not "Primroses," but plants distinguished by the name of Alisma.

<summary>Fabio Colonna identifies several plants known in the 16th century—including primroses and cowslips—as the "Alisma" described by Pliny the Elder, providing detailed physical descriptions and medicinal uses.</summary>
<keywords>Alisma, Damasonium, Pliny the Elder, botanical identification, primrose, cowslip, medicinal roots, sea-hare poison, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->HISTORIES OF SOME PLANTS<-

## Acinos of Dioscorides.

<margin>
Name and
Etymology.
</margin>
This plant was named **Acinos** from the letter *alpha*, which is used to indicate a negation, and the verb *kineo*, which means to soften, move, incite, or stir. Hence, in the writings of Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> <margin>Book 2, chapter on Cabbage.</margin> it is said that certain plants "move the bowels and the menses." This plant, however, because it has the opposite property—namely, that in the opinion of Dioscorides it stops the menses and the bowels when drunk—is therefore called *akinos*, because it "does not move" <note>original Greek: *akinei*</note>, or "stays the bowels and menses when drunk." Dioscorides <margin>Book 3.</margin> described it thus: *Acinos, or Aconos, is a herb with slender fruit, used for making garlands, very similar to basil <note>original Greek: okimo</note>, but hairier and fragrant; among some people it is even cultivated in gardens.* 

Pliny <note>The Roman author of Natural History</note>, however, speaking of various vegetables, adds: <margin>Book 21, chapter 15.</margin> *Acinos, which they call Epipetron <note>Literally "upon the rock"</note>, which never flowers.* From this text, many have been of the opinion that this is the same as the *Epimetron* of Theophrastus <note>The "Father of Botany," a student of Aristotle</note> due to the similarity of the name, which is undoubtedly the case. I offer this reason: Pliny composed those passages in which he mentions this Acinos by drawing from the eighth chapter of Theophrastus’s seventh book. If the matters he discusses there are weighed against the cited passage of Theophrastus, it will be clearly seen; there, his words are as follows: *Some plants are entirely without flowers, just like the Epimetron.* <margin>Book 7, chapter 8.</margin> This was also an indication that it might be the same as the *Epimedium* of Dioscorides, since he says that *Epimedium* never flowers either. 

That the *Epimedium* of Dioscorides and the *Epimetron* of Theophrastus are the same plant is suggested by a certain similarity in the name and the history, as well as the *Epimetron* of Pliny, who records that it is also called Acinos. In that passage, I would think that *Erineos* should be substituted for *Acinos*; this name is found among those various names listed for the *Epimedium* of Dioscorides. Since it is Pliny’s habit to transcribe different things from various sources, here he had Theophrastus’s eighth chapter of the seventh book at hand, but elsewhere <margin>Book 27, chapter 9.</margin> he had Dioscorides’ *Epimedium* before him, unmindful of the *Epimetron* he had already described. For he was not always able to weigh these matters in his spare hours and at night, to see the light <note>an idiom meaning to gain clear understanding or to publish</note> and show them to us. Entangled in these same difficulties, Pena <note>Pierre Pena, a 16th-century French botanist</note> thought that *akynos* should be written with a "y" <note>the Greek letter upsilon</note>, because that signifies a sterile and unfruitful plant:

<meta>catchword: plantam:</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the etymology and botanical identity of "Acinos," attempting to resolve confusion between the descriptions provided by Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus. Colonna argues that several different names used by these ancient authors likely refer to the same non-flowering plant.</summary>

# OF FABIO COLONNA

the plant: and he rises up against Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist whom Colonna frequently critiques</note>, as is his custom, barely seeing the beam in his own eye; but enough about these matters. This is not the *Acinos* which they call *Epipetron* <note>Literally "upon the rock"</note>, which is the *Acinos* of Dioscorides; for Pliny,
<margin>Book 21, chapter 27.</margin>
speaking of it, says: *The Egyptians sow Acinos both for the sake of garlands and for food; and it would be the same as Basil <note>Latin: Ocimum</note>, were it not that its branches and leaves are hairier, and it is exceedingly fragrant.* From these words, it is clearly evident that *Acinos* is suitable and useful for food, since it is sown for that purpose; the opposite is true of *Epipetron* or *Epimedion*. Secondly, *Epimedion*, *Epipetron*, or *Epimetron* is said by everyone to lack a flower; yet Pliny demonstrates the opposite to be true for *Acinos* with these words: *And it would be the same as Basil, were it not hairier, etc.* But Basil does not lack a flower; therefore, it is necessary that *Acinos* also has flowers, since it ought to differ from Basil in nothing except hairiness—a mark by which it is also distinguished from Basil by Dioscorides, since he writes that it is <term>very similar to basil <note>original Greek: ὠκίμῳ παραπλησία (okimō paraplesia)</note></term>. These words and this sentiment confirm Pliny’s description, since the preposition <term>beside <note>original Greek: παρὰ (para)</note></term> adds to the word <term>near <note>original Greek: πλησία (plesia)</note></term>; indeed, in composition, when joined to a noun or a verb, it reinforces its property, whether it signifies something right or something "wrong," as can be seen in the observations of the Greek language. And thus it must have flowers like Basil, since it differs from it only in hairiness. This argument has not been noticed by others until now, and it seems sufficient to me to prove that *Acinos* has flowers—besides what others have said, that since it is used for garlands, it is likely to have flowers,
<margin>Form.</margin>
though indeed not strictly necessary. For this reason, I present this plant as the truer *Acinos*, which gardeners sow every year in the Springtime, and which they call **Vrtichella** <note>Little Nettle</note> here, because it reflects the hairiness of a nettle in a certain way. It is indeed hairy, and somewhat rough in the stem, with leaves similar to Basil, though thinner and longer,
<margin>Time.</margin>
and serrated around the edges in the manner of Mercury <note>Latin: Mercurialis, a common herb</note>; its flower in summer is also similar to Basil, small and purplish. You would say it was the genuine plant of Dioscorides, were you not to recognize it as another type of Basil. For it has leaves similar to Mercury, which Dioscorides says the leaves of Basil possess; no other plant described by Dioscorides with leaves like Basil is more similar to Mercury in its leaves, positioning, color, smoothness, and stem than Basil itself. This plant, however, differs from those in the thinness of its stem (which Dioscorides requires) and in the hairiness of both the stem and leaves, which are lacking in Basil and Mercury. Its scent is most fragrant, approaching that of aromatic cloves; and if you only touch it,

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

...you touch it, it gives off a scent, and if you then bring your hand to your nose, your nostrils will be struck by a most pleasant fragrance. Therefore, it is quite aromatic and has a good scent; it is so similar in appearance to Basil <note>original: "Ocimum"</note> that there is no doubt it is a species of Basil, since it differs only in its hairiness and the thinness of its stem. Galen omitted any mention of the *Acinos* plant <note>Acinos usually refers to Basil-thyme or a similar fragrant herb</note>.

<margin>Properties</margin> But Paulus <note>referring to the 7th-century medical writer Paul of Aegina</note>, following the authority of Dioscorides, says: *Acinos, being similar to Basil, has moderately astringent powers <term>astringent: substances that contract body tissues and diminish discharges</term>, which you will clearly perceive by chewing and tasting it; for this reason, when drunk, it stops the bowels and menstrual flow; applied topically, it treats inflammatory swellings and skin infections <note>original: "panos, & erysipelata"</note>.* In Pliny’s text, I found the opposite properties recorded, but I have no doubt that this is an error by a corrector who assumed the plant had "warming" and "opening" powers. Such a corrector might have thought that just as Basil helps with difficulty in urination in Dioscorides' opinion, so too this species of Basil must promote urine and menstruation; therefore, he wrote: *It promotes menstruation and urine.* Although it could be argued—as some have done—that *Acinos* might possess both sets of powers, I believe we should trust two consistent texts rather than one that offers no explanation for its contradiction.

The plant identified as *Acinos* by various other authors is not the true *Acinos* of Dioscorides, for their versions are far removed from the historical description. For this reason, one author refutes another's supposed *Acinos*, while smiling at his own opinion. However, it is clear that their plant has no Basil-like leaves, nor does it bear that resemblance to Basil which would allow it to be called the same (aside from being hairier), as Pliny states following the mind of Dioscorides. 

Therefore, the author of the *History of Plants*, not knowing which to accept, preferred to depict that scentless—or rather foul-smelling—plant which is a most troublesome weed along roadsides as the true *Acinos*, even though the real plant ought to be quite fragrant. He perhaps did this fearing the criticism of Lobelius and Pena <note>Matthias de l'Obel and Pierre Pena, influential contemporary botanists</note>. He himself calls it "Wild Basil" <note>original: "Clinopodium sylvestre"</note>, rather than considering whether the hairy plant from the Basil family depicted by Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs, a famous German botanist</note> under the name "Middle Basil" was the true hairy Basil worthy of the name *Acinos*. But he was hardly able to consider such a vast multitude of illustrations, let alone the plants themselves.

HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

<margin>Name</margin>
## Dioscorides’s Alyssum.

<margin>Etymology</margin>
<margin>Book 3.</margin>
<margin>Book 24.</margin>
<margin>ch. 11.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial A featuring a landscape with a river and a building.</image-desc> This plant was named *Alysson*, from the Greek word *lyssan* <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ λυσσᾶν"</note>, which means to be mad or rabid, combined with "a," a privative particle <note>a prefix indicating the absence or removal of a quality</note>, because it takes away the madness. As Dioscorides says when speaking of this: *it is thought even to cure the madness of a dog* <note>original: "δοκεῖ δὲ καὶ λύσσαν κυνὸς ἰᾶσθαι"</note>. Pliny also notes: *It received its name because it does not allow those bitten by a dog to suffer from rabies* <note>original: "rabiem sentire non patitur"</note>.

<margin>Form</margin>
It is a small, branchy herb with a tiny white root divided into thin fibers. It has two leaves spread on the ground, which are round, very small, fleshy, hairy, and somewhat rough, with small, round serrations <note>original: "incisuris serrata"</note> along the edge. Sometimes it produces a single stem—and you will find the single-stemmed plant as often as those with branches—which is upright, round, hairy, without side-shoots, resembling a small twig about a foot or a foot and a half in height or length. Sometimes many branches emerge from each of the lowest leaves and even from the upper ones, which cling directly to the stem without a stalk <note>original: "absque petiolo"</note> opposite each other; these are larger than those spread on the ground and are spaced out. From the middle to the very top, these branches and the stem gradually produce small blue flowers, similar in appearance and color to the Water Pimpernel <note>original: "Anagallidi aquaticæ," likely *Veronica anagallis-aquatica*</note>. During this stage, the small shoots and leaves are arranged alternately, being smaller, longer, and less serrated. When the flowers fall, they are replaced by hairy, leaf-like fruits that are flattened and at first glance mimic the wider shape of a heart. They are disk-shaped <note>original: "δισκοειδεῖς"</note>, like the fruit of *Thlaspi* <term>Thlaspi: a genus of herbs in the mustard family, commonly known as penny-cress, known for their flat, disc-like seed pods</term>. These are composed of two small membranes, representing the image of two oval or round shields joined side-by-side. They bulge out on both sides, as if the edges of two shields were joined together. This is how the description should be understood, and not as others have wrongly thought, considering one shield to be resting on top of another; for if that were the case, all plants with leaf-like seeds would show this. But Dioscorides, as I said, distinguished this plant from others by the fact that its fruit—specifically each membrane—represents the image of a double shield with one side joined to the other. Only this plant and its relatives match this description. Four small leaf-like structures surround these, as is seen in the Pimpernel; inside are small, oval-

<header>HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS</header>

...containing compressed, yellowish, oval seeds, which have a sort of elevated ridge in the middle; for this reason, Dioscorides called them "somewhat flat seeds" <note>original: "ὑπόπλατυ σπέρμα"</note>. When these are fully formed, the pods open and the seed falls out. <margin>Time.</margin> The whole plant has a bitter taste. It begins to flower in the month of April and completes its fruit in May. <margin>Location.</margin> It grows in elevated and sun-drenched places, on stony, uncultivated ground, and here on walls; it also grows in valleys and meadows, though not as frequently. This plant seems to fulfill the entire <margin>Description.</margin> history given by Dioscorides, who says: *Alysson is a slightly rough shrubby plant, having round leaves; alongside which the fruit is like double little shields, in which is the somewhat flat seed. It grows in mountainous and rough places.* <note>original: "Ἄλυσσον φρυγάνιόν ἐστι ὑπότραχυ, φύλλα ἔχον στρογγύλα· παρ’ οἷς ὁ καρπὸς ὡς ἀσπιδίσκια διάδιπλα, ἐν οἷς τὸ σπέρ- μα ὑπόπλατυ. φύεται ἐν ὀρεινοῖς καὶ τραχέσι τόποις."</note> Alysson is a small shrub—though in some versions it is read as "single-stemmed"—somewhat rough, having round leaves, next to which the fruit is like double little shields <note>The author uses the term "clypeoli" to describe the shield-like shape of the seed pods</note>, in which the seed is somewhat compressed: it grows in the mountains and in rough places. <margin>Book 24. ch. 11.</margin> Pliny described a plant different from this one in these words, following his description of Red-root <note>original: "Erythrodanum," a name for Madder</note>: *It differs from that which is called Alysson only by the leaves and the smaller branches.* From this, it is clear that he made it similar to Madder <note>original: "Rubia"</note>. Thus, that is a different plant from the one Dioscorides described with round leaves, which cannot have any similarity with Madder, since many leaves would have to encircle the stem for it to be like Madder. But the opposite is true, and this is confirmed by that distinct name applied by Dioscorides, "simple-leafed" <note>original: "ἀπλόφυλλον" (haplophyllon)</note>, a word that designates a plant with a single leaf; that is, so that a single leaf clings to the side of the stem, not many together from the same part, as in Madder, which has three on each side sticking out opposite one another. This name is very foreign to Madder, and the plant proposed by me seems to reflect it, especially from the middle to the top, where the little shields <term>clypeoli</term> are located; from these, the name "little shield" <note>original: "ἀσπίδιον" (aspidion)</note> was given to it, on account of their similarity and frequency. Therefore, Pliny either described another plant, or in transcribing, he wrote one for the other from the chapter of Dioscorides' text immediately preceding Alysson—namely, that of Cleavers <note>original: "Aparine"</note>—since everyone knows that Cleavers differs from Madder only by having smaller leaves and stems. However, modern botanists <note>original: "Neoterici," referring to Renaissance scholars</note> have depicted various plants for Dioscorides' Alysson, none of which seem to correspond, since they have either long, smooth, or even hairy leaves, but not round ones; nor are they shrubby <note>original: "φρυγανώδης" (phryganodes)</note>, nor do they have pods positioned between the stem and leaves, but rather arranged in a spike at the top. This is contrary to the opinion of Dioscorides, who described the pods as clinging to the leaves, saying *alongside which the fruit...* <note>original: "παρ’ οἷς ὁ καρπὸς"</note> etc., as is also seen in other plants where the fruit...

<meta>catchword: stem,</meta>

<summary>On this page, Fabius Columna argues that his specimen matches the true Alysson of Dioscorides based on its "shield-like" seed pods and leaf structure, while critiquing Pliny and modern botanists for confusing it with Madder or Cleavers.</summary>
<keywords>Dioscorides, Alysson, Pliny the Elder, Madder (Rubia), Cleavers (Aparine), botanical morphology, seed pods, Renaissance botany, classical identification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical analysis of Alysson (Madwort), specifically addressing the placement of seeds and the plant's medicinal virtues as recorded by ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Galen, while correcting the misidentification of the plant as a species of Veronica (Speedwell).</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

...enclosed between the stem and the leaves, as seen in Gromwell <note>original: "Lithospermon"</note>, Knotgrass <note>original: "Polygonon"</note>, and similar plants. Nor can they have it any other way if they are to correspond to the text of Dioscorides; because some others, whose depicted plant did not resemble this, passed over it not without error, or did not notice it. This plant, which was called a species of Speedwell <note>original: "Veronica"</note> by others, I first brought forward as a smaller variety not previously depicted by others—or if they did depict it, they expressed it less correctly. It is not unlike others, except that its leaves are rounder, divided around the edges with round notches <term>crenated: having a scalloped or notched edge</term>, and are hairier and thicker, with a stem that is sometimes upright or sometimes lying flat. Its flowers and fruits, however, are not different, nor are its taste and medicinal powers.

<margin>Varieties.</margin>
For this reason, I would think that other Speedwells already accepted as ground-creeping and prostrate should be assigned to the variety of Alysson.

<margin>Powers.</margin>
They are indeed powerful in many virtues, as is established by the experiments of more recent authors. *They contribute miraculously to the healing of bloody and old ulcers and wounds. They cure the itch <note>original: "Scabiei"</note> and all defects of the skin. They disperse tumors, especially in the neck. For a cough and ailments of the chest and lungs, a distilled water is given to be drunk. They open obstructions of the liver and spleen; they cleanse the uterus, kidneys, and bladder. For fevers and contagious diseases, three drams <term>dram: a small unit of weight used by apothecaries</term> of the dried powder is recommended, drunk with a dram of Theriac <note>original: "Theriacæ"; an ancient herbal jam used as a universal antidote</note> in wine, provided the patients are immediately covered so that they sweat.* Dioscorides, however, says: *A decoction of this, when drunk, dispels hiccups that occur without fever. It does the same when held in the mouth or applied to the nostrils. But when rubbed with honey, it cleanses freckles and sunspots. It also seems to cure the madness of a dog <note>rabies</note> when crushed and presented with food. It is even said to be health-giving when hung up in a house, both for humans and other animals, so that they are not harmed by a spell <note>original: "fascino"; referring to the "evil eye" or malevolent magic</note>. Furthermore, tied in a reddish cloth, it drives away the diseases of cattle.*

<margin>Book 6 on the properties of simple medicines.</margin>
Galen indeed wrote: *This herb was named Alysson because it miraculously helps those bitten by a mad dog. But even when given to one already raving with madness <note>rabies</note>, it has often healed them entirely; and it effects this by the similarity of its whole substance. It was said before that such a faculty is perceived by experience alone and is established by no clear method. But if anyone tries it for many things, he will know it has a drying and digesting <term>digesting: in a medical sense, to dissolve or break down morbid matter</term> power, along with the fact that it also possesses something of a cleansing nature. For this reason, it cleanses leprosy <note>original: "vitiliginem"</note> and spots on the face caused by the sun.* If these powers are compared with those described above by more recent authors—aside from the power of healing rabies, which they did not know—they will be seen to be the same, for it is observed to digest, expel, dry, and cleanse, and to be an antidote against poison <note>original: "Alexipharmacum"</note>. From these facts, it can rightly be judged that this plant represents the *Alysson* of Dioscorides in both appearance and powers.

<summary>Colonna argues that the plant previously identified as a species of Speedwell (Veronica) is actually the Alysson described by ancient authorities. He details its extensive medicinal uses, ranging from healing wounds and skin spots to treating rabies and protecting against spells.</summary>
<keywords>Fabio Colonna, Alysson, Madwort, Veronica, Speedwell, Dioscorides, Galen, medicinal herbs, rabies cure, Theriac, botanical classification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of Alysson (Madwort), transitioning from the author’s own observations to the testimonies of ancient authorities such as Plutarch, Galen, and Dioscorides. It focuses on the plant's efficacy against hiccups and its identification with "Heraclean Sideritis."</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS</header>

<margin>Plutarch, Symposiacs, book 3, question 1.</margin>
Indeed, Plutarch celebrated the plant Alysson <note>Madwort</note> and numbered it among those things which can benefit a person through mere contact, breath, or even sight; however, he cited it not against the affliction of a rabid dog <note>rabies, for which the plant was traditionally named</note>, but against hiccups. His words are: <note>original Greek: "τὴν δὲ ἄλυσσον καλουμένην βοτάνην καὶ λαβόντες εἰς τὴν χεῖρα μόνον, οἱ δὲ καὶ προσβλέψαντες ἀπαλλάττονται λυγμοῦ. λέγεται δὲ καὶ ποιμνίοις ἀγαθὴ, καὶ αἰπολίοις παραφυταομένη ταῖς μάνδραις."</note> *Furthermore, concerning the herb called Alysson, some are freed from hiccups merely by taking it into their hands, while others do so just by looking at it. They say the same plant is beneficial to flocks of sheep and goats when planted near their pens.* For this reason, it ought to be called *Alyzon*, for *lyzein* signifies to hiccup; from this, the names *lynx* and *lygmos* <note>terms for a convulsive gasp or sob</note> are translated and derived as "hiccup."

# Alysson of Antonius of Cos. Heraclean Sideritis of Dioscorides.

<margin>Book 2, chapter 11, on Antidotes.</margin>
<margin>Description.</margin>
<margin>Aëtius, Tetrabiblos 1, sermon 1, chapter on Alysson.</margin>
<image-desc>Large decorative initial 'A' featuring ornate scrollwork, floral patterns, and a central cross-bar.</image-desc>
Antonius of Cos also describes a plant by the name of Alysson, as Galen records, in these words: <note>original Greek: "Ἄλυσσός δὲ ἐστὶ βοτάνη τῷ πρασίῳ παρεμφέρουσα, τραχυτέρα δὲ καὶ μᾶλλον ἀκανθώδης περὶ τὰ σφαίρια, ἄνθος δὲ φέρει κυανίζον."</note> *Alysson is an herb similar to Horehound <note>original: "Marrubio"</note>, yet rougher and more thorny around the small spheres <term>whorls: the circular clusters of flowers or seeds around the stem</term>, bearing a flower verging on blue.* Following him, Aëtius wrote: *Alysson, they say, is the plant called Heraclean Sideritis <note>Ironwort</note>, which grows everywhere along roadsides, having a purple flower and thicker leaves.* This is how the Latin manuscripts have it, but I believe that instead of "thicker leaves" <note>original: "folia crassiora"</note>, the Greek text originally read *trahythéra*, which should be restored as *trachéa*, meaning "rough" or "shaggy" leaves. This is because the comparative term "thicker" <note>Greek: "pachytera"</note> does not refer back to any positive quality, and in the text of Dioscorides concerning Sideritis, the word "rough" <note>Greek: "trachéa"</note> is indeed read; Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner, a famous 16th-century Swiss naturalist</note> also noticed this. If you compare this description with Dioscorides’ sketch of Heraclean Sideritis, you will judge them to be the same. In this, Dioscorides seems more diligent in describing the leaves; for after saying they were similar to Horehound, to more exactly express their form, he said they were longer and smaller, approaching the shape of Sage or Oak leaves, for they are proportionally longer than Horehound. His words are these: <note>original Greek: "Σιδηρῖτις, οἱ δὲ ἡράκλειαν, πόα ἐστὶ φύλλα ἔχουσα ὅμοια πρασίῳ, ἐπιμηκέστερα δὲ πρὸς τὰ τοῦ ἐλελισφάκου ἢ"</note> *Sideritis, which some call Heraclean, is an herb having leaves like Horehound, but longer, like those of Sage or...*
<margin>Book 4.</margin>

<meta>catchword: ...oak,</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes classical descriptions of the plant Alysson, identifying it with the "Heraclean Sideritis" mentioned by Dioscorides and discussing its traditional use as a cure for hiccups.</summary>

<margin>Dioscorides, Book 4, ch. 33.</margin>
...of the oak, yet smaller and rough. It puts forth square stems, a span <note>about 9 inches</note> high, or even larger, not unpleasant in taste, and somewhat astringent: upon which, at intervals, are round whorls like those of <term>horehound</term> <note>original Greek: πρασίου (prasiou); Latin: Marrubium</note>, and in them is a black seed. It grows in rocky places. <note>The author provides this description first in Greek and then in Latin to show the consensus among ancient sources.</note> *Sideritis <note>Ironwort</note>, which some call Heraclean; is an herb having leaves similar to Horehound, but longer, approaching the leaves of Sage or Oak, yet smaller and rough. It produces square stems, a foot long or more, of a not unpleasant taste and somewhat astringent, in which at intervals there are round whorls, as in Horehound, containing black seed. It grows in rocky places.* And thus, since Antonius of Cos says <term>Alysson</term> <note>Madwort</note> is similar to Horehound, and Dioscorides says the same of <term>Sideritis</term>, while Aëtius says it is what they call Heraclean Sideritis: they all seem to have described the same plant with different words and names. For the depiction of one seems to correspond to the other two, whichever you wish to compare: not only in form, but in its native habitat and its medicinal powers.

<margin>Tetrabiblos 2, series 2, ch. 24.</margin>
Some object that Aëtius, by his way of speaking, only meant that it was *said* that Alysson was called Heraclean Sideritis; but certainly, in that passage, the word "they say" <note>Latin: aiunt</note> acts as an affirmation, which I confirm from the text of the same Aëtius where he speaks of remedies for treating those bitten by a rabid dog, where he says:

> *<term>Hiera</term> <note>A "sacred" or "holy" purgative medicine common in antiquity</note> must be given daily, not for purging, but as an auxiliary medicine, the size of a hazelnut, with a cup of a decoction of Sage, or of the herb Sideritis, which they call Heraclean. Some even use only the herb itself, and say it is very beneficial; and for that reason they also name it Alysson, because it takes away the madness <note>The name "Alysson" comes from the Greek 'a-' (not) and 'lyssa' (rabies/madness)</note>.*

<margin>Book 5, ch. 23.</margin>
Paul of Aegina confirms this in almost the same words: <note>original Greek: "καθαίρειν δεῖ τὸν διὰ τῆς συκυωνίας διδόντα καθ᾿ ἡμέραν τοῦ φαρμάκου μετὰ ἀφεψήματος ἐλελισφάκου, ἢ τῆς σιδηρίτιδος τῆς ἡρακλείας, ἣν καὶ ἄλυσσον καλοῦσί τινες."</note> *It is expedient to purge with a medicine made from wild gourd, so that you administer it daily with a decoction of Sage, or of Heraclean Sideritis, which some call Alysson.* From these accounts, every reason for doubt remains weak: one must consider the Heraclean Sideritis of Dioscorides and the Alysson of Antonius of Cos and subsequent writers to be the same plant. I am also surprised that <term>Matthiolus</term> <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> says he does not know which Heraclean Sideritis Aëtius is talking about, since two are mentioned as "Heraclean": the first and the third. Nor did he notice that Dioscorides brings forward the *first* Heraclean Sideritis as being so named by common designation, but records that the *third* was called so only by Crateuas. Furthermore, the first grows in rocky places and along roadsides, while the third grows in vineyards and damp, shady places, and from...

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA
34
C
<meta>catchword: & less</meta>

...the first differs in its leaves and flowers, as is handed down by Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note>, and as I will discuss below in its proper place. The first, however, approaches **Horehound** <term>Marrubium: a bitter herb in the mint family</term> in its leaves, and also in its flowers and its entire shape, though smaller, as will be clear from the following history and illustration. At the same time, you will find here the **Heraclean Ironwort** <term>Sideritis heraclea</term> of Dioscorides and the **Madwort** <term>Alysson</term> of Antonius Cous <note>A physician from the island of Cos mentioned in ancient medical texts.</note> as described by Galen <note>Claudius Galenus, a prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note>. <margin>Habitat.</margin> This plant grows here around the wide, stony, and dry paths of the valleys, where there is little soil above the rocks. <margin>Season.</margin> It emerges in Autumn, begins to put forth its stem in Spring, and lasts until Summer.
<margin>Appearance.</margin> It has a small, woody root divided into fibers, from which it sends out leaves in the shape of **Horehound**, though longer—as Dioscorides says of **Sage**—reaching a certain length in proportion. They are quite similar to **Wall Germander** <term>Chamaedrys</term>, though slightly larger when they first sprout before the stem. They are rough with hairiness, grayish-white, and notched around the edges, but not as deeply as in Germander; rather, they are like **Betony** <term>Betonica</term>, which Dioscorides also says has leaves like the **Oak** <note>original: "Quercus"</note>. Several stems emerge; I have found them growing wild at a height of about a foot <note>original: "pedales"</note>, though they grow slightly larger under cultivation. They are somewhat creeping, and rise as if lying on their backs. When they stand about three fingers high from the ground, they are seen to have flowers in whorls at each joint <term>verticillatos: arranged in a circle around the stem</term>, as in Horehound, ranging from white to a purplish-blue. These decorate the stems, clinging around the joints at intervals. When they have dried and fallen off, the little funnels <note>the calyxes</note> that contained them remain, ending in five sharp, hard, and stinging points that stick out. Because of this, unless one is careful, they will feel the sting of those prickles if they try to pick them. The leaves clinging to the joints are smaller and more oblong than the first leaves. The whorls contain dark seeds within, which ripen in the month of June. To the taste, it is felt to be astringent—not unpleasant to the palate—having some bitterness and scent. From these facts, it is clear that this plant represents both the **Ironwort** <note>Sideritis</note> of Dioscorides and the **Madwort** <note>Alysson</note> of Antonius Cous, as it seems to agree in its place of growth, form, and medicinal properties. <margin>Medicinal Virtues.</margin> Dioscorides attributes these powers to it under the name of Ironwort: *Applied leaves, by their own power, join wounds without the danger of inflammation.*
<margin>Book 8 on the properties of simple medicines.</margin> Galen, however, writes: *Ironwort indeed possesses a certain cleansing faculty, but the greater part of it is moist and moderately cold; there is a small amount of astringency present. Therefore, it soothes inflammation <note>original: "phlegmonem"</note> and binds wounds together.* If these virtues are correctly weighed alongside the former ones attributed to the other Madwort—which Aëtius <note>A 6th-century Byzantine physician.</note> and Paul <note>Paul of Aegina, a 7th-century medical writer.</note> attribute to this same plant—they will be seen to be similar, and indeed...

He concludes by citing Dioscorides and Galen to confirm the plant's efficacy in healing wounds and reducing inflammation.</summary>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS
35
E 2
<meta>catchword: ta</meta>

...and it will be evident that they differ less than expected. Galen <note>Claudius Galenus, the influential 2nd-century Greek physician.</note> describes the method of using this for the bite of a **rabid dog** according to the opinion of Antonius Cous <note>A physician from the island of Cos.</note> as follows:

<margin>Book 2, On Antidotes, chapter 11.</margin>
> *Store away the **Madwort** <term>Alysson: literally "curing madness," traditionally used against rabies</term> herb once it has been crushed and sifted. When it is needed for those bitten by a rabid dog, you shall administer a spoonful in three cups of water and honey-water <note>original: "aquae & mulsae"</note>, immediately from the first day up to the fortieth; if not for that long, then at least for the first seven days.*

<margin>Tetrabiblos 1, series 3, chapter 31.</margin>
This herb ought to be collected during the heat of the **Dog Days** <note>The hottest weeks of summer associated with the star Sirius</note>; it must be kept dried and sifted so that its potency cannot dissipate. Aëtius <note>A 6th-century Byzantine physician</note> records that it possesses a purging power barely recognized by others, if six **scruples** <term>scrupulus: a small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams</term> of this Madwort powder are drunk with vinegar, a little salt, and sprinkled into water. This Madwort of Galen (or rather, of Antonius Cous) is not that Spanish plant similar to **Horehound** <term>Marrubium</term>, unless it is actually **Heraclean Ironwort** <term>Sideritis heraclea</term>, as Paulus <note>Paulus Aegineta, a 7th-century medical writer</note> and Aëtius maintain. It is no objection to say that Galen speaks of Madwort and Ironwort in different places; for nothing seems to stand in the way, since he provides the history of Madwort in the second book of *On Antidotes* not from his own mind, but as described by Antonius Cous. In that passage, he seems to act as a reporter rather than the primary author; there he also relates the remedies, antidotes, and similar things of other ancients. Thus, there should be no doubt about my opinion, since it is confirmed by two most learned ancient physicians, Paulus Aegineta and Aëtius.

### Pycnocomon of Dioscorides.

<margin>Name. Etymology.</margin>
<margin>Book 4. Description.</margin>
This plant is called **Pycnocomon** <note>original: Πυκνόκομον</note> from the words *pyknos* and *kome* <note>original: ἀπὸ τοῦ πυκνὸς, & κομᾶν</note>, namely from the density of its foliage, for it puts forth many leafy tufts; or because the foliage itself appears dense due to the plurality of flowers. Dioscorides <note>The 1st-century author of De Materia Medica.</note> depicted its features thus: *Pycnocomon has leaves similar to **Rocket** <term>Eruca: a peppery salad green</term>, but rough, thick, and sharper; a square stem; a flower similar to that of **Basil** <term>Ocimum</term>; fruit like that of a **Leek** <term>Porrum</term>; a black, round, pale root, like a small apple, smelling of earth; it grows in rocky places.* <note>original Greek: Πυκνόκομον φύλλα ἔχει ὅμοια εὐζώμῳ, τραχέα δὲ, καὶ παχέα, καὶ δριμύτερα: καυλὸν τετράγωνον: ἄνθος δὲ τῷ τοῦ ὠκίμου ἐμφερὲς. καρπὸν δὲ ὡς πράσου. ῥίζαν μέλαιναν, στρογγύλην, ὠχρὰν, ὡς μικρὸν μῆλον, γεῶδες ὄζουσαν, φύεται ἐν πετρώδεσι τόποις.</note>

<margin>Book 26, chapter 8.</margin>
Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist.</note> has it thus: **Pycnocomon** also relaxes [the bowels], with leaves like Rocket but thicker and more sparse; a round root of a yellow color smelling of earth, a square stem of moderate size and thin, and a flower like Basil.

<margin>Form.</margin>
With which characteristics the plant seems to agree...

# OF FABIO COLONNA
36
<warning>Some bleed-through from the reverse page is visible; the left margin is slightly dark.</warning>

...proposed by me, which has a fleshy root, obliquely jointed like the round **Birthwort** <term>Aristolochia: a plant traditionally used in childbirth</term>, protuberant and yellow; it divides itself obliquely into thick and numerous fibers, similar to the root of the older **Valerian** <term>Phu: an ancient name for Valerian</term> described by me. It smells of earth and has a bitter, sharp taste that stings the tongue. It sends up a slender, purplish stem, divided into branches and filled with shoots everywhere from the root to the very top, reaching a height of one, one and a half, or even three or four feet. The leaves, which cling to each joint on opposite sides, are hairy on the upper part, thick, and hard; their edges are divided into segments similar to **Wild Rocket** <term>Eruca sylvestris</term>, but cut deeper than is seen in the illustration. However, before it produces a stem, the leaves are the size of **Hoary Plantain** <term>Plantago media</term>, with few or no serrated divisions—a feature also observed in Wild Rocket, which does not always have incised leaves, especially at the base, where they represent the form of **Narrow-leaved Plantain**. It has a sharp and bitter taste. The leaves cling to the joints of the stem and branches in such a way that they give the stem and branches four "faces" or sides, not unlike the stems of **Basil** <term>Ocimum</term>, **Mercury** <term>Mercurialis</term>, and **Dwarf Elder** <term>Ebulus</term>. 

Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note> recorded that this plant has a "square stem" at its upper head; yet the stem itself does not actually have four angles, but rather the leaves, clinging to the joints in alternating opposite pairs, create that appearance for the stem. At the top of each stem and branch, and from their junctions, three heads emerge on long stalks, purple-blue in color, not unlike those of **Bituminous Trefoil** <term>Trifolii Bituminosi</term> or what is commonly called **Scabious**. They are surrounded by many flowers, but the individual flowers imitate the form of Basil flowers. When these fall away, the fruit remains in their place, containing oblong seeds in heads that are slightly elongated, in the same manner as the smaller **Common Scabious**. Regarding their form and likeness, Dioscorides says it produces fruit like 

<margin>Book 2, chapter on Leeks.</margin>
the **Leek** <term>Porrum</term>; that is, it produces a head like a Leek, the top of which the same Dioscorides called the "foliage" <note>original Greek: ἡ δὲ κόμη αὐτοῦ ("and its foliage")</note>, and 

<margin>Book 4, chapter on Fleawort.</margin>
also in the chapter on **Fleawort** <term>Psyllium</term>: *Its foliage begins from the middle of the stem; there are two or three small heads contracted at the tip.* <note>original Greek: ἄρχεται δ᾽ αὐτοῦ ἡ κόμη ἀπὸ μέσου τοῦ καυλοῦ, κεφάλια δὲ δύο ἢ τρία ἐπ᾽ ἄκρου συνεσταλμένα</note> This is noted in other places as well. But because it has many small heads, and thus many "tufts of foliage," he called it **Pycnocomon** <note>From the Greek *pyknos* (dense) and *kome* (hair or foliage).</note>. That **Pycnocomon** possesses these heads is 

<margin>Book 26, chapter 12.</margin>
testified to me by Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist.</note>, who says: *Everywhere and in any part of the body, and with the greatest discomfort, there arise what are called **boils** <term>furunculi</term>, sometimes a deadly evil in exhausted bodies. A remedy is the leaves of **Pycnocomon** crushed with barley meal <term>polenta</term>.*

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical description of Pycnocomon, comparing its roots, leaves, and stems to known plants like Valerian and Basil, while citing Dioscorides and Pliny to justify its identification and medicinal use for boils.</summary>
<keywords>Pycnocomon, Dioscorides, Pliny, botanical morphology, boils, medicinal herbs, Scabious, plant classification</keywords>

### BY FABIO COLONNA
38

...if they have not yet formed a head. From this, it is now clearly evident that **Pycnocomon** <note>original: "Pycnocomon"; a plant likely belonging to the Scabious family</note> has a head and bears fruit in the manner of a **Leek** <note>Latin: *Porri*</note>. Thus, the older manuscripts—which Marcellus Virgilius <note>a 15th-century scholar and translator of Dioscorides</note> testifies to having consulted—record the phrase "fruit like a leek" <note>Greek: καρπὸν ὡς πράσου (*karpon hos prasou*)</note>, rather than "horehound" <note>Greek: πρασίου (*prasiou*); Latin: *Marrubij*</note> as others read. This latter reading contradicts the system of Dioscorides, who attributes whorls <note>circular arrangements of flowers or leaves</note> to Horehound and plants similar to it.

<margin>Time of growth.</margin> It begins to flower in the month of August, and in September it completes its seeding. Ferrante Imperato <note>Ferdinandus Imperatus, a famous Neapolitan apothecary and collector of natural curiosities</note> provided me with a quantity of this plant pulled from the earthenware pots in which he was cultivating several specimens. I have not seen it growing wild, though I have heard that it is even more jagged and smaller when it lacks cultivation.

<margin>Location.</margin> Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs, a 16th-century German physician and one of the "fathers of botany"</note> says it grows in uncultivated mountain spots, in forests, and sometimes even in meadows and thickets, and that it has blackish roots. Since its ancient name was unknown, more recent authors have called it **Devil's Bit** <note>Latin: *Diaboli morsum*</note> or **Succisa** <note>meaning "cut off from below"</note>; this is because that tuberous part from which the root-fibers descend appears as if it were bitten off or gnawed—though this is not seen in all plants, except occasionally in the older ones. Since Pena <note>Pierre Pena, a French botanist</note> noticed this happens in other plants as well, he prefers—and rightly so—that it be named for its burning power, by which it "bites" buboes <note>swollen lymph nodes</note> and pestilential carbunculos <note>severe, infected boils</note>. Its leaves are applied as a great relief for these, and it is expressly recognized that in this sharpness it surpasses the leaves of **Rocket** <note>Latin: *Eruca*; a pungent salad green</note>.

<margin>Medical powers.</margin> Fuchs testifies that it is hot and quite dry, as it is exceedingly bitter and sharp. For this reason, he says: *It is used as a ready remedy for maturing and even healing carbuncles if the fresh herb is crushed and applied, or if wine in which it has been boiled is drunk.* The root is eaten by itself, or the wine from its decoction is drunk for pains of the womb. He testifies that a manuscript herbal contains these details. And today, he says, since it has the power of dispersing and "cutting" <note>a term for breaking up thick or stagnant bodily fluids</note>, they use it to dissolve **blood clots**.

<margin>Book 8 on the Faculties of Simple Medicines.</margin> Galen <note>the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note> says: *The root, fruit, and leaves of Pycnocomon all have the power of digesting and drawing out, seeing as they are sharp to the taste. Therefore, the leaves disperse tumors and boils; the fruit is even more powerful. Indeed, it can achieve these things when mixed with some digestive poultice, such as one made from barley meal: it draws out splinters and other such things stuck in the outermost skin. Furthermore, the root is inferior and weaker for the aforementioned uses, but it purges yellow bile.* 

<margin>Book 7.</margin> Aegineta <note>Paulus Aegineta, a 7th-century medical writer</note> subscribes to these views. Dioscorides, however, adds a few things, saying: *The seed causes **heavy and tumultuous dreams** when a drachm’s weight <note>about 3.4 grams</note> is drunk. It dissolves tumors when applied with pearled barley. It extracts arrows and thorns driven into the body.* The leaves, when applied, [heal] small swellings and boils...

<meta>catchword: culos (the end of "furunculos" or boils)</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the identification, growth habits, and medicinal uses of Pycnocomon (Devil's Bit). It references botanical authorities like Fuchs and Galen, highlighting the plant's use in treating boils, blood clots, and extracting splinters.</summary>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS
39

...they dissipate swellings. <note>The text likely begins by completing a thought from the previous page regarding the medicinal use of the plant for "tuberculos" (small swellings or lumps).</note> *Its root, taken in mead <note>original: "aqua mulsa"; a mixture of water and honey</note> at a weight of two drachmas, loosens the bowels and extracts bile.* Some authors attribute other powers to this plant that are usually assigned to the **Scabiouses** <note>plants in the genus *Scabiosa*, traditionally used to treat skin conditions like scabies</note>; however, I have observed that the Scabiouses we find growing wild here lack pungency, even if they are perceived by the palate to be slightly bitter. Furthermore, more recent authors—and especially Pena <note>Pierre Pena, a French botanist who co-authored the *Stirpium Adversaria Nova* in 1570</note>—attribute the opposite qualities to the Scabiouses. Indeed, in the chapter on **Scabious** and **Devil’s Bit** <note>original: "Diaboli morsu"; the plant *Succisa pratensis*</note> in his *Adversaria*, he writes that they are endowed with these same powers. Yet, in his *Observations* regarding the plant commonly called **Cornflower** <note>original: "Cyaneo"</note>, he writes that the **Scabious Gum-succories** <note>original: "Chondrillas Scabiosas"</note> and the Cornflowers possess the same temperament: namely, cooling and drying. Therefore, I believe little faith should be placed in them on this point, and that the Scabiouses differ significantly from this plant, even if they appear similar in their flower-heads.

Some object to this identification, arguing that the first leaves of this plant do not resemble **Wild Rocket** <note>original: "Erucæ syluestri"</note> but rather **Plantain**. To these critics, I respond as follows: Dioscorides did not see every plant he wrote about, as he himself testifies in his preface; instead, he recorded what he found described by others without contradiction. Therefore, either he or the person from whom he transcribed the description illustrated the plant’s stalk in its flowering and perfect state, without the first leaves closest to the ground. These lower leaves easily wither when they touch the earth during rainfall—just as in the case of **Wild Woad** <note>original: "Glasto syluestri"</note>, whose first plantain-like leaves he did not see; instead, because the leaves of the stalk surround it in the manner of lettuce, he said the leaves were similar to **Lettuce**. But surely there is no doubt that cultivated Woad differs from the wild variety in nothing but its cultivation. 

For this reason, I judged that little weight should be given to this objection, knowing that the Ancients did not apply the same diligence to describing all parts of plants, as is well known to any experienced person. Furthermore, as I have said, the leaves of Wild Rocket are not incised at the bottom and are broader; Dioscorides always designates the form of the leaves rather than their size, unless he specifies otherwise. Therefore, despite these objections, since this plant matches the name, form, and powers of **Pycnocomon**, I have determined it to be that plant—hitherto unknown and unnoticed by others. Pena describes a certain prickly plant <note>original: "acanaceam plantam"</note> under the name of Pycnocomon, which has absolutely no resemblance to Pycnocomon, and which even he did not dare to assert definitely was that plant. Recent authors have not recognized a purgative power in this plant under the name of **Devil's Bit** <note>original: "Sucisæ"</note>. A diligent and learned physician, who does not follow the beaten path of the cart <note>original: "Carri semitam sequatur"; a metaphor for following common opinion without thinking</note> like others, will now be able to test the power of this plant's root in the manner described by Dioscorides.

<meta>catchword: Sem- (likely beginning "Semina", meaning seeds)</meta>

<summary>Colonna defends his identification of the plant Pycnocomon by critiquing modern botanists like Pena and explaining discrepancies in ancient descriptions by Dioscorides regarding leaf shape and medicinal qualities.</summary>
<keywords>Pycnocomon, Dioscorides, Pierre Pena, Scabious, Devil's Bit, medicinal roots, purgatives, botanical nomenclature, Wild Rocket, Woad</keywords>

HEADER: OF FABIO COLONNA
40
<meta>catchword: Sem- [Sempervivum]</meta>

### *The Third Always-living Plant of Dioscorides and Pliny*
<note>original: "Semperuiuum tertium Dioſc. & Plinj."; this refers to a species of Houseleek or Stonecrop, likely *Sedum stellatum*.</note>

<margin>
The Name and its Etymology.

Habitat.
Form.
</margin>
Even if this plant does not live forever, it has earned the name **Always-living** <note>original: "Semperuiuum"</note> because it is evergreen-like <note>original: "ἀειζωώδης"</note> in its stem, leaves, flowers, and medicinal virtues. It thrives in the same places as its other relatives: namely, on damp rocks; however, once sown, it grows everywhere. The root of this plant is quite small, fibrous, and endowed with many thin filaments. The leaves are wider than those of **Purslane** <note>original: "Portulaca"</note>, mimicking the Greater Always-living plant, though they are smaller, more fleshy, and rounder—especially before the stem emerges. **Dioscorides** <note>a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> compares these to the leaves of the **Birthwort** <note>original: "Ariſtolochiæ Clematitidis"</note> in these words: *with somewhat round leaves, similar to the small always-living plant* <note>original: "φύλλοις ὑποστρογγύλοις ἀειζώου μικροῦ ἐοικόσι"</note>. These leaves are full of juice, hairy, and have four notches or lobes on their wider part. They alternately encircle a thick, reddish, succulent stem, much like the **Wood Spurge** <note>original: "Tithymali Characiæ"</note>. Sometimes the plant has many reclining side-shoots near the root; other times it has a single upright stem, divided at the top into two or three branches, where the leaves are seen to have fewer notches—only two. The first flower emerges tucked into the hollow of these branches; afterward, others appear gradually along the branches starting from the bottom. They are white or milky in color and resemble a star with five rays. In their center, five star-shaped pods of the same form and size develop. These are swollen with many tiny, blackish seeds, not unlike the **Second Stonecrop** <note>original: "Sedum"</note>. When cultivated, the whole plant reaches a height of about nine inches <term>dodrantem</term> <note>a Roman unit of measurement equal to three-quarters of a foot</note> at most. This plant’s foliage is lower than that of the Second Always-living plant, which has worm-like leaves.

<margin>
Time of growth.
</margin>
It flowers in the month of May and finishes in July and August. Its taste is watery at first, and in the end astringent without any stinging bitterness. I have published an illustration of a similar plant, though smaller, which I saw growing wild on the old wall of the **Church of St. Anthony** outside the city, and later in many damp places. In its general appearance, it is similar to the one above, but it differs in its leaves, which are not notched and more closely resemble Purslane leaves, but smaller. It produces yellow flowers instead of white, but matures at the same time and has a similar taste. There is no doubt that it is of the same species and possesses the same powers as the other two types...

<summary>Fabio Colonna provides a detailed botanical description of the "Third Sempervivum," identifying its physical characteristics, habitat, and flowering season according to both personal observation and ancient texts. He identifies a variant with yellow flowers found on a church wall, noting its similarity to the white-flowered variety described by Dioscorides.</summary>
<keywords>Sempervivum, Houseleek, Stonecrop, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, botany, Purslane, Birthwort, Wood Spurge, star-shaped flowers, St. Anthony's Church</keywords>

### HISTORY OF SOME PLANTS
41

They possess the properties of Houseleeks <note>original: "Semperuiui"</note>, according to Dioscorides' opinion, and they seem to fulfill the characteristics of the third kind described by the same author; for he says:

<margin>
Book 4. Description.
</margin>

*It seems there is also a third species of houseleek, which some call wild purslane or telephion, and the Romans call illecebra. It has thicker leaflets compared to those of the purslane, and they are hairy. It grows on rocks.* <note>translated from the original Greek: "Δοκεῖ δὲ εἶναι καὶ τρίτον εἶδος ἀειζώου..."</note> *It appears to be the third genus of Always-living [Sempervivum], which some call Wild Purslane, or Telephion; the Romans call it Illecebra. It has thicker leaves; some read "wider"* <note>Greek: "platutera"</note> *instead of "thicker"* <note>Greek: "pachytera"</note>*, and this is better, as Pliny also does. For the comparative "wider" refers to the leaves of the second Houseleek* <note>term: Sedum (Stonecrop)</note>*, whose leaves are thick and round, like little worms, than which they cannot be thicker; but they are certainly wider, and thus similar to Purslane leaves, for which reason it should be read thus: "It has wider leaflets, very close to Purslane, and hairy. It grows on rocks."* From which text Pliny transcribed thus:

<margin>
Book 25, chapter 13.
</margin>

*Similar to this [the two others described above] is the one the Greeks call Wild Purslane, and the Italians Illecebra, with small, wider leaves and a shorter top; it grows on rocks and is collected for food.*

<margin>
Medicinal Virtues.
</margin>

*The power of all these is the same: to cool and to bind [astringe], etc.* From these things, it is gathered that it is the same plant described above by Dioscorides; it seems to differ only by having contrary properties. Therefore, one must determine which text is to be believed, and what the proper virtues of this plant are.

And certainly, not only from the taste of this plant—which contains within itself the complete characteristics described by Dioscorides and Pliny—but from the conjectures of others, it is to be believed that these virtues, "It has the power," etc., inserted into Dioscorides' text, are spurious <note>Colonna suggests that the medicinal section in his copy of Dioscorides may have been a later, incorrect addition by a scribe</note>. No one doubts that Pliny was the very first to transcribe Dioscorides' text into his own books, even if he never mentions him by name; and it must be assumed that at that time he had an uncorrupted version. 

Therefore, if he faithfully transcribed the accounts of two plants, and also the account of the third Houseleek, it is to be believed that he recorded the characteristics and virtues together in an uncorrupted way. He did not describe those virtues by his own effort, but admits to having transcribed them from more trustworthy authors—not only these, but the entire history. This is clearly evident, for Pliny describes the first, second, and finally the third *Sedum* [Stonecrop], and he did not attribute virtues to each individually, nor only to the first, as in Dioscorides, but at the end of the description of all three he concludes by saying: *The power of all these is the same: to cool and to bind;* and he describes the virtues specifically following that. It is therefore easier and more correctly judged that the same person who [provided] those various names of plants...

F
<meta>catchword: Diosco- [Dioscorides]</meta>
