<meta>This page continues the philological and botanical analysis of the "Third Houseleek" (Sempervivum tertium) from page 42. The author, Fabio Colonna, is critiquing previous commentators and ancient sources who conflated various succulents due to overlapping names like "Wild Purslane" and "Stonecrop."</meta>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS
43

F 2

<note>original catchword: gustu (with the taste)</note>

He attributed these properties to Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De materia medica*.</note>, and added these medicinal powers, having been deceived by a commentary of Galen <note>Claudius Galenus, the influential 2nd-century Roman physician.</note> and by the variety and similarity of the names. For in his *Glossaries of Hippocrates*, Galen records that **Telephium** <note>A name used for various succulents, likely *Sedum telephium* or Orpine.</note> is a species of herb which is called both *Wild Houseleek* and *Wild Purslane*, but by the Romans, it is called **Illecebra** <note>The Latin name for Stonecrop, literally meaning "enticement" or "lure," because it grows on walls and roofs.</note>. This was noticed by the Author who published the *Emblemata* on Dioscorides printed at Basel, and other commentators confirm it; however, they speak of the various naming conventions, not about the falsely attributed medicinal powers, which is the more significant point. Indeed, those various names are found in Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*.</note>, but the powers are not mentioned at all. Therefore, he was more truly deceived by the account of *Wild Purslane*, thinking the **Third Houseleek** had the same powers because it was also called *Wild Purslane*. 

Marcellus <note>Likely Marcellus Empiricus, a 4th-century medical writer.</note> fell into this same trap, as did Virgil <note>The Roman poet, here cited perhaps for botanical descriptions in his *Georgics* or *Eclogues*.</note>, who attributed those things belonging to *Wild Purslane* to the **Third Houseleek**, thinking he had done so correctly. For he did not notice that the contrary account described by Pliny fits the **Third Stonecrop** <note>original: *Sedo tertio*</note> better and is more accurate. I will therefore bring forward another reconciliation of these texts. First, so the truth may be recognized, it must be demonstrated that the account of *Wild Purslane* <note>original: *Portulacae syluestris*</note> is a later addition or spurious. I am led to this because no mention of this *Wild Purslane* is found in Pliny, nor in Galen (who followed Dioscorides in many matters), nor in the records of Paulus <note>Paulus Aegineta, a 7th-century Byzantine physician.</note>. 

Furthermore, it is consistent with reason that there is no real difference between the cultivated and the wild—that is, the spontaneous—Purslane, except for the size resulting from cultivation. They differ in nothing regarding taste and powers, as all other more learned men have already noticed, who have removed that chapter entirely from their own commentaries. I judge that *Wild Purslane* is actually what is called **Peplis** and **Peplion** <note>Usually identified as a species of Spurge, such as *Euphuria peplus*.</note>; for these words of Pliny, speaking of cultivated Purslane, served as a sign to me:

<margin>
Book 20,
chapter 20.
</margin>

*"There is also a Purslane, which they call Peplion, not much more effective than the cultivated kind; whose memorable uses are recorded, etc."* 

He is speaking, of course, of cultivated Purslane, whose many powers he describes; but of the wild kind, namely *Peplion*, there is no other mention in his work. And in Galen:

<margin>
Book 8,
*On the properties of simple medicines*.
</margin>

*"The Peplion shrub has a juice like Spurge <note>original: *Tithymali*</note>, and grows mostly near the sea; having a useless root, just like the Peplos, etc."* 

According to Dioscorides:

<margin>
Book 4.
</margin>

*"Peplis, which some call Wild Purslane, and Hippocrates calls Peplion, grows mostly in maritime regions; it is a broad, shady shrub full of white juice, with leaves like cultivated Purslane, but round and reddish underneath; with round seed under the leaves, as in Peplos; of a fiery [taste]..."*

<summary>Colonna argues that the "Wild Purslane" described by ancient authors is actually a misidentified plant, likely Peplis or a type of Spurge, and corrects the medicinal properties wrongly attributed to the Third Houseleek.</summary>

<keywords>botany, philology, Dioscorides, Galen, Pliny, Houseleek, Purslane, Peplis, medicinal herbs, botanical identification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s philological investigation into the misidentification of succulents in classical texts. He is specifically addressing how later commentators wrongly attributed the caustic properties of "Wild Purslane" (Peplis) to the "Third Houseleek" (Sedum/Sempervivum), leading to potential medical errors.</meta>

### FABIO COLONNA’S
44

<note>original catchword: rem</note>

...with the taste: a slender root, etc. From these details, it is easy to believe that that surrogate description of **Peplis** <note>A species of Spurge, specifically *Euphorbia peplis*.</note>, marked with the name **Wild Purslane**, was written by some other author. This author likely thought it differed from **Peplis**, and being ignorant that they were the same plant, attributed it to Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the primary authority on medical botany in the ancient world.</note> because a distinction for **Wild Purslane** was missing there. The same thing happened to Marcello Virgilio <note>Marcello Virgilio Adriani (1464–1521), a humanist scholar famous for his Latin translation and commentary on Dioscorides.</note>, who, thinking that **Wild Purslane** was the same as the **Third Stonecrop** <note>A species of Sedum.</note> because they were given the same name, placed its history and medicinal powers under the chapter for the **Third Houseleek**. He was deceived by the similarity of the plant’s names and characteristics; therefore, the same words are found attributing the powers of **Wild Purslane** to the **Third Houseleek**. Indeed, those words are found preserved in their entirety in the section on this **Third Stonecrop**, and if any learned person, eager for the truth, wishes to compare them, they will find them to be identical without change. Others think that **Wild Purslane** should not be rejected, but that only the powers attributed to it should be reassigned to the **Third Stonecrop**, from which they believe those powers slipped away. Others confuse **Telephium** <note>Likely Orpine, *Hylotelephium telephium*.</note> and the **Third Stonecrop**, judging them to be the same plant. Others hand down various opinions on this according to their own minds, which do not persuade me in the least.

Therefore, this opinion of mine seems more correct: that **Peplis**, also called *Peplion*, is the same plant as the **Wild Purslane** falsely attributed to Dioscorides; for both are endowed with "fiery" <note>Meaning caustic or burning to the skin.</note> properties, and their form is the same. Therefore, this **Wild Purslane** should be struck out, as it reflects the fiery and ulcer-causing powers of **Spurge** <note>Tithymalus, or Euphorbia, known for its irritating milky sap.</note>. Only **Peplis** shall remain, under a double name, as it is already described by Dioscorides and by Pliny, who recognizes no other **Wild Purslane** except the one called *Peplion*. This grows mostly in maritime regions and on burning, sandy hills, as do many other coastal plants. 

**Telephium** also differs from the **Third Stonecrop**, for both are found in Pliny, and as Dioscorides says: *Telephium has bluish leaves, and two wings at each joint of the leaves, and it grows among vines and in cultivated places.* The **Stonecrop**, however, in his opinion, has hairy leaves and grows on rocks and nowhere else. There are those who prefer to think this **Stonecrop** has "dense" rather than "hairy" leaves, and they interpret the word *dasea* <note>original: δασέα</note> as "dense," which is contrary to Dioscorides’ practice. Indeed, he always uses the word *dasea* <note>original: δασέα</note> for "hairy" [shaggy], and for "dense" he uses *pykna* <note>original: πυκνά</note>, which is evident in many places, and especially in the 

<margin>Book 2 of Dioscorides</margin>
<margin>Book 3 of Dioscorides</margin>
<margin>Dioscorides, same location</margin>

chapter on **Aethiopis** <note>Likely a species of Salvia or Mullein.</note>, where he places both together, saying: *It has leaves that are very hairy and dense.* <note>original: ἔχει τὰ φύλλα δασέα λίαν, καὶ πυκνά</note> Wherefore—

<summary>Colonna clarifies botanical confusion between Peplis (Wild Purslane) and Stonecrop, arguing that caustic properties were wrongly attributed to the latter due to naming errors by commentators like Marcello Virgilio. He uses linguistic analysis of Greek terms for "hairy" versus "dense" to distinguish between species.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s philological investigation into the botanical descriptions of Dioscorides and Pliny. He is specifically refining the definitions of Greek terms like "daseia" (shaggy/hairy) to correctly identify species of Sedum (Stonecrop or Houseleek) and distinguish them from unrelated plants like Wild Purslane or Telephium.</meta>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS
45

...the matter could be interpreted by that word *daseia* <note>original: δασεῖα</note> as "dense," even if "dense" might also be interpreted through a metaphorical translation; however, we see that Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note> uses it to mean "hairy" or "shaggy," to distinguish it from the word *leia* <note>original: λεῖα</note>, meaning "smooth," which are properly opposites. For "dense," he always uses the word *pykna* <note>original: πυκνά</note>. Nor do the chapters on *Hypericum* <note>St. John's Wort.</note> and *Britannica* <note>Possibly a species of Dock or Water-betony.</note> stand in the way; for there too, Dioscorides uses this word *daseia* for "shaggy." Indeed, not only are the seed pods found to be shaggy in *Hypericum*, but the entire plant is tomentose <note>Covered in densely matted, woolly hairs.</note>. And if you take *Britannica* to be Bistort, you will see that the leaves are not "dense" (for it has few), but rather they are hairy, at least on the underside. Therefore, once the "Wild Purslane" is first struck out, and then those words attributing the powers of Purslane to the Stonecrop (*Sedum*) are removed, you will join together the three "Ever-living" plants (*Sempervivum*) of Dioscorides, as is done in Pliny. You will then restore those words after the notes on the second Stonecrop—"the leaves of this," etc.—to the end of the history of the third Houseleek, and thus restore the original text: *the leaves of these have the same power as the two previously mentioned* <note>original Greek: τούτων τὰ φύλλα δυνάμιν ἔχει τὴν ἀυτὴν τοῖν προειρημένοιν</note>. And in this matter, I judge that Pliny is to be believed rather than Galen <note>Aelius Galenus, the prominent Roman physician whose authority dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note>, who described only two Houseleeks, namely the Greater and the Lesser. For that reason, some have thought he described the third Stonecrop in place of *Telephium* <note>Orpine or Livelong.</note>; but certainly *Telephium* is a plant whose medicinal powers—not to mention its form—are foreign to the Stonecrop, nor does it have anything in common with it besides a variety of names.

<margin>*Powers.* Book 26, ch. 13.</margin>
Pliny is the authority that the third Stonecrop is similar in all its powers to the first and larger kind, except that it is less effective. As I have said, he attributes a cooling and astringent <note>A substance that causes the contraction of body tissues, typically used to stop bleeding or secretions.</note> power to all three Houseleeks, and he always made these plants similar in power, joining them with these words:

<margin>Book 25, ch. 13.</margin>
> *All Houseleek (Aizoum), but the juice from the larger one is most effective. And elsewhere: The power of all these is the same: to cool and to bind. The leaves applied, or the juice smeared on, heal eye discharges. For it cleanses ulcers of the eyes, fills them, and leads them to scarring; it glues the eyelids together. These same plants heal headaches if the juice or the leaf is smeared on the temples. They resist the bites of venomous spiders (phalangium), etc.*

The plants which have been depicted by others as the third Stonecrop—having acrid powers and being unfit for food, contrary to Pliny’s opinion—are not this third Stonecrop, but are *aeizooideis* <note>original: ἀειζωοειδείς; plants that look like Houseleek but are not.</note>, not as I have proposed, *aeizoousiai* <note>original: ἀειζωούσιαι; true Houseleeks in essence.</note>. And the smallest plant with worm-like leaves I would judge to be Pliny's second or third *Dactylus* <note>Literally "Finger-grass," often referring to types of couch grass or spiky grasses.</note>; for he says:

<margin>Book 24, last ch.</margin>
> *There are some who also call it "aculeated grass" (spiky grass): there are three kinds. Since there are usually five spikes at the top, they call it Dactylon: these they insert into the nostrils when rolled up, and pull them out for the sake of inducing a nosebleed.*

To the other, which is similar to Houseleek (*Aizoum*),

The Name.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' with floral and architectural motifs.</image-desc>**Strychnon manikon** <note>Mad Nightshade (likely *Datura stramonium* or a related species).</note> was so named because it causes madness, as Dioscorides says; he, having borrowed from Theophrastus, depicted it for us as follows. <margin>Book 4 Description.</margin> *Mad Nightshade, which some have called Persion, and others Thryon. From the description of the former, the following words must be deleted: *it bears fruit in clusters, with ten or twelve berries like the clusters of ivy.* The true sense and description should be this: *It has a black flower, and after the flower a round, black fruit, soft like a grape. A white, thick, hollow root lies beneath, a cubit long.* Which in Greek ought to be read thus: *Anthos melan... os pecheos, etc.* <note>original Greek: "Ἄνθος μέλαν... ὡς πηχέως, &c."</note> <margin>Book 21, chapter 31.</margin> According to Pliny, the leaves should be restored to the description; for he says, speaking of the Nightshades: *The third has the leaves of Basil <note>original: "Ocimi"</note>, which need not be demonstrated with much care to those treating remedies rather than poisons, etc.* It should be understood that the leaves have the form of Basil, but are much larger. But I return to the plant, and perhaps it should be accepted by everyone, since there is nothing that contradicts it. <margin>Form.</margin> It is, therefore, a plant resembling Nightshade with a foul odor, having a straight root...

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

...[the root is] compact, fibrous, and not very long in proportion to the stem, which is tall, matching a man's height, round, branched, and filled with many "wings" <note>Colonna refers to the branching pattern where the stem splits symmetrically.</note>, always dividing itself into two wings, which each in turn divide into two others. It has leaves the size of the "sleeping" and "lethal" Nightshade <note>original: "Solani somniferi & lethalis"; referring to plants like Belladonna or Mandrake.</note> leaves, but wider, soft, and jagged around the perimeter, like the wild narrow-leaved Orach, called Goosefoot <note>original: "pes Anserinus."</note>. It reflects the incisions or serrations halfway between wild Rocket <note>original: "syluestrem Erucam."</note> and the Acanthus called *Paederos*, as Dioscorides would have it. These leaves cling to the stem and branches by long stalks, alternating at intervals. From the ends of these "wings" emerge thick, short, and straight stalks which, in the summer, bear white flowers shaped like a cup or a little bell, like the common *Strammonium* <note>Thorn-apple or Jimsonweed.</note>, and they are scented. When these fall away, an oval pod <note>original: "pilula."</note> emerges; when it is small, it has soft hairs like those of a Plane tree, but when it is mature, it is more prickly and rough, like the pod of the *Xanthium* <note>The Cocklebur.</note>. Nor does it matter, as some say, that the Plane tree's pod is softer and not so rough and prickly; for the same Dioscorides made the roughness of the *Xanthium* pod similar to that of the Plane tree in these words: <note>original Greek: "ἀκανθώδη ὡς πλατάνου σφαίρας"</note> *Spiny, namely, like the spheres of the Plane tree.* However, when the pods of the *Xanthium* are incomplete, they are similar to those described by me, and even when they are complete and mature; therefore, there is no contradiction. The pods of this plant, when they dry out, divide into four parts at the top. They have four faces and as many rounded angles, which is why Dioscorides called it <note>original Greek: "πλατυτέραν"</note> *flatter*, from which the seed falls out. They are swollen with this seed, which is compressed and similar to the seed of the Metel Nut <note>original: "nucis Metellæ"; a common name for Datura seeds used in apothecaries.</note> of the pharmacies, but this one is black.

He is arguing that the ancient descriptions found in Dioscorides and Theophrastus have been corrupted over time because these authors (or their later transcribers) confused two different species of Nightshade—the one that produces prickly pods (Jimsonweed) and the one that produces soft berries (Belladonna).</meta>

->### PLANT. ALIQVOT HIST.<-

->49<-

...growing to a height of ten or twelve cubits <note>original: "vlnæ" (ells or cubits). Colonna is likely describing the impressive height the plant can reach in ideal conditions.</note>, they may be compared to another plant, namely the "sleeping" and "lethal" Nightshade <note>original: "Solano scilicet somnifero, & lethali."</note>, which we call **Belladonna**, and the "Greater" Nightshade, which sends out many stems from the root growing to the same height, as can be seen from the living plant. For not only is the text of Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note> corrupted, but the manuscript of Theophrastus <note>A 4th-century BCE Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany."</note> is also mutilated and debased; for he distinguishes nothing, and seems to confuse the description of the root with the outline of the leaves, and from these he describes a single plant. The opposite is true in Dioscorides, who, in the history of the other Nightshade—the one bearing black flowers and berries—describes that root which belongs to *that* Nightshade, but not to the plant I have proposed, which has a smaller and fibrous root. Furthermore, he neither distinguishes the plants nor describes two, but seems to create a single entry,

<margin><unclear>e . dia . f . a</unclear></margin>

and writes of their powers as if he were describing a single plant. But certainly, it will be known to anyone that he described two; for the same plant cannot produce fruits similar to those of the Plane tree, bristling with thorns, and also black berries, soft like a grape. Therefore, it must be said either that Theophrastus and Dioscorides did not describe them (which is unlikely), or rather, that one of them did; or even that the text of both was corrupted. Those who first transcribed the texts may have omitted intermediate words, and later commentators and interpreters, being ignorant of the plants which the ancients undoubtedly described, left the texts to us in this corrupted state. I am amazed that so many and such learned men, who have interpreted the text of Dioscorides and published commentaries on it, did not at least notice the corrupted text. If they did not know how to restore it, they ought to have made it known to us that it was corrupted and mutilated. I also wonder at Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of "Natural History."</note>, who, out of two "manic" <note>Referring to *Strychnon manikon* or Mad Nightshade.</note> Nightshades, describes only one and makes no mention of the two. Instead, he chose to describe what our people call *Belladonna*, or the **Nightshade with Basil-like leaves**, omitting the other which is found in Theophrastus and Dioscorides as having a **Rocket-like leaf** <note>original: "Erucæ folio"; referring to the jagged, arugula-like leaves of Datura.</note>, perhaps being unwilling to write of it lest it be recognized. For he says:

> *The third has leaves like Basil, which should be described with the least possible care by one treating remedies rather than poisons; for indeed, it causes madness even with a small amount of juice. Although the Greek authors turned it into a joke: for the weight of a <term>drachm</term> <note>A unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams.</note> causes a mockery of one’s modesty.* <note>Pliny is suggesting the plant acts as a powerful disinhibitor or aphrodisiac, causing people to behave shamelessly.</note>

He who wrote the *Emblems* on Dioscorides, because nothing regarding "modesty" is found in Theophrastus, judges the word "modesty" in Pliny’s text to be superfluous; but this was because he was ignorant of the plant's powers—

<sig>G</sig>
<meta>catchword: bat, (continued from "ignorabat")</meta>

<summary>Colonna critiques ancient authors and later commentators for confusing two types of Nightshade (Jimsonweed and Belladonna), arguing that transcription errors led to the mistaken belief that they were a single species. He specifically cites Pliny's warning about the plant's power to cause madness and "shameless" behavior.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s philological and botanical investigation into the "Mad Nightshade." He is arguing that the ancient descriptions found in Dioscorides and Theophrastus have been corrupted over time because these authors (or their later transcribers) confused two different species of Nightshade—the one that produces prickly pods (Jimsonweed) and the one that produces soft berries (Belladonna).</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

->### HISTORY OF CERTAIN PLANTS<-

->50<-

...was, and "the down of modesty" <note>original: "iulum autem pudoris." Colonna is likely interpreting a classical euphemism for puberty or sexual arousal, which he links to the plant's effects.</note> is the same as dealing with sexual matters; for this reason, we call it **Belladonna**. Dioscorides wrote that it produces not unpleasant imaginings, while Theophrastus wrote—more decorously—that it causes one to frolic and believe oneself to be most beautiful. They said these effects are produced, demonstrating that empty phantoms and conspicuous images appear before the eyes. *A double dose of this measure causes true madness. Whatever weight is added beyond that, however, brings about death.*

These powers of this Nightshade seem to have been attributed by Theophrastus and Dioscorides to another kind, which has a leaf like Rocket <term>Eruca: a genus of flowering plants, including what we now call arugula or rocket</term> approaching that of Acanthus. But if what Pliny writes is correctly weighed against Theophrastus and Dioscorides, it will be seen that these attributes belong not to the Rocket-leaved variety, but to the Basil-leaved Nightshade <note>original: "Ocimi folio" (with the leaf of Basil).</note>. 

<margin>Book 9, chapter 12.</margin>

For what Theophrastus writes, from the words: *But the 'manic' kind, which some call Thryoron,* <note>original Greek: "ὁ δὲ μανικὸν, οἱ μὲν θρύορον καλοῦσιν."</note> up to the words, *But it has a leaf:* <note>original Greek: "ἔχει δὲ τὸ μὲν φύλλον."</note> and what I have transcribed from Pliny, and from Dioscorides from the phrase, *it has a black flower, etc.* all the way to the end, will appear to be the same thing. 

Therefore, from these accounts, it will be clear that everyone was describing the Nightshade with Basil-like leaves—which we call **Belladonna**—and its powers. One must say that the powers of the Rocket-leaved Nightshade were either included under the same description, or not described at all, or else the descriptions are flawed. Indeed, it is not the root of that plant—even if it is found to be sleep-inducing by the experiment of more recent authors—but rather the seed that possesses "manic" <term>manicas: relating to "mania" or madness</term> powers, corresponding exactly to the powers of the other's root, as you can see in the works of the plant collector. 

Thus it should be said that Pliny perhaps only described the Basil-leaved Nightshade because it was known to him and grew in our climate. As for the other, the Rocket-leaved kind, he was unwilling to write about it as it was unknown and exotic to him; since he did not wish to describe even what he did record, he did not say a single word about the other. 

Therefore, I judge that thanks should be returned to God, since in our age we recognize things unknown to the ancients and their mutilated and corrupted history, and we have been made more certain of the truth. We now have two "Mad Nightshades" <note>original: "Strychna manica."</note> delineated with a more certain history, besides many other exotic plants mentioned by Theophrastus and others, now recognized and described by the same plant collector. 

The first person to provide me with a supply of this exotic plant was Leonardo Antonio Recchi <note>A physician who edited the works of Francisco Hernández on Mexican plants.</note>, a most skilled physician to Philip II, King of the Spains and true defender of the Christian faith. Through his industry and labor, a Latin history of a great many exotic plants has been described; by the generosity, mandate, and expense of the same King, it will be published and brought to light as soon as possible. This...

<margin>
Book on Aromatics, chapter on Datura.
</margin>
Christopher Costa <note>Cristóvão da Costa (c. 1515–1594), a Portuguese physician and botanist who documented plants from the East Indies.</note> calls this plant **Datura**; and physicians judge it to be the legitimate *Methel* nut of the Arabs <note>original: "nucem Methel Arabum." This refers to *Datura metel*, long used in Arabic medicine for its narcotic effects.</note>. They determine it to be cold in the third degree and dry at the end of the second. They assert that its powers—which are clearly "manic" <note>Colonna uses "manicas" to mean "madness-inducing."</note>—reside in the root and even more so in the seed. One may see its other powers and the method of its use in the work of that same author.

<margin>
Book 8, On the Properties of Simple Medicines.
</margin>
Galen, however, speaking above regarding all the Nightshades <term>Solanum: a genus of plants that includes modern staples like potatoes, but here refers specifically to the toxic "Mad Nightshades"</term>, says: "Furthermore, what remains of the proposed Nightshades is useless for medications applied internally to the body."

> *For if four drachmae <term>drachma: an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams or 1/8th of an ounce</term> are administered, they bring death: if fewer, at least insanity. One drachma is certainly taken without harm, yet it possesses nothing useful in itself. However, if it is smeared on the outside of the body in the manner of a plaster, it cures malignant ulcers and spreading sores <note>original: "nomode," referring to gangrenous or "eating" skin conditions.</note>.*

But for such uses, the bark of the root is preferred, being drying in the second degree once complete, and cooling at the beginning of the third, or cooling at the beginning of the second. If anyone wishes to restore the text of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*.</note> to its original state, he might, in my judgment, write and read it thus. In Greek, he would read it this way:

"Mad Nightshade <note>original Greek: Στρύχνον μανικὸν (Strychnon manikon).</note>, which some have called *Persion*, and others *Thryon*. It sends up from the root ten or twelve immense stalks, having the height of a fathom <note>original Greek: ὀργυιᾶς (orgyia), the distance of a man's outstretched arms.</note>: the flower is black: and after this it has fruit that is round, black, and soft, like a grape. Beneath is a root, white, thick, and hollow, a cubit long. It grows in mountainous places, exposed to the wind, and in groves of plane trees. The root, when drunk in wine in the amount of one drachma, has the power to produce imaginings that are not unpleasant. But two drachmae, when drunk, displace the mind for up to three days. Four drachmae, when drunk, actually kill. Its antidote is much honey-water drunk and then vomited up. The other variety has a leaf similar to rocket <term>Eruca: a genus of flowering plants, including what we now call arugula</term>, but larger, approaching that of the Acanthus, which some also call *paederos*: it sends up a stalk nearly a fathom long, with a head sitting upon it shaped like an olive, but shaggier, like the seed-ball of a plane tree, but larger and broader. The root and seed of this plant, when drunk in wine, have the same power as the previously mentioned one."

*Mad Nightshade, which some have called Persion and others Thryon; one kind indeed sends up from the root huge stalks, ten or twelve in number, having the height of a fathom, with a black flower; and after the flower, it has a fruit that is round, black, and soft like a grape. Beneath lies a white, thick, hollow root, a cubit in length: it is produced in the mountains, and in places exposed to the wind, and in Plane-tree groves. The root has the power, when drunk in wine in the weight of one drachma, of producing imaginings that are not unpleasant. But two drachmae drunk displace the mind for up to three days. Four drachmae drunk are fatal. The antidote for this is much honey-water drunk and vomited. The other variety, however, has a leaf similar to Rocket, though larger, approaching that of the Acanthus, which they call Pæderos; a stalk almost a fathom in length: a head sitting upon it with the shape of an olive, but shaggier, like the little ball of a Plane tree, but larger...*

<header>FABIO COLONNA</header>

...indeed, and more clearly. Its root and seed, drunk in wine, have the same power as mentioned above.

### The Clymenum of Dioscorides :

<margin>Name.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter Q, circular frame with floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>The plant that Theophrastus calls *Clymenon*, Dioscorides calls *Periclymenon* <note>original: "Periclymenon," likely Honeysuckle (Lonicera).</note>; and under these different names, they describe the same plant and attribute the same medicinal virtues to it. <margin>Book 25, Chapter 7.</margin> Pliny, however, who compiled his work from both authors, made no distinction between the *Clymenum* of Theophrastus and that of Dioscorides. When speaking of *Clymenum*, Pliny assigns to it the characteristics that Dioscorides gives to *Periclymenon* (taken from Theophrastus). He fails to notice that Theophrastus’s *Clymenum* is actually Dioscorides’s *Periclymenon*. 

Confusing the plants, Pliny seems to divide them into two species: the first, perhaps so named by the Latins, to which he attributes the leaves and seeds of Ivy—which Dioscorides attributes to *Periclymenon*; the second, he says, is so called by the Greeks (being unwilling to cite Dioscorides directly), and has leaves similar to Plantain, etc., which is the true *Clymenum* of Dioscorides. <margin>Book 27, Chapter 12.</margin> Afterward, having forgotten what he had already written about *Clymenum*, Pliny inserts into his book the history of *Periclymenon* written almost word-for-word from Dioscorides, thinking that no one would notice this or stand up for Dioscorides’s original work. 

Galen and later writers omitted the history of *Clymenum* entirely, mentioning only *Periclymenon*. Dioscorides, however, separates *Clymenum* from *Periclymenon* and relates its history separately, making it distinct in name, form, and properties; speaking of which, he says the following:

<margin>Book 4, Description.</margin>
*Clymenum* <note>original Greek: Κλύμενον</note> *bears a square stem similar to that of a Bean; its leaves are like those of the Ribwort Plantain <term>Arnoglossos: the Greek term for Plantain (Plantago), specifically the "lamb's tongue" or narrow-leaf variety</term>. It has small pods on the stem nodding toward each other, similar to an Iris and to the tentacles of an octopus* <note>original Greek: "polypodos plektanais." This refers to the curling, twisted shape of the seed pods, which Dioscorides compared to the suckered arms of a sea creature.</note>.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It will be evident that no plant matches this description except for the one I have depicted here. This plant is indeed endowed with a small white root divided into thin fibers, from which erupt leaves half a foot long, resembling the leaves of narrow-leaved Plantain, or like the leaves of Woad <term>Glastum: common woad (Isatis tinctoria), a plant used since antiquity for both medicine and blue dye</term>.

<meta>catchword: Dio-</meta>

He emphasizes the plant's distinctive square stem and octopus-like seed pods to distinguish it from similar species.</summary>

# FABIO COLONNA

Also in the opinion of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note>, [the leaf] is eroded along the circumference with shallow notches, and in their division, a black point is seen: it is fleshy, slightly shaggy, sticky to the touch, and juicy. It puts forth a square, shaggy stem, which two leaves joined together from opposite sides embrace at the very bottom; later, however, due to the abundance of moisture, they surround it alternately and at every angle, for it produces five angles, as is seen in the larger and smaller Figwort <term>Scrophularia</term> <note>a genus of flowering plants often used in folk medicine for "scrofula" or swollen glands</note>: it is filled with many other [stems]. From the back of the leaves, a kind of border descends through the angle of the stem, so that it seems to represent the angle of a Bean stem. It always produces deep yellow flowers at the top of the leaf axils <note>the "armpit" of the plant where the leaf meets the stem</note> and the stem from early Spring until Autumn, which are somewhat scented and imitate the color of diluted saffron. In size and form, they are in some way similar to the Chrysanthemum <note>literally "golden flower" in Greek</note> of Dioscorides with its jagged leaves; however, they differ in the interior part, which is not solid and filled with tiny florets imitating the orb of an eye, but is instead full of small leaves and <term>stamens</term>, sometimes turning from green to blue. When the petals of the flowers fall off, curved pods or husks arise, expressing the shape of the letter C. These contain an oblong, thin seed within, of the same shape but three times smaller, yellowish-brown or tending toward the color of ash. These husks are seen to be composed into little globes—especially in the many-flowered and prolific varieties of the plant—so that they seem to rival a Lily root made of scaly parts: for the individual small husks all strive to meet at a single point, which is why some lie upon others further inside. In the plant with a simple flower, the husks appear to be constructed differently and imitate the compressed form of an Onion; they are not all similar like the ones mentioned above, but are seen with a triple difference in form, though each contains the same seed in its back. Those that occupy the interior part of the globe are so bent inward that they almost complete a circle, and these are the smallest of all. Others, however, lying upon these, are wider and larger, and seem to imitate the shape of a little boat. The remaining outer ones, lying upon all the others, are longer and thinner than the second type; they are not twisted like the first ones, but complete a half-circle <note>a "hemicycle"</note>. When dried, these differ very little from the others, as is seen in the illustration. Therefore, if anyone considers this rightly, they will see the wide husks touching each other like the leaves of the Iris flower—from which the Iris <note>the Greek word for "rainbow"</note> took its name because they create an arch of various colors, as Dioscorides reports in these words: <note>original Greek: ἄνθη δὲ ἐπὶ καυλῷ παράλληλα / anthē de epi kaulō par-allēla</note> "the flowers [grow] parallel upon the stem..."

<margin>Book 1.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the seed pods of the plant previously described, specifically focusing on their curved shape and texture, drawing comparisons to the Iris flower and the tentacles of an octopus to explain the nomenclature used by ancient authorities like Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus.</meta>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...curved and varied <note>original Greek: λήλα, ἐπικαμπῆ, ποικίλα</note>; and one might compare the other longer outer pods, which differ only slightly from those, to the leaves of the Iris flower curved back toward the earth; he will say that these pods mimic the shape of the Iris flower quite beautifully. Indeed, Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician and father of pharmacognosy</note> only notes that they are "nodding toward one another," meaning they are curved inward. If you examine the individual pods, you will see that the back of each one is rough with small bumps; although the few longer outer pods are somewhat shaggy, those that occupy the inner position all have a back roughened with bumps, by which they attempt to imitate the suckers <term>acetabula</term> <note>literally "vinegar cups," used here to describe the suction cups on an octopus limb</note> of the octopus's tentacles. Therefore, a comparison is drawn: they are curved in the manner of an Iris flower, but they are similar to the tentacles of an octopus because they are rough with bumps, resembling the suckers of the octopus's limbs. By the word "tentacles" <note>original Greek: πλεκτάνας (plektanas)</note>, Dioscorides does not simply mean the limbs, but the suckers, or more correctly, the limbs filled with suckers. That this is the true meaning of Dioscorides is proven from the text of the chapter on the Polypody <term>Polypodium</term> <note>a genus of ferns</note>, in which it is described that the Polypody is so named because it has a root similar to the tentacles of an octopus filled with suckers; therefore it was called <gloss>polypodium</gloss> from the "feet of the octopus" <note>original Greek: ἀπὸ πολύποδος ποδῶν, πολυπόδιον</note>. His words are: "the root is hairy underneath, having tentacles just like an octopus," etc. This is confirmed by the text of Pliny,
<margin>
Book 26, 
Chapter 8.
</margin>
who says: *The root in use is hairy, of an herbaceous color inside, the thickness of the smallest finger, full of cavities like suckers, just like the tentacles of an octopus, etc.* If anyone looks at the root of a Polypody, he will recognize this: for it has many bumps, by which it seems to represent the suckers of the octopus's feet. This is clearly evident from Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany"</note>,
<margin>
Book 9, 
Chapter 13.
</margin>
who, speaking of the diversity of roots, says: "that of the polypody is hairy and has suckers, just like the tentacles of the octopus," etc. Furthermore, that the fruit of this plant is called "pods" <term>folliculi</term> and not "seeds" is clearly obvious, for the terms <gloss>pouch</gloss> and <gloss>small bag</gloss> <note>original Greek: θύλαξ (thylax) and θυλάκιον (thylakion)</note> refer to a capsule, a sheath, or any hollow thing, especially an oblong one, which contains or can contain something. Therefore, since these fruits, when broken open, contain a much smaller seed having its own skin, there should be no doubt that they are truly "small bags" <note>original Greek: θυλάκια (thylakia)</note>. If this does not satisfy someone, I bring forward another example from Dioscorides, namely the fruit of Woad <term>Glastum</term> <note>Isatis tinctoria, used for blue dye</note>, which has the appearance of little tongues; Dioscorides called these "small bags," and yet they have neither such a thick pod, nor more than a single seed each. On account of its abundant flowers and its vigor, by which it lasts even until Autumn and Winter, always putting forth flowers, this plant has been called by more recent authors
->the flower of every month<- <note>original Italian: fior d' ogni mese</note>

<summary>Fabio Colonna explains the morphology of seed pods by comparing them to octopus tentacles and Iris flowers, using this to justify botanical names found in the works of Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus. He also clarifies technical Greek terms for seed vessels and identifies the plant's common Italian name based on its long blooming season.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion regarding the identification and physical characteristics of plants described by ancient authorities, specifically debating whether a certain species is the "Caltha" of the poets or the "Chrysanthemum" of Dioscorides, while also analyzing its medicinal temperaments.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

...it was among the more robust: for which reason it is a resident of every garden and delights in cultivation, but in the woods it does not endure as well, nor does it become so lush, but is altogether more slender and smaller, and the stem is more often seen to be entirely square because of the lack of moisture. Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a German physician and one of the three "fathers of botany"</note> and all those after him thought this plant was the *Caltha* <note>likely referring to the Pot Marigold, *Calendula officinalis*</note> based on a rather trivial argument. It can be more easily and more truly demonstrated that the *Caltha* of the Poets is actually the *Chrysanthemum* <note>meaning "Golden Flower"</note> of Dioscorides, which in various lists of names is called *Chalcanthemon* <note>original: Chalcanthemõ; meaning "Copper Flower"</note>. It was so named *Chalc<a>* because of its golden or copper-like color <note>original: aureum, vel æreum</note>, and from there the name was corrupted to *Caltha*; it is also called *Buphthalmum* <note>meaning "Ox-eye"</note> because of the shape of the flower. 

Even here, the vegetable gardeners <term>olitores</term> retain the name *Buphthalmum*, for it is common at the edges of gardens. This is proved by the same argument they use: namely, by the heaviness of the scent, which in the *Chrysanthemum* is sensed not only in the flower but in the whole plant by anyone with a nose; and by the color, shape, and brilliance of the flowers; and by its very frequent growth around buildings (which is not the case with this plant, the *Clymenum* <note>original: Clymẽno</note>); and by the divided shape of the leaves, their smoothness, and their use, which is also retained by these gardeners—namely, that the tender young shoots are eaten in the manner of potherbs and are even included in salads. Many of these features seem to be missing in the so-called *Caltha* (let those perverters corrupt the text of Dioscorides as much as they wish, those who are envious and unskilled in the pure readings of Dioscorides and Pliny!). Believing this plant to be *Caltha*, these more recent writers have attributed "hot" powers to it.

<margin>Dioscorides, Book 1, Ch. 8</margin>
Although they say these powers have been proven by experiments, I do not know if they should be believed, since the taste of the plant demonstrates the opposite: indeed, no heat is perceived by the palate. These same writers admit that the heat of the leaves does not exert itself immediately because of the moisture joined to it, but certainly it should appear after some interval.

<margin>Dioscorides, Book 1, Ch. 13</margin>
Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an Italian doctor and naturalist</note> likewise says it is not lacking in astringency, even though everyone considers it "hot" because, as Fuchs says, it partakes of a moderate sweetness joined with bitterness. But certainly, it is harsh <term>acerba</term> in taste, leaning not at all toward bitterness, but toward astringency, and it is filled with a watery and cold juice. Even if it were granted (though not conceded) that it were slightly bitter—for what they thought was "sweet" is actually "watery"—it would not therefore be "hot." Even if a bitter taste proceeds from a hot-drying or "concocting" humor, since all plants have a mixed quality (for they consist of the four elements), their quality must be judged not from these alone, or from taste alone, but from all factors joined together. For all plants abound in watery humor, yet they do not all cool for that reason; likewise, those that are bitter...

<summary>Fabio Colonna argues that the plant often identified as "Caltha" is actually Dioscorides' "Chrysanthemum," citing its appearance, scent, and culinary use by gardeners. He also disputes the common contemporary belief that the plant has a "hot" temperament, arguing instead that its physical properties and taste suggest it is cold and watery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of plant temperaments and qualities, specifically addressing the misconception that all bitter-tasting plants are "hot" in nature, and moves into the specific medicinal applications for the eyes and menstrual regulation.</meta>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...not all plants have a heating effect; for even if a bitter flavor is dominant—being the most detectable of all, since even the smallest part of bitterness disturbs a large mass of tasteless or sweet substance, as well as austere and harsh ones—the power of a plant's quality does not depend on bitterness alone. Rather, it depends on the greater and more abundant force of its elemental mixture. This must be determined through experience and observed effects, alongside a proper tasting of the plant and consideration of whether its flavor is simple or mixed.

Those plants which are truly bitter and "hot" almost all share a certain acrimony <note>original: acrimonia; a sharp, biting quality</note>, or a strong aromatic or even heavy odor. However, those in which neither sharpness nor odor is perceived by the palate or tongue are, for the most part, "cold" and "dry," or "moist." Even if they are fragrant, such as **Roses**, **Violets**, and similar flowers, their cooling nature does not come from their scent, even though the scent itself is produced by the "concoction" <note>the process of medicinal maturation or "cooking" by internal heat</note> of heat. 

For that bitter flavor often proceeds from an unrefined earthy humor, as seen in all immature fruits, which possess a bitterness mixed with harshness. This is the opposite of those things that turn bitter from sweetness due to excessive heat or over-maturation. To conclude, it is certain that <term>Picrin</term> <note>literally "the bitter one," likely referring to a species of Bitter-cress or Ox-tongue</note>—which is so named for its bitterness—and the extremely bitter <term>Opium</term> and similar substances do not heat at all; rather, there is no doubt that they cool and dry. Their bitterness comes from a dense, earthy, and dry part, rather than a subtle or "airy" part that has been well-refined, as is the case in acrid and aromatic plants. Those aromatic plants retain their scent and flavor for a long time when dried; indeed, their scent and flavor often become more noticeable upon drying.

In contrast, in this plant and its relatives, the dried leaves lose both their flavor and whatever scent they possessed, a fact I have experienced more than once. Finally, they say that the distilled water of this plant in bloom wards off redness and inflammation of the eyes if it is dripped into them or if soaked linens are applied. I, however, know that plants and all things that are distilled <note>original: sublimantur; refers here to the process of distillation</note> lose a significant portion of their cooling power. This happens because the humor is thinned by the heat—contrary to the thing's true nature—and the earthy part, in which the greatest cooling power resides, is left behind. Against redness and inflammation, one should not use heating substances (especially at the beginning of the affliction, a distinction they fail to make), but rather cooling and "repelling" <note>original: repercutientibus; medicines intended to drive back inflammatory humors</note> ones.

Pena <note>Petrus Pena, a 16th-century French physician and botanist</note> adds: *It has been found that the juice and tender tops, taken in egg yolk as a cake and chewed, stop excessively flowing menses, but impel those that are lingering.* It is most certain that blood is created from eating eggs, especially the yolk, and when this blood becomes easily abundant, nature...

<summary>Colonna argues that bitterness in plants does not always indicate a "hot" temperament, citing Opium and Picrin as cooling examples, and critiques the use of distilled waters for eye inflammation.</summary>
<keywords>botany, pharmacology, plant temperaments, bitterness, Picrin, Opium, eye treatments, Petrus Pena, Galenic medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal analysis of plant temperaments, moving from the general theory of bitter flavors to the specific properties of a plant—likely a species of Clymenum—and its effects on internal flows and wound healing.</meta>

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

...[after] heating, it brings on the menses <note>the monthly menstrual flow</note> so that the body may unburden itself. However, because the yolks of eggs combined with this herb also stop excessive menstrual flooding, this must be attributed to the specific power of the herb. From this, if it is true, it follows that the plant does not heat, but rather cools—unless, I should say, it causes a burning sensation—agreeing with Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> who says, "The juice, when drunk, is effective for the spitting up of blood, for those with abdominal disorders, and for the red flux <note>original Greek: ῥοῦν ἐρυθρὸν (rhoun erythron); referring here to heavy menstruation</note>, being both astringent and cooling." This is certainly perceived by the sense of taste, as I have said, unless the palate is calloused; it is already established by the experience of women, and they use it to stop hemorrhoidal flows.

That it might be able to cure the "royal disease" <note>original: regium morbum; a historical term for jaundice, so named because it was thought to require expensive, "regal" remedies</note> would depend on the color and substance of the flower (if the <term>phytognomists</term> <note>those who believed a plant’s physical appearance revealed its medicinal use; for example, yellow flowers curing yellow jaundice</note> are to be believed). However, I think this comes not from a "propulsive force" but rather from a certain sympathy of color <note>original: <greek>συμπαθείᾳ</greek> (sympatheia)</note>, which I do not always trust. Thus, one would have to say that this plant is endowed with contrary and diverse powers in its different parts, if indeed this is true.

<margin>Powers.</margin>
Experimentalists have attributed to this plant the power of healing wounds, which is not surprising given the viscosity of its gluey humor; for the entire plant is sticky to the touch. Dioscorides, however, attributes these powers to **Clymenum**, writing thus: "The most excellent kind is the mountain variety: the whole plant is pressed with the root. The juice, when drunk, is effective for the spitting up of blood, for those with abdominal disorders, and for the red flux, being both astringent and cooling." It also restrains nosebleeds.

<margin>Book 26, chapter 14.</margin>
"Furthermore, the shredded leaves, or the small pods <note>original: folliculi</note> applied to fresh wounds, lead them all the way to scarring." Pliny, however, says: "The pods of **Clymenum** are applied to fresh strikes." Elsewhere, even if he mentions **Clymenum**, it should be understood as **Periclymenum** <note>likely Honeysuckle</note>. The plant depicted by Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> is not the **Clymenum** of Dioscorides, since it is a legume—which we call the **Chickling Vetch** <note>original: Cicerculam</note>, as others have also noticed—and it has tendrils. Had Dioscorides been describing this, he would not have remained silent about the tendrils to differentiate it from **Periclymenum**, which winds itself around trees by its flexibility. But even this objection would not be necessary if it matched in other respects. It is enough that it does not have pods resting one upon the other, similar to an **Iris**, or the tentacles of **Polypods** <note>octopuses</note> as I have said. By these marks, we can reject the **Clymenum** depicted by Dalechampius with its whorled flowers, as well as **Scrophularia** (or as they say, **Water Betony**), which was also thought to be **Clymenum** because of its round seed-vessels, like those of **Scrophularia**, and its leaves similar to **Betony**. We can also reject the "Sicilian" plant named by Lobellius and other plants in this way, including every plant which has pods...

H
<meta>catchword: non</meta>

## Dioscorides’ Astragalus
<note>Likely *Astragalus christianus* or a similar species of Milk-vetch</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring detailed floral and foliate scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>
<margin>& Etymology.</margin>
The *knucklebone-like* shape <note>original Greek: ἀστραγαλοειδής (astragaloeidēs)</note> of the SEEDS gave this plant the name Astragalus. Indeed, *astragalos* <note>original Greek: ἀστράγαλος</note> signifies a knucklebone or a game of dice, the shape and use of which the seeds of this plant recall. They are rounded in form, but flat on the upper and lower parts, or even concave like small sockets. It could also be so named from the tuberous or fleshy, compressed shape of the root, or even better, from the multitude of knotted fibers that resemble a whip—whence it is called a "knotted whip" <note>original Greek: ἀστραγαλωτὴς μάστιξ (astragalōtēs mastix)</note> by Pollux <note>Julius Pollux, a 2nd-century Greek grammarian and sophist</note>. 
<margin>Book 10. Form.</margin>
The root of this plant is dark on the outside and pale on the inside, having many knotted fibers that are hard, long, and tangled. In the middle, at the ends, and at the tips of these, one finds something like small, solidifying bulbs by which the root propagates itself. Occasionally—I have found them at the end of the month of May—certain fleshy, thick, purplish, juicy, hard, and smooth parts are found, which could be called *lower-knucklebones* <note>original Greek: ὑπαστράγαλοι (hypastragaloi)</note>; they seem to represent nothing else, yet they do not unfold but instead remain in this overflowing, superfluous state. It sends out three or four thin, square stems similar to a Chickpea <note>original: Ciceri; the common chickpea, *Cicer arietinum*</note>, rising only slightly from the earth and leaning toward it. From these stems, leaves are attached to stalks at intervals, just as in the Chickpea; first in pairs, then in fours (two opposite each other), then in fives, and finally in threes running opposite each other. In shape, they approach the leaves of Butcher's Broom <note>original: Rufci (Ruscus)</note>, but they are larger, twice as long, more tender, and smooth, resembling the new leaves of Ivy <note>original: Hederæ</note>. The stalks on which they sit have little "ears" <note>botanical term: stipules</note> at the bottom where they attach to the stem, as is seen in the Chickpea and similar legumes. By "ears," I mean those two small leaflets clinging to the stalk and the joint of the stem. At the very tip, there is something like a small thorn, but it is soft. Between the leaf stalks and the stem, small shoots emerge, about nine inches long <note>original: dodrantem; a Roman measure of about 3/4 of a foot</note>, nodding toward the ground. From the middle to the top of these, there are nearly twenty flowers, clustered at first and then arranged in the manner of Vetch <note>original: Viciæ</note>, similar to it in both shape and color. For they are purple on the outside and blue in the center,

<sig>H 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: inte- (internal/interior)</meta>

Colonna describes the plant's morphology, comparing its stems and leaves to the chickpea and its flowers to vetch.</summary>

### Dioscorides’ Astragalus
<note>Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician and pharmacologist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note>

<margin>& Etymology</margin>
The **knucklebone-like** shape <note>original Greek: ἀστραγαλοειδής (astragaloeidēs)</note> of the SEEDS gave this plant the name **Astragalus**. Indeed, the word *astragalos* <note>original Greek: ἀστράγαλος</note> signifies a knucklebone or a game of dice, the shape and use of which the seeds of this plant recall. They are rounded in form, but flat on the upper and lower parts, or even concave like small sockets. It could also be so named from the tuberous or fleshy, compressed shape of the root, or even better, from the multitude of knotted fibers that resemble a whip—whence it is called a "knotted whip" <note>original Greek: ἀστραγαλωτὴς μάστιξ (astragalōtēs mastix)</note> by Pollux <note>Julius Pollux, a 2nd-century Greek scholar who wrote a dictionary of terms.</note>.

<margin>Book 10. Form.</margin>
The root of this plant is dark on the outside and pale on the inside, having many knotted fibers that are hard, long, and tangled. In the middle, at the ends, and at the tips of these, one finds something like small, solidifying bulbs by which the root propagates itself. Occasionally—I have found them at the end of the month of May—certain fleshy, thick, purplish, juicy, hard, and smooth parts are found...

<summary>This page explores the etymology and physical characteristics of the Astragalus plant, linking its name to the knucklebone-like shape of its seeds and describing its complex root system. It reflects the Renaissance effort to accurately identify plants mentioned in ancient Greek medical texts through direct observation.</summary>

HISTORY OF VARIOUS PLANTS
61

...of [the roots]. On the inside, however, they are yellow and adorned with various colors. When these gape open, thin pods four fingers long emerge from their vessels; 
<margin>Book 4, chapter 1</margin>
at first they are green, but when dried they turn reddish, as does the seed. They hang down toward the ground and are filled with seeds shaped, as I said, like a knucklebone <note>original Greek: ἀστραγαλοειδεῖ (astragaloeidēs)</note>. 
<margin>Season</margin>
It flowers in May, and the fruit reaches maturity in the month of June. 
<margin>Habitat</margin>
It grows in mountains, valleys, and in both shady places and windy spots illuminated by the sun; it is found everywhere here. The root is hard and can only be crushed or broken with difficulty, even when dried. It has an astringent flavor, but the taste is flat <note>The author uses "fatuo" to mean tasteless or insipid</note>, with an earthy smell similar to mushrooms. This plant, more than others, seems to correspond to the description by Dioscorides, who says:

<margin>Description. Book 4</margin>
> Astragalus is a small shrub growing near the ground, with leaves and branches similar to the chickpea <note>original: *Cicer*</note>. The flowers are purple and small. Beneath is a round root, like a very large acorn <note>original: *balanus*</note>, which has solid, black, hard offsets like horns intertwined with one another, and is astringent to the taste. It grows in windy, shady, and snowy places. <note>This is a translation of the Greek and Latin text provided in the original: "Ἀστράγαλος θαμνίσκος ἐστὶ μικρὸς..." and "Astragalus paruus frutex est..."</note>

<margin>Book 26, chapter 8</margin>
Pliny, however, describes it differently: *Astragalus has long leaves with many incisions, slanted around the roots; three or four stems full of leaves, and a flower like a Hyacinth; the roots are hairy, tangled, red, and very hard.* He seems to have described the same plant; the difference is that he says it has leaves "with many incisions," a detail that Dioscorides would not have omitted. Just as Dioscorides said, "Astragalus is similar to the chickpea in its leaves and branches," he would have phrased it as he usually does when he wants to sketch the form and likeness of leaves, in this manner: "Astragalus has leaves similar to the chickpea." But because he made the general habit of the plant similar to the chickpea, not the leaves specifically, that is how he described it; for it is the arrangement of the leaves, not their shape, that it must imitate. Therefore, I believe that either Pliny himself, or the source from which he transcribed, or someone who revised Pliny's text wanting to make it match Dioscorides, added those words "with many incisions," thinking the similarity lay in the jagged edges of chickpea leaves. I consider those words to be superfluous, and I believe the account of the Astragalus in Pliny should be read as follows:

<margin>Book 22, chapter 9</margin>
*Astragalus has long leaves, slanted around the roots; three or four stems full of leaves, and a flower like a Hyacinth*—understand this to mean the color, just as he says the same of the Sea Holly <note>original: *Eryngium*</note> flower, and as Dioscorides does in the chapters on Snapdragons <note>original: *Antirrhinum*</note>, Birthwort <note>original: *Aristolochia*</note>, Safflower <note>original: *Cnicus*</note>, and similar plants—*roots that are hairy, tangled, red, and very hard.* And thus the plant proposed by me corresponds to both descriptions—

<summary>Fabio Colonna compares a physical specimen of the Astragalus plant with the classical descriptions of Dioscorides and Pliny, arguing that a perceived discrepancy in Pliny's description of the leaves was likely a later error or interpolation.</summary>

BY FABIO COLONNA
62

<margin>
Medicinal Properties.
Galen, Book 8 on the properties of simple medicines.
</margin>
...it seems to correspond not only in its form, but also in its medicinal properties. Indeed, it has astringent roots, as even Galen maintains, who says: *Astragalus is a small shrub having roots with binding properties; for which reason it is numbered among those that dry quite effectively. For it heals old ulcers and stops the bowels when troubled by a flux, if one drinks the root boiled in wine.* This shrub grows most abundantly in Pheneus in Arcadia. Dioscorides, moreover, says: *The root drunk in wine stops a loose bowel. It also promotes the flow of urine. It serves to dry out old ulcers when sprinkled upon them, and stops bleeding.* However, it is crushed only with difficulty because of its great hardness.

<margin>
Pliny, Book 26, chapter 8.
</margin>
Pliny, for his part, writes: *It has the power to tighten the body. The root drunk in wine stops the bowels, which results in it moving the urine by the repulsion of the liquid, just as most things do that stop the bowels. It also heals those with dysentery when crushed in red wine. However, it is crushed with difficulty. The same is very useful as a wash for suppurating gums.* It is gathered at the end of Autumn, when it has lost its leaves. It is dried in the shade. Therefore, the plants depicted by others are not the true Astragalus, since they deviate from the historical account either in the form of the roots or the arrangement of the leaves. For those that are depicted with roots similar to a Radish <note>original Latin: *Rhaphano*</note>, even if they correspond in other details, they still differ in the root, which is the principal identifying mark. That which is described last with a root imitating an acorn <note>original Latin: *balanos*</note> is far removed from the Chickpea <note>original Latin: *Cicere*</note> in the arrangement of its leaves; for it has only two, and single tendrils on each stalk, and the roots are not hairy, but only cling together in the manner of Galingale <note>original: *Cyperi*</note>; I will not even speak of its medicinal properties. Therefore, if any other plant seems to correspond in all things, it is the one proposed by me; even if some might say that it does not have the root of a Radish. To them I respond that the "root of a radish" <note>original Greek: ῥίζαν ῥαφάνου (*rhizan rhaphanou*)</note> is not round, but long, even if we are familiar with a species of round Radish, the seed of which we obtained from Ferrante Imperato <note>A famous Neapolitan apothecary and naturalist (1550–1625) who was Colonna's mentor.</note>. If they wish it to be so, nothing contrary will remain. But I certainly think it better to read "acorn" <note>original Greek: βάλανος (*balanos*)</note> rather than "radish" <note>original Greek: ῥάφανος (*rhaphanos*)</note>, as Pena and Lobel <note>Pierre Pena and Matthias de l'Obel, influential Flemish botanists who published *Stirpium Adversaria Nova* in 1570.</note> observed, for in other lists of names it is called "Earth Fig" and "Little Gland" <note>original: *Ficus terrae* and *Glandula*</note> among other names. The Author of the Plants <note>Likely referring to a specific contemporary botanical illustrator or author.</note> seems to call this the "Mountain Galega," but it is depicted less than accurately.

## Idaean Root of Dioscorides.

->The same as the *Hypoglosson* <note>Under-tongue</note>, Alexandrian Laurel, *Laurotaxa*, and *Chamaedaphne* <note>Ground-laurel</note>.<-

<image-desc>A botanical illustration of a plant sprig, possibly the Radix Idaea, showing a central stem with several dark green, lanceolate leaves. Small, bright red, spherical berries are shown growing directly from the base of the leaves along the stem.</image-desc>

<margin>Name and Etymology.</margin>
<margin>Book 27, chapter 2.</margin>
<margin>Description.</margin>
The **Idaean Root** <note>original: *Radix Idaea*</note> was so named from its location, either because it grows most abundantly on Mount Ida, or because it is more effective there, or was first discovered there. Pliny called the same plant the *Idaean herb*; the latter name comes from the whole substance of the plant, but the former from the use of its parts in medicine. *The Idaean root has leaves resembling the wild myrtle; and near them, little curls like tendrils have grown, from which the flower also comes.* <note>Translated from the original Greek: Ἰδαία ῥίζα, ὀξυμυρσίνῃ τὰ φύλλα ἔοικε, παρ' αὐτὰ δὲ ὡς ἕλικες μικραὶ πεφύκασιν, ἐξ ὧν καὶ τὸ ἄνθος.</note>

<margin>Book 4.</margin>
The Idaean root has leaves similar to Butcher’s Broom <term>Ruscus|A genus of evergreen shrubs, notably Butcher’s Broom</term>; upon the leaves themselves grow little tendrils, as it were, from which the flower also emerges. So Dioscorides wrote. Pliny transcribed it thus:

<margin>Book 27, chapter 11.</margin>
*The leaves of the Idaean herb are like those of the Wild Myrtle <note>original: Oxymyrsine</note>; clinging to these are what look like vine-tendrils, in which the flower sits.* After I had often turned over the origin and drawing of this plant in my mind, I thought it could only happen with difficulty that a plant related or similar to Butcher’s Broom would present itself before my eyes. This was especially so since so many tireless men have produced an almost innumerable number of plants, not only those rising under our own sky, but also those brought from the farthest shores of the world; among these I found none that was related to Butcher’s Broom, except for this one which I show here in the illustration. Since it seems to match in its origins, form, and powers, I judged that it ought to be distinguished by this name, the **Idaean Root**.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It has a root similar to Butcher’s Broom, but larger, from which many shoots come out in the manner of Asparagus or Butcher’s Broom, which are suitable for food. These later stretch out into twigs about a cubit long <note>roughly 18 inches</note>, or larger if cultivated; they are smooth, green, and flexible. From the middle to the top they have leaves shaped like the Sharp Myrtle, which is to say, similar to Butcher’s Broom. [Indeed, there is no other wild myrtle mentioned in Dioscorides or Pliny besides this *wild myrtle* <note>original Greek: μυρσίνην ἀγρίαν</note> and *sharp myrtle* <note>original Greek: ὀξυμυρσίνην</note>, which the Romans called *Ruscus* <note>Butcher's Broom</note>, as the same Dioscorides says;

<margin>Book 23, last chapter.</margin>
and Pliny also testifies that *Oxymyrsine* is called *Ruscus* by Castor; between these there is only a variety of name, even if the Author of the *History of Plants* <note>Likely referring to Theophrastus</note> dreams otherwise.] But the leaves are larger and...

<summary>Fabio Colonna identifies the "Idaean Root" mentioned by Dioscorides and Pliny as a relative of Butcher's Broom (Ruscus), using classical descriptions of leaf-borne flowers to confirm his specimen's identity. He notes the plant's connection to Mount Ida and critiques previous botanical authorities on their naming conventions.</summary>
<keywords>Radix Idaea, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Ruscus, botany, Oxymyrsine, Hypoglosson, Alexandrian Laurel, Mount Ida, plant identification</keywords>

<meta>This page (65) provides the descriptive text and medicinal applications for the plant illustrated on page 64, identified there as "Hypoglosson" or "Idaean Root." Colonna continues his project of botanical "detective work," comparing his observations with the works of classical and contemporary herbalists.</meta>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS
65

The shape is a bit narrower, resembling the young leaves of *Ruscus* <note>Butcher’s Broom</note>, greener and softer; they are not quite as prickly, though they are pointed. They surround the stalks alternately, but those closer to the ground cling around the stalks in threes, in the manner of *Rubia* <note>Madder</note>. On the leaves themselves, small shoots or stems grow out—which I have elsewhere taught are called <term>helikes</term> <note>original: ἑλίκες; tendrils or spirals</note>, <margin>In the description of Isopyrum.</margin> though they were mistakenly thought to be <term>glostidas</term> <note>original: γλωστίδας; little tongues</note> by an author of plant descriptions, whose incorrect opinion forced a change in the proper meaning of the words so that the plant depicted by him could be seen as *Hypoglosson* <note>Horse-tongue Lily</note>, as I shall discuss below. These shoots emerge thin and short, but longer than those of *Ruscus*. At their tip in the spring, a grassy, star-shaped flower emerges, composed of six small petals, with a purple center not unlike the flower of *Ruscus*. I have never seen it produce fruit, nor have many friends who have cultivated the plant for twenty years; they never observed it to produce anything but the flower. This must be attributed to the soil and climate, for elsewhere it is asserted that it produces red fruit the size of a chickpea, like *Ruscus*. Therefore, it is no wonder if the author of this description reported that it only produces a flower, for perhaps he neither saw nor described the same thing, as is observed with *Dictamnus* <note>Dittany</note> and Mountain Nard, and similar plants that produce fruit and flowers every year. The same must be said of the "little tongue" <note>the small leaf-like growth</note> emerging from the leaf, which I will demonstrate others have also not observed; for everyone has committed to history the particular characteristic they judged most unique to the plant. Thus it happens that a plant sketched by different writers is seen to be understood by different characteristics, names, and even medicinal powers, and yet it is certain that the same plant is being described. Now that the form of the plant is known, its powers must finally be compared: and certainly, it is perceived by taste to be sharp and somewhat bitter, not without the ability to provide heat. In these qualities, it is seen to have a mixed nature and power. This is established by the experiments of more recent authors.

<margin>Powers.</margin>
Swellings of the uvula, throat, and tonsils, as well as ulcers in those same areas, are healed by a gargle made from this plant. Marcellus <note>Marcellus Empiricus, a 5th-century medical writer</note> writes that in Italy, this plant is hung around the necks of children to suppress swelling of the uvula; from this use in contracting a prolapsed uvula, it commonly earned the name *Uvularia*. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> and those following him write that it is proven by experience that prolapsed hernias in children are contracted if a spoonful of this root powder is drunk with a decoction of Greater *Symphytum* <note>Comfrey</note>, and if the hernia is bound with a bandage in the first days so that the intestines do not burst out again. From these accounts, it is clear the plant has great power to constrict and dry, just as Dioscorides <note>A primary ancient Greek authority on herbals</note> says it does. The root of this plant is intensely astringent, suitable for those in need of constriction—

I
<meta>catchword: constriction</meta>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and medicinal uses of the Horse-tongue Lily (Hypoglosson), noting its star-shaped flowers and its common use in treating throat ailments and childhood hernias. Colonna also critiques previous botanical descriptions, emphasizing how environmental factors like soil and climate can change a plant's appearance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Idaean root" (Hypoglossum) from page 65. The author, Fabio Colonna, is comparing the medicinal descriptions and naming conventions used by ancient authorities like Pliny and Galen against contemporary botanists like Carolus Clusius.</meta>

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

<margin>
Book 27, chapter 2.
</margin>
...action is needed. It is also drunk to treat bowel movements <note>original: "alui fluxiones"; likely diarrhea or dysentery</note> and womanly flux, and it stops every eruption of blood. Pliny, however, writes: *The plant itself stops the bowels, the menses, and every abundance of blood, having a nature that thickens and restrains.* In these words, he transcribed "menses" in place of "womanly flux," <note>original: "ἔμμηνα" [emmēna] for "ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον" [rhoun gynaikeion]</note>.

<margin>
Book 7, on the powers of simple medicines.
</margin>
Galen describes it thus: *The Idaean root is quite bitter to the taste; and if you put its power to the test in practice, it will display the same efficacy, namely curing flows of blood, flows of the bowels, dysenteries, the flux of women, and all things of that kind, whether it is drunk or applied externally.* By these words, it seems that Galen—as in many other instances—did not actually know the Idaean root; for he is silent about its power to heat or cool, one of which this plant must surely possess. Nor does he write in what degree or excess it might heat or cool, as he usually does for other plants, nor whether its nature is moderate or consists of contrary qualities. Instead, he simply transcribes what was said by Dioscorides using general terms. 

Our people commonly call this plant *Bislingua* <note>Two-tongue</note>, a name taken from the little "tongues" <note>the small leaf-like structures or bracts</note> clinging to the leaves. The ancients—Dioscorides and those who followed him, such as Galen and Pliny—also called it *Hypoglossum* <note>Under-tongue</note> because, as Galen says, it was so named because it produces offshoots of small tongues beneath the highest clusters. 

<margin>
Book 8, on the powers of simple medicines.
</margin>
In place of this plant, the author of the *History of Plants* <note>referring to Carolus Clusius</note> depicted a relative of *Polygonatum* (commonly called Solomon’s Seal), first illustrated by Carolus Clusius, rather than this *Bislingua* and *Uvularia*. Clusius is of the opinion that *Hypoglossum* differs from *Hippoglossum*, and he asserts that his plant is the true *Hypoglossum*. Rising up against more learned men, he claims they poorly understand the origin of the names *Hypoglossum* and *Hippoglossum*. 

In maintaining such an opinion, Clusius preferred to significantly emend the text of Dioscorides, altering the sense and the words, rather than suggesting that the texts of Pliny or Dioscorides were merely corrupted by the change of one or two letters—or, more accurately, that Dioscorides’s *Hippoglossum* was simply called *Hypoglossum* by Pliny. There is indeed no other difference between their descriptions; it contributes nothing to the knowledge of the plant whether it is called *hippo-* <note>horse-</note> or *hypo-* <note>under-</note>, since the same plant can be fitted to either name. Namely, it might be called *Hippoglossum* <note>Horse-tongue</note> because the size of the leaves resembles a large tongue, while it is called *Hypoglossum* <note>Under-tongue</note> because of the little "tongues" clinging to the leaves—that is, <note>original: "ὑπὸ γλώσσης" [hypo glōssēs]</note> "under the tongue." 

<margin>
Book 27, chapter 11.
</margin>
Nor is there any other distinct characteristic by which they might differ from one another, as is clear from Pliny’s text; for he says: *Hypoglossa has leaves with the shape of wild Myrtle, concave and prickly, and upon these are what might be called tongues...*

<summary>Colonna discusses the medicinal uses of the Idaean root (Hypoglossum) for stopping bleeding and argues that the names Hippoglossum and Hypoglossum refer to the same plant, despite the differing opinions of Carolus Clusius.</summary>
<keywords>botany, pharmacology, Hippoglossum, Hypoglossum, Bislingua, Galen, Pliny, Carolus Clusius, plant naming, medicinal herbs, blood-restraining</keywords>
