# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

<margin>
Book 4.
Book 8, *On the Powers of Simple Medicines*.
</margin>
...with a small leaf emerging from the leaves. And Dioscorides also mentions what are like little tongues on the leaves. Galen provides the etymology for this, stating that *Hypoglossum* <note>Under-tongue</note> is so named because it produces offshoots of small tongues beneath the highest clusters. Although the wording of the text may vary in some places among these authors, one should not conclude that they are different plants; rather, we know that one author transcribed from another, and they all intended to designate the same plant. And if we are to believe—as I deem to be true, for reason demonstrates it—a certain very learned German physician <note>This likely refers to Valerius Cordus or perhaps Leonhart Fuchs, who published influential commentaries on Dioscorides</note> who wrote *Emblems* on Dioscorides, he says he possesses an ancient text of Dioscorides in which *Hypoglosson* <note>original: ὑπόγλωσσον, meaning "under-tongue"</note> is read instead of *Hippoglosson* <note>original: ἱππόγλωσσον, meaning "horse-tongue"</note>. No one doubts that this name fits the plant; and even though it is read corruptly in some places as *Hippoglosson* instead of *Hypoglosson*, one should not think there are different plants and descriptions, especially since we have such a clear text from Galen in which, as I said, he provides the etymology of this name *Hypoglosson*.

Indeed, I add this opinion, from which I shall not depart until I see plants that testify to a difference: for I have no doubt that the drawings depicted by others are fictitious. No diligent illustration of the plants is provided, nor is the location or the place from which they were brought recorded. Furthermore, not all plant collectors possess those images, and those that are depicted by others are merely copied from other illustrations. What is more convincing is that I see the more learned writers take no account of them.

Therefore, I also judge that *Hypoglossum* <note>Under-tongue</note>, *Laurus Alexandrina* <note>Alexandrian Laurel</note>, *Laurotaxa* <note>Yew-laurel</note>, and *Chamaedaphne* <note>Ground-laurel</note> are the same plant as the one mentioned above. They have nothing but a diversity of names imposed by different writers, which I shall show that Dioscorides and his followers did not notice. The shape of the leaves, similar to Laurel, gave this plant the name of *Laurus*, though not because it was of the Laurel genus; and so that some difference might be shown between this and the true Laurel, they called it *Alexandrina* or *Idaean Laurel* from the place where it grows. Similarly, it was called *Idaean Root* or *Idaean Herb* (as Pliny and others have it). Because it is low-growing, they called it *Chamaedaphne* <note>From the Greek "chamai" (on the ground) and "daphne" (laurel)</note>; others, from the shape and color of the fruit, called it *Laurotaxa*, as reported by Pliny. Because it has fruit positioned between the leaves, it is called *Epicarpophyllon* <note>original: ἐπικαρπόφυλλον, "fruit-upon-leaf"</note> and *Carpophyllon* <note>Fruit-leaf</note>. Others, however, because they noticed a "tongue" emerging from the middle of the leaf, called it *Hypoglosson* or *Hypoglotton*. This is proven, for what is called *Laurus Alexandrina* is called *Hypoglotton* (the Attic <note>A dialect of ancient Greek</note> form of *Hypoglosson*) by the same Dioscorides and Pliny. From this, it is clearly gathered that they are synonyms. But

<sig>I 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: & (And)</meta>

<summary>Colonna argues that several plants described by ancient authorities under different names—such as Hypoglossum and Alexandrian Laurel—are actually the same species, unified by the distinctive "tongue" structure on their leaves. He criticizes contemporary botanical illustrations as unreliable and fictitious, preferring to rely on textual etymology and physical characteristics.</summary>
<keywords>Hypoglossum, Dioscorides, Galen, botanical nomenclature, Alexandrian Laurel, Chamaedaphne, plant synonyms, Pliny the Elder, historical botany</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s detailed botanical investigation into the identity of ancient plants, specifically focusing on reconciling the descriptions of the Alexandrian Laurel and the Ground-laurel provided by classical authors.</meta>

-># OF FABIO COLONNA<-

Dioscorides testifies in the chapter on *Chamædaphne* <note>Ground-laurel; literally "dwarf laurel"</note> that it is also called *Laurus Alexandrina* <note>Alexandrian Laurel</note>; and that this is so is demonstrated not only by the synonymous names, but also by the illustration of the plant, and moreover by the power of its medicinal virtues, as I shall discuss below. 

<margin>Book 3, ch. 17.</margin>
The description of the Alexandrian Laurel—to begin with the older source and fountainhead, I mean Theophrastus, whom no one doubts described plants with the greatest purity—is as follows: *Those plants are also said to be peculiar to the region around Mount Ida, known as the Alexandrian Laurel, and a certain Fig, and a Vine. A unique feature of this Laurel is that it produces fruit upon the leaves, just like the wild Myrtle:*

<margin>Book 1, ch. 16.</margin>
*for both bear fruit upon the back of the leaf.* And elsewhere: *And some fruit-bearing plants carry the fruit caught between the leaves, such as the Alexandrian Laurel, which produces fruit upon the leaf.* This seems to be a private characteristic of this plant, that it is fruiting upon the leaf <note>original Greek: ἐπιφυλλόκαρπος (epiphyllokarpos), a botanical term for plants where flowers and fruit grow directly from the leaf blades</note> in the same manner as *Ruscus* <note>Butcher's broom</note>: I do not know of anyone who has ever seen, nor did Theophrastus describe, any other similar plant. This characteristic seems to have been diligently expressed by Dioscorides in the chapter on Alexandrian Laurel in these words: *fruit in the middle* <note>original Greek: καρπὸν ἐν μέσοις</note>, meaning of the leaves, *red, the size of a chickpea* <note>original Greek: ἐρυθρὸν ἐρεβίνθου τὸ μέγεθος</note>, and in the chapter on *Chamædaphne*: *round red fruit, growing upon the leaves* <note>original Greek: καρπὸν περιφερῆ ἐρυθρὸν, τοῖς φύλλοις ἐπιπεφυκότα</note>. A more observant writer, being not at all satisfied with these descriptions since they were shared with *Ruscus*, attributed a more specific characteristic as well as a nomenclature to this plant; leaving behind the description of the fruit, he handed down the fact that it has a small leaf, or a "little tongue," in these words:

<margin>Book 27, ch. 11.</margin>
*and at the tip like a little tongue next to the leaves* <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐπ' ἄκρου ὥσπερ γλωσσίδα παρὰ τοῖς φύλloις</note>. Which Pliny translates as, *And in these, as it were, tongues, with a small leaf emerging from the leaves.* And the same author, speaking of the

<margin>Book 15, last chapter.</margin>
*Laurotaxa* <note>Yew-laurel</note>, says: *With a very small leaf growing out in the middle of the leaf, like a fringe of the leaf.* Although we may observe this "tongue" in *Ruscus*, it is judged to be not a tongue of the leaf, but rather like a follicle of the flower; and thus, it has not yet been known that this tongue belongs to any other plant.

<margin>Book 4</margin>
Indeed, Dioscorides says: *Alexandrian Laurel, which some call Idaean, etc. has leaves similar to Ruscus, but larger, softer, and whiter: moreover, the fruit in the middle of the leaves is red, the size of a chickpea: the branches are a foot long from the ground, or larger. The root is very similar to Ruscus, larger, well-scented,*

<margin>Book 15, last chapter.</margin>
*and softer.* It grows in mountainous places. From which Pliny writes: *There is the Ground-laurel, a wild shrub, and there is the Alexandrian, which some call Idaean, others Under-tongue <note>original: Hypoglotton</note>, others Laurel-like <note>original: Daphnitin</note>, others Fruit-leaf <note>original: Carpophyllon</note>, and others Under-fir <note>original: Hypelaten</note>. It spreads branches nine inches long from the root, useful for topiary and garland work, with a leaf sharper than the Myrtle's, softer, whiter, and larger, with the seed between the lea-*

<meta>catchword: -ves (Latin: -folia)</meta>

-># HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS<-

...red seeds. Most grow on Mount Ida and around Heraclea in Pontus <note>a region on the southern coast of the Black Sea</note>, and only in mountainous places.

<margin>Book 4.</margin>
The descriptions of *Chamædaphne* <note>Ground-laurel; literally "dwarf laurel"</note> seem to correspond to these: indeed, Dioscorides says, *Ground-laurel, which some also call Alexandrian Laurel, produces shoots a cubit long, straight, thin, and smooth. And its leaves are similar to Laurel, but much thinner and greener; the fruit is round, red, and grows upon the leaves.*

<margin>Book 24, ch. 15.</margin>
From these sources Pliny writes: *Ground-laurel has a single small branch, about a cubit long, with a thin leaf like that of a Laurel: a reddish seed is attached to the leaves.*

<margin>Book 15, last chapter.</margin>
The things Pliny says about the *Laurotaxa* <note>Laurel-yew</note> do not differ from these. He adds that in ornamental gardening the *Taxa* is used, with a small leaf growing out in the middle like a fringe of the leaf.

<margin>Book 27, chapter 11.</margin>
Which is the same as his *Hypoglossum* <note>Tongue-under-leaf</note>. *Hypoglossa has concave, prickly leaves like wild Myrtle, and on these are what look like tongues, with a small leaf emerging from the leaves.*

<margin>Book 4.</margin>
Which Dioscorides had previously described thus: *Hypoglosson is a small shrub similar to Butcher’s-broom with thin leaves; but it has a prickly head, and at the top something like little tongues on the leaves.*

<margin>Book 8, On the Power of Simple Medicines.</margin>
Galen provides the etymology for this: *It was named Tongue-under-leaf [Hypoglosson] because certain little tongues emerge from the tops of the clusters.*

I shall demonstrate that the plant which people commonly call *Bislingua* <note>Two-tongue; known today as Ruscus hypoglossum</note> corresponds to all these descriptions of the plant *Hypoglosson*. Therefore, this plant not only grows on Mount Ida and in Heraclea of Pontus, as Pliny says, but as Lobellius <note>Matthias de l’Obel, a Flemish physician and botanist</note> reports, it grows in the mountainous, rocky, and rough uncultivated areas of Genoa, the port of Luna, Savona, Nice, and other barren Alpine regions of warm tracts. It has a root like that of Butcher's-broom [*Ruscus*], but larger, sweet-smelling, white, and softer, as Dioscorides says in the chapter on Alexandrian Laurel. From this root first emerge tender shoots in the manner of Butcher's-broom and Asparagus, which can be eaten, as Galen says in the chapter on Ground-laurel; they are indeed harsh and slightly bitter but not unpleasant. These later stretch out into shoots a foot or a cubit long, and for that reason it is called Ground-laurel. They are singular, without branches, and therefore Pliny says, speaking of Ground-laurel <margin>Book 6, On the Power of Simple Medicines.</margin> *it has a single small branch: thin, smooth, flexible, and suited for ornamental gardening and wreath-making,* as the same Pliny says when speaking of the Alexandrian Laurel and the *Taxa*. On these shoots are leaves in the shape of young Butcher’s-broom leaves, whose leaves are narrower, but the leaves of this plant are larger and of a longer shape, like both Butcher’s-broom and Myrtle, and similar to Laurel. They are indeed whiter and softer, like the leaves of small Butcher’s-broom, to which Dioscorides compares them, and they are softer, whiter, and smoother than Laurel leaves, as the same Dioscorides says. The leaves cling to the shoots, first in threes from the middle to the top, then individually and alternately; some of these have little tongues facing the ground, others...

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna cross-references descriptions of the Alexandrian Laurel and Hypoglossum from Dioscorides, Pliny, and Galen to identify them as the modern Bislingua. He details the plant's physical characteristics, such as its edible shoots and unique "tongue-like" leaf structures, and its geographic distribution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical analysis of the plant known as the Alexandrian Laurel and the Ground-laurel, specifically focusing on the physical characteristics of the flowers and fruit, as well as the medicinal properties attributed to the plant by ancient authorities like Galen, Dioscorides, and Pliny.</meta>

-># FABIO COLONNA<-

looking up toward the heavens, because of which it was called *Hypoglosson* <note>Greek for "tongue-under-leaf"</note>. All parts are indeed sharp, but not as prickly as Butcher’s Broom <note>original: *Rusci*</note>. From the middle and the back of the leaf—as Theophrastus, Dioscorides, and Pliny say—but also from the little tongue <note>original: *ligula*</note>, as is commonly seen in Butcher’s Broom, the flowers emerge in the manner of Butcher's Broom. These are succeeded by fruit that is round, red, and the size of a chickpea, similar to Butcher’s Broom as everyone agrees. They grow singly or in pairs upon individual leaves, having thin and short stalks <note>original: *petiolos*</note>—this refers to the individual fruits. However, when two fruits are seen together, each of these has a longer stalk. It flowers in the spring; I have not yet seen it produce fruit, as it bears fruit late. But I have stated that it has them based on the opinion of other writers who were able to see them. Since these things are thus known, I have shown that all the descriptions and the plant itself correspond to one another. Therefore, having made a comparison of the form, I undertake to demonstrate the power of its medicinal virtues. Namely, that it has a mixed faculty: besides the ability to dry and astringe, it is hot, sharp, and bitter as perceived by taste; therefore, it will be able to digest, cut <note>in the sense of breaking up thick humors</note>, disperse, open, and soften.

When drunk, it stimulates both urine and the menstrual flow. Furthermore, the tender shoots of Ground-laurel are also usually eaten. Its virtues are very similar to those of the Alexandrian Laurel, etc.* Regarding *Hypoglossum* <note>Horse-tongue Lily</note>: *The root and the juice of the shrub possess a softening power, just as Dioscorides also says.* Indeed, by comparing them, I shall demonstrate that the effects of these faculties are the same. --- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical analysis of the medicinal virtues of the plant known as Alexandrian Laurel or Tongue-under-leaf, referencing various classical and contemporary authorities before transitioning to a new entry on the Third Sideritis.</meta>

-># HISTORIES OF SEVERAL PLANTS<-

<note>original catchword: quando-</note> seeds are drunk with wine; and Dioscorides says, "The same, when drunk with wine, settles griping pains <note>original: *tormina*; intestinal cramps</note>." Corresponding powers have been recognized by more recent authors. *Bislingua* <note>original: "Double-tongue," another name for the Alexandrian Laurel</note>, when drunk, assists women in difficult labor, stimulates menstruation, and expels stones. Likewise, Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus, a famous 16th-century German botanist</note> says that the dry root matures buboes <note>swollen lymph nodes</note> during the plague, according to very reliable folk experiments. Likewise, the leaves and root of the *Vuularia* <note>literally "Uvula-herb"</note> heal swellings of the throat and tonsils, as well as ulcerations of the same, when used as a gargle. Marcellus <note>Marcellus Empiricus, a 4th-century medical writer</note> writes that in Italy, this plant is hung around the necks of children to repress the *uva* <note>the uvula</note>; it acquired the name *Vuularia* from its use in contracting a prolapsed uvula. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a renowned 16th-century physician</note> also writes that the powder, drunk with a decoction of Comfrey <note>original: *Symphytum*</note>, contracts protruding hernias. It is known that these plants share the same powers as *Hypoglossum* <note>Greek for "tongue-under-leaf"</note>, the Alexandrian Laurel, and the plant called *Chamædaphne* <note>Ground-laurel</note>. Indeed, it is seen to have a mixed faculty in its effect and taste; the leaves are quite bitter, as is the root, and they share a certain sharpness. Therefore, in my opinion, this is not presented without reason. Valerius Cordus, a most learned man, also noticed this and is of the opinion that *Hypoglossum* and the Alexandrian Laurel are the same plant; even if the Author of Plants <note>referring to a specific botanical authority, likely Theophrastus or a contemporary critic</note> tried to reject this, he did not achieve his aim. It is not the *Radix Idaea* <note>the "Root of Ida"</note> which is suggested by others, for it has no affinity with Butcher’s Broom <note>original: *Ruscus*</note>. Nor is it the Alexandrian Laurel that is depicted by others besides Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs, author of a 1542 herbal</note>; for those are either fictitious or do not correspond. Nor is it *Chamædaphne*, since it does not have fruit growing upon the leaves—that is, *epipephykota* <note>original: ἐπιπεφυκότα</note>—as Dioscorides says.

### *The Third Sideritis of Dioscorides.*

<margin>
The Name
and Etymology.
Book 4.
</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring a figure with a horn, floral scrolls, and woodcut architectural elements.</image-desc>Sideritis <note>Iron-wort</note> was so named because, as Dioscorides says, "of this plant, the power when applied is adhesive for bloody and fresh wounds" <note>original: ταύτης, δύναμις ἐπιτιθεμένης, κολλητικὴ ἐναίμων, καὶ νεοτρώτων</note>. That is to say, it has the power of soldering together <note>original: *ferruminandi*</note> fresh and bloody wounds made by iron, which is called *sideros* <note>original: σίδηρος</note> in Greek, and from this, it was called *Sideritis*. This plant has a fibrous
<margin>Form.</margin>
root, black on the outside and whitening on the inside, from which many leaves—ten, more or less—grow in a round cluster. But they are five-parted, just like the plant called Dove's-foot Crane's-bill <note>original: *Geranium columbinum*</note>, with deeper lobes, resembling the leaves of Coriander before it produces a stalk; they are quite similar in form and smoothness, but are twice as hard and large.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the medicinal uses of the Alexandrian Laurel (or Bislingua) for throat ailments and hernias before introducing the Sideritis, explaining its etymology as a "wound-healer" for iron-inflicted injuries.</summary>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS ###

sometimes more serrated all around: dark green above, but whitish-green and smoother below) from individual thin stalks a span <term>dodrantem: an ancient measurement of about nine inches, or three-quarters of a foot</term> high, or less, whitish-green at the top; on the other hand, reddish where they emerge from the root; from which also, and from the middle of the leaves, several stems (sometimes only one, especially in young plants) emerge, very similar to the stem of the second Sideritis, commonly called Pimpinella <note>original: *Pimpinellæ nobis vulgò dictæ*—likely referring to Burnet Saxifrage or a similar herb</note> by us, smooth, round, striated, with the same purplish color, sometimes slightly exceeding the leaves, but in cultivated or richer soils reaching two feet; divided into one or two wings <note>branches</note> emerging from the leaves that cling to the stem. At the top of these, and the stem, as if in clusters <note>corymbs</note>, four small stalks emerge, with a single shorter one in the middle: the remaining three form a triangle: each having three clusters of flowers: but the middle one has a single, larger one. The flowers are quite small, and before they open, they are hairy and purplish, but later become whitish, adorned with many purplish, somewhat hard stamens, which are rough to the touch: from the mixture of these, a crimson color is presented to the eyes. In taste, they are sweetish, bitter, and astringent; not unlike the second Sideritis of Dioscorides <note>A famous 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years</note>. However, these are perceived by the palate to contain something more aromatic. <margin>Time.</margin> It flowers in the months of April and May: in July, the rough and hairy seed is perfected. <margin>Place.</margin> It is frequent here along the shady paths of valleys and hills, growing upon mossy tufa <note>a porous limestone</note> with little soil. This plant seems, in my opinion, to correspond sufficiently to the description by Dioscorides, who says: <margin>Book 4.</margin> <margin>Description.</margin> <note>original Greek: Ἔτι καὶ ἑτέρα εἶναι λέγεται σιδηρῖτις... γλίχρα.</note> "There is also said to be another Sideritis, which Crateuas <note>A Greek physician and pharmacologist from the 1st century BCE</note> also calls Heraclea, growing on walls and in vineyards, having many leaves from one root, similar to coriander, around stems a span long, smooth, tender, whitish, and reddish. The flowers are crimson, small; to the taste, bitter and sticky." *Furthermore, they report there is another Sideritis, which Crateuas calls Heraclea, growing in walls and vineyards, with many leaves from a single root, similar to Coriander, around foot-long stems, tender, smooth, whitish, and reddish: with a small crimson flower: bitter and sticky to the taste.* Since this plant appears similar in form and flavor, I judge it to also correspond in its medicinal powers. But certainly, the powers of this plant are not only equal to Sideritis, but even greater than those attributed to it by Dioscorides, as is established by the experience of more recent authors; hence, there is a common adage among the French regarding the powers of this plant, for they say: *Those who have Sanicula <note>Wood Sanicle, *Sanicula europaea*</note> (for so they call it) have no need of a surgeon.*

<meta>This page continues the botanical identification of "Sideritis" (Ironwort). On the previous page, Colonna began describing the physical appearance of the plant; here, he focuses on its medicinal properties and argues why his identification is more accurate than those of his predecessors, specifically refuting the common identification of Herb Robert (Geranium robertianum).</meta>

# FABIUS COLONNA #

<margin>Medicinal Properties.</margin>
[Dio]scorides attributes these medicinal properties to the third *Sideritis* <note>original: *Sideritidi tertiæ*. *Sideritis* or "Ironwort" was historically used to treat wounds made by iron weapons</note>: 
> *“And the power of this plant, when applied, is that it heals bloody and fresh wounds.”* 
Lobel <note>Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist after whom the flower Lobelia is named</note>, however, says: 
> *“Boiled, drunk, or applied, it assists internal and external ulcers, hemorrhages, dysentery, ruptures, and sprains.”* 
Therefore, I decided that its more ancient name should be restored to it, since its form and properties reflect the third *Sideritis* of Dioscorides <note>The 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*, the standard botanical reference for centuries</note> more than any other, and it should be judged as being the same plant. The third *Sideritis* is not *Geranium robertianum* <note>commonly known as Herb Robert</note>, even if that plant is recommended for wounds by the same author; for its leaves, stem, and the entire plant are always covered in hair, which contradicts the opinion of Dioscorides, who says it has: *“stems a span long, smooth, and tender,”* <note>original Greek: καυλίοις σπιθαμιαίοις, λείοις, τρυφεροῖς (*kauliois spithamiaiois, leiois, trypherois*)</note> etc. Furthermore, I have never seen Dioscorides describe a plant with coriander-like leaves as being hairy without explicitly mentioning that hairiness. Likewise, the leaves of the *Geranium* are deeply incised—more similar to an Anemone than to Coriander—although the Anemone itself has coriander-like leaves. But Dioscorides noticed this, saying: *“leaves like coriander, but more finely divided,”* <note>original Greek: φύλλα δ'ὲ κοριοειδῆ λεπτοχινώτερα (*phylla de korioeidē leptochinōtera*)</note> etc. As he did in other cases, he would have mentioned it here, since the leaves are quite divided into deep segments, similar to the first "Bulbous Geranium" of Dioscorides, which he records as having Anemone-like leaves, not Coriander-like ones; indeed, he had previously provided a description of the Anemone as having leaves much incised, as I said above. Nor are the flowers of the *Geranium* bitter or sticky, nor is anything similar in flavor or taste perceived in them, as I have demonstrated to many. Furthermore, it has a "crane’s beak" <note>the distinctive long, pointed seed pod of the Geranium family, from the Greek *geranos* meaning crane</note>, which Dioscorides would have described here just as he did for the second *Geranium*; by this mark, he would have more easily identified it for us, since it is a unique characteristic of that plant and its relatives. We cannot accept the plant depicted by Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note>, since to this day no one has seen it except in that illustration, where it has the appearance of a *Ranunculus* <note>Buttercup</note>. Everyone believes it to be among the fictitious plants sent to him by flatterers and deceitful friends—plants which he himself, though a faithful student, did not seek to see or examine in person.

HISTORY OF VARIOUS PLANTS
K 2

## *The Second Tragium of Dioscorides.*

---

<margin>Book 4</margin>
This plant obtained the name *Tragium* because, as the same Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*</note> says:
> “In autumn it puts forth leaves with the odor of a male goat, from which it is also named *tragium*.” <note>original Greek: προβάλλει κατὰ τὸ φθινόπωρον τὰ φύλλα τράγου ὀσμήν, ὅθεν καὶ ὠνόμασται τράγιον (*proballei kata to phthinopōron ta phylla tragou osmēn, hothen kai ōnomasthai tragion*). The Greek word *tragos* means a male goat.</note>

It differs from the first *Tragium*, for that one does not smell of goat-stench, but was so named because goats pierced by arrows shake the shafts out of their wounds by feeding on it; it also has a private distinction, in that it possesses a milky sap not unlike gum, and grows only in Crete.
<margin>Book 9, chapter 1.</margin>
But Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC), often called the "Father of Botany"</note>, speaking of the same plant (though he did not know the other), says these things: “*Ixia* also has a tear [sap], which grows in Crete and is called *Tragacantha* [Goat’s-thorn].” This was previously thought to grow only in Crete,
<margin>Book 9, chapter 15.</margin>
but now it is certain that it is found also in Achaia, the Peloponnese, and other places, and in Asia around Media; elsewhere, *Tragacantha* grows abundantly in Arcadia, and is not considered inferior to the Cretan variety,
<margin>Book 9, chapter 8.</margin>
but is even more beautiful to the sight. And elsewhere: “Therefore, in some plants the sap settles spontaneously and thickens like a tear, as in the one called *Tragacantha*: for it is not necessary to cut this one.” From these descriptions, there is a suspicion that *Tragium* and *Tragacantha* are the same plant, which Gaza <note>Theodorus Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a Greek humanist who translated Theophrastus into Latin</note> translates as *Hircispina* [Goat-thorn], about which I shall write elsewhere.
<margin>Book 4, Description.</margin>
Regarding this plant, its characteristics are noted, as Dioscorides says:
> “The other *Tragium* has leaves similar to spleenwort, but a thin, white root, very similar to wild radish.” <note>original Greek: Τράγιον ἄλλο τὰ μὲν φύλλα ἔχει ὅμοια σκολοπενδρίῳ, ῥίζαν δὲ λεπτὴν, λευκὴν, παραπλησίαν ῥαφάνῳ ἀγρίᾳ (*Tragion allo ta men phylla echei homoia skolopendriō, rhizan de leptēn, leukēn, paraplēsian rhaphanō agria*).</note>

<margin>Form.</margin>
I shall demonstrate that the plant proposed by me corresponds to this description: it has a root similar to a wild radish or a wild parsnip, a foot long, equaling the thickness of a little finger, white, and divided with sparse fibers. It puts forth leaves attached to the stalks in opposite pairs of four, with a single one at the end, in the manner of the second *Sideritis* of Dioscorides, or what is commonly called by us *Pimpinella* <note>Likely *Pimpinella saxifraga*, or Burnet Saxifrage</note>; (because of this similarity, they also call this the "Goat Pimpernel" [*Pimpinella hircina*]). The leaves are rounder and more deeply divided. But if you sow it from seed, it first puts forth two leaves attached to each stalk, round and similar to coriander; then two on each stalk, and finally four opposing each other.

<summary>Colonna identifies and describes the "Second Tragium," a plant named for its goat-like scent in autumn, distinguishing it from other varieties and linking it to the historical descriptions of Dioscorides and Theophrastus.</summary>
<keywords>Tragium, Dioscorides, Theophrastus, Tragacantha, Goat's-thorn, Pimpinella hircina, Burnet Saxifrage, botany, plant identification, Crete, Arcadia</keywords>

## *The Second Tragium of Dioscorides.*

---

<margin>Book 4</margin>
This plant obtained the name *Tragium* because, as the same Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*</note> says:
> “In autumn it puts forth leaves with the odor of a male goat, from which it is also named *tragium*.” <note>original Greek: προβάλλει κατὰ τὸ φθινόπωρον τὰ φύλλα τράγου ὀσμήν, ὅθεν καὶ ὠνόμασται τράγιον (*proballei kata to phthinopōron ta phylla tragou osmēn, hothen kai ōnomasthai tragion*). The Greek word *tragos* means a male goat.</note>

It differs from the first *Tragium*, for that one does not smell of goat-stench, but was so named because goats pierced by arrows shake the shafts out of their wounds by feeding on it; it also has a private distinction, in that it possesses a milky sap not unlike gum, and grows only in Crete.
<margin>Book 9, chapter 1.</margin>
But Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC), often called the "Father of Botany"</note>, speaking of the same plant (though he did not know the other), says these things: “*Ixia* also has a tear [sap], which grows in Crete and is called *Tragacantha* [Goat’s-thorn].” This was previously thought to grow only in Crete,
<margin>Book 9, chapter 15.</margin>
but now it is certain that it is found also in Achaia, the Peloponnese, and other places, and in Asia around Media; elsewhere, *Tragacantha* grows abundantly in Arcadia, and is not considered inferior to the Cretan variety,
<margin>Book 9, chapter 8.</margin>
but is even more beautiful to the sight. And elsewhere: “Therefore, in some plants the sap settles spontaneously and thickens like a tear, as in the one called *Tragacantha*.” <note>original Latin/Greek context: Theophrastus is describing how resins and saps exude from plants to form "tears" or droplets.</note>

<summary>This page identifies and describes the "Second Tragium" mentioned by Dioscorides, specifically highlighting its distinctive goat-like odor in autumn. Colonna uses classical authorities like Theophrastus to distinguish it from the Cretan variety and "Goat’s-thorn" (Tragacantha).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s detailed botanical description of the "Second Tragium of Dioscorides," a plant known for its distinctive goat-like odor. The previous page introduced the plant and began describing its physical characteristics; this page concludes the description of its parts and moves into a scholarly debate about its identification based on historical texts.</meta>

HISTORY OF VARIOUS PLANTS.
<insert>Note.</insert>

at the end of which a single one is found. It sends out very thin stems, about a foot or a foot and a half high, separated by two or three joints <note>nodes on the stem where leaves or branches emerge</note>, from which very thin leaves emerge. At the top of these are umbels <note>flat-topped flower clusters, like those of a carrot or parsley</note> filled with white flowers, which are followed by oblong seeds similar to Anise, but much smaller, tending toward a green color and hairy. <margin>Season.</margin> It flowers in Summer; at the beginning of Autumn, the seeds are found fully formed. <margin>Location.</margin> It grows in mountains, among rocks, and in earthy places; it is frequent on the high and cold mountain called Monte Vergine <note>literally "Mountain of the Divine Virgin Mary," a peak in the Campania region of Italy</note>. The leaves, when rubbed, smell of the stench of a goat, as does the whole plant. The taste is sharp and <note>original: γαγγίζοντι (*gangizonti*)</note> pungent, not only in the Autumn season, but always. For this reason, it is possible that Dioscorides, or the person from whom he transcribed, saw this plant in the month of October; for then it is found here without umbels and with the stem decayed, with new leaves breaking forth. At that time, it is observed to have a great similarity to the leaves of Hart’s Tongue fern <note>Scolopendrium</note> while it stands stripped of its stem. And so, at that time only, he brought the leaves to his nose, and being ignorant of whether it gave off the same odor throughout the whole year, he passed down to posterity only the time of Autumn, in which he himself found and examined it (as he did with many other plants which he described without a stem or flower). For at that time, on account of the abundance of moisture, the leaves smell more of the stench, just as is observed in the plant more truly thought to be Tutsan <note>Androsimum</note>, called *Siciliana* <note>a type of St. John's Wort</note>, a similar and related plant, whose leaves, when they begin to dry, seem to give off the odor not of a goat, but of wine. The opinion of some regarding the shape of the leaves might oppose this plant, as they say it is not similar to the leaves of Hart’s Tongue fern; I have decided that this opinion should be rejected thus: Spleenwort <note>Asplenium</note> consists of a leaf with the division of Polypody <note>a type of fern</note>, which is similar to the Fern in the opinion of Dioscorides. But it is an axiom <note>a self-evident truth in logic</note> that those things which are similar to one given thing are said to be similar to each other. Therefore, not without reason and necessary similarity, the leaves of this plant are similar to the leaves of Spleenwort, since Polypody is similar to the Fern, and the same Dioscorides compares the second Ironwort <note>Sideritis</note> and Maidenhair Spleenwort <note>Trichomanes</note> to the Fern; he says that both have leaves placed opposite each other with a resemblance to a lentil. Regarding Ironwort he says: <note>original: φύλλα ὅμοια τοῖς τῆς πτέριδος (*phylla homoia tois tēs pteridos*)</note> "leaves similar to those of the fern," because they are similar only in their division. Regarding Maidenhair, <note>original: τριχομανὲς, &c. ὅμοιον πτέριδι (*trichomanes... homoion pteridi*)</note> "Trichomanes... is similar to the fern," because it is similar to the Fern in both the substance and the division of the leaves; just as <note>original: δρυοπτερὶς ὁμοία πτέριδι (*dryopteris homoia pteridi*)</note> "Oak-fern is similar to the fern," but not <note>original: φύλλα ὅμοια σκολοπενδρίῳ (*phylla homoia skolopendriō*)</note> "leaves similar to Hart's Tongue," as here; which is only to be understood regarding the division of the leaves, as with Ironwort...

<meta>This page continues the botanical analysis of Fabio Colonna, specifically focusing on the identification of the "Second Tragium" and comparing it to various fern species. It addresses discrepancies between his observations and the ancient descriptions by Dioscorides.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

<meta>catchword: liver,</meta>

I have called them fertile, as they represent the shape of Fern leaves arranged on both sides; and there is a great difference between having leaves similar to the Hart’s Tongue fern <note>original: *Scolopendrio*</note>—that is, the leaves themselves—and being similar to the Hart’s Tongue fern as a whole. This latter point is understood regarding the substance and form of the entire plant, as I have said concerning the Oak fern <note>original: *Dryopteride*</note> and the Maidenhair spleenwort <note>original: *Trichomane*</note>. Some say it is an obstacle that Dioscorides wrote of the Female fern: "leaves similar to a fern" <note>original Greek: *φύλλα πτέριδι ὅμοια* (*phylla pteridi homoia*)</note>, and yet in its substance and the division of its leaves, it is similar to the Male fern. To this, I say Dioscorides responds for me, and in this way: the Female fern, although it is similar to the Male in the substance and division of its leaves, nevertheless has a very different arrangement of those leaves. For the female is branched and does not consist of a single leaf-stalk <term>petiole | the stalk that joins a leaf to a stem</term> as the Male fern does, but of many wings <note>pinnae or side-branches</note>. Therefore, since plants are mostly distinguished by the similarity and division of their leaves, he described it by a more notable mark by which it could be more easily recognized; he said it has leaves similar to a Fern, even though it differed in the habit of the plant—namely, in the multitude of its branches. Furthermore, it has not yet been discovered that any plant of the fern family has a root similar to a Radish <note>original: *Rhaphano*</note>; rather, they are oblique, like the Polypody fern, while the rest are hair-like <note>fibrous</note>. Nor should one doubt the flowers, as some do, which are passed over in silence by Dioscorides; for this happened because at that time he saw the plant stripped of its stem and flowers, which happens with many plants whose flowers he did not describe, and yet we know for certain they have them. From some plants he removed them entirely and said they lacked flowers; and here too, it would have been necessary for those who denied the flowers to write that this plant lacks them, if it did not produce flowers at all. Rather, it is more believable that he wished to describe this plant by a better-known characteristic so it could be more easily recognized: by the shape and division of its leaves, and by its goat-like smell. These things being thus understood, the plant proposed by me, now freed from doubts, seems worthy of attaining the name of Tragium <note>a plant named for its "goatish" odor, from the Greek *tragos*, meaning goat</note>, whose powers Dioscorides expressed thus:

<margin>Powers.</margin> This root, whether eaten raw or cooked, is helpful for those suffering from dysentery <note>a severe intestinal infection</note>.

The moderns deny that it has this power, because this plant is not recommended for that purpose; they only acknowledge that it is fit for breaking up stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note> and eliciting urine, and for this reason, they judge it to be rather contrary for those with dysentery. But if the substance and quality of the plant are considered, it will be perceived by taste to have a certain acridity along with a certain heaviness of smell, and it is not lacking in astringency. By this property, it could prevent dysentery, especially if it arises from a weakness of the internal organs (for there are many causes), by strengthening...

### THE HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of the plant's effects on internal organs.</meta>

...the liver, and evacuating weak parts through the urine; by drying and diverting the humor <note>In ancient medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids like blood and bile that needed to be balanced</note>, I believe it binds the flow, as also happens in *Alisma* <note>Water Plantain</note>. This plant possesses an acrid power, and Dioscorides says it works against dysentery when mixed with an equal part of *Daucus* <note>Wild Carrot</note>, the power of which is also acrid and especially moves the urine. Although modern writers have not experienced this faculty, Galen does not deny it; he says that because of the astringency the plant possesses, it can stop dysentery and similar flows. Indeed, speaking of both, he says:

> *The leaves, fruit, and sap of Tragium possess a drawing and digesting faculty.*
> *It consists of thin parts and is hot in its faculty at the beginning of the third order.* <note>The "third order" refers to a scale of intensity in ancient pharmacology, where the third degree was quite potent</note>

It extracts stalks <note>thorns or splinters</note> and things stuck in the body, breaks stones <term>calculi | kidney or bladder stones</term>, and moves the menses <note>promotes menstruation</note> when a drachm’s weight is drunk. Furthermore, the *Tragium* that is similar to the Lentisk <note>the Mastic tree</note> grows only in Crete. However, this other *Tragium*—which is smaller and has leaves similar to *Scolopendrium* <note>Hart’s Tongue Fern</note>—is seen in many places. It possesses no small amount of astringent power, making it suitable for conditions involving "flows" <note>such as diarrhea or hemorrhaging</note>. Thus, by the force of its "hot" faculty, it can cut and cleanse, diminish stones, and move the urine; and by its drying and astringent power, it can correct dysentery and other flows caused by an opposite condition. Many examples of these exist in Dioscorides and Galen, showing that the same substances can break stones and promote menstruation while also stopping dysentery and diarrhea due to their astringent faculty being mixed with a "hot" power. But these matters must be left to the physicians.

<margin>A Variety</margin>
There is also another plant to be called *Tragium*, which differs little in appearance from the one described above, just as it does not differ in its powers. The first leaves from the root are similar to the upper ones, round on individual stalks, smooth, and very close to Coriander; then they become jagged, and two are seen on each stalk, then four, and finally six. But in all of them, there is a single leaf at the end that is rounder and larger, in the manner of the plant called *Pimpinella* <note>Burnet Saxifrage</note>, but larger. It sends out a stem like Fennel, which is hollow, smooth, and divided by wings at each joint; it is thicker at the top, with "umbrellas" <term>umbels | clusters of flowers spreading from a common center</term> and seeds not dissimilar to Fennel, which ripen in summer. I received the seeds of this plant from Ferrante Imperato <note>original: *Ferdinando Imperato*, a famous Neapolitan apothecary and naturalist</note>, and I also had it growing spontaneously from seeds brought from the island of Capri <note>original: *Capreis*</note>. If you taste the root and seeds, you will recognize that they have an acridity and the smell of a goat <note>original: *hirci*</note>, just as the former plant does. However, you will observe that the leaves have very little "goat-like scent" <note>original Greek: *τραγίζοντα* (*tragizonta*), from 'tragos' meaning goat</note> at any time. This other *Tragium* is not the *Lunaria botrytis* <note>Grape Fern</note>, for that has only one leaf; if it were similar to *Scolopendrium*, it would not have a white root, but a black one, nor would it equal the size of a wild Radish...

<summary>This page discusses the medicinal applications of Tragium for treating kidney stones and dysentery, citing Galen and Dioscorides. Colonna also describes a specific variety of Tragium found in Capri, distinguishing it from ferns and other similar plants by its goat-like scent and leaf structure.</summary>

Nor is it that exotic plant depicted in the appendix of the *History of Plants*, which is also called the *Sucudus* of Avicenna <note>Avicenna was a Persian polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text for centuries</note> with heads like thyme; indeed they report it has no goat-like odor.

## Epimedium of Dioscorides

<margin>
Name. 
Book 7, 
ch. 8 
on the hist. 
of plants. 
Book 21, 
ch. 15. 
Book 27, 
ch. 9. 
Book 4.
</margin>
<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'Q' set in a square frame featuring a foliage-surrounded face.</image-desc> Why this plant was called *Epimedium*, I confess I do not know, unless it is from *epi Media*, because it grows frequently in Media <note>An ancient region of North-Western Iran</note>. In the works of Theophrastus it is read as *Epimetron*, and in place of that in Pliny, *Epipetron*; elsewhere, as in Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and the father of pharmacognosy</note>, it is *Epimedium*; in other nomenclatures it is *Erineos* and *Thryon*, because it enjoys watery places. Dioscorides, however, has it as *Epimedium*, and he described the plant thus: *Epimedium is a stem that is not large, having leaves similar to Ivy, about ten or twelve; it bears neither fruit nor flowers. The roots underneath are thin, black, heavy-smelling, and tasteless to the taste; it grows in watery places.* <note>original: "Ἐπιμήδιον καυλός ἐστιν οὐ μέγας... μωραί. φύεται ἐν τόποις ὑδρηλοῖς"</note>

<margin>Form.</margin>
From which text Pliny, having borrowed it, transcribed it almost word-for-word thus:
<margin>Book 27, ch. 9.</margin>
*Epimedium is a stem not large, with ten or twelve Ivy leaves, never flowering; with a thin root, black and of a somewhat heavy odor; it is born in damp places.*
<margin>Book 7, ch. 8 on the hist. of plants.</margin>
In Theophrastus, however, discussing the difference between flowers, these words are found: *Some are also entirely flowerless, just like the Epimetron.* <note>original: "ἔνια δὲ καὶ ὅλως ἀνανθῆ, καθάπερ καὶ τὸ ἐπίμετρον."</note> Which Pliny also
<margin>Book 21, ch. 15.</margin>
placed in his own work, as I said in the chapter on *Acinos*: *Acinos, which they call Epipetron, which never flowers.* In Dioscorides, however, it is read that it lacks not only a flower, but also fruit; and perhaps here it should be considered that Dioscorides used *karpon* <note>original: καρπὸν</note>, *fruit*, in place of "seed," as in the following chapters on *Xiphium*, *Sparganium*, *Xyris*, and many others. This opinion is also supported by this: for it is not read, neither in Theophrastus nor in Pliny, that this plant lacks fruit or seed, but only the flower. Therefore, I judge the words "nor fruit" <note>original: οὔτε καρπὸν</note> to be suspect and added in this place by someone else; or it ought to be read and interpreted as, *it bears no seed, nor flower:* and it could also be said that it remains suspect, for every plant first [flowers...]

<meta>The catchword "rem," indicates the start of the next page's first word, likely "remittit" or a similar continuation of the argument regarding the plant's nature.</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna examines the botanical identity of "Epimedium," comparing descriptions by Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus. He specifically debates whether the plant truly lacks both flowers and fruit/seeds, suggesting potential errors or later additions in the ancient Greek manuscripts.</summary>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'Q' set in a square frame featuring a foliage-surrounded face.</image-desc> Why this plant was called *Epimedium* <note>A genus of flowering plants in the family Berberidaceae, often called Barrenwort or Bishop's Hat</note>, I confess I do not know, unless it is from *upon Media* <note>original: "epi Media"</note>, because it grows frequently in Media <note>An ancient region of North-Western Iran</note>. In the works of Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany"</note> it is read as *Epimetron*, and in place of that in Pliny, *Epipetron*; elsewhere, as in Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical reference for 1,500 years</note>, it is *Epimedium*; in other nomenclatures it is *Erineos* and *Thryon*, because it enjoys watery places. Dioscorides, however, has it as *Epimedium*, and he described the plant thus: *Epimedium is a stem that is not large, having leaves similar to Ivy, about ten or twelve; it bears neither fruit nor flowers. The roots underneath are thin, black, heavy-smelling, and tasteless to the taste; it grows in watery places.* <note>original: "Ἐπιμήδιον καυλός ἐστιν οὐ μέγας... μωραί. φύεται ἐν τόποις ὑδρηλοῖς"</note>

<margin>Form.</margin>
From which text Pliny, having borrowed it, transcribed it almost word-for-word thus:

. OF FABIUS COLUMNA

...the matter, then completes the fruit and the seed: and he would have placed first <term>neither flower</term> <note>original Greek: οὔτε ἄνθος (oute anthos)</note>, then <term>neither fruit</term> <note>original Greek: οὔτε καρπὸν (oute karpon)</note>, as can be seen in the Leafy Dittany <note>original: "Dictamno Phyllitide"; likely a type of fern or Gesneriad then associated with Dittany</note>, and Maidenhair <note>original: "Adianto"</note>, and similar plants. However, regarding the idea that plants might lack fruit, seed, or flower, it must be understood that it perhaps happens that we do not see them, either because they appear almost imperceptible and are not clearly observed by us—as in the capillary plants [ferns] and the Fern genus, and also other plants whose seeds appear like impalpable atoms and whose flowers are not observed. In some Orchids, Wintergreen <note>original: "Pirola"</note>, and similar plants, flowers are indeed seen; but the seed is like dust, which someone might say they lack, for it is not at all certain from such a very thin seed that a plant can arise. From experiments made, plants that propagate themselves by the root indeed either never or rarely complete a seed by which they might sprout, especially those mentioned above; though some do the opposite.

<margin>Description.</margin>
These things being known, I shall describe the plant, which is frequent on the Mountain of the Blessed Virgin Mary, commonly called *Monte Vergine* <note>A mountain range in the Campania region of Italy</note>, cold and high, in damp, watery, meadowy places: It comes out in Spring, namely being found in the month of March, from a thin root, blackish on the outside, yellowish within, creeping and divided into fibers. It produces round leaves in the shape of Ivy <note>original: "Hedera"</note>, smooth like Sea Bindweed <note>original: "Soldanella marina"</note>, but smaller, nearly equal to the first leaves of the "third Stonecrop" <note>original: "Sedi"</note> described by me, which are also similar to Ivy, but not so thick, and softer. They cling to a single small stalk alternately from opposite sides, ten or twelve in number, or sometimes even more or less, according to the weather of the place and the year. From the middle of the stem, a little below the first leaves, a thin petiole is produced, bearing at its top several clusters on both sides of the petiole, equal in number to the leaves. These have crowded berries, few and green, which never produce a flower, nor is a seed seen to be contained within; but in its place, a very thin and impalpable powder of a pale color is found. Whether the plant might grow if this were sown is uncertain; indeed, though I have often tried, I have never known anything to sprout.

<margin>Time.</margin>
It is found with its clustered berries in May and June. The whole plant is about nine inches <note>original: "dodrantalis," a Roman unit of three-quarters of a foot</note> at its highest.

<margin>Powers.</margin>
The root gives off a heavy, earthy, and foul odor, with a tasteless, watery flavor. By the experience of more recent authors, it is established to be cold and dry in temperament: wherefore it is effective for knitting wounds together, bringing a scar to ulcers, and joining dislocations. Likewise for the "white flow" [leukorrhea] of women. L'Obel <note>original: "Lobellius," referring to Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist</note> says for her-

<meta>catchword: -nias (original: rocelas),</meta>

He provides a detailed description of the species found on Monte Vergine and notes its medicinal use for wound healing and female ailments.</summary>

# HISTORIES OF SEVERAL PLANTS

He says that it is of great commendation and utility for scrotal swellings <note>original: "rocelas," likely referring to sarcocele or similar swellings</note> and hernias when drunk or applied; he asserts it has an excellent power for knitting wounds together <note>original: "ferruminandi uim," literally the power of soldering</note>. 

<margin>Book 4</margin>
**Dioscorides** indeed says: *The leaves of this, crushed and rubbed with oil, are applied as a poultice to the breasts to prevent them from growing too large. And the root hinders conception. Also, the crushed leaves, in a weight of five drachms <term>drachma</term> <note>A classical unit of weight roughly equal to 3.5 grams or 1/8 of an ounce</note> drunk with wine for five days after menstruation, keep women from conceiving.* 

<margin>Book 27, chapter 9</margin>
**Pliny** truly says: *This plant has a nature for cleansing and cooling, and should be avoided by women. The leaves crushed in wine restrain the breasts of virgins.* 

<margin>Book 6, On the powers of simple medicines</margin>
**Galen** truly says: *The power of Epimedium is moderately cooling, with a watery moisture; for this reason, it has no remarkable quality. Applied to the breasts, it can keep them firm. They say, however, if it is taken as a drink, it causes sterility.*

This plant seems to correspond to these authorities no less in its appearance than in its powers. This is not the plant called **Three-leaved Liverwort** <note>original: "Hepaticum trifolium"; Colonna is distinguishing Epimedium from Hepatica</note>, for it produces beautiful and conspicuous flowers at the same time as the leaves. Nor do the leaves cling to a single small stalk as **Dioscorides** says, who writes that "it is a stalk... having leaves" <note>original Greek: καυλός ἐστιν... ἔχων φύλλα</note> numbering ten or twelve; it is not that the individual leaf-stalks have leaves separate from the main stem. Nor is it that large plant called **Ethiopian Seseli** or **Libanotis**, as some say, for that is a large plant and is afflicted with the same fault as the former; it does not have ten or twelve leaves on a single small stalk, but always has nine on each leaf-stalk; "it is a stalk," says Dioscorides, just as in the chapter on **Broomrape** <term>Orobanche</term> <note>A parasitic plant that grows on the roots of others</note> and similar plants which have a simple stalk, even if he sometimes confuses his way of writing.

A plant called **Adder’s-tongue** <term>Ophioglosson</term> <note>A primitive fern with a tongue-like spore-bearing spike</note> seems related to this one in its habitat, size, nature, and powers. It has an oblong, ivy-like leaf and an oblong "tongue," as if composed of two clusters joined in order; it is seen neither to flower nor to have seed, but a yellow dust (as in the aforementioned plant) which is useless. Its powers are also similar according to the experiments of more recent authors.

HISTORY OF SOME PLANTS

## The Skirret of Dioscorides

<margin>Name.</margin>
SKIRRET <note>original Latin: Siser</note> is called *Sisaron* <note>original Greek: Σίσαρον</note> by the Greeks. Because it was common and well-known, Dioscorides was silent on its identifying features; he left these writings concerning it: "Skirret is well-known; its root, when boiled, is pleasant to the mouth, good for the stomach, promotes urine, and stimulates the appetite" <note>original: Σίσαρον γνώριμον, οὗ ἡ ῥίζα ἑφθὴ, εὔστομος, εὐστόμαχος, οὐρητικὴ, ὀρέξεως προκλητική</note>. 

<margin>Book 20, chap. 5.</margin>
<margin>Book 19, chap. 5.</margin>
Pliny says there are two kinds: the wild Skirret is similar to the cultivated one in appearance and effect; it stimulates the stomach, clears away nausea, etc. And elsewhere, having spoken above about Parsnips <term>Pastinaca</term>, he adds: "Among medicinal plants, the Skirret must be mentioned; the Emperor Tiberius himself made it famous, demanding it every year from Germany. It is grown at a fortress called Gelduba <note>a Roman auxiliary fort on the Rhine, near modern Krefeld-Gellep, Germany</note> situated on the Rhine, where the best quality is found." From this, it appears to suit cold locations. There is a woody core <term>nervus</term> <note>literally "nerve," referring to the tough, fibrous center of the root</note> along its length which is removed after boiling, though much of the bitterness remains; however, when tempered with honey-wine <term>mulsum</term> in food, it even becomes pleasing. The same core is found in the larger Parsnip, at least the year-old ones. The sowing of Skirret occurs in the months of February, March, April, August, September, and October, etc. He says a little later: "Both this, the Skirret, and both kinds of parsnip are sown in Spring and Autumn, with large intervals between seeds—no less than three feet—since it bushes out extensively. However, it is better to transplant Skirret."

<margin>Book 8, On the Power of Simple Medicines.</margin>
Galen, however, handed down only these things about Skirret: "The cooked root of Skirret is pleasing to the stomach and moves the urine; it is warming in the second degree, having a certain bitterness added with a slight astringency."

<margin>Book 11, On Agriculture, chap. 3.</margin>
Columella, dealing with the care of gardens, says: "Parsnip, Skirret, and Elecampane <term>Inula</term> <note>a plant of the daisy family used for medicine and flavoring</note> grow strong in a deeply turned and manured place, but they must be planted very sparsely so they may grow larger. It is appropriate to sow Elecampane at a distance of three feet because it creates vast bushes and spreads by its roots like the 'eye' of a Reed; there is no other cultivation for all of these except that weeds be removed by hoeing." From all this, it is gathered that the plant I have proposed is undoubtedly the Skirret of the Ancients. For its root is similar in shape and flavor to the cultivated Parsnip, though more bitter and more fragrant. All those among the more recent authors who have written about plants have assigned it to the Parsnip genus and called it the common or domestic Parsnip. In this root, there is a woody core along its length, which is removed after it has been 
<meta>catchword: boiled (elixata)</meta>

<summary>This page identifies the Skirret (Siser) by synthesizing classical botanical descriptions from Dioscorides, Pliny, Galen, and Columella, highlighting its cultivation, medicinal properties, and culinary preparation. It emphasizes the plant's German origins and its distinction from the parsnip.</summary>
<keywords>Skirret, Siser, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Galen, Columella, Parsnip, botanical history, medicinal roots, Gelduba, Emperor Tiberius, Elecampane</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical analysis of the Skirret, moving from its culinary qualities and identification to its cultivation methods and physical description. The catchword from the previous page, "fertim," relates to the word "praesertim" (especially) found on this page.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

...when the root has been boiled, not only for the sake of its sweetness, but because the remaining outer part is more tender, more suitable for food, and more pleasant. This is observed in both the common cultivated Parsnip <term>Pastinaca</term> and the Radish <term>Rhaphanus</term>, especially <note>original: præsertim</note> when the roots are fully mature; they then contain a harder core <term>nervus</term> <note>literally "nerve," referring here to the tough, woody vascular center of the root</note> which is unfit for eating. Finally, some depict this plant as *Elaphoboscum* <note>Wild Parsnip or "deer-fodder"</note>, though it is far removed from the description of that plant; for it differs in the shape of the root, the taste, and the seed, which is unlike Dill <term>Anethum</term>, being leafy and compressed. For this reason, one should not fear to say that it is not *Elaphoboscum*, and that it corresponds perfectly to the Skirret <term>Siser</term>, since it still retains the taste, form, and use described in the records of the Ancients.

<margin>Pliny, Book 19, chap. 5.</margin>
This plant is sown by seed, and after it has sprouted, it is transplanted into a field that has been previously deeply dug and manured, so that the root may become larger; it is planted more sparsely, just as other pot-herbs are set out. Jacques Daléchamps <note>a 16th-century French botanist</note>, following others, thought and wrote that it was sown not by seed but by the root, because the plant he described had several roots. However, he was mistaken, because he wished to follow the writings of Pietro Andrea Mattioli <note>a famous Italian physician and botanist</note>, who had transcribed those things from Marcello Virgilio <note>Marcello Virgilio Adriani, a humanist scholar</note>, who only commented on Dioscorides with words. Nor did he notice those words of Pliny: *With eyes cut out from the root, like the Reed is sown...* which demonstrate the planting of Elecampane <term>Inula</term>, not of the Skirret <note>Siser</note>, about which he was speaking. The same author later says that Elecampane, Skirret, and Parsnip are sown at the same time with large intervals between the seeds, but that the Skirret is indeed better when transplanted. Every gardener does this here: first he sows Radishes, Parsnips, Onions, Lettuces, Cabbages, and other vegetables by seed; then, at the appropriate time, after another patch of soil has been dug and manured, he transplants the roots so that they may grow stronger and become larger. Columella <note>a Roman writer on agriculture</note> openly orders this, as I said above in the cited passage, and his words are understood in this way. Having established these facts, I return to the description of the plant.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It is anchored by a long root, similar to the cultivated Parsnip, white, sometimes turning purple in its upper part, which contains a core <term>nervus</term> inside, like the Parsnip and similar plants, but harder and woody, not at all suitable for eating. In the first year, it equals the thickness of a finger; afterwards, it indeed becomes larger and exceeds the Parsnip. It puts forth leaves born from the seed, first in pairs, then in threes, and later in fours, arranged opposite each other, clinging to a leaf-stalk <term>petiolus</term> that is reddish at the bottom, with a single wider leaf at the end of each stalk. They are smooth on the upper side, somewhat hairy on the lower side, saw-toothed around the edges, and very similar to Water-parsnip <term>Lauer</term> <note>likely *Sium latifolium*</note>, and furthermore divided, especially...

<summary>Colonna discusses the culinary preparation of the Skirret root, distinguishes it from Wild Parsnip, and corrects botanical errors by Mattioli and Daléchamps regarding its cultivation by seed versus root division.</summary>
<keywords>Skirret, Siser, cultivation, transplanting, root morphology, Mattioli, Pliny, Columella, botanical identification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Skirret (Siser), following the discussion of its root texture. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the word "praesertim" (especially) from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...especially <note>original: præsertim</note> when the plant has reached three years of age: it sends forth a fennel-like, hairy, reddish, jointed stem, divided at each joint into branches. In its second year, it is four feet tall; afterward, it truly equals or even exceeds the height of a man. At the top of the stem and the many branches it produces, there are yellow umbels <note>umbrella-like flower clusters</note>, and in these later appears a double, flattened, leafy seed, similar to the seed of the plant commonly called <term>Angelica</term> <note>an aromatic herb in the celery family</note>.

<margin>Time.</margin>
<margin>Place.</margin>
It reaches maturity in the month of August; it loves meadow-like locations. The flavor of this root is more bitter than that of the <term>Parsnip</term> <note>original: Pastinaca</note>, yet more fragrant, possessing a more pronounced aromatic quality and a more pleasing taste. For this reason, it is still sown by many today, and especially in Cisalpine Gaul <note>Northern Italy</note>, as I have learned from friends who are inhabitants of that province. It is most greedily sought after as food, and to make it more pleasing to the palate, they usually prepare it in this way: They take the mature root and wash it, cut away the small fibers with a knife, and then boil it. Once it has cooled, they split it lengthwise and remove the core <term>nervus</term> <note>the woody vascular center</note>. Having cut it into pieces and first coated them in flour, they fry them in a pan full of hot oil in the manner of fish. They say it turns out with such a flavor that those eating at the table no longer care to taste even well-seasoned fish. It is no wonder, then, if the Emperor <term>Tiberius</term>, as reported by <term>Pliny</term>, ordered it to be brought from <term>Germany</term> every year, since it is so pleasing and beneficial to both the palate and the stomach. <term>Pliny</term> specifically described its properties, among others, as follows:

<margin>Medicinal Powers.</margin>
<margin>Book 20, ch. 5.</margin>
> *The wild <term>Skirret</term> <note>original: Siser</note> is similar to the cultivated variety in its effect: it stimulates the stomach and removes a loss of appetite. Taken with vinegar seasoned with Laserwort <note>original: Laserpitium, a prized ancient spice resin</note>, or with pepper and honeyed wine, or with fish sauce <note>original: garum</note>, it promotes urine (as Opion believes) and acts as an aphrodisiac. <term>Diocles</term> is of the same opinion. Furthermore, it is suitable for the hearts of those recovering from illness, and is extremely useful after many bouts of vomiting.*

<term>Heraclides</term> gave it as a remedy against quicksilver <note>mercury poisoning</note> and for those repeatedly afflicted by poison, as well as for the sick who are regaining their strength. <term>Hicesius</term> said it seemed useful for the stomach for this reason: because no one could continue eating more than three <term>Skirrets</term> at a time; nevertheless, it is useful for those recovering who are moving on to drinking wine. Specifically, the juice of the cultivated variety, drunk with goat's milk, stops a loose stomach <note>diarrhea</note>. From these accounts, therefore, it is known that this plant retains the use of the <term>Skirret</term> of the Ancients and the powers attributed to it by them. For this reason, I have judged that other plants described by others do not at all reflect the powers, taste, and use of the Skirret. Rather, that plant with a root similar to the <term>Asphodel</term> <note>a hardy perennial with fleshy roots</note> seems to represent the account of the <term>Elaphoboscum</term> <note>literally "deer-fodder," often identified as Wild Parsnip</note>, which I shall discuss next.

<summary>Fabio Colonna describes the physical appearance, culinary preparation, and medicinal virtues of the Skirret (Siser), citing its popularity in Northern Italy and its endorsement by the Emperor Tiberius and ancient physicians.</summary>

### *Dioscorides’ Elaphoboscon.*

<margin>
*The Name and its Etymology. Book 3.*
</margin>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial Q featuring a circular border with scrolling floral or leaf motifs.</image-desc>

Since, as it is reported, stags resist the bites of serpents by means of this fodder, this plant was named *Elaphoboscon*, as if to say "stags' food" <note>original Greek: ἐλάφων βόσκη</note>, as Dioscorides says: "they say that stags, having grazed upon this herb, resist the bites of reptiles" <note>original Greek: ταύτην φασὶ τὴν πόαν τὰς ἐλάφους γεμηθέσας, ἀντέχειν τοῖς τῶν ἑρπετῶν δήγμασι</note>. Pliny also says that they call this herb, which hinds have grazed upon, a resistor of the diseases <note>likely referring to the effects of venom</note> caused by crawling creatures.

<margin>
*Book 22, chapter 22.*
</margin>

Indeed, the story goes that stags resist serpents with this fodder; and elsewhere: "More simply, hinds revealed the *Elaphoboscon*, about which we have spoken." Dioscorides depicted it with these characteristics:

<margin>
*Book 25, chapter 8. Description.*
</margin>

"Elaphoboscon is a stem similar to frankincense-herb <note>original: Libanotis</note> or fennel, and it is jointed. The leaves are two fingers in width, sufficiently long like those of the Turpentine tree <note>original: Terebinthus</note>, bent back, and somewhat rough. The stem has many offshoots, bearing umbels <note>umbrella-like flower clusters</note> similar to Dill <note>original: Anethum</note>; the flowers are yellowish-honey colored, and the seed is similar to Dill. The root is about three fingers in length and one finger in thickness; it is white, sweet, and good for eating. The stem is also eaten as a vegetable when it is newly grown." <note>This section translates the Greek description provided by Dioscorides, which the author then mirrors in his own Latin text.</note>

<margin>
*Book 22, chapter 22.*
</margin>

*And Pliny says: "Elaphoboscon is like a giant fennel <note>original: ferulaceum</note>, jointed, a finger in thickness, with the seed hanging in clusters in the shape of Siler <note>an aromatic plant, possibly Laserwort</note>, but not bitter; the leaves are like those of Alexanders <note>original: Olusatrum</note>."* This description seems mutilated <note>incomplete</note>, since he describes neither the flowers nor the root, and the thickness of a finger seems attributed to the stem rather than the root, about which he says nothing. For this reason, I believe Dioscorides’ description should be followed more closely in this place, since we have the whole plant sufficiently outlined by it. The plant depicted by me

<margin>
*Form.*
</margin>

seems to correspond well enough to this: for it consists of these parts. It has white roots, sweet, fit for eating, and aromatic, with the flavor of the "Black Parsnip" of Theophrastus <note>a philosopher and the "father of botany"</note>. They are a finger in thickness, but truly three or four fingers in length, or sometimes less, though with cultivation and age they may grow larger. Many of them hang down from a single origin in the manner of an <term>Asphodel</term> <note>a hardy Mediterranean plant with tuberous roots</note>, from which come leaves similar to the Water-parsnip <note>original: Lauer</note> or the Skirret <note>original: Siser</note> previously depicted by me, but they cling in threes opposite the leaf-stalk, and a single one at the

### *Dioscorides’ Elaphoboscon.*

<margin>
*The Name and its Etymology. Book 3.*
</margin>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial Q featuring a circular border with scrolling floral or leaf motifs.</image-desc>

Since, as it is reported, stags resist the bites of serpents by means of this fodder, this plant was named *Elaphoboscon*, as if to say "stags' food" <note>original Greek: ἐλάφων βόσκη</note>, as Dioscorides says: "they say that stags, having grazed upon this herb, resist the bites of reptiles" <note>original Greek: ταύτην φασὶ τὴν πόαν τὰς ἐλάφους γεμηθέσας, ἀντέχειν τοῖς τῶν ἑρπετῶν δήγμασι</note>. Pliny also says that they call this herb, which hinds have grazed upon, a resistor of the diseases <note>likely referring to the effects of venom</note> caused by crawling creatures.

<margin>
*Book 22, chapter 22.*
</margin>

Indeed, the story goes that stags resist serpents with this fodder; and elsewhere: "More simply, hinds revealed the *Elaphoboscon*, about which we have spoken." Dioscorides depicted it with these characteristics:

<margin>
*Book 25, chapter 8. Description.*
</margin>

"Elaphoboscon is a stem similar to frankincense-herb <note>original Latin: Libanotis; often referring to plants like rosemary or Seseli</note> or fennel, and it is jointed. The leaves are two fingers in width, sufficiently long like those of the Turpentine tree <note>original Latin: Terebinthus</note>, bent back, and somewhat rough. The stem has many offshoots, bearing umbels <note>umbrella-like flower clusters</note> similar to Dill <note>original Latin: Anethum</note>; the flowers are yellowish-honey colored, and the seed is similar to Dill. The root is about three fingers in length and one finger in thickness; it is white, sweet, and is eaten both raw and cooked. 

The root is effective against the bites of venomous spiders <note>original Latin: phalangia</note> and other serpents, for provoking the menstrual flow, and for promoting urine and soothing griping pains <note>original Latin: tormina</note> in the bowels. Furthermore, this herb is said to be a defense against sorceries and enchantments <note>original Latin: fascinationes</note>." These things having been said by the ancients, I have deemed them worthy of recording for those who are eager...

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s detailed botanical examination of the Elaphoboscon (Stag-fodder). <header>OF FABIO COLONNA</header>

at the tip, as in the Turpentine tree <note>original: *Terebintho*</note>, but around the edge it is serrated with sharp teeth, rough to the touch. For this reason, that word <term>tracheia</term> <note>original Greek: τραχεία; meaning rough or rugged</note> refers not to the surface of the leaves, but to the circumference; and in this passage, it must be interpreted not as "hairy," but as "rough." We see an example of this same usage by Dioscorides in the chapter on Black Hellebore, where he said the leaves were rough, using the word <term>tracheia</term>, which in that case cannot be interpreted as hairy. Indeed, we know that no leaves of Black Hellebore are actually hairy, but are instead rough due to the sharp serrations around them. Furthermore, Dioscorides usually describes hairy leaves with the word <term>dasea</term> <note>original Greek: δασέα; meaning thick, shaggy, or hairy</note>, which is the proper term, as I noted in the third chapter on the Houseleek <note>original: *Semperviuo*</note>.

The leaf is two fingers wide and three fingers long. The plant sends out a round, fennel-like stem, which is jointed; from each of these joints, it produces side-shoots. At the top of these, just like the main stem, it bears umbels <note>flat-topped flower clusters</note> with pale flowers. Each flower is followed by two seeds that are not flat, but somewhat bulging, similar to Dill <note>original: *Anetho*</note>. When cultivated by me, it bloomed at the end of

<margin>
Time.
</margin>

Summer and perfected its seeds in Autumn. In taste and flavor, it is like the aromatic Black Parsnip <note>original: *Pastinacæ nigræ*</note>, but not bitter, as Pliny notes. I have not seen it

<margin>
Place.
</margin>

growing wild, but Lobel <note>Matthias de l'Obel, a Flemish physician and botanist</note> reports that it grows in the territory of Narbonne <note>in southern France</note>.

This plant seems to correspond sufficiently to Dioscorides’ *Elaphoboscon*, even if there appears to be an obstacle, as some claim, in that Dioscorides made no mention of the roots being multiple. To this it may be answered that while this plant does have multiple roots, it does not always have many; since it grows from seed with difficulty and spreads by the root, a single root might be torn away from the rest, as everyone now knows. Therefore, it would not be absurd to suggest that it perhaps happened that Dioscorides saw a plant brought to him with only a single root. Or, more likely, he may have relied on the word of a reporter who affirmed the root was three fingers long and a finger thick, without specifying whether it was part of a cluster. Thus, Dioscorides—perhaps unaware of the multiplicity, or simply remaining silent on it—described the root of this plant in this way, thinking it sufficient to describe a single root as being one finger thick and three long.

Indeed, no root has yet been seen that could have such a shape and size unless it were that of an Asphodel, a Female Peony, or similar plants. A root that is not fleshy or bulbous could never reach the thickness of a finger unless it were more than a foot long, such as a Radish <note>original: *Rhaphani*</note> or something similar. This response seems sufficient to me, as the plant corresponds in all other respects; I believe this doubt regarding the root has been defended and removed. The root of this plant is sweet without
