<sig>M 2</sig>

<margin>Book 8 on the properties of simple medicines.</margin>is without any bitterness, in everyone's opinion. Therefore, these same authors doubt whether it is the *Siser* <note>Likely Skirret (*Sium sisarum*) or a type of Parsnip.</note> of Dioscorides. For Galen and Pliny maintain that *Siser* is not without a certain bitterness; and since modern authors did not find a trace of bitterness in this plant, they attributed the variation to differences in the location and climate. But no one has ever tasted bitter roots of this plant anywhere else. Therefore, their opinion on this must be rejected, since in all places it is sweet to eat and pleasant in taste. Consequently, although the root of this plant differs from Dioscorides’ account only because it is manifold <note>meaning it has multiple roots or branches</note>, it resembles his description in thickness, shape, length, color, and flavor. Furthermore, since it seems to correspond in its leaves (which are very similar to the Turpentine tree), its stem, flowers, seed, and taste, I do not hesitate to present it as the *Elaphoboscon* <note>Greek: *elaphoboskon*, meaning "deer-fodder"</note> of Dioscorides, as it fits his description well enough. Concerning this, Galen, speaking on the subject, says:

<margin>Powers.</margin>
<margin>Book 6 on the properties of simple medicines.</margin>
> *Elaphoboscon is of a warm quality and composed of subtle parts; therefore, it is drying in the second degree of intensity. Dioscorides indeed asserts it has this power. They report that hinds <note>female deer</note>, by feeding on this herb, are able to withstand the bites of serpents. For this reason, its seed is given to those bitten by serpents, to be drunk with wine.*

Pliny indeed holds:
<margin>Book 22, ch. 22.</margin>
> *This is also praised as a food. Indeed, even when preserved, it is used to provoke urine, to soothe pains in the side, to heal ruptures and convulsions, and to dispel flatulence and the torments of the colon <note>colic</note>. It is effective against the stings of serpents and all stinging creatures. For the story goes that deer resist serpents by eating this fodder. The root, applied as an ointment with nitre <note>original: "nitro"; a mineral salt like soda or saltpeter</note>, also heals fistulas. However, it must first be dried for those uses so that it is not moist with its own juice, which does not make it less effective against serpent bites.*

Everyone has depicted this plant as *Sisar* and they affirm it to be the true one. But in my description of *Siser*, I have already shown that they were all deluded <note>original: *hallucinatos*</note>, since they thought this was *Siser* because it was reported to grow from a single root plucked from the cluster—an idea they took from the commentary of Marcello Virgilio <note>A famous 15th-century translator of Dioscorides</note>. This is to the shame of them all, as they are revealed to be insufficiently careful in their reading, or inept at weighing words and meanings, and far too credulous. For simply because Marcello Virgilio wrote, *The ancients said this is sown not by seed, but by bulbs, which—as happens in the Lily, Elecampane, and Arum—are plucked from the rest of the root and set out in gardens for new offspring,* that was no reason to ignore the actual texts of Pliny and Columella cited by him. Nor should they have failed to grasp the truth of the meaning, which, as I said, I revealed in my description of *Siser* and need not be repeated here. Anyone will judge from these facts that the plant depicted by others is not the *Elaphoboscon* of Dioscorides. Indeed, by the taste of the root,

## Hercules’ Poppy of Theophrastus and Dioscorides

<margin>Name and Etymology</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a seated figure amidst architectural elements and scrolling floral motifs.</image-desc>
The name of "Poppy" <note>original Latin: Papaveris</note> was given to this plant, not because it belongs to the genus of true Poppies, but because it grows among grain just like the Red Poppy <note>Papaver rhoeas</note> with leaves that are not dissimilar, also possessing small heads <note>seed pods</note> like a Poppy. It is called "Herculean," however, because of its outstanding strength in inducing vomiting to expel the humors that cause the "comitial disease" <term>comitialis morbus: epilepsy, so-called because if a person had a seizure during the Roman 'comitia' or assembly, the meeting was cancelled</term>, by which Hercules is said to have been afflicted; hence epilepsy is called the "Herculean disease." It is called *aphrodes* <note>original Greek: ἀφρώδης; meaning foamy or frothy</note> by Dioscorides, because it is white and its hairiness gives it the appearance of foam. Indeed, "foam" is said of anything that is rare, white, and light, such as "salt-foam," which is found like a mold or a kind of down clinging to rocks; which Dioscorides called *achne* <note>Greek: ἄχνη; fuzz/froth</note> and *epiphegma* <note>Greek: ἐπίφηγμα; surface-growth</note>—words which signify a fine down—or because it is similar in form and color, such as <margin>Book 4, chapter on salt-foam.</margin> "Aphronitrum" <note>a salt-based efflorescence or "foam of niter"</note>. It can also be called "foamy" from its effect, for when drunk in honeyed water, it expels foamy humor—namely phlegm—through vomiting, as Theophrastus and Dioscorides say, and Galen agrees.

<margin>Book 9, chapter 13.</margin> Theophrastus depicted the characteristics of this plant thus: *Another poppy is called Herculean: having a leaf like Soapwort <note>original Greek: στρουθός (struthos); Soapwort or Saponaria, used for cleaning</note>, with which they bleach linens; a white root near the surface of the ground; and white fruit.*

<margin>Book 4.</margin> Dioscorides, however, has it: *Foamy poppy <note>original Greek: Μήκων ἀφρώδης</note>, called by some Herculean, has a stalk a span <note>about 9 inches</note> long, and very small leaves similar to Soapwort, and white fruit alongside them; the whole little herb is white and foamy, with a thin root extending further along the surface.*

<margin>Book 20, chapter 19.</margin> Pliny, moreover, following Theophrastus, wrote: *Another of the wild kinds is called heraclion...*

<meta>catchword: by</meta>

<summary>This page explains the etymology and physical description of the Hercules' Poppy, linking its name to the "Herculean disease" (epilepsy) and comparing its appearance to foam and Soapwort based on ancient botanical texts.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules’ Poppy, Papaver heracleum, epilepsy, Theophrastus, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, botanical etymology, medical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Hercules’ Poppy. Colonna is specifically addressing the confusion found in ancient texts between this plant and "Struthium" (Soapwort), caused by linguistic similarities between the Greek name for the plant and the word for "sparrow."</meta>

->[THE WORK] OF FABIO COLONNA<-

...by others called *aphron* <note>original: "aphron"; Greek for foamy/frothy</note>, with leaves (if viewed from afar) presenting the appearance of Sparrows; with the root in the top layer of the earth, and a foamy seed: from this, linens derive their splendor in Summer. In this description, everyone has now noticed that Pliny was mistaken <note>original: "hallucinatum"; literally hallucinating or wandering in mind</note>, being ignorant of the herb and misled by the similarity of the word; *strouthion* <note>original Greek: στρούθιον</note> or *strouthos* <note>στρουθός</note>, meaning *Struthium* or *Lanaria* <note>Soapwort, used for washing wool</note>, sounds like *strouthion* or *strouthos*, words which signify a "Little Sparrow" and a "Sparrow." For this reason, he said the leaves present the appearance of Sparrows. But the truth is that the leaves of this Poppy are similar to the leaves of *Struthium*, the "Wool-herb" <note>original: "Lanariæ herbæ"</note>, by which linens are whitened. However, Pliny attributed this whitening power not to the *Struthium*, but to the Poppy, saying: *From this, linens derive their splendor in Summer;* attributing the relative pronoun not to the nearer *strouthos*, but to the Poppy. I do not know, and I wonder, how he fell into this error; for the relative pronoun is not of the feminine gender, as is *mecon heracleia* <note>original Greek: μήκων ἡράκλεια; Hercules' Poppy</note>, but neuter or masculine, *strouthion* or *strouthos*. Nevertheless, by paying very little attention (the candle was being snuffed then <note>a Latin idiom, "emungebatur tunc candela," suggesting Pliny’s attention slipped or he was working by poor light</note>), he wrongly attributed it to the Poppy. Finally, unless we have a certain drawing of the leaves of *Struthium*, we will not be able to assert that the leaves of this Hercules' Poppy have any similarity to the leaves of *Struthium*. Therefore, as far as it is possible, I will first depict the *Struthium* of the ancients, by the authority and description of the same Theophrastus, and then the plant for the Poppy, with leaves much smaller than the *Struthium*, as Dioscorides notes.

<margin>Etymology and description of *Struthium*.</margin>
Therefore, to begin with the etymology of *Struthium* itself, I believe it was so named because its leaves are jagged on both sides in the manner of the feathers of the bird called *Struthio* <note>the Ostrich</note>, commonly called *Struzzo*. For other plants are also named from a 
<margin>Book 4</margin>
similarity to feathers, such as the Fern, which is called *pteris* <note>original Greek: πτέρις</note>, because the leaves are arranged on both sides in the manner of a wing <note>or feather</note>, as Dioscorides says in the chapter on the Fern. So also the leaves of the Fir seem to be, 
<margin>Book 3, ch. 10</margin>
as Theophrastus says, and also those of the Service-trees <note>original: "Sorborum"</note>: *Each one indeed,* he says, *consists of oblique parts in the manner of a wing, as if the whole were one, having lobes* 
<margin>Book 3, ch. 11</margin>
*cut out all the way to the nerve, but the individual parts differ in their number.* Indeed, it is not the parts that fall, but the whole leaf with the appearance of a wing. That the leaves of plants are also said to be similar to the feathers of the Ostrich bird, the same 
<margin>Book 4, ch. 5</margin>
Theophrastus is a witness, who, speaking of the trees of India, says: *Another which has a leaf of a very long sort, similar to the feathers of Ostriches, which are placed upon* 
<margin>Book 6, ch. 3</margin>
*helmets, etc.* Likewise, it ought to have thorny or prickly leaves, for Theophrastus reckons *Struthium* among the *akanthophylla* <note>original Greek: ἀκανθόφυλλα; thorn-leaved plants</note>; and maintains that the Sow-thistle, and *Struthium*, and similar plants, are not of the prickly...

The same Theophrastus says it produces beautiful, odorless flowers in Summer. The *Iris* also blooms in Summer, as does the so-called *Struthium* <note>original: "Struthium"; likely Soapwort (*Saponaria officinalis*), used for cleaning wool</note>; its flower is beautiful to look at but lacks a scent.

Athenaeus records that in ancient times its flowers were added to ceremonial crowns. According to the opinion of Dioscorides, it ought to have a smooth and long root similar to *Polemonia* <note>Greek Valerian or Jacob's Ladder</note>; speaking of *Polemonia*, he says: a dense, whitish root, similar to *Struthium* <note>original Greek: "ῥίζα πυκναία, ὑπόλευκος, ἐμφερὴς στρουθίῳ"</note>. <margin>Book 19, ch. 3</margin>
those who think *Struthium* has leaves like an olive tree, moved by the authority of Pliny, who says: *But that which is called the Little Root* <note>original: "Radicula"</note> *has a juice for washing wool; it is remarkable how much it contributes to whiteness and softness. It is sown everywhere, but the best grows wild in Asia and Syria, in rocky and rugged places. However, the most praised grows beyond the Euphrates; it has a thin, fennel-like stalk, which is itself sought after for food by the natives; it tinges whatever it is boiled with, and has a leaf like an olive...* But I would judge this "wool-herb" of Pliny’s with the olive leaf not to be *Struthium* (whose leaves ought to be "acanaceous" or thistle-like), but rather the plant Dioscorides describes as having "near-thistly" leaves, namely *Hippophaës* <note>Sea buckthorn</note>. For *Struthium*, I would instead substitute one of the plants commonly called *Iacea* <note>Knapweed</note> or those falsely thought to be *Stoebe* <note>A type of knapweed or burnet</note>, or something similar to them. These are indeed thorny-leaved like the *Sonchus* <note>Sow thistle</note>, and they have similar heads that are not thistly; in some of these, I have perceived a sharp and foul breath that is bothersome to the brain. But I decide nothing on these matters for now, for I am specifically searching for the power to whiten wool in the root. For now, it is enough to consider the *Iacea* and *Stoebe*, which reflect the outline of the *Struthium* in the form of their leaves and the whole plant. This is not because they possess the same power as *Struthium*, but because I have undertaken to demonstrate the "habit" of the plant, to which the Hercules' Poppy is very similar (though with much smaller leaves), just as Dioscorides intends.

<margin>Form. Location.</margin>

This plant is very frequent in the fields, and is no less an infestation than the wild Poppy; for this reason, its heads are even found among unwinnowed barley. Farmers call it *fior campese* <note>Italian: "field flower"</note>. It is anchored by a small, thin root that descends very little into the earth—much less than the Horned Poppy, whose root Theophrastus and Dioscorides say is carried through the very top of the soil. The root is white and divided into small fibers. It puts forth leaves similar to the first leaves of the wild Poppy, and also to the *Iacea* and *Stoebe*. Even when the plant has matured, the leaves remain similar to those, but are twice or three times smaller, whiter, and softer, being very downy, which makes them appear hoary or greyish. It puts forth a stem at the end of Spring, divided into branches from the base, upon which and upon the leaves...

<summary>Colonna critiques Pliny's botanical descriptions, arguing that Soapwort (Struthium) should be identified by its thistle-like leaves rather than olive-like ones. He identifies the Hercules' Poppy as a common field weed with downy leaves and a shallow root system.</summary>
<keywords>Struthium, Soapwort, Pliny the Elder, Dioscorides, botanical classification, Hercules' Poppy, Iacea, Knapweed, wool processing, field flower</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Colonna’s botanical analysis of the "Heraclean Poppy," specifically focusing on its physical description—comparing its downy texture to foam—and its medicinal use as an emetic (a substance to induce vomiting) in ancient texts.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA

<margin>Time.</margin>
Beyond the natural hairiness of other leaves, a certain down is seen, as if a thin layer of cotton <note>original: "Bambacium"; medieval Latin for cotton</note> had been cast over it, which seems to reflect the color and thinness of foam. I do not believe any plant reflects foam in any other way; nor do I believe, as some think, that it exudes a foamy liquid. For the boiling of external heat is required in a liquid for it to be able to foam, which I believe can least of all happen in plants, as I have not yet observed it. In Summer, however, from the top of the branch-pits <note>axils</note> of each leaf—as in the Great Celandine, which Dioscorides says has a flower "beside each leaf" <note>original Greek: παρ’ ἕκαστον φύλλον</note>—there are white, scaly heads, producing various flowers, for they are seen in white, purple, and blue colors. Furthermore, that the heads of this plant are similar to Poppy heads, the same 
<margin>Book 3</margin>
Dioscorides reports in the chapter on Great Centaury in these words: "on which are heads similar to a poppy" <note>original Greek: ἐφ’ ὧν κεφαλαὶ ὁμοιαι μήκωνι</note>. Indeed, these are similar to Centaury. It has a seed like Safflower <term>Cnicus: Safflower, a thistle-like plant used for dye and medicine</term>, small and grayish-white, which is why it is called "foamy" <note>original Greek: ἀφρώδες (aphrodes)</note>; such indeed is the color of foam, for the opacity of the water is seen through its own transparent parts. It has a downy tuft <note>pappus</note>, and is bitter and sharp; when fully formed, it falls away of its own accord once the head opens. 
<margin>Powers.</margin>
Theophrastus attributes these powers to it: "the root of this plant purges through the upper parts [induces vomiting]; some use it for the falling sickness <term>falling sickness: epilepsy; traditionally called 'comitial disease' because Roman assembly meetings (comitia) were cancelled if a participant had a seizure</term> in honey-water <term>aqua mulsa: a mixture of honey and water, also known as hydromel</term>." Dioscorides, however, describing the powers and the method more carefully, says: *"The fruit of this plant, not the root, is collected in Summer when it has fully matured and falls off when dried. It purges by vomiting if a measure of an acetabulum <note>A Roman unit of volume, roughly 60ml or 1/8 of a pint</note> is taken with honey-water. This type of purging is particularly beneficial for those with epilepsy."* Pliny indeed states: *"The seed is crushed in a mortar for the falling sickness, in the measure of an acetabulum in white wine; for it causes vomiting."* And he understands this as agreeing with Dioscorides regarding the seed, for Theophrastus attributes the power to the root drunk in honey-water. Pliny disagrees with both on this, for he wrote it should be drunk in white wine; deceived by him, Gaza, in his version of this passage, translated "honey-water" <note>original Greek: μελικράτῳ</note> as "wine-honey" <note>original Latin: vino mulso</note>. But the error of both seems clear, for in inducing vomiting, one should use not wine, but honey-water, 
<margin>Book 5, ch. on honey-water.</margin>
since honey-water has this specific power of inducing vomiting, as witnessed by Dioscorides. Galen, however, left these notes on this plant: 
<margin>Book 7, on the powers of simple medicines.</margin>
*"Heraclean Poppy is also called 'aphrodes' [foamy] because it is entirely white and foamy, being small. It has a seed that purges phlegm."* More recent authors have not recognized this power of the seed to induce vomiting under the name of the *Cyaneus* flower <note>The Cornflower (Centaurea cyanus)</note>, mentioned by Pliny among other plants used for making wreaths. 
<margin>Book 21, ch. 9 and 11</margin>
Whether this is different from the foamy Poppy, which he did not know, just as...

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>Colonna describes the physical "foamy" appearance of the Heraclean Poppy (likely a species of Silene) and discusses its medicinal use as an emetic for epilepsy, noting contradictions between ancient authorities like Theophrastus, Dioscorides, and Pliny.</summary>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Heraclean Poppy" or "Foamy Poppy" from page 96, specifically addressing the botanical identification and debates over whether it is the same as the Cornflower or Soapwort.</meta>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...whether it is the same as the Cornflower <note>original: "Cyanus"</note> is uncertain, but I believe it could be both. For the flowers of Soapwort <note>original: "Struthium"</note> are not unlike these and are also used for garlands; just as the flowers of this plant, whose beauty and variety of colors allow for elegantly composed bouquets to be made in Summer, mixed with the flowers of Roses, Oranges, and Carnations <note>original: "Caryophyllorum"</note>, so that both the eyes are delighted by the variety of color and the nose by the scent—and for this reason, it is sown in gardens. Therefore, it can be called the <term>aphrodes mekon: foamy poppy</term>, specifically that of Venus. Since it was slightly similar in its leaves to Gum Succory <note>original: "Chondrilla"</note> and Endives <note>original: "Intybus"</note>, they <note>the ancient authorities</note> considered its nature to be similar; and they attributed the same powers to it as Succory <note>original: "Serides"</note> has—namely, cooling and drying. However, as is evident from the taste, it seems to me to participate in a certain sharpness, and to differ not a little from those. They say that the man from Piacenza <note>likely the botanist Melchior Guilandino</note> mixed the Cornflower into Chicory syrup, assigning it to the genus of Chicory, but it certainly stands apart from it, as everyone has now observed. Furthermore, I believe that he used not this plant, but another species that always produces a blue flower, which Dodonaeus calls "Blue Gum Succory" <note>original: "Chondrillam caeruleam"</note>, and which produces milk. Others depict this as the "Small Sesame-like plant of Dioscorides" <note>original: "Sesamoide paruo Dioscoridis"</note>, though it is far different from that in its flower, the color and taste of its seed, and its powers.

<margin>Variety.</margin>
There is also another plant, commonly called the Greater Cornflower <note>original: "Cyanei maioris"</note>, which is frequent not in the fields but in mountainous places, with larger, broader leaves that are grayish-white, hairy, and filled with an overabundance of that same down. It has a shorter, thicker, and woolly stem. The flowers are purple; the calyx <note>the cup-like base of the flower</note> is not white and smooth, but has green scales divided along the edges by yellow fringes. The root is perennial and creeping, but the seed does not differ. From these facts, it is clear that the plants depicted by others as "Foamy Poppy" have no similarity to the leaves of Soapwort, nor do they correspond to their own drawings.

HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

## *Dioscorides’ Phyteuma.*

Some commentators, following Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*.</note>, have thought it an easy and plausible excuse to claim that because the use of Phyteuma is recorded only as a love-charm, it is unnecessary to describe or track down its history. However, the search for this plant was actually difficult for them; therefore, having found an excuse to avoid appearing lazy, they rested on that point. To be sure, the history of this plant is very brief, but it possesses distinctive characteristics. If any diligent and studious person should find the <term>Lanaria herb</term> <note>original: "Lanariam herbam"; likely Soapwort (*Saponaria officinalis*), used for cleaning wool.</note>, or consider the form of the plant based on the words left by the ancients, they will easily identify it by the hallmark of the holes in its fruit, by which it is said to be divided. Relying on these signs, I have endeavored to describe this plant.

<margin>Etymology.</margin>
Although the etymology <note>The origin and meaning of the name.</note> of this word seems common to all plants, because they are all "produced" <note>original Greek: φύτεύονται (*phyteuontai*)</note>; nevertheless, the word "to plant" <note>original Greek: φύτεύω (*phyteuō*)</note> is said to signify not only to plant, but also to produce, generate, and give birth.

<margin>Name.</margin>
Since this plant is especially suited for love-potions, and thus for generation, it was called *Phyteuma* <note>Meaning "that which is planted" or "a seedling/offspring."</note> above all others.

<margin>In matters of Venus.</margin>
The word "to plant" <note>original Greek: φύτευειν (*phyteuein*)</note> is used not only for grasses and plants, but also for children; hence Aristophanes says:

> Then it is necessary to beget children, and to nourish them.
> <note>original Greek: Ἔπειτα παῖδας χρὴ φυτέυειν, καὶ τρέφειν.</note>

And Diogenes <note>Diogenes Laertius, a biographer of Greek philosophers.</note>, as recorded in Laërtius, said when he was attending to his children that he was "planting a human." Among the writings of Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD.</note>, this is the description:

<margin>Description.</margin>
Phyteuma has leaves similar to Soapwort, but smaller; abundant fruit with holes; a small, thin root near the surface. <note>original Greek: φύτευμα φύλλα ἔχει ὅμοια στρουθίῳ, μικρότερα δὲ: καρπὸν πολὺν τετρημένον. ῥίζαν μικρὰν, λεπτὴν, ἐπιπόλαιον.</note> Some read "long" instead of "small" for the root. Pliny, however, described only its use, which I will discuss later. The plant depicted by me seems to correspond to this description. It has a long, thin root that descends only slightly under the earth, spreading out sideways and springing up above the ground. From this root emerge leaves that are slightly hairy and a whitish-green color, similar in shape and division to the plants commonly called <term>Jacea</term> or <term>Stoebe</term> <note>Types of Knapweeds or related thistles.</note>, though the first leaves are broader, with more rounded, eroded edges, and are smaller. Later, it produces stalks a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or the length of a forearm.</note> long...

<summary>Columna argues against botanical laziness, using linguistic roots and classical descriptions from Dioscorides and Aristophanes to identify the Phyteuma plant by its "perforated fruit." He links the plant's name to the Greek word for "planting" or "begetting," reflecting its historical use in love potions.</summary>

<keywords>Phyteuma, Dioscorides, etymology, botanical description, Soapwort, classical botany, love potions, plant identification</keywords>

such stalks, even two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches.</note> long, where at intervals through the joints, leaves cling in opposite pairs. These are thinner and more pinnate <note>Feather-shaped, with leaflets arranged on either side of a stem.</note>, from which "wings" emerge. At the top of these and the main stem, perched on long stalks, stand the small heads of purple flowers
<margin>Location.</margin>
verging toward blue. They are similar to the **Bituminous Clover** <note>original: *Trifolio Bituminoso*; likely *Bituminaria bituminosa*, known for its scent of pitch.</note>, or even more to the plant I have called **Pycnocomon** <note>A name Columna used for a type of Scabious.</note>. Because of the pleasantness of the climate here, these are seen blooming all year round everywhere along the margins of the roads,
<margin>Season.</margin>
refreshing the eyes, but especially so in Summer. Once the flowers fall, they are succeeded by numerous fruits arranged around the head, forming an oblong shape; for this reason, Dioscorides called it *copious fruit* <note>original Greek: καρπὸν πολὺν (*karpon polyn*)</note>. These fruits are formed with the diligent industry of nature: for each one is sharper at the bottom where they are joined, thicker in the middle, and they become wider from the middle to the top, constructed with a certain thin, lined cartilage. If you turn them over, you will see they represent the shape of a cap or a small bell: for the wider, cartilaginous part forms the rim, while the rest forms a pyramid shape. This entire part is broken along its length into arches and open bridges, an exceedingly elegant structure. If you wish to see these openings clearly, look at the fruit of the "Greater Spanish" plant, a relative named by herbalists; since it is larger, it has larger holes, so that a needle can pass through each arch. The seed of this Spanish plant is hairy at the bottom, which differs from our local variety. If one is to indulge in conjecture, I believe this plant corresponds more closely to its own illustration; unless the <term>Lanaria herb</term> <note>Soapwort, often used for cleaning wool.</note> is more certain, and despite different leaves, this must be discussed further.

<margin>

</margin>
Regarding its medicinal powers, Dioscorides described no others than that the root is useful for love-potions. Pliny, for this reason, said: *I judge it superfluous to describe what kind of plant Phyteuma is, since its use is only for love-matters.* But I certainly believe it can be useful for other things; for simply because it was used only for love-charms does not mean it cannot have other powers that they did not use, or which were least known to them. Therefore, it is no wonder if more recent authors have recognized other powers in this plant (and not for love-charms, the use of which has faded away) under the name of **Scabiosa** <note>Scabious, a group of flowering plants in the honeysuckle family.</note>, because they think it useful when applied as a salve for curing and drying out the "scab" <note>original: *Scabiem*; refers to various skin diseases or "the itch."</note>. <margin>
Note: See the chapter on Bluebottle.
Book 6, ch. 3
Book 7, ch. 9
</margin>
...to **Gum-succories** and **Chicories**, which are cooling, drying, and friendly to the heart. However, these powers attributed by more recent authors—such as their being useful when mixed into **Theriac** <note>A famous ancient "universal antidote" or medicinal jam composed of dozens of ingredients, used against poisons and plague.</note> against venom and pestilential diseases, or their being cooling and binding when mixed with **Plantain**, **Horsetail**, and similar plants—do not satisfy my mind in the least. For any plant at all, unless its properties are entirely contrary, could be said to achieve the same effect once useful things are mixed into it. In this plant, although some bitterness is present, it is not perceived to be sharp or acrid; therefore, it is no wonder if some have said it possesses drying and astringent powers, particularly those who believed it to be the *Stoebe* of Dioscorides. Indeed, the author of the *History of Plants* <note>Likely referring to Theophrastus or a contemporary commentator.</note> says that *a decoction of **Scabious** is wonderfully effective against the "itch" when drunk, just as the juice is when smeared on bodies afflicted by scabies.* But while I know that warm and dry applications are useful externally for those with scabies, I know that for internal use, cooling and drying syrups—not heating or acrid ones—should be administered. I leave this to be decided by more experienced men and those devoted to this faculty of medicine. Dioscorides wrote that this plant was useful for "love-matters," but he omitted the method of use; thus, it remains unknown to this day, especially since it might achieve this through magical rather than physical properties—much like other things that are claimed to be useful when hung around the neck or the house, or merely touched. This is not the **Phyteuma** described by others like Mattioli and Pena; none of those have "copious fruits," but rather few, which contradicts the opinion of Dioscorides, who says it has <term>copious fruit</term> <note>original Greek: καρπόν πολύν (*karpon polyn*)</note>. Nor does it have the leaves of the **Soapwort** plant <note>original: *Struthium*.</note>, which has pinnate leaves and is of the <term>thorn-leafed</term> <note>original Greek: ἀκανθοφύλλων (*akanthophyllon*)</note> genus; and it is judged to have the head of a **Sow-thistle** according to Theophrastus, of which we have spoken above.

### *Erinus of Nicander and Dioscorides.*

The ancients called this plant **ERINON**. Because it possesses and emits a milky sap in the manner of the **Wild Fig**, it is called <term>wild fig</term> <note>original Greek: ἐρινεὸς (*erineos*)</note> by the Greeks. For this reason, Pliny, after his description of the Wild Fig, followed it with this: *The herb which the Greeks call Erineon must also be recorded in this place because of its kinship in name.* And because it has the leaves of **Basil** <note>original: *Ocimum*.</note> before it produces a stem, as is observed in the **Basil-like** plant...

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

<margin>Book 4.</margin>...moides <note>likely referring to a resemblance to another plant, possibly Alyssum or a similar genus</note>, and because it delights in aquatic places, it was called *hydriron* <note>original Greek: ὑδριρὸν; meaning "watery" or "water-loving"</note>. Dioscorides depicted it with these marks: <margin>Description.</margin> *Erinus grows by rivers and springs; it has leaves similar to Basil <note>original Latin: Ocimum</note>, but smaller and notched at the upper parts; it has five or six branches, a span in height <note>about 9 inches</note>; white flowers; and black fruit, small and sour. The stem and leaves are full of juice.* <note>original Greek: Ἐρίνος φύεται παρὰ ποταμοῖς καὶ κρήναις . φύλλα ἔχει ὠκίμῳ ὅμοια . μικρότερα δὲ καὶ ἐπεσχισμένα ἐκ τῶν ἄνωθεν μερῶν . κλώνια δὲ ε' ἢ ϛ' . σπιθαμιαῖα . ἄνθη λευκά . καρπὸν δὲ μέλανα , μικρόν , συφόν . ὁποῦ δὲ μεσός ἐστιν ὁ καυλὸς καὶ τὰ πέταλα.</note> <margin>Book 23, ch. 7.</margin> Pliny transcribed it thus: *It is a palm high <note>about 3 inches</note>, with roughly five little stems, in the likeness of Basil; the flower is white, the seed black and small.*

<margin>Form.</margin> The plant depicted by me seems to correspond to these descriptions; it is found nowhere except in watery places, on the banks of springs and <margin>Location.</margin> rivers, and in valleys at the foot of mountains, being very frequent everywhere here. It consists of a fleshy root, not large, equaling a small radish <note>original Latin: Rhaphanum</note> at its largest; it is white, with a flavor that is pleasant, transitioning from sweet to bitter-sour, and is sought after for salads <note>original Latin: acetarijs</note>. It produces a milky sap. In the middle or at the bottom, where it is slightly swollen, the leaves emerge in early Spring; these are similar to Basil leaves, but smaller (equal to the leaves of a medium-sized Basil), saw-toothed <note>term: serrated</note> around the edges, slightly hairy on top and green, but smooth, polished, and whiter underneath.

Later, it sends out several stems from the root, depending on the mildness of the location and the year; these are a foot or more in height, angular, and hairy. Leaves surround these stems alternately without stalks <note>term: sessile leaves</note>; these are much longer, narrower, and less saw-toothed, so much so that anyone who saw the plant without a stem, and later saw it endowed with a stem, would undoubtedly judge it to be a different species, unless they had observed it cultivated at home. Indeed, the leaves which it had touching the ground before it produced stems wither and vanish, and are no longer found upon it.

From the leaf-axils <note>the angle between the leaf and the stem</note> little branches emerge, on which, as on the stems, flowers come out in May; these cling to small stalks and are hollow, divided into five segments at the tip. Their color is whitish, and also purplish, having a stalk <note>term: thyrsus; the central part of the flower structure</note> inside which is divided into three curved parts. When these dry out, the base of the flowers swells up; it is hairy and ends in five small green leaves, producing a seed in June and July that is dark, tiny, and flattened, with a <margin>Time.</margin> bitter taste. When the seed is mature and dry, it falls through the openings at the base which are then passable. The entire plant, from the root all the way to the cups <note>term: calyces</note> of the flowers, flows with a sweet milk when broken. Therefore, in its native location, the shape of its leaves, and its abundance of milk and flavor—which are its private marks—it seems to correspond to the classical description. To this, Dioscorides...

It highlights the plant's unique "milky" sap and the dramatic change in its leaf shape as it matures.</summary>

# BY FABIO COLONNA

<margin>Medicinal Properties.</margin> Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> attributes these powers to it: *The seed of this plant, in the weight of two drachms <note>a drachm was a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</note>, mixed with four measures <note>original Latin: Cyathos</note> of honey, is applied to stop discharges from the eyes. But the juice, when dripped in with sulfur and nitre <note>natural soda or saltpeter</note>, soothes ear pain.* Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note> states that, when crushed with Attic honey <note>highly prized honey from the mountains near Athens</note>, it heals eye inflammations; however, whenever it is gathered, it flows with much milk, and as a sweet herb, it is very useful for ear pain when a little nitre is added. *The leaves resist poisons.* Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> does not list *Erinon*, but describes a plant called *Echinon* in such a way that it seems to refer to *Erinon*. Paul of Aegina <note>a 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician</note>, however, lists both *Erinon* and *Echinon* (transcribed from Galen), so that he considers them to be different from *Erinon*. *Erinos*, he says, *is an aquatic herb, two drachms of whose seed, smeared on with four drachms of honey, stop eye discharges; the juice is effective for ear pain.* Like Dioscorides, he does not pass on anything regarding the power to repel poisons, which Pliny adds when he says, *The leaves resist poisons.* But Nicander <note>a 2nd-century BCE Greek poet and physician</note> recorded that this power resides in the root in his *Theriaca* <note>a poem about medicinal treatments for bites of venomous animals</note>, in these verses:

> Whether you grind the root of Eryngium <note>Sea Holly</note> and healing Acanthus,
> And carry an equal weight
<margin>* elsewhere: "clinging" or "leaning upon."</margin>
> Of both, and of the Erinus flourishing at the foot of the mountain.
> <note>original Greek: Ἠ'ὲ σύγ' ἠρύγγυιο καὶ ἀλθήεντος ἀκάνθου / Ρίζαν λειήνεο, φέροις δ' ἰσοῤῥεπὲς ἄχθος / Ἀμφοῖν καὶ χλοάον τος ἐν ἀρπέζαισιν ἐρίνου.</note>

The Greek commentator on Nicander also relates these things about the *Erinus*. Diocles <note>Diocles of Carystus, a 4th-century BCE physician</note>, in his *Rhizotomicon* <note>a treatise on "root-cutting" or herbalism</note>, says it is similar to Basil <note>original Latin: Ocimo</note>: it is beneficial against venomous beasts; it grows near rivers, springs, sunny places, and the roots of mountains. From these words of Nicander and Diocles, it is clear that the root resists poisons, and that this plant described by me has a root that is suitable for being preserved, according to Diocles' opinion—a feature which is least apparent in other plants thought by others to be *Erinus*. It is not the *Erinus* that is depicted by Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> as a mutilated plant without flowers, which no one has seen except as he depicted it, besides himself, who asserts that he stained his hands with its milk when it was pulled up. But certainly, the leaves depicted by him do not resemble Dioscorides' *Erinus*; indeed, Mattioli did not perceive his words correctly, for when Dioscorides says the leaves are "notched in the upper parts," it is understood to mean the leaves themselves, not the stem as Mattioli falsely and fictitiously depicted it. Dioscorides' words are these: <note>original Greek: καὶ ἐπεσχισμένα ἐκ τῶν ἄνωθεν μερῶν</note>, as is also observed in the chapter on *Argemone* <note>Poppy-like plants</note>. Therefore, I judge that neither is that plant an *Erinus*, nor are the things asserted in that place true: for there are not many plants rising near rivers and springs and the roots of mountains, which to us and others...

...unknown to investigators. Nor is it the *Erinus* which is mentioned by Pena and L’Obel <note>Pierre Pena and Matthias de l’Obel, influential 16th-century botanists who co-authored the 'Stirpium Adversaria Nova'</note>, for they themselves do not assert it with certainty. <margin>History, Book 9, Chapter 171.</margin> Nor is it the one which Daléchamps <note>Jacques Daléchamps, a French physician and botanist</note> proposes last, which has leaves not at all incised, nor notched near the tops of the stems—"split from the upper parts," <note>original Greek: ἐκ τῶν ἄνωθεν μερῶν ἐπεσχισμένα</note> as Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of 'De Materia Medica'</note> says; he describes them as split long and narrow, almost like the Stone Pine <note>Latin: Pinastri</note>. This is how he translates it; however, whether he interpreted it correctly, and whether the plant depicted by him is the true one, anyone can judge from the descriptions mentioned above.

## Hippophæstum or Hippophæës of Dioscorides.

<margin>Name. Etymology.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial V featuring a miniature landscape with a building and trees.</image-desc>
Not only is the naming of this plant varied, but its spelling is as well. In some versions one reads *hippophaiston, hippophyes, hippophaes,* or *hypophaiston* <note>Original Greek: ἱππόφαιστον, ἱπποφυές, ἱπποφαές, ὑπόφαιστον</note>; in others, *Hippope, Hippopaestum, Hypophaestum,* or *Hippophyes*. It can be called *Hippophaestum* from the words for "horse" and "shining" <note>original Greek: ἵππο (hippo) and φαῖσος (phaisos), from φαίνομαι (phainomai)</note>, that is to say, "very splendid." The prefix "horse" <note>original Greek: ἵππο</note> indeed signifies greatness or augmentation, as in "horse-parsley" <note>original Greek: ἱπποσέλινον - hipposelinon</note> and similar terms—a compound commonly used by Dioscorides. Thus, I would call it the "very splendid thorn," which seems to suit the small heads of this plant; for the spines are smooth and shining, arranged in a circle around the flower heads. For this reason, it is commonly called *Stellaria* <note>Star-thistle</note>, and also *Calcitrapa* <note>Caltrop, after the spiked weapon</note>, because since it has hard, piercing spines, it is harmful to men and other animals who tread upon it with their feet. <margin>Book 22, Chapter 12.</margin> For this reason, in the text of Pliny where it is corruptly written as *Hippope*, I believe it should have been written as *Hippopaeston*; this name seems to be composed of "horse" and "to strike" <note>original Greek: ἵππο and παίω (paio)</note>, from which it could be called the "very striking thorn." Some write it as *Hypopaestum*, for *Hypophaestum* is read in the codex of Paul of Aegina <note>A 7th-century Greek physician</note> and also in Pliny; and by that adverb "under" <note>original Greek: ὑπὸ - hypo</note>, it would mean "the thorn striking from beneath," or "somewhat bright." Therefore, the name *Stellaria* remained in common use for this plant due to the smoothness and shape of the spines, while *Calcitrapa* remained because of its effect; both are quite consistent with the ancient names *hippopaiston* or *hypopaiston*, and *hippophaiston* or *hypophaiston*. <margin>Book 22, Chapter 12.</margin> Pliny, lest he seem ignorant of the etymology of these plants, *Hippophyes* and *Hippope*, said by way of guessing: *They must be suited to the nature of horses, nor have they received their name from any other cause.* But to me...

O
<meta>catchword: Hip-</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a critique of botanical identifications for the plant Erinus by authors like Daléchamps and L'Obel before beginning an etymological study of 'Hippophaestum' (Star-thistle). The author argues that the name refers to the plant's 'splendid' or 'striking' spines rather than a direct connection to horses, as Pliny had guessed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical investigation into the plant Hippophaestum (or Hippophaës), following the introduction to the chapter on the previous page. Colonna is here comparing the descriptions found in classical authorities like Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus to reconcile conflicting accounts of the plant's nature.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA'S
### ON PLANTS

<margin>Book 4. Description.</margin>
Only the name *Hippophyes* seems to correspond, for *hippophyes* <note>original Greek: ἱπποφυές</note> seems to signify "born from horses"; and it is interpreted not as "under" but as "horse." *Hippope*, however, which Pliny calls *Hypophaestum* in another place, does not correspond at all, since it has "under" <note>original Greek: ὑπὸ</note> and not "horse" <note>original Greek: ἵππῳ</note>. These are the notes on this plant in Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose 'De Materia Medica' was the standard botanical text for 1,500 years</note>: *Hippophaiston*—some also call this *Hippophaës* <note>original Greek: Ἱππόφαισον, οἱ δὲ καὶ τοῦτο Ἱπποφαὲς καλοῦσι</note>. It grows in the same places in which *Hippophaës* grows, and it is itself a species of fuller’s thorn <note>Latin: spina fullonia; plants with hooked prickles used in the textile industry to "tease" or raise the nap on wool</note>. It is low-lying, having only small thorny leaves and empty heads. It bears neither stem nor flower. The root is thick and soft.

<margin>Book 22, chap. 12.</margin>
Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the 'Natural History'</note> has it that, *There is also another Hippope, without a stem or flower, with only tiny leaves.* And elsewhere, speaking of herbs that grow <margin>Book 16, last chap.</margin> upon other plants, he says, *But as for what is on the fuller’s thorn, called hypophaestum, with empty little calyxes, small leaves, a white root, etc.* And elsewhere, <margin>Book 27, chap. 10.</margin> *Hypophaestum grows on thorns, from which the brazen* (some read "balls") *fuller’s tools are made; it is without a little stem, without a flower, with only empty little heads, and many small leaves of a grassy color, having white, soft little roots, etc.* From these latter descriptions, Pliny seems to say that *Hypophaestum* is a different thing growing *upon* the fuller’s thorn <note>i.e., a parasitic plant like mistletoe</note>, which is not found in Dioscorides or others. Therefore, I judge that he was mistaken in these passages, and thought that something grew upon this plant just as on Thyme, which is called *Epithymon* <note>Dodder, a parasitic plant that looks like tangled threads</note>, another species of which <margin>Book 26, chap. 8.</margin> the same Pliny asserts is called *Hippopheon*. He says there: *Likewise Epithymum, which is the flower from Thyme, similar to Savory. The difference is that this one is herbaceous, while the other thyme's is white; some call it Hippopheon.* Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> has nothing about this *Hypophaestum*, although he seems to describe the other when speaking of <margin>Book 6, chap. 5.</margin> plants with leaves alongside thorns <note>original Greek: παρακανθοφύλλοις</note>, saying there: "not like the bulrush and *hippopheos*, which are thornless in their leaves." In this description, Marcello Virgilio <note>A 15th-century translator of Dioscorides</note>, and the one who wrote the *Emblemata*, and finally Daléchamps, were deceived by error and joined the following words which seemed to fit this plant; nor did they notice that the description expresses the outline and nature of the Caper.

But I return to the plant, and the one I have depicted seems to correspond to Dioscorides’ outline better than any other known so far, even exotic ones. It is very frequent here <margin>Place. Form.</margin> in sandy places near the shores and has thin leaves; at the end of Autumn it emerges from the root, which is a finger’s width or more thick, soft, and juicy, having a fiber like other roots, but somewhat hard, and in color...
<note>The sentence continues on the next page; the catchword is "whitish".</note>

<summary>Colonna critiques Pliny and other botanical authorities for confusing Hippophaestum with parasitic plants, arguing it is a distinct species of "fuller's thorn." He identifies his own specimen as the true match for Dioscorides' ancient description.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s detailed botanical analysis of "Hippophaestum," specifically reconciling the physical characteristics he observes with the often contradictory descriptions found in the works of ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

# FABIO COLONNA’S

whitish and a foot long, similar to a Radish <note>original: *Rhaphano*</note>, from which leaves emerge spread in a circle on the ground, green in color, slightly hairy and soft compared to other spiny plants, very small and deeply jagged; having prickles along the edges like a Sow-thistle <note>original: *Sonchus*</note>, on account of which they can be called "thorny" <note>original Greek: ἀκανθώδη (*akanthōdē*)</note>. However, in Pliny’s text, nothing is mentioned regarding spines on the leaves, and in the text of Dioscorides, a comma is marked between the words "small" and "thorny"; for this reason, Marcello Virgilio <note>Marcello Virgilio Adriani (1464–1521), a scholar known for his translation of Dioscorides</note> interpreted it as *having only small leaves and thorny heads*. But since it is a "fulling" spine <note>original Greek: γναφική (*gnaphikē*); refers to the process of "fulling" or cleaning and thickening wool</note>, the use of which is in carding clothes—which is the specific meaning of the verb "to card" <note>original Greek: γνάφω (*gnaphō*)</note>, from which the instruments for combing wool are called "cards" <note>original Greek: γνάφαι (*gnaphai*)</note>. As Suidas <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note> has it: *an instrument having points in a circle*, etc. Indeed, the commentator on Aristophanes in *Plutus* says: *a knaphos or gnaphos is a certain thorny thing with which they scrape clothes*. The heads also appear to be "thorny" <note>original Greek: ἀκανθώδη (*akanthōdē*)</note>, which can easily provide this use, since they have prickles in a circle and cling to clothes; hence some have called it *Echinion* <note>from the Greek for hedgehog</note>, *Lappago* <note>Burdock-like</note>, or the smaller *Lappula*, which although they were written above regarding *Hippophaes*, nevertheless suit this plant. For *Hippophaes* has soft berries, not prickly ones, and the names are confused at the beginning of this chapter where two plants are described. In the middle of the "navel" <note>the center of the leaf rosette</note> of the leaves, however, in the month of November, there are found in

<margin>Time.</margin>
all of them a single head, or several, without a stalk, but nestled among the leaves, whitish-yellow in color. These are nothing other than spines emerging in the manner of small leaves, and they appear composed like the leaves of our local Aloe, coming out alternately one among the others. They contain nothing inside, which is why they were called "hollow" <note>original Greek: χαῦνα (*chaina*)</note>, and the plant "low-growing" <note>original Greek: χαμαιπετές (*chamaipetes*)</note>, which Pliny says is without a stalk and without a flower, having only empty heads. Nor is it surprising that this plant was described so incompletely by the Ancients—without a stalk, low, without a flower, and with empty heads—for although this could be held against them as a fault (since they described many plants that produce a stalk or flower late, or even early ones without their stalk and flower), it nevertheless seems to have been done for a reason. More often than not, since they are devoid of stalk and flower for a long time, they are found without them; therefore, they could be seen as described by their more principal and consistent characteristics. From that time it remains so low, and with an empty head, until the beginning of Summer <note>original: *Aeſtatis*</note>; and from then on it begins to produce a hairy stalk a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> long, but when dried it becomes smooth. It is divided into many branches, and at the ends of each branch thin leaves are attached, among which the heads [grow]...

<summary>Fabio Colonna describes the physical structure and seasonal growth of the Hippophaestum, arguing that its prickly heads were historically used as wool-carding tools. He explains that ancient botanists often described the plant in its winter state—stalkless and "hollow"—which led to taxonomic confusion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s botanical investigation into the plant "Hippophaestum," transitioning from its physical description to its medicinal properties and then contrasting it with the "Myacantha" described by Theophrastus and Clusius.</meta>

->### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS.<-

The heads come out divided by spines like the previous ones, ending in colors of green, purple, yellow, and whitish; later, swelling out into a belly or body, they produce purplish flowers in the summer, similar to Cornflowers <note>original: *Cyanoides*</note> but filled with tiny petals <note>original Greek: πετάλοις (*petalis*)</note>. When these have faded, the seeds are perfected, wrapped in down <note>original: *pappo*</note> in the manner of Safflower <note>original: *Cnici*</note>, but much smaller and compressed, ash-colored, and bitter in taste. The root has an unpleasant flavor and a sharp smell. Dioscorides attributes these powers to this plant:

<margin>Properties.</margin>
From the juice expressed and dried from its leaves, root, and head, 
<margin>“Having extracted the juice, dry it,” not “defined.” <note>original Greek: χυλίσας, ξηραίνε (*chylisas, xeraine*), non ὁριζομένη (*horizomene*)</note></margin>
administer a weight of three obols <note>an ancient Greek unit of weight</note> with honey-water to whomever you wish. For through this, a purging draws out water and phlegm. Specifically, this purging is suitable for those suffering from shortness of breath <note>original: *Orthopnœam*</note>, the falling sickness <note>epilepsy; original: *comitialem morbum*</note>, and affections of the nerves. Pliny says: "The juice of these is expressed in Summer to loosen the bowels, as in

<margin>Hippophaë. Book 27, ch. 10.</margin>
three obols, especially for the falling sickness, and for those trembling with dropsy <note>edema; original: *hydropicis*</note>. Also against vertigo, shortness of breath, and incipient paralysis." Galen makes no mention of this plant, just as he did not the previous one. Paul of Aegina transcribed what was described by Dioscorides almost word for word and reports nothing further. More recent authors, however, although they do not use the collection of this plant's juice as handed down by Dioscorides (since it is not in common use), Pena <note>Pierre Pena, a French botanist</note> says that common folk use a decoction of the root and the crushed seed to loosen the bowels, to wear away stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>, and to provoke urine. It has such great power that, by causing an opening <note>original: *anastomosi*</note> of the renal veins or ureters, it may even draw out bloody urine. It is not the *Hippophaestum* that the author of the *History of Plants* <note>Theophrastus</note> depicts, for he himself did not wish to affirm that, since it differs enough from his own history. Dalechamp identifies this as Theophrastus’s *Myacantha* on account of its structure, because it has spines next to the leaves, and therefore belongs to the

<margin>Book 6, ch. 4.</margin>
class of "plants with thorns near the leaves" <note>original Greek: παρακανθοφύλλων (*parakanthophyllōn*)</note>, among which *Myacantha* is numbered by Theophrastus without any other distinguishing mark of the plant. But *Myacantha*, according to the authorities Dioscorides, Pliny, and others, belongs to the genus of Asparagus;

<margin>Book 2, History of Rare Plants, ch. 83.</margin>
wherefore, if one must indulge in conjecture, I would rather suggest the third species of Wild Asparagus <note>original: *Corrūdam*</note> depicted by Charles de l'Écluse <note>Carolus Clusius</note> as *Myacantha*. It has, he says, stalks a cubit long <note>about 18 inches</note>, branched, white, woody, and similar to the previous kind, bristling with firm, whitish, and downward-pointing spines placed alternately according to the irregular bending of the rods and branches. At these spines, five or six leaves emerge together from the same head, longer and softer than the leaves of the previous species, which when chewed are found to be somewhat sweet and slightly gummy. The flower hangs between the leaves from the crook of the stalks <note>the leaf axil</note> on a long, thin pedicle, turning from green to pale; then the fruit

->OF FABIO COLONNA<-

...nearly round, appearing like a three-seeded berry (although it contains a single seed, rarely two, black on the outside, white and firm inside, as in the previous examples). It is green at first, becoming red and fleshy when ripe, or containing a slow and viscous juice and pulp. It is anchored by a many-branched root, like other wild Asparagus <term>Asparagus: a genus of plants; here Colonna refers to a wild, woody variety</term>, which is long, thick, white on the inside, and yellowish on the outside. It bears several stalks <note>original: *thyrsos*</note>, which are eaten raw or cooked with oil, vinegar, and salt, just like other varieties of Asparagus. Nowhere have I observed it more abundantly than above Lisbon <note>original: *Olysiponem*</note> in rugged places next to the roads not far from the Tagus River <note>original: *Tago*</note>. However, it also grows in other parts of Portugal <note>original: *Lusitaniæ*</note>, and nearly all of Andalusia <note>original: *Bætica*</note>. I found it with ripe fruit in October, and also a few rare flowers scattered here and there.

## Apocynum of Dioscorides.

<margin>Name.</margin>
Most plants received their names from their discoverer and were subsequently called by that name; others from the place where they grow, but very many from their power or effect, among which this one should be counted. Because it kills dogs, it takes its name from dogs, called *Apocynum* <note>original Greek: *ἀπόκυνον* (*apokynon*), meaning "away from the dog" or "dog-bane"</note>. Other names also correspond to this power: *Cynanchon* <note>original Greek: *κύνἀγχον*, "dog-strangler"</note>, *Cynomoron* <note>original Greek: *κυνόμορον*, "dog-death"</note>, *Cynoctonon* <note>original Greek: *κυνοκτόνον*, "dog-killer"</note>, and *Cynocrambe* <note>original Greek: *κυνοκράμβη*, "dog-cabbage"</note>. They even added a name from its power to kill panthers, *Pardalianches* <note>original Greek: *παρδαλιαγχές*, "panther-strangler"</note>. Furthermore, so that men might be careful of it, as it is poisonous, and lest they mistakenly take it for Swallow-wort <note>original: *Asclepiade*</note> because of their similarity, Dioscorides carefully described it as he did other poisonous plants. He attributed to it a particular mark distinct from Swallow-wort: namely, a yellow juice <note>original: *luteum succum*</note>, with which he asserts it swells. His words are these: 

<margin>Description.</margin> 
"Apocynum, which some call *Cynanchon*, others *Pardalianches*, others *Cynomoron*, and others *Cynocrambe*, is a shrub having long, supple, hard-to-break rods; the leaves are similar to Ivy, but softer and sharper at the tip, heavy-smelling, somewhat sticky, and full of honey-yellow sap. The fruit is like a bean pod about a finger's length, pouch-like, in which there are small, hard, black seeds." <note>original Greek: *Α᾿πόκυνον... σπερμάτια σκληρά, σμικρά, μέλανα*</note> *Apocynum* or *Dog-cabbage* is a shrub having long, flexible branches—in some versions it reads "foul-smelling" <note>original Greek: *δυσώδεις* (*dysodeis*)</note>—resistant to breaking. The leaves are similar to Ivy, but softer and sharper at the top, foul-smelling, somewhat viscous, and filled with honey-colored juice. The fruit is like a bean <margin>Book 24, ch. 11.</margin> pod, nearly a finger's length, appearing as a little pouch, in which the seeds are (the text of Marcellus <note>Marcellus Virgilius Adriani, a 15th-century translator of Dioscorides</note> has "white" <note>original Greek: *λευκά* (*leuka*)</note>, that is, white), small, hard, and black. Pliny...

### THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

Pliny, indeed, describes it as a shrub with the leaf of **Ivy** <note>original Latin: *Hedera*</note>, though softer, and with shorter vines, a sharp, divided, and downy seed, and a heavy odor. He mentions nothing of its yellow sap; this is not surprising, for he was occupied with other more serious matters. Thus, Dalechamps <note>Jacques Daléchamps (1513–1588), a French physician and botanist</note> and Lobel <note>Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist</note>, having made no trial of its properties, wrote that this plant was a species of **Asclepias** <note>Milkweed</note> because it was similar to it, except that it had yellow sap and dark flowers. Why should it not rather be named **Apocynum** <note>Dogbane</note>, since it also resembles other types of Dogbane in appearance, and perhaps is the same as the non-creeping Dogbane—differing only in the color of the flowers—and possesses yellow sap? Human nature is indeed unfortunate, being subject to the errors of others, through which it happens that life is exchanged for death.

<margin>Physical Form.</margin>
The form of this plant is as follows: It relies on a fibrous root not unlike **Asclepias**. It has flexible, smooth, long vines that climb any support or other nearby object no less than the other species of Dogbane, extending to a length of four cubits <note>about 6 feet or 1.8 meters</note> or more. It has ivy-like leaves, soft, green, and smooth, somewhat similar to **Asclepias** but pointed at the tip. Its flowers are similar to the common creeping Dogbane, but of a dark color, inclining slightly toward purple, which is why it is said to have a black flower. It produces pods and downy seeds like the other common Dogbane, compressed and black, in the month of August. It swells with yellow sap, which is unpleasant, as is the whole plant.

<margin>Location.</margin>
Lobel records that this grows on the hills of the territory of Montpellier near Frontignan under the name of "Black Asclepias," though I have already demonstrated whether that name was correctly applied.

<margin>Properties.</margin>
Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and pharmacologist (c. 40–90 AD)</note> records that this plant has certain powers: its leaves, kneaded with fat and formed into loaves, kill **dogs, wolves, foxes, and panthers** when cast before them. For it immediately relaxes <note>or paralyzes</note> their hip joints. In Pliny, however, we read: *Thus for those with pleurisy and all pains of the side, the seed of Dogbane is taken in water. The shrub etc. kills dogs and all four-legged animals when given in food.* From these accounts, it would seem that the plant is healthy for humans but a poison for quadrupeds.

<margin>Book 6, On the properties of simple medicines.</margin>
Galen <note>The influential Roman physician and philosopher (129–c. 216 AD)</note>, however, seems to contradict this, for he says: *Some call it Apocynum or Dog-cabbage [Cynocrambe]. Some call it Dog-death [Cynomorum], since it kills dogs very swiftly, just as Wolf-bane [Lycoctonum] kills wolves. It is also a poison to humans; a herb that smells very heavy: therefore it is entirely and vigorously hot, yet it does not dry in the same proportion. Thus, when applied as a poultice, it has the power of dispersing [humors].* Some interpret Pliny not unreasonably, suggesting that the seed of Dogbane in water should be understood as a topical application, not taken internally.

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna critiques earlier botanists for misidentifying a species of Dogbane (Apocynum) as Milkweed (Asclepias) and discusses its extreme toxicity to animals and potential danger to humans.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the properties of Apocynum (Dogbane), focusing on a philological debate regarding how the plant was administered in ancient texts. It then begins a new chapter on "Phu," which we identify today as Valerian, praising the clarity of Dioscorides' original description compared to modern confusion.</meta>

PLANT HISTORY, PAGE 113

For since it possesses the property of dispersing <note>original Latin: *digerendi*, referring to the medicinal power to dissolve or scatter morbid humors</note> fluids, it would be able to heal pains of the side, and indeed if it should be said of all such pains; but certainly Pliny seems to understand it as being taken internally, since he speaks in the same manner as the authors before him, saying "Thus Saturn," etc. However, this must be left to those who perform experiments. Finally, it must be observed that the writer of the *Emblems* <note>Likely referring to Andrea Alciati (1492–1550), whose *Emblemata* often included botanical and moral lessons</note> and the Author of the *History of Plants* who followed him have handed down a tradition that, in my judgment, is not correct. For they read the text of Dioscorides in this way: *These leaves made into bread with flour.* In the Greek, however, it is written: ταύτης τὰ φύλλα σὺν στέατι ἀρτοποιηθέντα <note>translated as: "the leaves of this [plant] made into a loaf with fat"</note>. Yet the Author of the *History of Plants* even attempts to "correct" the text by following the Latin interpretation mentioned above, and decides that instead of *steati* <note>fat or tallow</note>, one should read *saiti* <note>flour or meal</note>. To these men, it must be replied that they are perverters of the text and poor interpreters. For the text of Dioscorides always—and I have many editions from various places—reads σὺν στέατι (*syn steati*), that is, "with fat." The word στέαρ, στέατος (*stear, steatos*) is interpreted as "fat" or "tallow"; it is certainly not σαιτί (*saiti*), from σαὶς σαιτός (*sais, saitos*), which signifies flour kneaded with water. Furthermore, one must consider that dogs would not so easily want to eat the heavy-smelling leaves of Dogbane mixed with flour unless they were dying of hunger. But, enticed by fat, even if they do not crave food, they will eat the leaves mixed into it, deceived by the richness. For it is neither the flour nor the fat that kills—which otherwise nourish dogs—but the leaves of the Dogbane. So that the dog might devour many of them greedily, they must be mixed with something pleasing to the dog, such as fat, and thus shaped into little balls or cakes in the manner of bread. In my opinion, this is the correct view.

## *Phu of Dioscorides.*
<note>Phu is the ancient name for Valerian, likely *Valeriana officinalis* or *Valeriana phu*</note>

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'N' featuring floral motifs and scrolls within a square frame.</image-desc>
Dioscorides could not have described the plant *Phu* with more specific marks, both general and particular—from the root all the way to the flowers. Indeed, if it had pleased [God, the Best and Greatest] that he had handed down the history of other plants as accurately in all his descriptions, there would surely be no plant that remained unknown to us. I do not know how it is that no great man has yet noticed his description, which is so clear, nor this famous plant, whose root must be mixed into the composition of **Theriac** <note>a famous ancient "universal" medicinal paste used as an antidote to poison and for many ailments</note> in no small dose. Indeed, what is worse, everyone with one voice...

<note>This illustration is a primary example of Fabio Colonna's contribution to botany. In his 1592 work *Phytobasanos* (The Touchstone of Plants), Colonna used copperplate etchings rather than woodcuts. This allowed for much finer detail in the depiction of the root structures, leaf margins, and floral anatomy, which was essential for correctly identifying species that had been confused since antiquity. The plant shown is the Great Valerian, characterized by its "pinnate" leaves (leaflets arranged like a feather) and its thick, horizontal rhizome.</note>

# ACCOUNTS OF SEVERAL PLANTS

The plant called "Greater Valerian" by certain authors is used by them in place of Dioscorides’ <term>Phu</term> <note>The ancient Greek name for Valerian, *Valeriana officinalis*.</note>. They not only believe it matches the description of Dioscorides and is the genuine species, but they even wish to ultimately amend his text in several places to fit the form of their "Greater Valerian." They are exceedingly stubborn, judging those who deny that it is Dioscorides’ *Phu* as being in error. At the same time, they reject the so-called "Lesser Valerian" or "Wild Valerian." They claim that even if its leaves match Dioscorides’ *Phu*, it does not match in its root and other characteristics—not without grave error on their part. This must be attributed to their negligence in comparing the text of Dioscorides with the plant they propose; or rather, it must be said that they have failed to understand Dioscorides’ words, let alone his meaning in this passage, as will be made clear from what follows.

For Dioscorides says: <margin>Book 1. Name. Description.</margin> "Phu, which some also call Wild Nard, grows in <term>Pontus</term> <note>A region on the southern coast of the Black Sea.</note>. It has leaves similar to <term>Elaphoboscum</term> <note>Literally "deer-fodder"; likely a species of wild parsnip (*Pastinaca sativa*).</note> or <term>Hipposelinum</term> <note>Literally "horse-parsley"; likely *Smyrnium olusatrum*.</note>. It has a stem a cubit high or more, smooth, tender, purplish, hollow in the middle, and divided by joints. The flowers are similar to those of the Narcissus, but smaller and more delicate, purplish from a whitish base. The root, however, is about the thickness of a little finger at the top, and has oblique rootlets like a rush or black hellebore, tangled together, yellowish, fragrant, and recalling Nard in its scent, though with a certain heavy, foul odor." <note>original Greek: Φοῦ, οἱ δὲ καὶ τοῦτο ἀγρίαν νάρδον καλοῦσι... μετά τινος βάρους βρωμώδους. original Latin: *Phù, quod & ipsum aliqui syluestrem Nardum appellant... Nardum cū odoris grauitate quadam referentes.*</note>

How much the plant called "Lesser Valerian" (or Wild Valerian)—which I have proposed and which they have rejected—responds to this description, and how far the one they have accepted is from the truth, will be made clear in the following pages.

<margin>Form.</margin> This plant indeed has leaves—so that its history matches Dioscorides’ depiction—similar to *Hipposelinum*, but in their arrangement similar to *Elaphoboscum*. However, it has more leaflets on both sides arranged <term>pinnately</term> <note>Arranged like a feather, with leaflets on opposite sides of a central stalk.</note>, attached to a long opposite petiole, as in <term>Water Parsnip</term> <note>original: *Sio aquatico* (*Sium latifolium* or *angustifolium*).</note>. They are more jagged around the edges, though sometimes divided by few or no incisions, and they lean toward the ground. The stem is a cubit or even more in height, and with years and cultivation it rises to the height of a man; it is round, but sometimes hairy only at the very bottom, and thereafter entirely smooth, striated, hollow, tender, and purplish, as are also the leaf stalks near the roots. It is divided by joints,

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

on each of which [the joints] two rows of leaves surround the stem, positioned opposite one another, and occasionally even three rows, as is seen in the leaves of the **Yellow Loosestrife** <note>original: *Lysimachiæ luteæ*</note>. At the very top, it has many flowers clustered together in an <term>umbel</term> <note>An umbrella-shaped cluster where all flower stalks radiate from a single point.</note>, similar to **Mountain Nard** with its bulbous root and the so-called **Greater Valerian**. These flowers are quite small, oblong, and hollow, ending in five small petals and emerging from individual oval vessels shaped like a flattened clay oil flask. When the flowers fall away, the "lips" of these vessels swell up and finally dissolve into a downy fluff that opens in a circle. These small vessels, which are the seeds themselves, fall off when dry and fly away on that downy fluff emerging from the lips, just as other <term>pappose</term> <note>Seeds with a tuft of hair or down, like a dandelion, designed for wind dispersal.</note> seeds do. 

The color of the flowers turns from white to purple. In shape, they seem to imitate the flowers of the **Narcissus**; for Narcissus flowers also have that swelling at their base where the seed is contained, and then they end in five or six thinner petals. Nothing more is required for this comparison to Narcissus flowers, for Dioscorides recorded that it was the *shape* of the flowers, not their *placement*, that was similar to the Narcissus. These flowers are more delicate and much smaller, and on this point everyone agrees—and it is the truth—that the corrupted text of Dioscorides should be corrected: instead of "larger" <note>original: μείζω (*meizō*)</note>, "smaller" <note>original: μικρότερα (*mikrotera*)</note> should be read. 

In its upper part (from which the stem breaks out), the plant has a root as thick as a little finger. Many fleshy fibers share the same starting point, like a **Rush** or **Black Hellebore**, to which Dioscorides compares them. (The roots of both types of Hellebore, as Dioscorides described, are seen hanging from a certain small head similar to an onion; he noted that they were "roots" <note>original: ῥίζας (*rhizas*)</note>, and so on, "as if hanging from a certain small onion-like head" <note>original: οἱονεὶ ἀπό τινος κεφαλίου κρομμυώδους ἠρτημέναι</note>). Other roots, arranged in rows of three or four, one above the other, lean obliquely and surround the plant; those seen resting on others divide the circle of the root into five or more parts, so that they represent a divided star. 

They are always found just under the earth, leaning laterally and spreading themselves annually by creeping obliquely; the new root is folded over the older one, as is also observed in Hellebore. Therefore, if those Greek words—"the root at the top is about the thickness of a little finger: it has side-roots growing from it, just like a rush or black hellebore, intertwined with each other" <note>original: ῥίζα δὲ ἡ μὲν ἀνωτέρω, περὶ δακτύλου μικροῦ τὸ πάχος : πλάγια δὲ ἐπιῤῥίζια ἔχει καθάπερ σχοῖνος, ἢ μέλας ἐλλέβορος, ἀλλήλοις ἐπιπεπλεγμένα</note>, and so on—are considered, they will seem to describe this and the root of no other plant. In color they are somewhat pale, but more yellow when dry, and fragrant like Nard, but with a certain goat-like rankness <note>The text breaks off here at the catchword "gra-", likely for "graveolentiam" or "gravitatem," referring to a heavy or pungent smell.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical examination of Valerian, specifically debating the structure of its roots.
