### THE HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

...carrying a mixed heaviness, from which it obtained the name **Phu** <note>The Greek name for Valerian, *phu*, is said to be an onomatopoeia for a sound of disgust, similar to "phew" or "ugh," reacting to the plant's strong, often unpleasant smell.</note>: for anyone shrinking from the heaviness of the scent immediately expresses the sound "Phu" by natural instinct. Nor can it be understood that the upper part of the root is in some way slanted in the manner of **Butcher's Broom** <term>Ruscus</term> <note>A low-growing evergreen shrub with stiff, prickly leaves.</note>, with whose root **Phu** is considered to be adulterated <note>meaning "counterfeited" or "mixed with a cheaper substitute"</note> in the writings of **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist, author of *De Materia Medica*.</note>; for the response is that, if it had been similar to Butcher's Broom in its entirety, Dioscorides would not have made it similar to a **Rush** and **Hellebore**, in which the slanted fibers are produced from a single starting point, as if from a head. Rather, he would have said it was slanted and jointed in the manner of Butcher's Broom (given that the older root of Butcher's Broom is such). Likewise, it is not the upper part of the root in **Phu** that is slanted, but only the fibers that are carried sideways through the earth, and the whole root is more similar to the root of a Rush and Hellebore, as I have already said. Indeed, the root of **Phu** can more easily be adulterated with a young root of Butcher's Broom, since its form differs less from the **Phu** root; though it does differ because the fibers of Butcher's Broom are very difficult to break and reduce to powder, which is the opposite in the **Phu** root, which is very easily broken and crushed. 

However much the root of Butcher's Broom—which must be very old to rival the root of the so-called **Greater Valerian**—differs in color (for it is white, not yellow), in its joints, and in the multitude of its "eyes" <note>Buds or growth points on a tuber or rhizome.</note>, by which it is not produced in a straight line like the said Valerian, but represents the line of a "spiked" work, anyone will be able to recognize this by a visual inspection of the roots of both plants. I do not say this shrinks from the description of Dioscorides: for when he wished to designate a slanted, jointed root resting on the top of the turf—such as the root of **Sweet Flag** <term>Acorus</term> and **Asarabacca** <term>Asarum</term> and similar plants—he wrote <term>slanted root</term> <note>original Greek: *ῥίζαν πλαγίαν* (*rhizan plagian*)</note>, not <term>slanted rootlets</term> <note>original Greek: *ἐπιῤῥίζια πλαγία* (*epirrhizia plagia*)</note>, which at other times he called "roots." 

Therefore, the opinion of those who thought the top of the **Phu** root ought to be spread out sideways must be rejected; they were perhaps moved by the text of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Naturalis Historia*.</note>, who said, <margin><unclear>Pliny book</unclear> <unclear>25. ch. 5.</unclear></margin> *with a slanted, hairy root, imitating the feet of birds.* They say the upper part of the root ought to be slanted, to whom I respond that, in the opinion of Dioscorides, the "hairs"—meaning the <term>root-fibers</term> <note>original Greek: *ἐπιῤῥίζια* (*epirrhizia*)</note>, not the primary root—ought to be spread sideways. And thus, if both the upper part of the root and the fibers were slanted, it could draw no nourishment from the earth, <margin>**Book 12** **Ch. 12.**</margin> since both parts would be carried above the ground; for the slanted and upper part of the so-called **Greater Valerian** always sprouts above the earth, and if it is buried, it immediately withers. Nor would they be able to offer any similarity to the feet of birds. Therefore Pliny, because he left a shorter description of the **Phu** plant, [drew] from Dioscorides...

<meta>catchword: de,</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna argues that the root of Valerian (Phu) is often misunderstood; he distinguishes between the main rootstock and its lateral fibers to reconcile conflicting descriptions by ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s detailed botanical argument regarding the identification of the plant "Phu" (Valerian). # BY FABIO COLONNA

<margin>Book 4, chapter on Black Hellebore</margin>
...from which it is believed to have been transcribed, it must be interpreted in the same way: namely, that the entire root is understood to be slanted, and that same root is divided into fibers. Indeed, **Pliny** <note>A Roman author and naturalist (23–79 AD) who wrote the monumental *Naturalis Historia*.</note> understands "shaggy" <note>original: *villos*</note> to mean thick fibers, not only in this passage but also in his description of **Astragalus** <term>Milk-vetch</term>, which he said had a "shaggy" root, and elsewhere. Indeed, it has no single primary root, but all the fibers are themselves roots, which **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist whose *De Materia Medica* was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years.</note> correctly expressed with the word "root-growth" <note>original: ἐπιῤῥιζία (*epirrhizia*)</note>, and which he said were "sideways" <note>original: πλάγια (*plagia*)</note>, just as can be seen in the plant proposed by me. He did not mean some other slanted part of the root; from this, it is perceived that all those fibers are the roots themselves. One of them is no more primary than another, but all of them together make up the root, as in **Hellebore** <note>A genus of perennial flowering plants; in antiquity, it was famous as a powerful (and dangerous) purgative for treating madness.</note> and other similar plants, and for that reason, he named it "root-growth." This is recognized because if you remove as many of those little roots as you wish, you will bring no harm to the plant; indeed, if you leave but a single one, the plant will not fade away because of it, but will sprout again. I add this further point, which I have observed many times in this plant: when a single one of those rootlets is planted, leaves emerge after a few days, and then it propagates itself widely and establishes a "root-growth," for they are always carried slantedly, putting out buds and propagating themselves in this way every year; they are indeed very long-lived. The young root of this plant seems to represent **bird feet** <note>original: *auium pedes*</note> quite beautifully, <insert>even if the author of *The History of Plants* wanted to correct Pliny, suggesting that what he expressed in Latin as "bird feet" was a version of a corrupted Greek text, "the feet of birds" <note>original: τοὺς τῶν ὀρνίθων πόδας</note>; he judges that it should be corrected and replaced with **Melampodion** <note>A name for Black Hellebore.</note> instead of "bird feet." In Latin, it is called Melampodion because the shepherd **Melampus** <note>A legendary healer in Greek mythology.</note> was the first to discover its purging power, by which he restored the daughters of **Prœtus** <note>The King of Argos; his daughters were struck with madness, and Melampus cured them using Hellebore and rituals.</note>, who were raving mad, to their former health, as Dioscorides also relates; and thus it will be similar to Hellebore, supporting my opinion:</insert> for you will find it has four or five fleshy fibers, slanted by nature, which, with their fingers spread out, represent the feet of birds. If anyone studious of the truth wishes to see this, he will truly recognize it in the young root of the plant proposed by me, and even in the older one, though in that one he will find more fibers arranged in the same order. This is something he will by no means find in the root of the **Valerian** accepted by others, even if it were artificially twisted, nor could it be persuaded by any version of Dioscorides’ text. Therefore, having removed the argument of the root, by which everyone has hitherto been deceived, if the remaining

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL PLANTS

<margin><unclear>Class 1, Part 2</unclear><br/><unclear>... 1. 2</unclear></margin>
...the parts of **Valerian** <term>A perennial flowering plant, historically used as a sedative.</term> accepted by these authors will seem to correspond much less to the text of **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note>. Having now made and rejected a comparison of the root with his text, I come to the leaves. These have no similarity with **Alexanders** <term>original: *Olusatrum*; a wild, celery-like pot herb.</term> or **Wild Parsnip** <term>original: *Elaphoboscum*.</term>; rather, they perfectly reflect **Mountain Nard** <note>A plant in the Valerian family (*Valeriana montana*).</note> and the leaves of **Eryngo** <note>Sea Holly.</note>, as well as the entire habit of the plant. If Dioscorides had wished to describe a plant with leaves similar to Mountain Nard, he would not have said they were like Wild Parsnip, nor would he have said it differed only by a root that is not bulbous, but slanted and jointed. For the first leaves are oblong, in the shape of **Plantain**, and not jagged; <margin><unclear>HIST</unclear><br/><unclear>... 3</unclear></margin> however, those that cling to the stem are divided into deep notches—not unlike **Rocket** <note>original: *Eruca*.</note> or the plant commonly called **Scabious** <note>A plant used historically to treat skin conditions.</note> and Eryngo—as is seen in Mountain Nard. For the leaves do not cling to the stalk in opposite winged pairs from the beginning, before the eruption of the stem (which it puts forth late, after a year or even <margin><unclear>...st i u</unclear></margin> two), as they do in Wild Parsnip and **Water Parsnip** <term>original: *Sio aquatico*.</term>, which in Dioscorides’ judgment is similar in its leaves to **Horse-parsley** <term>original: *Hipposelino*.</term>. But the leaves of the plant proposed by me do approach the form of Horse-parsley, being divided from the start into lobes that are serrated around the edges, like the leaves of Horse-parsley and Wild Parsnip. I wonder with what eyes or what mind they saw these things and passed them down to us. <margin><unclear>cl. 3. di. 3</unclear><br/><unclear>... m ... i</unclear><br/><unclear>... s. home</unclear></margin> Neither I nor my friends have ever happened to see a reddish stem, though we have inspected many plants; it always remains the ashen color of **Cabbage** <note>original: *Brassica*.</note> and Mountain Nard. This is the opposite of the stem in the plant proposed by me, which always purples from a green base all the way up to the flowers. Likewise, the flowers of that other plant do not turn purple like the one I described, but are either paler or not colored at all. Therefore, I would judge that plant commonly called the **Greater Valerian** to be a species of non-bulbous Mountain Nard, and perhaps the same as the **Hirculus** <term>literally "Little Goat," referring to its smell.</term>, with a root more like **Celtic Nard**. Indeed, if anyone considers a young specimen of that plant, which is small and stemless, they will not disagree that it is similar to Celtic Nard, though smaller and not as bitter; nor does it give off a scent like true Celtic Nard, but rather something "goat-like," just as this wild Valerian also smells, and with a paler color. This seems to respond well enough to the account handed down by Dioscorides, for he says:

> *It is also adulterated once pulled up*—some read "when placed with it"—*by a similar herb; <margin>Book 1, chapter [7]</margin> which they call **Hircus** <note>The Goat.</note> because of the rankness of its foul odor. Yet the recognition of **Celtic Nard** is easy. For it is a stemless herb, whiter, with leaves that are less oblong, and it has neither a bitter root nor one smelling of spice.*

<meta>catchword: things,</meta>

<summary>Colonna argues that the common "Greater Valerian" is misidentified, as its leaf and stem characteristics do not match Dioscorides' ancient descriptions of "Phu." He suggests it is actually a relative of Mountain Nard or the foul-smelling "Hirculus" used to adulterate more expensive herbs.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

<margin>Book 12, Chapter 12.</margin>
...it truly possesses **Celtic Nard** <term>A fragrant Alpine plant related to valerian.</term>. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*.</note> states that an herb called **Hirculus** <note>Latin for "little goat," named for its pungent, goat-like smell.</note> always grows alongside Gallic Nard; it is frequently used to adulterate the Nard because of its heavy scent and similarity. It differs in that it lacks a small stalk, has smaller leaves, and roots that are neither bitter nor fragrant. Indeed, such a small and imperfect plant cannot possess the bitterness or scent of a larger, perfected plant. Having set these points forth, the plant I have proposed must hereafter be called the true and genuine **Phu** <term>The ancient Greek name for Valerian, used by Dioscorides.</term> of Dioscorides and used in medications. The other plant, which was first falsely marked with that name by others, should be reserved for other uses, since I have now demonstrated—contrary to the opinion of everyone else—that it is not that plant, but the one I have proposed, which better corresponds to the description given by **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist.</note>.

<margin>Season.</margin>
It flowers in the month of May and reaches completion in July.

<margin>Location.</margin>
It is frequent here in mountains and hills that are not arid, but somewhat shady, and even in marshy areas and meadows.

<margin>Properties.</margin>
The smell and taste of this plant's root far surpasses the so-called **Great Valerian**: and as Dioscorides says: *It has the power of warming and provoking urine when drunk as a dry powder; but a decoction of it can also achieve the same result. It also helps with pain in the side, brings on menstruation, and is mixed into antidotes.*

<margin>Book 8, On the Properties of Simple Medicines.</margin>
**Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note> truly states: *Phu, the root of this herb is somewhat aromatic, similar in power to Nard: but nevertheless weaker for most things; it moves urine more than Indian or Syrian Nard, just like the Gallic variety.*

Recent authors, however, attribute these powers to it under the name of **Wild Valerian**. It brings aid to those suffering from ruptures, torn muscles, or those who have fallen from a height. The leaves are boiled down to treat canker sores and mouth ulcers. It is likewise included in antidotes. In addition to these powers attributed to the plant, I add this specific property, which has long been tested in many people and in myself: that a half-spoonful of the powder from the root of this plant—grown wild and uprooted before it produces a stem—taken once or twice with wine, water, milk, or any other suitable liquid (depending on the patient’s convenience and age), frees those seized by **Epilepsy** <term>Historically called the "falling sickness."</term>. I ordered this to be given to children, and especially infants who easily suffer from this disease, to be drunk with milk, and I gave it as a gift to friends. They then—having first glorified the Divine favor—affirmed that health was restored to them by the powder of this plant. This has also been effective for several other adults.

<summary>Fabio Colonna identifies the "true" Valerian (Phu) of the ancients, distinguishing it from lookalikes like Hirculus, and provides a personal account of using its root powder to successfully treat epilepsy.</summary>
<keywords>Valerian, Phu, Dioscorides, Galen, Pliny the Elder, Epilepsy treatment, botanical classification, medicinal roots</keywords>

## Concerning the Guitarfish, or Squatinaray

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'E' featuring leafy vines and floral patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

The name "Rhinobatos" <note>Guitarfish</note> is composed of two names, namely *Rhine* <note>original Greek: Ῥίνη, referring to the angel shark or monkfish</note> and *Batos* <note>original Greek: Βάτος, referring to a ray or skate</note>. Gaza <note>Theodore Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a Byzantine humanist whose Latin translations of Aristotle's zoological works became standard in the Renaissance.</note> translated this into Latin as "Squatinoraia," because it is believed to originate from a cross between a *Squatina* <note>Angel shark</note> and a *Raia* <note>Ray</note>, as Aristotle asserts. His words are these: "Of the other fish, none have been seen mating outside of their own kind. The angel shark alone, however, seems to do this with the ray. For there is a certain fish called the Guitarfish. It has the head and front parts of a ray, but the hind parts of an angel shark, as if being born from both of these fish."

<margin>Book 6, chapter 11 of the *History of Animals*</margin>
*Indeed, no one has seen other fish mate besides those of the same kind. But the angel shark alone, and the ray, seem to do so. There is, in any case, a certain fish called the Guitarfish. For it has the head and foreparts of a ray, but the hind parts of an angel shark, as if it draws its origin from both.* Pliny transcribed this text almost word for word; for he says:

<margin>Book 9, chapter 51</margin>
*Different kinds of fish do not mate except for the angel shark and the ray, from which is born a creature similar to a ray in its forepart, and among the Greeks it takes a name composed of both.* Nor have I found anything about it in other authors, except in the same Aristotle, where he speaks of the conjunctions of different species as follows: "Among marine creatures nothing worthy of mention has been seen, but those called Guitarfish seem most of all to be born from the mating of an angel shark and a ray."

<margin>Book 2 of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 5</margin>
*In marine creatures nothing worthy of remembrance has been seen, but those called Guitarfish seem most likely to be produced from the joining of an angel shark and a ray.* From these passages, I conclude that Aristotle is affirming the union of the angel shark and the ray rather than reporting uncertain rumors about them. I do not know why Rondelet <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a premier Renaissance ichthyologist. He was skeptical of the hybrid theory because he had never personally seen the fish.</note>, a man of such great learning, could think that neither Aristotle nor Pliny ever saw this fish—simply because neither he himself (despite applying the greatest diligence) nor any fishermen ever happened to see it. He thinks the fish is entirely fictitious and does not exist in reality. For this reason, he tries to twist Aristotle's words, saying: *“He says the angel shark 'seems' to mate with the ray; for indeed, Herodotus, Theophrastus, and Dioscorides use this manner of speaking—and similar phrases like 'as they say' or 'it is believed'—when they mean to hold something as uncertain.”* To the first point I reply: Aristotle easily could have seen the Guitarfish, since he records its specific characteristics, saying, "For there is a certain..."

<summary>This page discusses the etymology and classical origins of the "Rhinobatus" (guitarfish), arguing against Renaissance skeptics like Rondelet by citing Aristotle and Pliny's descriptions of it as a hybrid between an angel shark and a ray.</summary>
<keywords>Rhinobatus, Guitarfish, Aristotle, Pliny the Elder, Rondelet, Squatinoraia, hybrid fish, ichthyology, angel shark, ray</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and scientific defense of identifying the Rhinobatos (Guitarfish) as a hybrid of a ray and an angel shark, responding to critics like Rondelet by analyzing the specific vocabulary used by Aristotle and Dioscorides.</meta>

->OF FABIO COLONNA<-

<margin><unclear>Dioscorides, Book 4, Chapter 11</unclear></margin> ...the fish which is called the Rhinobatos <note>Guitarfish</note>, for it has the head <note>original Greek: ἰχθύς ὃς καλεῖται Ῥινόβατος, ἔχει γὰρ τὴν μὲν κεφαλὴν; "the fish which is called Rhinobatos, for it has the head..."</note>, &c. and likewise Pliny, *from which is born a creature similar in its front part to a Ray, &c.* In these words, there is nothing that can be called into doubt. To the second point, I say that not only Aristotle, but other Greek Authors (whose examples are widely available), always use the word *dokei* <note>original Greek: δοκεῖ; "it seems" or "it is thought"</note> in matters of certainty. Dioscorides, for example, in many chapters—and especially in the chapter on *Dorycnium* <note>A type of plant, possibly Convolvulus dorycnium</note>—uses the word *dokei* in the preceding sentence to signify certain facts and what he himself judges to be true. He then uses *phasi* <note>original Greek: φασὶ; "they say"</note> in the following sentence for uncertain things. There would be no need to use both if he intended them to mean the same thing, especially in such short sentences where the manner of speaking is identical. I confirm this from the interpretation of the most learned Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist known for his Latin translations of Dioscorides.</note>, who, seeing that this word *dokei* has such weight in Greek, clearly translated it as an established fact. Therefore, the Greek use of the word *dokei* differs from the Latin *ferunt* (they report), *aiunt* (they say), and similar terms, since the latter suggest uncertainty, while the former presents certainty. 

Added to this is the other text of Aristotle cited in the second place, where after the word *dokousi* <note>original Greek: δοκοῦσι; "they seem"</note>, he places the adverb *malista* <note>original Greek: μάλιστα; "most especially"</note>, which is translated as *most of all, above all*, and *in a greater measure*. Everyone uses these terms when something certain is to be said, and Aristotle himself would not have used such a way of writing if he had felt otherwise about this fish or held the matter to be uncertain. And since he himself did not see the 
<margin>Book 1 of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 6</margin>
mating of the fish—which he records is carried out and finished very swiftly, and that the true mating of egg-laying fish is rarely seen—and since it would be difficult to witness the 
<margin>Book 5 of the *History of Animals*, chapter 5</margin>
mating of different fish, which, as he himself testifies, happens rarely, he therefore said, *nothing noteworthy has been seen* <note>original Greek: οὐδὲν ἀξιόλογον ἑώραται</note>. But because he believed it could happen by nature, he said, *they seem most especially* <note>original Greek: δοκοῦσι δὲ μάλιστα</note>, &c., which he interpreted as *they are believed* <note>original Latin: creduntur</note>. 
<margin>Book 2 of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 5</margin>
Indeed, *to believe* means to hold a thing not seen as certain, which happens when we give our trust to someone speaking the truth, or when we think it is certain for some reason. 

But I return to the *Rhinobatos* <note>Guitarfish</note>. It will be difficult to lead the minds of the more learned to this conclusion of Rondelet <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a famous French physician and naturalist who doubted the hybrid origin of the guitarfish.</note>: *If the Angel Shark and the Ray mate for this reason, so that a fish is produced which is named Rhinobatos, similar to both...* judging that reasoning to be weak and stretching it toward the impossible, he concludes by false logic <note>original Greek: παραλογιζόυμενος; "reasoning falsely"</note>, *we shall say that both Hippocentaurs and Tragelaphs <note>Mythical creatures: the Hippocentaur is half-man, half-horse; the Tragelaph is a "goat-stag."</note> can exist if a Centaur is mixed with a Mare, and a Goat with a Doe.* Let the more learned judge how absurd this argument is: for it would be a different matter entirely if a Hippocentaur were actually found and one were then to say it was born from a Centaur and a Mare.

<meta>catchword: born,</meta>

<summary>In this page, Colonna defends Aristotle’s account of the guitarfish as a hybrid by performing a detailed philological analysis of Greek verbs, arguing that classical authors used "it seems" to denote scientific certainty. He specifically rebuts the naturalist Rondelet, who had dismissed the hybrid theory by comparing it to the existence of mythological monsters.</summary>
<keywords>Guitarfish, Rhinobatos, Aristotle, philology, hybrid species, Guillaume Rondelet, Jean Ruel, Greek translation, ichthyology, Squatina, Raia</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s defense of the Rhinobatos (Guitarfish) as a hybrid species. Having previously discussed the philological evidence in the works of Aristotle and Dioscorides, he now addresses biological objections regarding whether such different species can actually interbreed.</meta>

->HISTORY OF SOME FISHES<-

...to do one thing, the other the opposite; for then the argument proceeds, and is probable, when it is drawn from some existing thing. <margin>Book 3, *On the Use of the Parts*, chapter 1</margin> He confirms his conclusion by the testimony of Galen, saying: it cannot happen according to nature that bodies so different are mixed, etc. Nor, if diverse forms of living creatures—though not entirely dissimilar—mate, do they produce an animal that is like this parent in one part and that one in another; rather, it is similar to only one of the parents, or becomes a certain third thing. For example, when a Donkey mates with a Mare, a Mule is born, an animal similar to neither in every respect. If a Wolf mates with a Dog or a Fox, the offspring is similar to only one of them. To these points I respond: Aristotle did not say, as he [the opponent] suggests, that bodies so different and fictitious could be joined—such as a Centaur with a Mare—but rather a Ray with an Angel Shark. These seem to have a certain <term>sympathēian <note>From the Greek συμπάθεια (sympatheia), meaning a natural affinity or correspondence between two things.</note></term> and similarity between them, since they are from the same genus of Cartilaginous fish. Aside from the mouth and the narrower habit of the body (by which the Angel Shark differs from the Ray), both are endowed with "wings" <note>Colonna refers to the large, wing-like pectoral fins of rays and angel sharks.</note>, and two appendages behind them, and two small fins on the tail. Nor do they differ so much in length as to prevent a Ray from mating with a female Angel Shark by the rubbing of their underparts, since both (and the rest of the Cartilaginous genus) are egg-laying and mate in the same way, as the same Aristotle says; <margin>Book 5, chapter 5, *On the History of Animals*</margin> for they swim in the same manner, as he also records: <note>original Greek: οἱ δὲ Βάτοι, καὶ τὰ τοιαῦτα ἀντὶ πτερύγιων τῷ ἐσχάτῳ πλάτει νέουσι.</note> *Rays and similar fish swim by the extreme width of their bodies instead of using fins.* <margin>Book 4, *On the Parts of Animals*, chapter 13</margin> Add to this what he cites from Galen: namely, that a Mule is born an animal similar to neither parent in all respects. He does not, therefore, exclude the possibility that the offspring could be similar to both in some part of the body, or in its behavior and nature, as can be said of the Mule and other animals born from different species. This is clear to anyone practiced in searching out and contemplating natural things. Indeed, I add something which I would consider a wonder and incredible if I had heard it from others; but because Caterina Pellegrina—my grandmother and most dear to me, a woman of such authority, faith, and learning—narrated it, I am persuaded it is certain. For she says that during the life of her father, Hieronymo <note>Jerome</note>, the Governor of Abella <note>Avella, a town in Italy.</note> and other towns and cities, there was found in that town in the year of our salvation 1528, and brought to him, a monstrous animal born from a Sow. Its head was in every way similar to a human head, but the rest of the body was like its mother. When it was cast into doubt whether a swineherd had consorted with her, he ordered an inquiry into the matter at...

<meta>The catchword "iu-" likely refers to "iustitiam" (justice) or "iudicium" (judgment/legal proceeding), indicating that the next page describes the legal or official investigation into this "monstrous" birth.</meta>

<summary>Colonna argues that the Guitarfish is a natural hybrid of a Ray and an Angel Shark based on their shared anatomical features and mating habits. He supports the possibility of interspecies offspring with a family anecdote about a pig born with a human-like head in 1528.</summary>

### BY FABIO COLONNA

...to be held by a judge. And so the swineherd, brought before the judge, confessed to having behaved with her <term>effeminately <note>Original Greek: θηλυωδῶς (thelyōdōs). In this context, it refers to engaging in unnatural or animalistic intercourse.</note></term>, and was found guilty of the crime. 
<margin><unclear>History of Animals, book 6, chapter 11.</unclear></margin>
I believe this happened by divine judgment, so that he who committed such a great and execrable crime should not go unpunished; witnesses of the event are even now still alive. Finally, let the passage of Pliny cited before be added, in which there is not even a single word that is doubtful, or that could be twisted and rendered ambiguous; for he says that "Different kinds of fish do not mate, except for the Angel Shark and the Ray," and so forth. He [Pliny] seems either to have interpreted the words of Aristotle according to his [Aristotle's] mind, or to have seen the mating of these fish himself and reported it as certain. However the matter may stand, it is now sufficiently clear that Aristotle and Pliny described the *Rhinobatos* fish by these characteristics: namely, having the head and foreparts of a Ray, but the hindparts of an Angel Shark. All these features appear to be present in the fish I am proposing, as will be clear from the following description; and to proceed in order, I shall begin with its name. 

This fish, which I consider to be the *Rhinobatos* of the ancients, is commonly called *Cetola* by our fishermen, which means <term>Cithara <note>A stringed instrument, ancestor to the guitar and cittern.</note></term>. For this is a musical instrument strung with brass wires that is plucked with a piece of quill; it is known and used by them and the lowest of the common people. 
<margin><unclear>Pliny, book 9, chapter 40. History of Animals, book 9, chapter 37.</unclear></margin>
Therefore, it was easy for them, because of its resemblance, to give this name to the fish. They imagine the tail to be the neck [of the instrument], the rest of the body to be the soundbox, and the slit of the mouth to be the bridge-like fretboard by which the strings are raised from the instrument. I have described these things so that those to whom this instrument is unknown might not feel cheated of the explanation. 

It is, therefore, a cartilaginous fish, common in our sea, reaching a size—as I have learned from fishermen—not exceeding four feet, and weighing twelve pounds at most. It is yellowish in color, white on its belly, and has rough skin as if sprinkled with tiny grains of sand, similar to the skin of the <term>Angel Shark <note>Original Latin: *Squatina*.</note></term>. If you run your hand downward, you will judge it smooth, but if you go from the tail toward the head, you will feel the roughness. In shape, it resembles a Ray at first glance, much like the <term>Sharp-nosed Ray <note>Original Greek: ὀξυρύγχῳ βάτῳ (oxyrhyncho batō), likely referring to the *Dipturus oxyrinchus* or a similar long-snouted ray.</note></term>, but narrower, for the snout extends long, and it has wings [fins] narrower than other Rays. The body almost simulates the shape of a heart and makes up a third part of the total length. It lacks those two fleshy appendages located between the wings and the cartilaginous appendages with which Rays are endowed; however, the male has genitals between the tail and the cartilaginous appendages, just as Rays do,

<meta>catchword: females</meta>

<summary>Colonna identifies the Rhinobatos with the modern 'Cetola' or Guitarfish, explaining how its physical shape resembles a stringed instrument. He provides the first detailed morphological description of the specimen, noting its size, skin texture, and heart-shaped body.</summary>
<keywords>Rhinobatos, Guitarfish, Cithara, Angel Shark, Ray, ichthyology, Aristotle, Pliny, fish morphology, Naples fishermen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s anatomical description of the fish he identifies as the Rhinobatos (Guitarfish). The previous page ended while discussing the name "Cetola" (Cithara) and presumably the male’s anatomy; this page begins by contrasting the female specimen's features.</meta>

# HISTORY OF SOME FISH
<meta>catchword: Stingray</meta>

The female indeed lacks those <note>Refers to the male intromittent organs or "claspers" mentioned on the preceding page.</note>, but possesses only two cartilaginous appendages in the manner of fins, like the **Angel Shark** <note>Original: *Squatina*</note>, which take their origin from a bony sinew positioned above the vent; from there, a tail proceeds that is twice as long as the other parts, broad and fleshy, resembling the tail of the **Angel Shark**. On its lower part, it is flat, and its sides are defined by a thin cartilaginous edge, in which there are two fins dividing the tail into three parts—as seen in the **Angel Shark**—which ends in a single fin. It has eyes with that small membrane covering the pupil as seen in **Rays**, but similar to the **Angel Sharks**, and behind them are two openings <note>Spiracles, used for breathing while resting on the seabed.</note>. Behind these, the back proceeds, becoming rougher and extending to the very end of the tail; upon it, spines are likewise arranged in a single row, just as in the tail of the **Angel Shark**. There are also others located on the bony diaphragm behind the gills, which separates the head from the belly. Near this point, two lines arise on each side, positioned at the flanks of the back; the two lines closer to the sides of the tail are curved at their origin, separating the tail from the rest of the body, and all of them terminate with the tail. 

On the underside, there are two oblong openings, differing from those of the **Rays**, situated as nostrils; beneath these lies the mouth, protected by bony scales instead of teeth. There are five gill slits on each side, the lower two of which are contained within the bony diaphragm, marked by a certain curved and wavy line that separates the belly from the head. Within the belly are contained all the intestines, similar to the intestines of **Rays**, though the eggs differ from those of **Rays** and are instead similar to those of the **Angel Shark**; the female carries these in her womb from the winter solstice. At what time it may give birth, and with what habits and nature the male and female are endowed, I have not yet been able to observe; it is almost always sold alongside **Rays**. 

Therefore, since it is similar to the **Ray** in its head and foreparts, but resembles the **Angel Shark** in its tail and skin, it seems to me that it can rightly be called the <term>Rhinobatos <note>Guitarfish</note></term> of Aristotle and Pliny. Whether it actually arises from the mating of a **Ray** and an **Angel Shark**, I have yet to discover for certain; however, it seems sufficient to me now that it can be named <term>Rhinobatos</term>, from the likeness of <term>rhinē <note>Original Greek: ρ'ινος; referring to the rough, file-like skin of a shark.</note></term> and <term>batos <note>Original Greek: βάτος; meaning a ray or skate.</note></term>.

### The Sea Stingray of Dioscorides.

Many species of **Sea Stingrays** are already recorded and depicted by more recent authors based on their similarities, which you may see in the works of Conrad Gessner <note>A Swiss naturalist (1516–1565) known for his monumental "History of Animals."</note>, that most diligent student and collector of natural things. It would be too tedious to transcribe them all here again. Instead, I shall describe this one, which has been very little noticed by others [whose tail, armed with a spine, the same Gessner showed alone, without the rest of the body, among his other illustrations]. I do this because it seems to correspond to the description by Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical and pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>, whose meaning and words I have realized others have not fully grasped. 

His words are these: <note>original Greek: "Τρυγόνος θαλασσίας τὸ κέντρον, ὃ δὴ ἀπὸ τῆς οὐρᾶς αὐτῆς πέφυκεν ἀνεστραμμένον ταῖς φολίσιν, ὁδόντα πονοῦντα πραΰνει: θρύπτει γὰρ καὶ ἀποβάλλει."</note> "The sting of the sea ray, which grows from its tail and is turned back with scales, relieves a painful tooth; for it shatters it and casts it out." I have rendered this into Latin as: <note>original Latin: "Pastinacæ marinæ aculeus, qui ab radice ipsius enascitur reflexus squamis: dentem dolentem mitigat: frangit quidem illum, èquè eijcit."</note> "The sting of the sea stingray, which arises from its base and is bent back with scales: it eases an aching tooth: it breaks it indeed and ejects it." <note>Ancient medicine often suggested that the potent toxins or physical structure of the stingray spine could be used to treat dental pain by causing a decayed tooth to break apart and fall out.</note>

I have noticed that no one has weighed the words of this very brief description and compared them with the fish itself, except for the most learned Guillaume Rondelet <note>A famous 16th-century French physician and naturalist known for his work on fish.</note>. Even he, in interpreting it at length, saw that the specific meaning of the word "scales" <note>original: φολίσιν (pholisin)</note> did not seem to fit the fish he was depicting. He concluded, while excusing himself, that either the passage in Dioscorides was uncorrected or that the author had used an improper term. I shall attempt to interpret this word and demonstrate it in the depiction of this fish’s tail.

This fish is commonly called **Muchio** <note>Likely a local Italian name for a species of ray, possibly the Eagle Ray or a similar stingray.</note> by our fishermen. It is cartilaginous, flat, smooth, and of an ashen color tending toward yellow—though smaller ones are more yellow—while the underside is whitish. Its body seems to form a square, for the ends of the fins make two corners, while the snout and the beginning of the tail complete the other two. On the upper part, the head is seen in the manner of a **Ray** <note>Original: Raia</note>, and it is rough with small scales beneath the snout. It has eyes, and behind them are openings <note>Spiracles, which allow the fish to breathe while lying on the sandy seafloor.</note> as in other such fish. On either side, not very far away, are two scales <note>Original: lepides</note>, and two others on the head between the eyes and the diaphragm of the gills. On the lower side, there are two openings above the mouth, which is protected by bony scales <note>Rays have "pavement teeth" that look like hard, interlocking scales rather than individual teeth.</note>. Behind this, there are five gills on each side, which are separated from the rest of the belly by a bony diaphragm...

<meta>catchword: ...phragmate (diaphragm)</meta>

<summary>Fabio Colonna describes a specific species of stingray (locally called "Muchio"), arguing that its unique anatomy proves the accuracy of Dioscorides' ancient descriptions which other naturalists had doubted. He focuses on the "scales" of the tail spine and the fish's square-shaped cartilaginous body.</summary>

His words are these: <note>original Greek: "Τρυγόνος θαλασσίας τὸ κέντρον, ὃ δὴ ἀπὸ τῆς οὐρᾶς αὐτῆς πέφυκεν ἀνεστραμμένον ταῖς φολίσιν, ὁδόντα πονοῦντα πραΰνει: θρύπτει γὰρ καὶ ἀποβάλλει."</note> "The sting of the sea ray, which grows from its tail and is turned back with scales, relieves a painful tooth; for it shatters it and casts it out." # OF FABIO COLONNA

[the organs] are divided by a diaphragm; at the beginning of the tail and the sides of the vent are two cartilages in place of fins, and in the males, those fleshy appendages of the genitals <note>Colonna is describing the "claspers," the external male reproductive organs of sharks and rays.</note>. It has a long tail, nearly equal to one and a half times the length of the body, thick and square near the beginning, round at the end, and much thinner, having a border below with which it steers its course. Above, however, is a bony spine, hard and serrated on both sides with teeth pointing back toward the root of the tail; I have seen one nine inches <term>dodrantem</term> <note>A Roman unit of length equal to about 9 inches or 3/4 of a foot.</note> long, an inch and a half wide <term>sesquiunciam</term> at the bottom, and sharper at the end, situated at a distance from the start of the tail equal to a third of the tail's total length. Although other more recent authors have depicted this fish as lacking scales (drawing from a small specimen), they have certainly not seen the larger and grander ones. 

<margin>Pliny, Book 9, ch. 48.</margin>
This was also the case with Pliny, who describes a five-inch <term>quincuncialem</term> <note>A length of five-twelfths of a foot, roughly 5 inches.</note> spine as if it were the largest. But if a fish that does not exceed ten pounds has a similar one, what must be said of that which exceeds two hundred pounds, which has a spine equaling an iron dagger, as I have mentioned? Furthermore, this fish has a tail made rough on both sides by bony scales similar to our local limpets <note>original Latin: "patellis"</note>, but prickly at the top. The Greeks call these by the specific name <term>lepades</term> <note>original Greek: λεπάδες</note> among aquatic creatures, and they are called <term>lepades</term> because they protrude in the manner of scales <note>original Greek: λεπίδων</note> growing out from the skin. 

From this, the type of disease which the Latins call "scurf" <term>porrigo</term> <note>A general historical term for scaling skin conditions, like dandruff or eczema.</note> was so named; for just as many limpets are seen clinging to a rock, so these "leeks" <note>A play on the Latin "porrum" (leek), which is the etymological root of "porrigo."</note> are seen protruding from the skin, much like eruptions <term>exanthemata</term>. Theophrastus testifies that the fig tree suffers a similar erupting mange. 

<margin>Theophrastus, Book 5, ch. 13.</margin>
I noticed this same eruption on a certain fig tree a few days ago. Now, <term>pholides</term> <note>original Greek: φολίδες</note> are properly the scales of crawling animals <note>reptiles</note>, but the terms are often confused. For they say that <term>pholides</term> are the <term>lepides</term> <note>scales</note> of serpents; and Hippocrates 
<margin>Hippocrates, Book on Vision.</margin>
spoke of a "scale of copper" <note>original Greek: φολίδα τοῦ χαλκοῦ</note>, which Dioscorides called "the scale of copper" <note>original Greek: λεπίδα τοῦ χαλκοῦ</note>. From these examples, it is clear that the words <term>lepis</term> and <term>pholis</term> commonly signify all scales of any kind. 

However, although Dioscorides used the word <term>pholidin</term> <note>original Greek: φολίδιν</note> (by which the scales of serpents are denoted), it can be said that in this passage he used the word deliberately; for the tail of this fish truly looks like a scaly serpent. And thus it is understood that those scales, or <term>pholides</term>, are regarded as being reflected by the teeth of the spine, as Dioscorides says. This is not to be interpreted as "turned-back scales," as Ippolito Salviani <note>A 16th-century Italian physician and naturalist (1514–1572) famous for his "History of Aquatic Animals."</note> wrongly interpreted it, but rather as "reflected teeth." There is no doubt that this is the true interpretation. Therefore, Guillaume Rondelet <note>A French physician (1507–1566) and major figure in early ichthyology.</note> does not further...
<meta>The catchword "plius" at the bottom of the page completes the word "amplius" (further/more) on the following page.</meta>

<meta>This page continues Fabio Colonna’s discussion on the Stingray (Pastinaca). While the previous page touched on the physical anatomy and the Greek term for scales, this page shifts to the historical reputation of the fish’s venomous spine and its various medicinal applications as recorded by ancient authorities like Pliny, Aelian, and Dioscorides.</meta>

->### HISTORY OF SOME FISHES<-

[One] ought not to dwell longer on explaining the word *pholides* <note>original Greek: φολίσιν, referring to the horny scales discussed previously</note>, but should instead visit the fish market or speak with fishermen. From them, even if one does not wish to see the fish itself, one can hear the description and nature of this creature.
<margin>Book 9, ch. 48.</margin>
Speaking of this, Pliny says: *But there is nothing anywhere more cursed than the spine protruding above the tail of the Stingray, which our people call the Pastinaca, being five inches <term>quincunciali</term> <note>A Roman measurement of five-twelfths of a foot, roughly 5 inches.</note> in size. Fixed into trees, it kills them; it pierces armor like a weapon with the force of iron and the evil of poison.*
<margin>Book 32, ch. 2.</margin>
And elsewhere: *Nothing is ever more poisonous than the Stingray in the sea, since, as we have said, trees are killed by its spine.*
<margin>Book 13, ch. 6.</margin>
Aelian, however, writes: *The Stingray has a deadly sting (of which I made mention before), such that it can quickly destroy not only men but also other animals. This very fact, while it causes wonder, certainly holds a stupendous marvel: if you bring the sting to a very large, vigorous, and green tree and drive it in, not long after it loses its leaves; once they have fallen, the whole trunk becomes like trees that have dried out in summer during the greatest drought.*
<margin>Book 13, ch. 5.</margin>
Similar things are found elsewhere, as in the *Theriaca* <note>A famous ancient poem by Nicander regarding venomous animals and their cures.</note> of Nicander, and in Oppianus.
<margin>Book 13 ch. 1, Book 2.</margin>
Fishermen do not bring this Stingray and its relatives to the market for sale unless the tail has been removed, for it is forbidden; only for friends, and those who know how to avoid the poison, after many favors have been granted, do they secretly bring it home with the tail attached.
<margin>Book 7.</margin>
Dioscorides has recorded the signs of this poison. There are many remedies, which Salvianus collected, but the Stingray itself, torn apart and placed upon the wound, is especially a cure for its own strike; among other things which the same Dioscorides writes, and also Pliny:
<margin>Book 8, ch. 6.</margin>
*The Stingray is a remedy against its own strike, using its own ash applied with vinegar, or that of another.* And many other things; but this same Stingray is not only an antidote <term>antipharmacum</term> <note>From the Greek 'antipharmakon,' meaning a counter-remedy or antidote.</note> against its own poison,
<margin>Book 32, ch. 5.</margin>
but it also helps other conditions, such as soothing toothaches and drawing teeth out by breaking them. The method of use is described by Pliny in this way:
<margin>Book 32, ch. 7.</margin>
*It is also very useful in pain to scarify the gums with the spine of the Stingray. It is ground up and, when applied with white Hellebore, extracts teeth without distress.*
<margin>Book 6, ch. 9.</margin>
Celsus, however, says: *The sting is roasted, then ground and mixed with resin; when the tooth is surrounded by this, it loosens it.*
<margin>Book 32, ch. 8.</margin>
Pliny adds that it even cures scrofulous swellings <term>strumas</term> <note>Swelling of the lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis.</note> with these words: *The spine of the Stingray and the Sea Hare have the same power when applied, provided they are quickly removed,* and elsewhere:
<margin>Book 32, ch. 10.</margin>
*The spine of the Stingray is believed to aid childbirth and make it easy when tied to the navel, provided it was taken from a living fish and the fish itself was cast back into the sea.* And elsewhere:
<margin>Book 32, ch. 10.</margin>
*The liver of the Stingray boiled in oil most effectively calms the itching and mange not only of men but also of four-footed animals.*

->The Ten-Armed Shaggy Sea-Star <note>original Latin and Greek: *Stellæ δεκαδασυακτινοειδης* (dekadasuaktinoeides). This compound term is derived from the Greek *deka* (ten), *dasys* (shaggy or hairy), and *aktis* (ray or arm), describing the feathery appearance of the creature's limbs.</note><-

## **Another Sea Star**
->Ten-rayed shaggy form<-
<note>original Greek: "δεκαδασυακτινοειδής" (deka-dasy-aktino-eides). This refers to a ten-armed star with a "shaggy" or hairy appearance, likely a species of Feather Star (Crinoidea) or Brittle Star.</note>

The form of this Star is new and most elegant, differing from all others and not yet described by other authors. It is common on our coast, so much so that no nets are drawn back without these being entangled in them; it is even found in the market among the fish. This creature is exceedingly fragile; while alive, it allows itself to be broken rather than be removed from the net, so tightly does it hold itself entangled with its <term>cirri</term> <note>Small, hair-like appendages or "tendrils" used for grasping.</note>. No force should be used when extracting the Star, as the rays are <term>geniculati</term> <note>From the Latin "geniculatus," meaning jointed or having "knees."</note>, and the cartilage joining these joints is very thin, which is why the joints break apart so easily. Yet, even if all the rays are broken off, the creature does not die except after a long interval.

Its color is reddish, but its form is seen to differ from other sea stars. For it has a very small body and five rays, but each ray is divided into two, so that at first glance ten rays are seen. I have seen them a half-foot long, but no larger as of yet; the Star’s total diameter equals one foot. 

All the rays consist of small joints, and each joint has the shape of a triangle. The intersecting lines are joined now to the right and now to the left, in the manner of spirals, which alternately form opposite angles. From the base of each triangle, more <term>cirri</term> are produced alternately on both sides, just like the bases themselves; these are jointed in a similar way, about the length of a little finger and the thickness of a hemp thread. It is with these that the creature is most effectively entangled, walks, and seizes things. The upper ones of these appendages slightly exceed the lower ones in length and thickness all the way to the end. 

From the center of the body, other thin <term>cirri</term> emerge, also the thickness of a thread and jointed in the same way, about the length of a thumb. The upper ones of these are smaller than the lower ones and are curved in the opposite direction from the larger rays. I believe the creature uses these to seize food and bring it to the mouth, which can be seen located in their <term>umbilicus</term> <note>The central point or "navel" on the underside of the sea star.</note>. On the opposite side, the body is seen to be round, smooth, and soft, the interior of which I was unable to examine because of its thinness. This part is very easily separated from the Star itself; it is entirely smooth, but rough to-

[OF] FABIO COLONNA

...it seems to the touch <note>original: "tactu"</note> because of the multitude of joints and <term>cirri</term> <note>slender, hair-like appendages</note>: it is covered by a somewhat hard crust, so that it seems composed of bony joints, but they are not very hard. Whether it shines at night, as other stars do, I was not able to test, since I could not keep a living specimen at home.

## That the shells commonly called *Goose-bearing* are not terrestrial fruits, nor do *Geese* arise from them: but they are a *species of sea barnacle*.

<image-desc>Ornate decorative woodcut initial 'D' depicting a figure seated within a lush garden or forest setting, framed by intricate scrolling foliage and floral designs.</image-desc>

WHILE I had not yet seen these shells, from which it is falsely asserted that <term>Geese</term> <note>original: "Anates." This refers to the Barnacle Goose, which medieval folklore claimed grew from barnacles on driftwood.</note> arise; and while I observed in the works of <term>Lobelius</term> <note>Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist.</note>, <term>Pena</term> <note>Pierre Pena, a French botanist who collaborated with Lobel.</note>, and those who followed them, that there was a tree on sea rocks which produced these fruits—equal in shape and size to an <term>Almond</term> <note>original: "Amygdalæ."</note>—the thought of this matter frequently disturbed my mind. I could not believe it, even though I sometimes inclined my mind toward belief, moved by this reason: because these things were written by two most learned physicians and were finally accepted by others. Yet, on the other hand, this imagination was rejected by my senses with a stronger argument: because the tree producing the fruit was shown in a drawing, and by those same physicians, these same fruits were given the name of "Shells," which they testify to have been plucked from the hull of an old ship. These things are indeed considered contradictory and impossible. A terrestrial fruit of a tree can by no means be a sea shell, and if it were, it could hardly cling to the hulls of ships.

Driven by this opinion, I began to burn with a desire to see these shells. Not many days had passed when a great sea tide began to rush onto our shore with violence and sound (it was then winter), which continued for nearly ten days. During this time, because the rain also blocked the roads, my mind was drawn even more to my studies. I was comparing the *Histories of Fishes* published by the most learned man <term>Rondelet</term> <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a famous French anatomy professor and naturalist.</note> with the descriptions of others. It happened at that time, while leafing through the genus of <term>shellfish</term> <note>original: "testaceorum."</note>, that I considered the <term>Barnacles</term> <note>original: "Balanos," literally "acorns," the common name for barnacles.</note> described and depicted by him. The memory of these "goose-bearing" shells was stirred by their form and their similarity to these barnacles. And knowing, because I had read it, that shells are easily cast up by the washing of the sea...

<summary>Fabio Colonna expresses skepticism regarding the common myth that "Barnacle Geese" hatch from sea-shells growing on trees, arguing that biological contradictions make the theory impossible.</summary>

<keywords>Barnacle Goose, Matthias de l'Obel, Pierre Pena, Guillaume Rondelet, marine biology, spontaneous generation, shells, barnacles, mythology vs observation</keywords>

### HISTORY OF SEVERAL FISHES 15

...to be able to [demonstrate this], I ordered my servant to scout a part of our shore and bring back whatever he found cast up by the sea's tide. By good fortune, among many other things, he brought back **Eschara** <note>A type of bryozoan or "lace coral."</note> clinging to seaweed, a species of **Alcyonium** <note>A genus of soft corals often called "dead man's fingers."</note>, the **Starfish** whose image I have displayed, a **Sea-Pen** <note>original: "Pennam." A colonial marine organism that looks like an antique quill pen.</note>, and various **Shells**; these were clinging to reed stalks, roots, rocks, and pumice stones, not without joy to my soul. Their form is as follows:

The shell is smooth, shaped like an **Almond** fruit, and almost equal in size, but more compressed at the sharper end. Its color turns from white to ash-gray. It is constructed of five parts; for those which serve as valves are seen to be divided obliquely near the outer and narrower part. These parts are joined by cartilage and end in a point at the rear of the shell. These four parts are also joined by cartilage to a fifth, narrower part, which is the back of the shell, so that they cannot be opened from that side. This part of the back proceeds wider from the base and ends thinner near the shell's extremity. It is curved and wrinkled with additions, by which the back is seen to be serrated near the bottom with five or six teeth.

The part by which it hangs is wider and more swollen; for this reason, the base of the valves would be seen open, were it not that in place of a base, a certain fleshy, dark, leather-like part, entirely wrinkled—thinner at the bottom where it clings and widening to the width of the base—forms the base of the shell. This clearly resembles that wrinkled stem that holds the almond fruit; for this reason, because they are seen elegantly arranged and hanging even on the shoots of trees <note>referring to driftwood</note>, men thought they were the fruits of a tree. 

Indeed, it is a marine shell, born from the foamy mud of the sea, just like all other **testaceans** <note>shelled animals</note>, adhering to rocks, any kind of wood, or pumice stones that retain that moisture. It contains an animal inside that never leaves the shell; it is joined at the base, and from there perhaps it seems to suck moisture for food when the water recedes from them. There is a fleshy body at the bottom; whether it has a mouth and in what manner—though I do not think it lacks one—I could not rightly observe. However, it has many <term>cirri</term> <note>slender, hair-like appendages used for feeding</note> or feet, segmented and turning from dark to yellowish. 

<margin>Book 1 on testaceans, chapter 29.</margin>
The British, as **Rondeletius** <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a foundational French naturalist and physician.</note> asserts, call them **Poussepiez** <note>The French name for the goose barnacle, *pouce-pied*.</note>, and others call them **Pollicipedes** <note>Latin for "thumb-feet," the scientific name for this genus of barnacles.</note>. Their upper parts are longer than the others and seen to be curved, jointed, and hairy on the inner part, mimicking a feather divided lengthwise, or the curved tail of a rooster; for which reason those people believed [they became]

# FABIO COLONNA’S

[They claim these] are feathers and a tail, and the beginnings of a growing little bird sent out from the shell. But to me they seem rather to be **cirri** <note>original: "cirros"; the hair-like feeding appendages of a barnacle.</note> and the arms of the animal, which nature gave to the shell so that it could hunt for food and pull it inside; indeed, they would have been born on it in vain if they were not fit for preserving its life. But I do not know how they could believe marine shells are the fruit of a land tree, a claim that should not be believed even by those living on mountain peaks who had never seen the sea, much less marine shells. Indeed, if these were shown to them under the name of "tree fruit," they would promptly respond that it could not happen according to nature for a land tree to produce such fruit—covered in that shell-like bark and containing flesh and a fully formed animal inside; for the same animal is always contained within.

I say that I agree with them; certainly they are never seen without an animal (even though I have very small specimens of these same shells hardly equal to a millet seed, and all contain a formed and complete, though tiny, animal), and it is always the same kind. Therefore, they do not arise first from a worm, as **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BCE) who theorized that some marine life arose through spontaneous generation from mud or slime.</note> asserts many marine animals are born; but from the beginning, that sticky fluid is gathered into an animal, and the more earthy part of the fluid thickens around it and is shaped into the form of a shell. As time passes, it grows along with the animal; and when the animal is mature, as a foamy and mucus-like fluid endowed with a certain seminal power comes out, or even by the intervention of that same first muddy fluid, other small shells are born around it in the manner of **mussels**. 

From these facts, it will be certain that it is a **shell-bearing animal** <term>testaceum animal: a creature with a hard, stony outer shell, like a mollusk or crustacean</term>, and not the fruit of a tree, as it is depicted by others. Indeed, these shells quickly die when cast onto dry land due to the loss of moisture, but in the sea (where they arise spontaneously) they cannot rot at all. Even if something were to arise from their rotting, it would necessarily be a marine thing—granted for the sake of argument, though not conceded. Therefore, having removed the primary claim, their subsequent conclusion is consequently rejected: namely, that birds arise from the rotting of those shells cast into the sea. They attribute the generation of flying creatures to the sea, arising from the rotting of fish. 

<margin>From the facts stated.</margin> How far this is from the truth, and whether it could even happen, I leave to those same people to judge. But it must be concluded that the assertion of those who say **Ducks** <note>original: "Anates"; Colonna is referring to the Barnacle Goose, which was long rumored to hatch from these shells.</note> arise from these shells is false; perhaps they thought they arose only in **Scotland** and **Britain**, places far away from us, and for that reason, they [tried to]

->thrust<-

# THE WORKS OF FABIO COLONNA

...they have thought it easy to thrust these myths upon the credulous; but both in our own and neighboring regions, especially on the island of Procida <note>original: "Prochytæ," the modern island of Procida near Naples</note>, they are frequent. They grow to a remarkable size, adhering not only to branches, rocks, and the hulls of ships, but also floating freely in the water. An illustration and history of these will be published by Ferrante Imperato <note>A Neapolitan apothecary and naturalist (1550–1625) whose "Dell'Historia Naturale" was a landmark work</note> along with other wonderful and new things. The most learned Rondelet <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a French physician and pioneering ichthyologist</note>, a seeker of truth, asserts that these arise in his own Normandy and Brittany. He does not report that birds are generated from them, but instead presents them as a species of <term>Balanus</term> <note>From the Greek "balanos," meaning acorn; here referring to acorn barnacles</note>. He describes how men and women, even those who loathe other foods, delight in this kind of dish, eating the internal flesh with vinegar after it has been boiled in water and cracked open. I agree with him that this shell is a *Balanus*, even though he describes another kind of *Balanus* whose shape more closely resembles an acorn. For although that other kind represents only the upper or half-part of an acorn, I judge this one to be a *Balanus* as well, even if it resembles an almond fruit rather than an oak acorn. For <term>Balanos</term> <note>original Greek: "Βάλανος"</note> is not only the name for the fruit of the Oak, but is a generic name for the fruits of chestnuts, beeches, and other trees, as others have noted. Similarly, among the Latins, the word <term>Glans</term> <note>Latin for "acorn"</note> signifies not only the acorn but any fruit whatsoever. Thus, <margin>Book 71</margin> Ulpian, writing on the Edict, says: "Under the name of **Acorn**, all fruits are contained." <note>Ulpian was a Roman jurist; Colonna uses legal definitions to support his broad biological classification</note> Therefore, if anyone wishes to compare the resemblance of the second type of *Balanus* with oak acorns, he will see that it does not reflect the whole acorn nor the position of acorns as closely as the shells of the first type, clinging to a branch, resemble almond fruits. Both are to be called by the name *Balanus*, for they contain the same animal within and are found adhering in the same places, differing from one another only in the form of the shell. This difference in the shell arises from the material—namely, the mud. For the first kind, which adheres to wood cast into the sea, to rocks, and to pumice, lives in sandy places where the water is purer. Therefore, the mud is also purer and thinner, mixed with finer earth, which is why a solid, smooth, and delicate shell is produced. The second kind, however, adheres to muddy cliffs where the filth is more earthy and less pure; therefore, it is covered by a thicker and rougher shell, like the oyster, the thorny oyster <note>original: "spondylus"</note>, and similar creatures. Aristotle describes this origin in these words: 
> Where there is mud, 
<margin>Book 5 on the Generation of Animals, chapters 15-16</margin>
> there the origin of oysters consists. Shells, clams, razor-clams, and scallops take the beginnings of their growth in sandy places. 
And a little further down: 
> Finally, all shellfish arise spontaneously in the mud in different ways, according to the difference of the mud: for Oysters arise in the slimy...

<summary>Fabio Colonna classifies barnacles as "sea acorns" (Balanus), drawing on etymological evidence from Roman law and biological observations from Aristotle to explain how environmental conditions like water purity and mud types determine the shape and texture of their shells.</summary>

## *Basil-like Campion with a creeping root.*

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' at the start of the first paragraph, featuring a human figure or putto integrated into scrolling floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

<margin>Location.</margin>
This plant stands halfway between the *Ocymoides* <note>Basil-like plant; from the Greek 'okimon' for basil</note> and the *Lychnis coronaria* <note>The Rose Campion</note>. It seems similar to the *Ocymoides* because it has small leaves growing in pairs at each joint immediately from the root; yet it resembles the *Lychnis* because its leaves are hoary, hairy, and fleshy—no less suited for making lamp-wicks <note>original: "ellychnia." The name Lychnis comes from the Greek 'lychnos' meaning lamp, as the fuzzy leaves of these plants were historically used as wicks</note> than the leaves of the *Lychnis* itself. It grows wild and frequently on the mountain named after the Blessed Virgin Mary <note>Monte Vergine, a mountain range in the Campanian Apennines of Italy near Avellino</note> in damp, stony places.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It is anchored by a very thin root that descends only slightly below the earth and creeps around. From this root sprout many round, hoary, and hairy little shoots, which have two small leaves clinging to each joint. These leaves are smaller at the bottom and become larger in the middle of the shoot—which is the opposite of what usually happens in plants that are leafy right from the root before the stalk emerges, as one reads in Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 371–287 BC) often called the "Father of Botany"</note>. The leaves are hairy, fleshy, and hoary, similar to the *Lychnis*, but in size and shape they resemble the *Ocymoides*, which is called *Polemonia* <note>likely Jacob's Ladder or a similar Valerian species</note> by the Belgians. Like its relatives, it produces white flowers in the months of April and May; these are made of five petals with five yellow stamens inside. Each petal is divided at the top where it is widest, much like the pods of the *Thlaspi* <note>Pennycress or Shepherd's Purse</note>.

<margin>Time.</margin>
In June, it ripens its seeds in long, soft, and gristly pods; the seeds are yellowish-brown, angular, and hairy. These pods are not covered by a bladder-like skin as in the aforementioned *Ocymoides*, but merely have a base made of five small leaves, as in the plant called *Gallinae morsus* <note>Literally "Hen's bite," usually referring to Chickweed or Henbit</note>. The root of this plant is quite hardy, and for that reason, it sprouts much, lasts many years, and its foliage covers the ground.

<margin>Virtues.</margin>
The taste of the plant is sweet. For this reason, it is not uncommon for herbalists and the common folk of women <note>Referring to traditional female healers and midwives</note> to use it to produce an abundance of milk. They cook the plant just like other pot-herbs, mixing it into broths with cracked eggs, adding a little pepper and marjoram; or, as others are accustomed, they form it into a cake. They assert from experience—and still use it today—that eating this produces a great supply of milk. From these qualities, therefore, [we may conclude it is] warm and...

<summary>Colonna describes a specific type of Campion (Ocymoides Lychnitis) found on Monte Vergine, detailing its physical appearance, its comparison to ancient botanical texts, and its local use by women as a galactagogue to increase breast milk production.</summary>
<keywords>botany, Monte Vergine, Lychnis, Ocymoides, galactagogue, herbal medicine, Theophrastus, plant morphology</keywords>

### Goatsbeard with jagged leaves
<note>original: "Tragopogon laciniatis folijs." The name comes from the Greek "tragos" (goat) and "pogon" (beard), referring to the fluffy seed heads.</note>

<margin>
Name.
Book 4, ch.
on Groundsel.
Book 25,
last ch.
Book 7,
ch. 7.
Book 2, ch.
on Goatsbeard.
Book 7,
ch. 7.
</margin>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'N' featuring botanical and scrolled ornamentation.</image-desc>

**N**ot less than it is called **Groundsel** <note>original: "Senecio"</note> by the Latins, it was called **Erigeron** <note>original: "ἠριγέρων," meaning "early-old"</note> by the Greeks, because in Spring the flowers grow gray in the manner of hair, as reported by Dioscorides and Pliny. It was called **Goatsbeard** <note>original: "Tragopogon"</note>, as Theophrastus says, because a long gray beard hangs from the very top. It is not as is read—falsely in my judgment—in Dioscorides: "but on the stem is a large calyx, and at the end a black fruit, from which it also takes its name" <note>original: "ἐπὶ δὲ τοῦ καυλοῦ κάλυξ μέγας, καὶ ἐξ ἄκρου καρπός μέλας, ἀφ’ οὗ καὶ τὸ ὄνομα ἔχεν"</note>. Rather, it has a large calyx on the stem, and at the top a black fruit from which it takes its name. For it was not named from a black fruit, which it does not have, but from the long **pappus** <term>pappus|the feathery or hairy bristles on a seed, like those of a dandelion</term>, or narrow beard, similar to a Goat, exceeding the calyx; thus it was called **Tragopogon** <note>original: "Τραγωπόγων"</note>, as Theophrastus accurately described: "Others call it by a thousand other names, this plant which indeed has a long and sweet root, leaves similar to Saffron <note>original: "Croco"</note> but much longer: a short stem, upon which is a large calyx, and from the top a large gray beard <note>original: "παππὸν πόλιον" or "πώγωνα"</note>; whence it is called Goatsbeard."

<margin>
Form.
</margin>
This is a variety of Goatsbeard. It has a long, round root, black on the outside, white within, yielding milk just like the whole plant, and sweet and suitable for eating. From the root many leaves emerge, as in other species, which are green and smooth on the upper side, but rough on the reverse, divided into three or four lobes <note>original: "lacinijs"</note>, like the common **Buckshorn Plantain** <note>original: "Coronopi"</note> herb called "Star," but a foot long and narrow like Saffron leaves. It sends up a stem after a year, having grown from seed; the stem is rough, hollow, one or two feet high, and divided into branches. On these branches, and at the top of the stem, are calyxes, as in other species, but fluted <note>original: "striati"</note>, in which yellow flowers open in the morning; then after an hour, when the Sun comes upon them, they are closed and no longer open. But the seeds <note>original: "femina," likely a variant of "semina"</note> are perfected as in other related plants; which later, once the calyx has opened, are arranged into a sphere

## Mountain Wormwood with the flower of Female Southernwood
<note>original: "Absinthium Montanum Abrotonifæminæ flore." Wormwood refers to the genus *Artemisia*.</note>

<margin>Place.</margin>
<margin>Name.</margin>
<margin>Form.</margin>
<margin>Time.</margin>
<margin>Book 3, ch. 2. On Southernwood.</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative woodcut historiated initial 'I' depicting a figure standing in a landscape, possibly a gardener or scholar, contained within a square border.</image-desc>On the high mountain called Saint Mary the Virgin <note>likely Monte Vergine in the Campania region of Italy</note>, a new, elegant, and rare species of this **Wormwood** <note>original: "Absinthium"</note> arises, and it does not emerge except from sterile cracks in the rocks from which it bushes out. From a woody, small, black, and fibrous root, it sends out hoary, woolly, thin leaves divided by deep incisions, midway between Sea Wormwood and Male **Southernwood** <note>original: "Abrotonū marem"</note>, arranged in a circle. In Spring, it puts forth several trailing stems with a few leaves clinging at the bottom; in Summer, it has single flowers at the top of each stem in the manner of Female Southernwood, but more similar to Chamomile; small white petals surround yellow centers. In flavor, taste, and scent, this plant resembles common Pontic Wormwood; for this reason, I have given it the name of Wormwood rather than Female Southernwood, to which it might seem more similar—especially in the flower, which is far different from the flowers of Wormwood. But because it is not **tree-like** <term>tree-like|original Greek "δενδροειδὴς" (dendroeidēs); referring to a shrubby, upright growth habit</term> as Female Southernwood is, as Dioscorides says, but **few-leaved and low-stalked** <term>few-leaved and low-stalked|original Greek "ὀλιγόφυλλος, καὶ χαμαίκαυλος" (oligophyllos, kai chamaikaulos)</term>, and also because it has a more bitter and astringent taste, very similar to Wormwood, it should be judged as such. This is true even if this plant differs from Wormwood in appearance when it is in bloom. Because of the taste and scent, and thus from its **faculties** <term>faculties|the medicinal properties or "virtues" of a plant in early modern science</term>, I preferred to call it Wormwood rather than to ascribe it to a variety of Female Southernwood based on a likeness that often deceives. For it has more bitterness than Southernwood, and thus consists of fewer "thin parts" than that plant; so it is endowed with less power to thin the humors, even if it contains no less, but rather more, drying power.

<meta>catchword: Trachelium (Throatwort)</meta>

## *Mountain Throatwort with Goat’s-beard Leaves*
<note>original: "Trachelium Tragopogi folio montanum"</note>

<margin>
Name.
Location. Form.
Season.
</margin>
This new plant, which grows on the mountain commonly called the "Mount of the Blessed Virgin Mary," <note>Monte Vergine, a mountain range in the Campania region of Italy</note> should be classified among the varieties of Throatworts <term>Trachelium<gloss>a genus of plants, such as the bellflower, historically used to treat ailments of the "trachea" or windpipe</gloss></term>. This classification is based not only on the shape of the flowers, the native location, and the time of year it blooms, but also on its abundant milky sap, the texture of the leaves, and the flavor of the entire plant. It was named "Throatwort" by recent authors because it was thought to heal the windpipe and the surrounding areas. It has a somewhat thick, whitish root that produces milk; from this root grow several shoots with narrow, long, and somewhat tough leaves, similar in shape and size to saffron or Goat’s-beard <note>original: "Tragopogi"</note>. At the end of spring, it puts forth foot-long stems that are reddish and hairy, to which the leaves are attached alternately and sparsely. At the top of these stems, six or seven purple flowers emerge together, shaped like small bells <note>original: "nolae forma"</note> in the manner of the common Throatwort, each arising from a base constructed of five small green leaves. The fruit and seeds are also similar to the Throatwort. It reaches maturity in the summer. The root is perennial and sweet to the taste, suitable for eating just like the common Throatwort and Rampion <note>original: "Rapuntium", a bellflower with edible roots</note>.

---

## *Another Local Wild Mustard*
<note>original: "Sinapi alterum agreste nostras"</note>

<margin>
Location.
Form.
</margin>
In the valley above the church of St. Mary of Life <note>Santa Maria della Vita</note>, among brambles, Dog’s Mercury <note>original: "Cynocramben"</note>, Dandelions <note>original: "Leonis dentes"</note>, and Buttercups <note>original: "Ranuncula"</note>, there grows a certain new and elegant plant; I do not know of it growing anywhere else, and other herbalists say they have not seen it. It is anchored by a small, thin, fibrous root that descends only slightly underground and is whitish. From this root emerge leaves with the same lobes as Greater Celandine <note>original: "Chelidonij maioris"</note>, though they differ in that they do not produce yellow sap, nor are they a bluish-gray color. Instead, they are green, covered in a rough hairiness, and the tip of the leaf is sharp rather than rounded like Celandine; they are also smaller. The stem is angular and divided into many "wings" or branches. it produces purple flowers, arranged almost like umbrellas <note>original: "umbellatos"</note>, composed of four small petals like Celandine. Afterward, it produces seed pods <term>siliques<gloss>long, thin seed capsules typical of the mustard family</gloss></term> that attach alternately and in great number. These pods are straight, with their tips pointing toward the sky, forming a spike of pods about two fingers long, similar in length to Water-parsnip <note>original: "Sioaquatico"</note>. When these begin to dry out, they turn yellow, and...
