When the wind blows, or something touches them, they burst from the bottom, and with the valves twisting on both sides, they shoot out yellow seeds in the manner of Water-parsnip <note>original: "Sij"</note> or Bittercress <note>original: "Cardaminæ"</note>, which are small and sharp in taste, just like the leaves. ### *A new plant with the appearance of Chickweed.*

<margin>
Name and Etymology Book 4

Book 2.
Book 4.
Description.
</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative historiated woodcut drop cap 'A' featuring floral patterns and a central grotesque or human-like face.</image-desc>
CHICKWEED <note>original: "ALSINE"</note> is so called because it loves shady places and groves <note>The name is derived from the Greek "alsos," meaning a grove</note>, as the same Dioscorides <note>An ancient Greek physician and the author of "De Materia Medica"</note> says. By some it is called Mouse-ear <note>original: "Myosota"</note>, because it has leaves imitating the ears of mice. In the chapter on Mouse-ear, Dioscorides says: *There are those who call Chickweed "Mouse-ear," but Chickweed is different.* As he also says: <note>Translating the Greek passage provided in the source:</note> "Chickweed, or mouse ears, because it has leaves like mouse ears; but called Chickweed because it grows in shady and wooded places. It is a herb similar to Pellitory-of-the-wall <note>original: "Helxinæ"</note>, but lower and with longer leaves and not hairy; and when rubbed, it smells of cucumber." <note>original Latin translation of the Greek: "Alsine, aliqui Myosota appellant..."</note>

<margin>
Book 27.
ch. 4.
</margin>
Pliny <note>A Roman author and natural philosopher</note> writes: *Chickweed, which some call Mouse-ear, grows in groves, whence it is also called Alsine: it begins in mid-winter and withers in mid-summer. When it creeps forth, it imitates the ears of mice with its leaves [but we shall teach of another which is more rightly called Mouse-ear].* This would be the same as Pellitory <note>original: "Helxine"</note> were it not smaller and less hairy. It grows in gardens and especially on walls; when rubbed, it gives off the scent of cucumber. In the drawings of these authors, no other plant depicted by others seems to correspond so well in appearance; it seems to refer more to Mouse-ear than to Chickweed. However, the one depicted by me seems to differ only in its smoothness and size; the rest reflects the habit of Pellitory.

<margin>Form.</margin>
It has a thin, white, fibrous root, from which many nine-inch-long <note>original: "dodrantales"</note> little vines grow out—round, smooth, slightly reddish, fleshy, full of sap, and spread upon the ground unless they hang from a wall. The leaves are frequent at the joints, first in pairs and later arranged alternately on both sides, having the form and position of Pellitory <note>original: "Helxines," referring to Parietaria</note>. This is indeed a lower-growing Pellitory <note>original: "Parietaria"</note>, which cannot be understood if you thought it were Bindweed <note>original: "Cissampelo"</note>, for that is a climbing plant according to the testimony of Dioscorides. These leaves are smaller, however, and slightly rounder, and quite moist—

<summary>This page describes the seed dispersal of Wild Mustard before transitioning to a detailed botanical and etymological study of Chickweed (Alsine). Colonna compares his observations with classical descriptions from Dioscorides and Pliny to clarify the plant's identity and characteristics.</summary>

<keywords>Chickweed, Alsine, Mouse-ear, Myosota, Pellitory-of-the-wall, Parietaria, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, botanical taxonomy, seed dispersal</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of a plant resembling Chickweed (started on page 28) and introduces a new specimen of dwarf Rampion. The author, Fabio Colonna, continues to compare his observations with the classical authorities Dioscorides and Galen.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>THE HISTORY OF NEW PLANTS</header>

similar to [mouse] ears, fleshy, less hairy, resembling the leaves of Wood Sorrel <note>original: "Oxalidis rotundi foliae"</note>, for they are covered in the same way with a kind of dusting, as is also seen in Orach <note>original: "Atriplice"; a plant often used as a potherb</note>. From these joints, two other small leaves emerge, as are also seen in Pellitory-of-the-wall <note>original: "Parietaria"</note>, which also imitate the ears of mice. Near these, flowers grow in pairs or threes, mossy and of a whitish-green color, consisting of two twisted little leaves facing each other, with many thin stamens inside. When these fall away, an equal number of fruits succeed them—round, hard, first green, and later a grayish-black.

<margin>Time.</margin> It comes forth in the month of October, flowers in Spring, and completes its cycle in Summer. When crushed, the herb clearly recalls the scent of Cucumber, more so than any other plant that yields such a scent. The leaves have a tasteless, watery flavor, but in the seed there is a certain quality of thin parts; when chewed, the tongue perceives a broad cleansing sensation, yet it does not produce heat. From this, it is gathered that it has the power to cleanse, bind <note>to act as an astringent</note>, and moisten, just like Pellitory <note>original: "Helxlne"</note>, as Dioscorides says; he claims it possesses the same powers as Chickweed <note>original: "Alsine"</note>. Galen also asserts that Pellitory has the power of cleansing and binding, along with a slightly cold moisture.

<margin>Book 6 on the faculties of simple medicines.</margin> But in the chapter on Chickweed, he says that Chickweed has the powers of Pellitory and is of a watery essence, and has only a cooling and moistening power, without binding. These powers seem rather contrary than similar to those of Pellitory; for Pellitory has a greater power of cleansing and binding, but less of cooling through moisture. However, I leave this to more learned men and those who conduct experiments.

<margin>Place.</margin> It seems to love the same places as Pellitory, and I had it brought from the Island of Capri <note>original: "Insula Capreis"</note>, where it grows frequently on its own.

## *The smallest kind of Erinus, or Rampion.*

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'N' featuring a landscape with trees and a distant tower.</image-desc>

This is a new and quite pretty little plant brought from the mountain of the Blessed Virgin Mary <note>likely referring to Monte Vergine in Italy</note>. It has a very small root divided into fibers, tender and similar in flavor to the Rampion <note>original: "Rapunculo"</note> or Erinus <note>original: "Erino"</note> previously depicted by me; it also yields a milky sap. It puts forth very small leaves, the length of a finger, slightly hairy, oblong, and broader at the top. These are serrated along the edge with sparse divisions; for those nearer the root seem to have eight teeth around them, while the upper ones have fewer, and two teeth cling to the flowers. It puts forth a stem—

-> **Erinus** <note>original: *Erinus*. In the context of 16th-century botany, Colonna was attempting to clarify the "Erinus" mentioned by the ancient physician Dioscorides. FABIO COLONNA’S <note>original Latin: *FABII COLVMNÆ*. Fabio Colonna was a distinguished Neapolitan botanist who pioneered the use of copperplate etchings for botanical illustration.</note>

<page-num>32</page-num>

[It emerges] from the root, divided from individual leaf-wings <note>The Latin text begins mid-sentence; "leaf-wings" likely refers to axillary branching.</note>, so that it appears shrubby; it is angular, hairy, and thin. It produces small flowers in the months of April and May among the leaves, from a broad, slightly hairy calyx ending in five leaves, as is seen in Rampion <note>original: *Rapuntium*, likely *Campanula rapunculus*, used then as a salad vegetable.</note> and the plant called the Pyramidal Bellflower. These flowers emerge as oblong, hollow tubes similar to the flowers of Throatwort <note>original: *Trachelium*.</note>; they are purple, containing within their tip a yellow spike—or thyrse—that is twisted into a triple division at the top, as is seen in related species. It perfects its seed in June and July, which is very small and dark. The whole plant swells with a sweet, milky sap, just like Rampion, so that its flavor is pleasant to the palate.

## *Lesser Phyteuma, Small Scabious, with*
## *fringed and fragrant Basil-like flowers.*

<margin>Form.</margin>
<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'P' within a square frame, decorated with intricate floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc> This Scabious <note>The Scabious or "Pincushion flower" was historically used to treat skin conditions like scabies.</note> is most beautiful and pleasing to both the eyes and the nose. It has a long, woody root from which various leaves emerge; at the base, they are either like those of wild Rocket <note>original: *Eruca*.</note> or similar to Buckshorn Plantain <note>original: *Coronopus*.</note>, but smaller and hairy. On the stem, however, they resemble the fringed leaves of the common small Scabious, as do the flowers. These flowers are a purplish-white color, rather than the blue-purple found in other varieties. They grow in rounded heads, and the individual blossoms imitate the flowers of Basil <note>original: *Ocimum*.</note>; for they are hooded <note>original: *galericulati*, meaning they have a cap-like shape.</note> and fringed at the edges, very similar to exotic Basil with its purple-spotted and fringed leaves. Furthermore—something not observed in other types of Scabious—
<margin>Time.</margin>
they are well-scented. It blooms in the Summer, and even in the Winter because of the 
<margin>Place.</margin>
mildness of the climate. It grows in the mountains of the Amalfi coast.

->THE END.<-

<keywords>Fabio Colonna, botanical description, Phyteuma, Scabious, Rampion, Amalfi coast, medicinal plants, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page serves as an "Errata and Addenda" section, providing corrections and supplementary information for Fabio Colonna's botanical work. It follows a page describing Scabious and Phyteuma, adding specific medicinal observations and nomenclature corrections. The page opens with a laudatory Greek poem dedicated to the author.</meta>

->An epigram by Francesco Guerrini of the Society of Jesus<-
->for Fabio Colonna.<-

Nature once worked upon the earth, the sea, the air, and all things within them, 
In which work Nature even surpassed Art itself.
Then, desiring greatly to fashion her own form and image,
She skillfully fashioned FABIO.
He now fashions—as a second Nature—every bird,
Everything the sea holds, and everything the earth bears.
<note>Guerrini's poem praises Colonna's skill in illustration and description, suggesting his botanical works are so accurate they rival Nature’s own creations. Original language: Ancient Greek.</note>

---

->Omitted items which the Reader shall restore to their proper places, which<-
->are also noted in the Index.<-

**Page 1. after the words**, emerging from the side they end, **add:** The little heads also have a pyramidal shape and represent the flames of fire, so that the etymology of the name can be taken from their very shape. <note>Colonna often sought to explain plant names based on their physical appearance, a common practice in early modern science.</note>

**Page 3. after**, and as if divided into several parts, **add:** equal and similar to the wild Nigella <note>original: *Melanthium*, likely *Nigella arvensis*, known for its finely divided leaves.</note>,

**Page 45. after**, <term>auxoousias</term> <note>original: <term>ἀυξωουσιας</term>. A Greek term likely referring to the "essential growth" or "vitality" of a plant.</note>, **add:** Its juice smeared on, or linen cloths soaked in it and placed on burns, brings them to a scar as quickly as possible; nor does it allow—as I have experienced many times—the power of the fire to spread further.

**Page 54. after**, somewhat similar; **add:** For which reason it is also found named *Calycanthemon* <note>original: "cup-flower."</note> and *Heliophyes* <note>original: "sun-natured."</note> in those various lists of names; because it has a flower in the manner of a calyx, and it turns around like the Heliotrope <note>The "sun-turner," a plant traditionally believed to follow the sun's path.</note>, for which reason, as Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist whose commentaries on Dioscorides were the standard reference of the era.</note> reports, some have wrongly thought it to be the Heliotrope:

**Page 65. after**, at the end of Spring, **add:** or even in Autumn,

**Page 90. [after] Female Peony**, and similar things, **add:** such as Celery—specifically the tuberous Spurge <note>original: *Tithymali*. This refers to *Euphorbia*, a genus of plants containing milky, often irritating sap.</note>; its root is tuberous and has the shape of a Pear, and Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary source for botany and pharmacology for 1,500 years.</note> and Theophrastus <note>The ancient Greek successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany."</note> said it was similar to the root of the Asphodel, which has a multiple root; nor did Apion <note>Likely a reference to an ancient commentator or grammarian.</note> note that it had only a single root.

->Ardicinus Biandrà, Vicar General of Naples.<-

<summary>This page concludes the index of Fabio Colonna's work, detailing botanical entries from T to X and providing a list of typographical corrections. It concludes with the official ecclesiastical "Imprimatur" granting permission for the book to be printed in Naples.</summary>
<keywords>botany, pharmacology, Theophrastus, Valerian, diuretics, errata, Imprimatur, Naples, medical history</keywords>
